MBPLE book Dedications
To Prof. Dr. Danilo Beuche and Marco Ferrogalini, whose mentorship and
inspiration shaped my career and introduced me to the world of Product
Line Engineering.
To my grandparents, Angelo, Annamaria, and Maria, who remind me daily
of the strength and wisdom of my Italian roots.
And to my wife, Maria, and my dog, Kyoto, my companions on every
adventure around the world. Your support and love make every journey
worthwhile.
Marco Forlingieri
To all the fantastic engineers who are committed to making the world a
better place and to my family who make the world a great place for me
every day.
Tim Weilkiens

support have led me to where I am and made me who I am.

OceanofPDF.com
Foreword
Once upon a time, a unique trio got together: an Italian tool-vendor, a
French-Mexican executive, and German subject matter expert (it sounds
like the start of a joke, but this is serious stuff, folks!). We come from
different backgrounds, possess diverse skills, and pursue distinct goals, but
we share one undeniable passion called Model-Based Product Line
Engineering (MBPLE).
Several years ago, while working at Renault, Guillermo, the executive, had
an intuition: “What if we bring Model Based Systems Engineering (MBSE)
and Product Line Engineering (PLE) together?” It was the origin of
MBPLE, a concept Guillermo later applied at Alstom and Thales. Marco,
now an executive tool vendor, read the work of Guillermo and decided to
make PLE and MBSE his main expertise, applying it at Bombardier
Transportation and later at Airbus. Tim, the subject matter expert, wrote
VAMOS (Weilkiens 2016), a book about modeling the variability with
SysML, which captured the attention of the other two, being the first and
only source about the topic. The world of systems engineering is small and
thanks to International Council on Systems Engineering (INCOSE) we got
to know each other at the point that we, the trio, decided to put in writing all
the knowledge and expertise gathered about the topic. We wanted to keep
the concepts as simple as possible to reach managers, engineers, and
students. This is how the idea of this book came to be.
In fact, only a few books exist about product lines, much less about their
development, and nothing, so far, about MBPLE. This is because product
lines are akin to mythological creatures – mysterious and elusive. Like the
legendary Nessie in Loch Ness, the enigmatic UFO sightings or a politician
keeping promises once elected, product lines are talked about by some who
claim to have experienced them, but fail to prove their existence! And yet,
paradoxically, you encounter them in your everyday life. They manifest
when you purchase a car, wield a smartphone, drill the wall, dry your hands,
board a plane, or even when you turn on the air conditioning.
What is the secret behind product lines? How are they developed? Can
someone unveil the product line that, like Aristotle’s “first motor,”
generates countless derived products? Such a revelation proves elusive! Just
like a witness to an UFO sighting, the Loch Ness monster or a trustworthy
politician, many speak of it, pretending to know the truth, but most, again,
are unable to provide concrete evidence.
You might be skeptical, assuming that we, the authors of this book, are
merely another group of individuals pretending to have witnessed
something incredible without being able to prove it. But what about the five
industrial cases on the concrete application of MBPLE from Airbus, Thales,
MBDA, Raytheon and Belimo at the end of the book? Or the many
references provided throughout this text?
We do not ask you to believe us, we extend an invitation, a summons to join
us on this extraordinary expedition, where the mysteries of product lines
and their secret recipe await your discovery. Together, let us unravel the
enigma, peer behind the veil, and embark on a journey that will forever
transform our perception of the way we carry out your business thanks to a
deeper understanding of reuse, assets sharing and variability.
Welcome, fellow traveler, to this captivating odyssey and, paraphrasing
Ulysses’ words in Dante’s Divine Comedy (Alighieri 1995), keep in mind
that “you were not made to live as brutes, but to follow virtue and
knowledge of MBPLE.”
Marco Forlingieri
Tim Weilkiens
Hugo Guillermo Chalé-Gongora

New York: Knopf, distributed by Random House.
Weilkiens, T. (2016). Variant modeling with SysML. MBSE4U.

Preface
In the field of systems engineering, Product Line Engineering (PLE) has
emerged as a transformative approach for managing the complexity of
modern product families. First introduced in the 1990s, PLE laid the
groundwork for systematically developing families of software and
hardware. Over the years, it has evolved to meet the needs of increasingly
complex industries, including automotive, aerospace, defense, and beyond.
In today’s era of digital transformation, PLE is essential for maintaining
competitiveness. The complexity of product development cycles now
demands an integrated, model-based approach that enables organizations to
achieve digital continuity and efficient lifecycle management. For decades,
International Council on Systems Engineering has played a central role in
the evolution of systems engineering, embracing emerging disciplines like
PLE and adapting to model-based advancements.
Recognizing the importance of PLE in advancing systems engineering, the
International Council on Systems Engineering (INCOSE) established the
PLE Working Group in 2012. This group plays a critical role in
standardizing PLE practices and fostering global collaboration across
sectors. Aligned with INCOSE’s mission to promote systems engineering,
the PLE Working Group has contributed significantly to the evolution of
PLE and, more recently, Model-Based Product Line Engineering (MBPLE).
The authors of this book are recognized international experts, bringing
extensive experience in both the theory and practical application of MBPLE
across industries. Their deep involvement in INCOSE and contributions to
major organizations and international standards make them uniquely
qualified to present this comprehensive guide.
The publication of ISO/IEC 26550:2015 marked a significant milestone for
PLE, providing a foundational standard for product line engineering and
management. This was further strengthened by ISO/IEC 26580:2021, which
introduced feature-based methods for managing PLE across industries.
INCOSE’s PLE Working Group has played a vital role in formalizing these
standards, ensuring a cohesive framework for PLE across sectors and
fostering collaboration within the systems engineering community.
The combination of Model-Based Systems Engineering (MBSE) with PLE
– now known as MBPLE – has become a cornerstone of innovation. By
leveraging models, MBPLE enables engineers and organizations to manage
product family variations and configurations with unprecedented precision
and flexibility. This integrated, model-based approach has made it possible
to address the complexities of today’s product lifecycles, delivering
improved digital continuity and competitive advantages.
While the evolution of MBPLE continues to advance, it is accompanied by
diverse interpretations and emerging trends, creating a need for clarity and
guidance. This book fills a critical gap by offering a comprehensive guide
to MBPLE, combining strategic insights with foundational concepts and
practical implementation techniques, particularly in Systems Modeling
Language (SysML). Through real-world case studies and insights from PLE
pioneers, it explores the transformative power of MBPLE, including the
potential impact of Artificial Intelligence and its associated opportunities
and risks. This book serves as an essential resource for anyone aiming to
learn and leverage MBPLE in today’s increasingly complex and
competitive product development environments.
Ralf Hartmann, INCOSE President

Chapter 1
Introduction
Model-based product line engineering (MBPLE) is a broad topic involving
many stakeholders. For most companies, MBPLE’s transformational nature
concerns almost all engineering activities and operations. Leaders need to
understand MBPLE to plan and implement it strategically, as well as to
understand and frame its application. But MBPLE principles, concepts, and
methods must also be understood by a wider population, including the
following:
Process owners who create and adapt MBPLE methods and tools to
meet organization-specific requirements.
Engineers who use MBPLE methods directly to create feature models
and shared assets supersets.
Engineers and product owners who develop parts of the product line.
Students who learn systems engineering, product line engineering
(PLE), and model-based approaches.
This book discusses MBPLE as a central topic, covering all its facets,
including the enabling factors for a successful implementation of MBPLE
in an organization. For readers who are new to the topic or who would like
to acquire a broad understanding of MBPLE, reading the book following
the order of the sections might be the best approach. However, readers who
are interested in a particular topic or who have previous knowledge of PLE
can browse freely through the different sections of the book.
Part I discusses the motivation of MBPLE, which is important for all
MBPLE stakeholders. Like all engineering approaches, MBPLE is only a
means to an end. Therefore, it is important and helpful to have a good
understanding of the purpose of MBPLE.
Part II describes the fundamental concepts of MBPLE, independently of
concrete implementations of modeling languages and tools. Like Part I, Part
II is also an important reading for all MBPLE stakeholders. The chapters in
Part II look at the historical development of model-based approaches and
PLE, as well as relevant standards such as ISO/IEC 26550 (ISO/IEC 2015)
and ISO/IEC 26580 (ISO/IEC 2021), which provide the framework wherein
the MBPLE concepts are presented.
Part III explains the technical implementation of MBPLE and is of
particular interest to those who use MBPLE directly. One focus is on the
models with SysML, whereby both the widely used SysML v1 and the new
SysML v2 are used.
Part IV explains the adoption of MBPLE in organizations, a significant
topic for leadership roles. The chapters consider both the investment and
return on investment of introducing MBPLE, the processes, the methods,
the organization, the information model, and the tool chains. This part is
rounded off by a chapter with an interview of two PLE pioneers, Dr. Danilo
Beuche from PTC and Dr. Charles Krueger from Big Lever Software.
Part V intends to show that the book presents MBPLE not only in theory
but also in real-life practice. Five chapters focus on industrial cases about
adopting MBPLE from five different organizations.
In the appendix, a glossary lists the most important MBPLE terms for
reference.

Part I
Motivation
The first part of the book focuses almost solely on the purpose of Model-
Based Product Line Engineering (MBPLE). This emphasis is intentional,
based on the authors’ experiences that company members often lose sight of
the overall purpose of their initiatives. Frequently, they mistake tools and
methods (such as digital transformation, modeling, agile practices, or digital
twins, to name just a few) for end goals. Avoiding this confusion is crucial
for MBPLE due to its transdisciplinary nature, the required investments,
and the need to implement it across the whole company.
Chapter 2 begins by drawing a picture of the context in which today’s
products are developed. The chapter briefly explores complexity and
variability as well as their implications on the methods needed to develop
and deliver products that are adapted to the needs of different customers.
This is the object of Chapter 3, which presents different reuse patterns along
with their advantages and disadvantages. Special attention is given to the
dangers of implementing unplanned reuse strategies and to the prerequisites
that should be in place to ensure a successful implementation of a product
line approach.
Chapter 4 explains the difference between document-based engineering and
model-based engineering. The growth in the adoption of model-based
engineering as well as the perspectives of its evolution are also explored.
The chapter presents the benefits and challenges associated with model-
based engineering. Chapter 5 is a logical complement to Chapter 4 as it
proposes a similar approach to explain the differences between the delivery
of single products and the delivery of products in a product-line-based
approach. This chapter explains the advantages of transitioning to a
programmatic approach to manage the products of a product line as a single
entity, as opposed to managing a plethora of separate development projects.
Chapter 6 closes Part I with an analysis of digital threads and digital
continuity as enablers to allow managing data during all the stages of the
product line life cycle and across the different products of a product line in
a consistent way. The chapter also underlines the importance of the
openness of IT solutions and the need to connect these in integrated
networks to support MBPLE in an effective manner.
OceanofPDF.com
Chapter 2
Complexity and Variability
2.1 Introduction
As the discipline of systems engineering evolves, it must confront the
growing complexity inherent in its expansive scope. This chapter first
explores in Section 2.2 the dual drivers of complexity identified by the
International Council on Systems Engineering (INCOSE) in its Vision 2035
(INCOSE 2022): technology-driven and scope-driven complexity.
Technology-driven complexity is fueled by fast technological
advancements, while scope-driven complexity emerges from the increasing
size and interdependencies within the development scope. We explore
deeper each driver, emphasizing their distinctive impacts on the field.
Section 2.3 addresses the concept of variability driven by customization in
product development. Customization, while enhancing individuality and
flexibility, often results in a diverse range of product variants.
The combination of complexity and variability are discussed in Section 2.4,
two trends that are deeply interrelated and form the challenges in modern
product and systems engineering, emerging as a key focus of the chapter.
Through a few examples, it illustrates the difficulties of balancing high
variability demands with the need to manage complexity effectively.
Ultimately, this chapter contextualizes these concepts within the broader
framework of systems engineering, emphasizing the criticality of innovative
development paradigms to address the combined challenges of increasing
complexity and variability demands in the development of complex
systems.
2.2 Complexity
In systems engineering, complexity has always been a key aspect to
address. The field itself developed to better handle the increasing scale,
interconnections, and complexity in systems development. Professor de
Weck (Conservation of Complexity 2023) discussed the rising complexity
in today’s products and systems, noting that it is much higher than in the
past, as highlighted in Figure 2.1. This complexity is intended to lead to
enhanced performance and, possibly, increased resilience. It is a common
understanding among systems engineers that complexity is escalating each
year due to rapidly changing contexts, the growing interdependencies of
systems, and more challenging projects taken on by organizations and
governments. He also pointed out how monumental achievements like the
lunar landing, the International Space Station, 20-hour transoceanic flights,
or capturing images of the earliest proto-galaxies post-Big Bang are made
possible by the coordinated effort of numerous components, including
hardware, software, and human collaboration. However, he also emphasized
that many aerospace programs are facing rising costs and challenges in
management, partly due to a limited grasp of how system performance,
complexity, and the effort needed for design, construction, and validation
are interrelated. This can be also explained by what he theorizes as the
“First Law of System Science for Conservation of Complexity.” In simpler
terms, it suggests that the complexity of a system is directly related to
expected improvement in its performance, adjusted by the amount of work
and resources put into its development and construction (Conservation of
Complexity 2023).
Figure 2.1 Representation of the increasing complexity in history.
Source: Adapted from INCOSE 2022.
In systems engineering, the typical process that aims at managing
complexity involves breaking down a “problem” into smaller, more
manageable parts, designing solutions for these individual parts, and then
reassembling them to form the complete system. This approach is effective
for “complicated” systems with fixed interactions among parts, even if they
contain many interrelated components and may exhibit unpredictable
behavior (INCOSE 2016).
However, this method encounters difficulties when new technologies are
integrated into traditional systems and when the scope of these systems
expands to ambitious developments. In complex systems, the emergent
properties crucial to the system’s functionality cannot be fully understood
by examining the individual components separately. These properties only
become apparent when considering the system as a whole. According to
INCOSE’s Vision 2035 (INCOSE 2022), the complexity in engineering is
continuously escalating, primarily due to two key factors: technology-
driven complexity and scope-driven complexity. Other factors contribute to
an increase of complexity in systems engineering, such as the complexity of
the business context or of the organization in which the systems are
developed. However, the focus here is on the complexity intrinsic to the
system under development. Let’s examine both drivers in detail.
2.2.1 Technology-driven Complexity
Technology-driven complexity arises from the rapid pace of technological
advancements and their integration into systems and products. This type of
complexity is often characterized by the incorporation of cutting-edge
technologies, which, while enhancing capabilities, also add challenges to
the system’s design, operation, and maintenance, as exemplified in Figure
2.2.
Figure 2.2 Representation of the technology-driven complexity
driver.
Source: Adapted from INCOSE 2022.
For instance, the implementation of software-defined vehicles in the
automotive industry describes the complexity of technology-driven vehicles
well. A software-defined vehicle is any vehicle that manages its operations,
adds functionality, and enables new features primarily or entirely through
software. Tesla cars are the most famous example. Software-defined
vehicles are the next evolution of the automotive industry. Their
architecture usually divides the vehicle’s functions into different server
zones, such as infotainment, safety systems, and vehicle control. Each zone
integrates advanced technologies like sensor fusion, connectivity modules,
and real-time data processing. The challenge lies in harmonizing these
technologies to work together, ensuring vehicle performance and safety in
diverse driving conditions. This complexity is compounded by the need to
constantly update and maintain each server zone over the air to meet
evolving technological standards and consumer demands (Burkacky et al.
2023).
2.2.2 Scope-driven Complexity
Scope-driven complexity emerges from the expansion in the scale and
interconnectedness of systems. It reflects the transition from developing
standalone products to creating systems part of more extensive, often
interconnected networks, as illustrated in Figure 2.3.
Figure 2.3 Representation of the scope-driven complexity driver.
Source: Adapted from INCOSE 2022.
Extending the example of software-defined vehicles, scope-driven
complexity becomes apparent when these vehicles connect to more
extensive networks. Features like over-the-air software updates and vehicle-
to-everything communication add layers of complexity. The challenge is
ensuring the vehicle’s different server zones work well with these external
connections, maintaining reliable and secure performance in an
interconnected setting. This shows how expanding the scope of vehicle
systems naturally makes them more complex.
In summary, understanding complexity in systems engineering involves
recognizing the multifaceted challenges posed by technological
advancements and expanding project scopes. As we continue to push the
boundaries of what is possible, mastery of complexity becomes a critical
skill in the engineer’s toolkit.
2.3 Variability
In product development, customization refers to the process of tailoring
products or systems to meet the specific requirements of a customer or
market segment. This often involves modifying or configuring the design,
features, functionalities, or even the aesthetics of a product to align with
distinct preferences, needs, or operational environments. From a portfolio
perspective, customized products or systems exhibit variability, as their
characteristics may differ among the members of the product portfolio.
Unlike mass production, which focuses on uniformity and scale,
customization emphasizes individuality and flexibility, often resulting in
diverse product variants. Customization is the primary driver of variability
within a product portfolio.
With increasing demand for customization, companies face the double
challenge of providing market – or customer-specific variety while
mastering the consequences of high variability in engineering and
production. Especially in an increasingly saturated market, new products
must find ways to differentiate themselves from the competition, like
distinctively meeting specific customer needs to enhance customer’s
satisfaction (Simpson et al. 2005).
However, as observed by Meyer and Lehnerd (1997), focusing on
individual customer preferences often leads to overlooking commonality
and standardization across product lines. This can result in an
overwhelming diversification of products and parts. While offering a
comprehensive product variety has merits, it can also incur significant costs
and complexity within a company.
Have you ever noticed, while traveling on different flights, even within the
exact airline or across various airlines, which the airplane model might be
the same, yet many aspects inside differ significantly? For instance,
consider the variability in-cabin configuration and layout, the number and
style of toilets, infotainment systems, or even the types of movies available
during the flight. These differences result from airlines’ deliberate
customization and high differentiation to enhance their brand identity and
customer service offerings. However, this level of variability often becomes
a “nightmare” for airplane manufacturers and their suppliers. They
frequently face the challenging task of reworking complex systems to
accommodate these customizations, sometimes questioning the necessity of
altering even minor details, like the exact millimetric position of power and
water outlets within the galley of a commercial aircraft!
If not adequately managed, variability can significantly impact the design
and development of systems in several ways:
Increased complexity: high variability introduces complexity into the
design process, requiring engineers to consider a broader range of
variables and potential configurations.
Design flexibility: More flexible design approaches are needed to
accommodate changes and variations without extensive redesigns or
cost overruns.
Production and supply chain adjustments: Variables impact production
processes and supply chains, which need to be adaptable to produce a
variety of customized products efficiently.
Increased costs: while customization can lead to higher customer
satisfaction and market differentiation, variability often leads to
increased production costs and complexity in inventory management.
In summary, customization in product development reflects the dynamic
and varied needs of customers and market trends. However, this leads to
increased variability, which, if not properly managed, can hinder project
success and overall organizational performance.
2.4 The Trade-off Between Complexity and
Variability
The increasing complexity in engineering and the escalating demand for
customization leading to higher variability are not isolated trends; instead,
they are profoundly interconnected and often feed into each other, creating
additional challenges in modern systems engineering.
Have you ever considered that a European, South America, or Southeast
Asia metro system might be based on the identical product family? This
idea was pursued by Bombardier Transportation in 2014 with their “Entry
Segment Metro” concept (Forlingieri 2014). They aimed to create a metro
design that could be adapted across different regions. However, the project
encountered significant challenges and ultimately did not materialize.
One of the primary reasons for this was that metro systems and light rail
vehicles often symbolize a city’s identity, necessitating unique and strongly
differentiated designs. Additionally, the need to comply with diverse local
norms and regulations significantly increased the complexity and the
potential number of variants required. However, other rolling stock
products, such as high-speed trains, may require heavier customization,
going beyond style and aesthetics into the specific train architecture (Chalé
Góngora et al. 2014).
This example showcases the trade-off between the desire for high
customization and the goal of limiting complexity, which arises from the
variability of the product variants. The effort to customize a metro system
or a light rail vehicle across various geographical and cultural contexts
shows how challenging it can be to manage both these aspects effectively.
This applies in particular to a category known as complex products and
systems (CoPS). They consist of prime products that demonstrate a high
degree of customization and complexity (Hobday 1998). These are high-
cost, engineering-intensive items, encompassing a broad range of tailored
components, specialized knowledge, and involvement from various
organizations. The complexity of CoPS arises not just from their technical
difficulties but also from the extensive customization required to meet the
unique demands of each project (Forlingieri 2014). Naval, Aerospace, and
Civil Engineering are just some of the most representative industries where
this category of systems is produced.
The CoPS category is just one of the possible areas where the combination
of variability and complexity closely interact.
To conclude, addressing diverse business context factors, specific customer
needs, and increasing complexity, particularly within a highly regulated
environment, requires new engineering practices and methods. These
approaches help to balance variability and complexity in today’s systems.
You will find some answers later in this book!

OceanofPDF.com
Chapter 3
Reuse Strategies
3.1 Introduction
In today’s highly competitive markets, most organizations usually try to
leverage previously developed assets for their reuse into a new product.
This understanding of reuse, shared by most common folk, is one of the
more widespread in the engineering of systems: using again what one has
done before to produce a solution that is similar or close to a previous one.
The purpose of reuse, however, is often much less formalized or even
understood: produce the same thing or something similar, only faster and
better. In other words, a reuse strategy should be set to produce clearly
stated outcomes, such as improving product characteristics, like quality and
performance, or program objectives, like cost-effectiveness, time to
delivery, or risk mitigation.
Indeed, in most industrial organizations, practically no system is created
from scratch. Engineers are most likely to reuse knowledge from a previous
project or product in the form of documents, procedures, diagrams, or
models. The problem is that this knowledge, albeit optimal for local usage,
is very often partial and sometimes even inadequate when used in a
different context. In siloed organizations, this usually results in
incompatibilities of engineering artifacts, inconsistent technical data, and
duplication of information in different repositories, making the
implementation of the principles exposed in Chapters 5 and 6 extremely
challenging. In a nutshell, these practices make reuse very problematic at a
cross-company level and hamper the creation of well-structured, corporate-
wide product lines. Whilst Section 3.2 introduces some of the industrial
reuse approaches, Section 3.3 provides a brief analysis of the benefits and
risks of those reuse patterns.
3.2 Different Reuse Approaches
As an engineering practice, reuse is well-documented in software and
manufacturing, where examples of successful reuse approaches are well
known, such as configurable software modules, standard software
architectures, flexible manufacturing systems, or automobile platform
sharing (Holweg 2008). Reuse is also associated with concepts like
modular architectures, system families, and standardization of parts.
The origins of product line engineering (PLE), as the main reuse practice of
this book, are often traced back to McIlroy’s work on “Mass Produced
Software Components” (McIlroy 1968) (see Chapter 10 for a more detailed
history of PLE).
However, the formalization of PLE and other reuse practices for their
application to the engineering of large-scale systems is still scarce and
relatively new (International Council on Systems Engineering [INCOSE]
2019) despite historical, de facto, or casual reuse in industry, like the
reutilization of existing specification documents, technical drawings, or test
procedures, and the existence of some reports on successful industrial
applications (Chalé Góngora et al. 2014; Flores et al. 2013; Gregg et al.
2016). Figure 3.1 represents a spectrum of different reuse patterns explained
in the sections below.
Figure 3.1 Different reuse patterns.
Source: Adapted from Czarnecki, K.
3.2.1 Clone and Own
Usually associated with the search for short-term benefits, clone-and-own
(or branch-and-merge) approaches are the easiest to implement since
engineering teams are usually independent and able to manage engineering
assets as they like. There is practically no sharing among the different
branches, and separate efforts to maintain each individual branch are
required. This approach might be a suitable scheme for businesses in which
single product instances are delivered over very large periods of time. It
might also be appropriate for companies that want to reuse existing
products to enter a new market or propose a groundbreaking application in
an opportunistic way. The success of this approach relies on mastering the
technical debt and on top-class configuration management processes.
3.2.2 Component Library and Framework
Component library and framework approaches produce more substantial
benefits than the clone-and-own approach. The component library approach
is based on the definition of standard components. Besides the need to
master the interactions and interdependencies amongst the components, the
biggest challenge of the component library approach is to ensure that the
emerging properties of the composed product are those expected by the
customers. In the framework approach, efforts would instead be dedicated
to the definition of standard interfaces in such a way that any component
that complies with the standard can potentially be used to “compose” a
particular product. The Autosar (Automotive Open System Architecture)
framework (Autosar 2024) is a well-known example of a standardized
software framework supporting a composable approach for electric and
electronic system architectures in the automotive industry.
In practice, these two approaches are carried out simultaneously to foster
compsability and ease of integration. They are adapted to products evolving
in markets that require the delivery of a rather stable set of functionalities
over time. These functionalities can be allocated to dedicated components
that would evolve at a slow pace, while isolating those components that
support fast-evolving functions or technologies. The main difficulties of the
component library and framework approaches are that the evolution of
standard components must be thoroughly planned and controlled and that
managing the definition of standard interfaces can be a complicated task in
large-scale systems.
3.2.3 Superset Platform
The superset platform approach is closely related to advanced PLE
approaches proposed in this book, as explained below. In this approach, a
“platform” of asset supersets is designed and made available for its use in
customer projects. This is done through the configuration of the supersets to
match the needs of a given customer. The term 150% is used to illustrate the
fact that the supersets contain more than what it would take to define a
single customer solution, which would sum up to 100%. The benefits of this
approach include substantial reuse and automation of the production of
engineering assets, which requires capabilities to control variability
efficiently, master the dependencies amongst the supersets, and control the
configuration mechanism of specific products to keep this from becoming a
complex task.
3.3 Quick Benefits Versus Risk Analysis of
Reuse Patterns
To decide which reuse pattern should be implemented in a given
organization, a business case must be established, taking into consideration
the business context and strategy of the organization. The business case
should consider the cost-effective investment of engineering resources and
time evidenced by the elimination of low-value, trivial, repetitive,
demotivating, and energy-consuming tasks. This liberatory effect should
translate into a more profitable investment of energy and time in high-value
activities and innovation. Examples of possible activities and outcomes
include, but are not limited to, improving products, reducing organizational
complexity, reducing time to market and lead time, reducing engineering
costs and efforts, exploring possibilities, and advancing business objectives.
Chapter 21 presents the elements that can be used to build a compelling
business case for MBPLE.
When the products within a portfolio are engineered as individual, unique
solutions, clown-and-own techniques usually result in duplication and
divergent engineering efforts for every “branch” that is created. Since
controlling the consistency of the different branches usually relies on tacit
knowledge and interpersonal communication, it is inevitably an error-prone
activity. This difficulty (usually known as “branching hell”) increases in
organizations in which each discipline adopts ad-hoc techniques for
managing the variation among the different products of the portfolio,
introducing further inconsistencies and misunderstandings. This way of
working systematically translates into process-induced complexity, which
adds on top of the intrinsic complexity of the products and of the business
contexts in which organizations evolve.
What sets mature PLE practices apart from the approach mentioned above
is their holistic nature and the systems engineering groundings that support
them. PLE can be defined as the engineering of a product line (i.e. a family
of similar products with variations in features and functions) as a single
entity, using a shared set of engineering assets and an efficient means of
production, taking advantage of the commonality shared across the family,
while efficiently and systematically managing the variation among the
products (more details are given in Chapter 5). This implies that a
programmatic approach must be put in place to manage the portfolio of
customer projects (i.e. those that benefit from the shared assets supersets of
the product line) as a single entity too, instead of managing a multitude of
individual projects separately. Such an approach enables an organization to
perform informed trade-offs and make balanced cost-benefit decisions
regarding investments, with a view on short-term expectations and long-
term goals, particularly business profitability.
The consolidation of shared asset supersets within disciplines eliminates
duplication, inconsistencies, and divergence across the products of the
family, on the one hand. On the other hand, consistent transdisciplinary
management of variation across the different supersets and across the
product line life cycle, made possible by its formalization in a variability
model, helps avoid miscommunication and misalignment amongst
disciplines (Chalé Góngora and Greugny 2017). Although this delivery
approach might be appealing and appear as sheer common sense, it
represents a true breakthrough and, for many organizations, a major shift
that requires changes in governance and organizational structures, as well as
strong commitment from all functions, disciplines, and leadership. These
aspects will be further developed in Part IV of this book: Adoption of
MBPLE.
To conclude, PLE relies on processes that help manage the life cycle of the
portfolio of products of an organization (as well as the underlying reusable
assets used to perform the architecting, design, and evolution of the
portfolio) in order to maximize the benefits of reuse. Reusing assets,
however, should be the result of a well-documented decision process,
meaning that implementing PLE should require upfront investment and
forethought. And yet it is safe to postulate that, in most organizations, one
could find examples where reusing an asset actually proved to be less
profitable over the system life cycle than developing a new one: while the
initial engineering cost of the asset might have been low, the overall costs
induced by debugging, repair, validation, warranty expenses, or penalties
slowly but surely end up consuming the originally planned profits (Wymore
and Bahill 2000; Chalé Góngora et al. 2014). To put it simply, copy-pasting
assets from one project to another without an overarching strategy cannot
be considered PLE (or engineering, for that matter), and it cannot yield by
itself all the potential benefits of PLE.

Chapter 4
From Document-based to Model-based
Engineering
4.1 Introduction
The increasing use of models in engineering is not a new topic. However,
the adoption rate remains modest, with document-based engineering still
prevailing in many sectors. As discussed in Chapter 2, with the escalating
complexity of systems and the organizations developing them and the ever-
accelerating pace of developments, adopting models is the most effective
alternative to document-centric engineering.
Section 4.2 discusses the challenges of document-based engineering, and
Section 4.3 presents model-based engineering (MBE) as an approach to
overcoming these challenges.
4.2 Document-based Engineering
The number of documents increases, as does the number of dependencies
between their contents. A text document might reference a figure from a
spreadsheet, or a presentation may use a figure from a text document, and
so on (Figure 4.1).
Figure 4.1 Documents along the engineering V models.
With increasing complexity and demands for system capabilities, along
with growing constraints on quality improvement and cost-effectiveness,
attempting to apply “old” practices and tools to overcome these challenges
would be ill-advised. Here, “practices and tools” encompass mechanical
and software tools, methods, and modeling languages. Our progress in
systems engineering must not lag behind the need for system capabilities.
Figure 4.2 illustrates the “gap of missed improvements.” Commonly, the
systems we develop surpass the capabilities of the tools used to create them.
Project heroes often bridge the gap – people who, through their dedication
and expertise, enable organizations to develop great systems. As systems
evolve, this gap widens.
Figure 4.2 Gap of missed improvements.
This strain becomes apparent in organizations where the gap grows too
large. Symptoms include employees reaching their limits, experts leaving
the company, and a shift from proactive to reactive management. Such
organizations accumulate technical debt, which, if unrecognized, diverts
resources to “firefighting” rather than improving engineering tools. This
“vicious circle” exacerbates problems over time as the capabilities of
engineering tools remain stagnant.
Any improvement, including that of engineering tools, often follows a
“hockey stick” curve, where performance initially declines before rising
and achieving the desired enhancements.
4.3 Model-based Engineering
The introduction of MBE is generally regarded as an improvement in
engineering tools and practices. There are many advantages of MBE. In a
nutshell, it allows us to capture knowledge formally or semi-formally,
making valuable engineering information accessible to machines. With this,
machines can then support engineering processes. If information is stored in
digital documents, machines can “understand” and support the document
structure, such as layouts, page counts, graphics, and references. However,
they do not understand the meaning – or the semantics – of the contents.
If machines understand the semantics of engineering information, they can
help ensure better quality control throughout the development process, as
models can be automatically checked for consistency, completeness, and
correctness. This reduces the risk of errors and helps to ensure that the final
product will meet all requirements.
MBE also allows greater collaboration between different stakeholders, as
everyone can understand, work, and see changes in real time using the same
model. This can help reduce interpretation errors and misunderstandings
that may occur when working with traditional document-based approaches.
The model serves as a single source of truth for the entire project and
ensures all stakeholders have access to the latest information. Figure 4.3
shows, in contrast to Figure 4.1, the usage of models in engineering.
Figure 4.3 Models along the engineering V’s.
Manifold advantages exist; nevertheless, it is fair to state that the
investment is also high. Introducing a model-based approach requires
investments in people’s training, process adaptation, and licenses of the
necessary software tools. It is widely accepted that MBSE brings benefits or
a return on investment, but it should also be noted that there is little proven
evidence to date (Henderson and Salado 2021). No evidence does not mean
absence.
The increased usage of model-based approaches occurs in many
engineering disciplines. For example, in mechanical and electrical
engineering, the shift from drawing boards to computer-aided design (CAD)
(including electronic computer-aided design [ECAD] and mechanical
computer-aided design [MCAD], or in software engineering, from source
code in simple text editors to development environments that understand
the semantics of the code and provide support functions, or the modeling of
software with the Unified Modeling Language (Object Management Group
[OMG 2007).
The shift from document-based to model-based systems engineering
(MBSE) can be well observed in the vision documents published by the
International Council on Systems Engineering (INCOSE)1. It clearly states
that MBSE is “systems engineering enhanced by the usage of models over
documents.” For those who do not know, INCOSE is the leading worldwide
organization for systems engineering. The vision documents state the
consolidated opinion of the systems engineering community about how the
future of systems engineering will look. The development of the three
visions 2020, 2025, and 2035 clearly shows how the systems engineering
community anticipates the importance of MBSE, embraces it, and then
takes up the capabilities that build on it.
In 2007, INCOSE published its 2020 vision (INCOSE 2007). Also, in the
same year, MBSE was already a hot topic because it was the year when
SysML v1.0 was officially released by OMG (2017). However, although the
community intensively discussed it, MBSE approaches were not widely
adopted in the industry. The vision forecasted the state of systems
engineering for 13 years in the future in 2020. It addresses model-based as
follows:
“In many respects, the future of systems engineering can be said to be
“model-based”. A key driver will be the continued evolution of complex,
intelligent, global systems that exceed the ability of the humans who design
them to comprehend and control all aspects of the systems they are
creating.”
The year 2020 has already passed, and in retrospect, the forecast was
appropriate. Seven years later (in 2014), INCOSE published its next vision
for 2025 (INCOSE 2014). The vision 2025 strengthens the statement about
MBSE by saying: “MBSE will become the “norm” for systems
engineering.”
The latest INCOSE Vision 2035, published in 2022, adds to this list of
forecasts “a family of unified, integrated MBSE-systems modeling and
simulation frameworks” by 2035 (INCOSE 2022).
That statement adds simulation, which is an important purpose of model-
based approaches. If machines understand the content, they can simulate
aspects to support or even drive analysis, verification, and other engineering
tasks.
Only the transition from document-based to descriptive MBSE, which
already offers many advantages, lays the foundation for comprehensive
simulations, analyses, and the use of artificial intelligence technologies.
These technologies can all help close the gap in Figure 4.2.
So, what exactly is “model-based,” you might be asking yourself by now? It
may be obvious in the first view, but it can raise several questions in the
second view. For example, using SysML seems to be “model-based,” but
what if you just draw the diagrams in a drawing tool? Is a spreadsheet
“model-based”? You will find answers and a discussion of the definitions of
MBSE and models in Chapter 8.
References
Henderson, K. and Salado, A. (2021). Value and benefits of model-based
systems engineering (MBSE): Evidence from the literature. Systems
Engineering 24 (1): 51–66. https://doi.org/10.1002/sys.21566.
International Council on Systems Engineering (INCOSE) (2007). Systems
Engineering Vision 2020. San Diego: International Council on Systems
Engineering.
International Council on Systems Engineering (INCOSE) (2014). Systems
Engineering Vision 2025. San Diego: International Council on Systems
Engineering.
International Council on Systems Engineering (INCOSE) (2022). Systems
Engineering Vision 2035. San Diego: International Council on Systems
Engineering. https://www.incose.org/about-systems-engineering/se-
vision-2035 (accessed 20 April 2023).
Object Management Group (OMG) (2007). ‘OMG System Modeling
Language Version 1.0, formal/07-09-01’. Object Management Group.
Object Management Group (OMG) (2017). ‘Unified Modeling Language
Version 2.5.1, formal/17-12-05’. Object Management Group.

1 www.incose.org.
OceanofPDF.com
Chapter 5
From Single Product to Product-line-based
Delivery Approach
5.1 Introduction
Companies across all industrial sectors are constantly striving to deliver
better (safer, more reliable, more attractive) products at a faster pace and
with higher profits in extremely competitive markets. Delivering the
appropriate product with ever shorter time-to-market and at competitive
costs becomes not only a critical factor for survival in historically saturated
markets but also to get a foothold in new emerging markets. There are many
different ways to face this challenge. However, in both product-push
markets, where companies can manufacture off-the-shelf products ready for
the delivery to customers, and in more traditional tender-driven markets,
organizations are increasingly recognizing the necessity to transition from
traditional project-oriented methodologies to a more holistic and efficient
product-line-based approach.
This chapter explains the critical importance of this shift, beginning with
displaying the challenges derived from project-oriented delivery approaches
in Section 5.2. These challenges often manifest as limitations in scalability,
adaptability, and resource optimization, hindering an organization’s ability
to remain competitive.
Section 5.3 shows how the concept of product-line development emerges as
a central solution to these challenges. A product-line-based delivery
approach is finally introduced in Section 5.4. This approach contrasts
strongly with the isolated and often siloed nature of project-oriented
approaches, offering a more integrated and efficient path to product
creation.
In Section 5.4, we introduce the concept of product line and other ancillary
concepts such as product platform and product family. Section 5.5
concludes by underlining how organizations can exploit the synergies of
commonality and variability within their product lines, leading to enhanced
innovation, reduced time-to-market, and greater cost-effectiveness.
This chapter aims to provide the motivation for a change in the delivery
approach, highlighting how a product-line-based methodology is not merely
a response to the challenges of project-oriented approaches but a strategic
move towards sustained success and market leadership.
5.2 Project-oriented Approaches
In many organizations, projects serve as the foundation of their operations.
Even when organizations have a centralized engineering function and
release their products incrementally, they often deliver each product as a
standalone project, resulting in a lack of synergies across products (Young
and Clements 2017). While standalone projects offer advantages such as
temporary organization with framed resource allocation and clear time-
based deliverables, they can also lead to a siloed culture with limited
collaboration and minimal reuse of solutions across projects. Consequently,
the overall utilization of time and resources within the company may
become inefficient when considering the collective scope of all projects.
Organizations that adopt a project-oriented approach to manage each
project as a single entity, and face challenges when trying to manage a
portfolio of products due to the limited synergies within the portfolio.
In this setup, each product is often developed and evolved in isolation from
others, or the best-case scenario, begins as a copied version of a similar
product. This “clone” is then modified to meet the unique requirements of
the new product or customer. Unfortunately, this project-centric approach
does little to capitalize on the shared aspects or commonality among
products in a portfolio after the cloning process. This approach, known as
“clone-and-own” (see Chapter 3), also offers minimal benefits during the
maintenance and repair of a product, where data indicate that most products
utilize up to 90% of the project resources (Young and Clements 2017). In
the end, all this translates into a loss of knowledge and investments across
projects.
Within project-oriented environments, especially in large projects within the
naval, rolling stock, aerospace, and defense sectors, specific customer
requirements are often the primary driving force behind product
development. As introduced in Chapter 2 for the complex products and
systems (CoPS) category, these requirements emerge from a detailed and
competitive bid process, often beginning with calls for tenders and
culminating in the award and negotiation of customer specifications. For
each tender, companies design or adapt an existing product to meet these
specific and detailed customer requirements. This leads to design and
sourcing activities being heavily customized for each individual project,
which, along with complex systems and project organizations, make reuse
challenging.
This situation often results in an excessive number of hours dedicated to
parallel product developments. Sometimes, engineers discover the potential
for reuse almost inadvertently. For example, opportunities for reuse are
identified only after specific customer requests are raised or when an
engineer who worked on a previous project identifies similar requirements,
leading “by accident” to carryover of functions or components from past
projects (Forlingieri 2014).
5.3 Shifting Toward a More Efficient
Paradigm
Although organizations recognize the need for reuse within their product
portfolios, the implementation of these strategies faces numerous barriers
related to project constraints, the mindset of people, and the nature of the
product itself, similar to the CoPS category discussed in Chapter 2.
In many industries, there is a constantly growing need for systems that are
qualitatively better, safer, and more cost-effective, with shorter times to
market. Engineers work hard to make their systems simpler and easier to
use while incorporating the latest innovations. Moreover, as previously
mentioned, in most industries, systems are rarely built entirely from scratch.
Engineers often reuse elements from previous projects or products,
including documents, processes, or models (Chalé Góngora et al. 2014), in
an effort to improve quality, reduce costs, and speed up delivery.
While software development often relies on well-established reuse
practices, the formalization of reuse techniques in systems engineering is
relatively recent development. There is still a lot to learn about reuse
practices in real-world industrial settings (see Chapter 3), even though
engineers have been informally reusing requirements and design artifacts
for a long time.
Platform strategies are common practices in the industry aimed at reducing
resources such as cost and time during all stages of product development
through systematic reuse. In particular, in the automotive industry, they
refer to standardization and differentiation strategies for product
development, production process, and procurement based on the reuse of
underbody structures, which usually include the front floor, underfloor,
engine compartment, and frame (Muffatto 1999).
Product platforms enable the transfer of technology and the reuse of assets
across different projects, significantly cutting down lead time and
engineering hours for each new product compared to creating a new design
from scratch (Forlingieri 2024).
Standardized and pre-tested components lead to better product performance
and lower risk, thanks to the benefits of accumulated learning and
experience. This approach, also known as composability, helps companies
manage the increasing demand for diverse and customized products while
keeping complexity in check. Especially in stable markets that do not often
experience product disruption, establishing a strong product platform can
give companies a dominant position in the market and open doors to new
opportunities. Platform solutions are strategic means that can provide a
technological edge and facilitate the use of common assets in various
products or projects. However, the move toward strategic reuse, proactively
planned and supported by product platform solutions, often encounters
resistance from key players in project-oriented organizations, namely
project managers and engineering managers.
If you are reading this chapter, it is likely that you are involved in a project
or have experience working on one. You are aware that the project manager
holds the reins of authority in this temporary organizational setup. The
scenario we are about to explore is a familiar one in the context of projects,
especially when a company aims to implement reuse strategies within a
project-oriented culture.
Let us assume that the centralized engineering function of a rolling stock
manufacturer came up with the idea of developing a product platform to
offer enhanced adaptability for future customizations and upgrades. The
platform promises long-term industry leadership. However, the project
manager faced a tough decision: Adopting this platform would mean facing
the “lead penalty”–a delay in investment returns until the break-even point
was reached, resulting in increased initial costs and project timelines. The
project manager’s primary obligation is to ensure that the project remains
within its allocated budget and schedule!
The project manager’s performance is evaluated solely on the success of the
current project. Investing resources in a platform that would yield benefits
for future projects appears to be a risky and unappealing move. This
scenario, while specific to rolling stock development, mirrors similar
dilemmas across various industries, illustrating the often-difficult balance
between short-term project objectives and long-term business benefits.
Project managers are not the only actors who can pose obstacles in
promoting reuse practices within project-oriented organizations; middle
management and, to a certain extent, engineers can also play a significant
role in it. The “Not-Invented-Here” syndrome is a notable example where
engineers might be skeptical about reusing solutions developed by others in
a different project. This skepticism often comes from the belief that
externally developed solutions may not meet the specific needs of their
project. This might result in unnecessary duplication of engineering efforts,
inconsistencies in technical data, and missed opportunities for learning and
innovating.
To address these challenges, forward-thinking organizations have turned to
a product-line-based delivery approach. This approach aims to maximize
commonality within and across product lines, fostering collaboration,
innovation, and efficiency. Rather than treating each product as an isolated
project, these organizations focus on developing common and variable
assets that can be reused across multiple products and on managing these as
a single entity. The organization’s structure, policies, and development
processes are also adapted to a product line-based delivery approach.
By allocating most resources to the product line itself, rather than to
specific projects, organizations can establish a centralized hub of expertise.
This concentration of resources promotes collaboration and knowledge
sharing among functions and teams, breaking down the barriers between
projects and fostering a culture of continuous improvement. Since team
members have a clear view on long-term objectives and roadmaps and on
short-term deliveries expected by customer projects, they are better
equipped to find the right balance when making technical decisions.
So far, we have introduced some new concepts, such as “Product Platform”
and “Product Line.” It is now time to define these concepts and shed light
on them.
5.4 Product Platform, Product Family, and
Their Derived Products
The concept of “product platform,” or simply “platform,” is now a familiar
term among R&D engineers and product managers across various
industries. Its widespread usage and implementation have led to diverse
interpretations. Broadly speaking, platform thinking involves recognizing
and leveraging similarities across products and target markets to create and
deliver new offerings. It enables companies to structure and develop a
product platform, which is essential “a collection of subsystems and
interfaces forming a common foundation from which a variety of derivative
products (also known as “member products”) can efficiently evolve”
(Meyer and Lehnerd 1997).
Terms such as “product platform,” “product family” (or “product line”), and
“member products” are often used in this context. It is crucial to understand
that these terms have hierarchical differences and are not interchangeable,
with the exception of “product family” and “product line,” which can be
used synonymously (Forlingieri 2014). A product line is defined as a set of
products that share common features, functions, or characteristics and are
developed from a common set of core assets in a prescribed way (ISO/IEC
2015). A member product is a product that is part of a product line”
(ISO/IEC 2015). A product platform, which is technically a composable
product-line architecture is therefore neither the same as a member product
(quite intuitive at this point!) nor the same as a product line; it is one
possible way to implement product lines, and it refers to the common basis
of all individual products within a product line.
For instance, in automotive, sharing underbodies “platforms” between car
models can lead to a decrease in capital investment by up to 50%,
particularly in welding equipment, and a 30% reduction in product lead
time (Simpson et al. 2005). A prime example of a product platform from the
late 1990s is Volkswagen. Many are familiar with Volkswagen’s Platforms
A and C, which included essential components such as the floor group,
drive system, running gear, and parts of the cockpit. This platform is
effectively shared across four brands: Volkswagen, Audi, Seat, and Skoda.
In simple terms, models developed using Platform A or C is what we call
product lines, from which various member products can be created
(Simpson et al. 2005).
For simplification, in this case, we can refer to the member products as the
finished and configured cars, which customers ultimately purchase from the
dealership.
5.5 Product-line-based Delivery
To understand the concept of product-line-based delivery, it is a prerequisite
to introduce two key dimensions of the approach, first defined in the ISO
26550 (ISO/IEC 2015): domain engineering and application engineering.
While the terms have been quite used in the software domain, they may
result in misleading system development, probably due to their software
domain-related origin.
Domain engineering consists of the phase where the product line is
consistently defined in terms of features and related domain assets (aka
shared assets). On the other hand, application engineering represents the
phase where a product is configured out of the product line by selecting the
in-scope features and deriving the related domain assets into application
assets for their usage in the resulting products. As illustrated in Chapter 2,
the high level of customization required by a certain type of system does
not permit pure product standardization. The only way to boost reuse is the
implementation of a product-line-based development where the reuse is not
only focused on the product itself but also on the related engineering assets
(Chami et al. 2017).
Product-line delivery approaches always require the strategic development
of engineering assets that can serve multiple products. Figure 5.1 simplifies
the differences between a single-product development and a product-line
development.
Figure 5.1 Differences between single-product development and
product-line development.
In the first approach, products A’s and B’s requirements, design, and
realization assets are developed in parallel in an isolated manner. In
contrast, in a product-line development, both common and variable assets
are developed with the intent to serve both products A and B, avoiding
duplication and rework.
By embracing a product-line-based approach, organizations can unlock
several advantages:
Collaboration and synergies: as teams align around the product line
and share knowledge, resources, and best practices. This collaborative
environment fosters innovation, accelerates development cycles, and
ultimately leads to improved product quality.
Reuse of verified and validated engineering assets: rather than
reinventing the wheel for each product, organizations can leverage
existing assets that have undergone rigorous testing and validation.
This not only saves time and resources but also reduces the risk of
errors and improves overall product reliability.
Strategic collaboration within the supply chain: by aligning processes,
standards, and requirements across the product line, organizations can
establish stronger relationships with suppliers, partners, and other
stakeholders that are committed not just to a single product but to a
product line. This collaboration streamlines the development and
delivery of multiple member products, enhances supply chain
efficiency, and drives overall competitiveness.
Smoother identification of defects and solutions: in traditional project-
oriented development, if a defect is identified, all impacted projects
must individually address and rectify the issue. In contrast, within a
product-line-based development, the defect can be fixed within the
product line itself, benefiting all member products. This defect
resolution approach not only saves time and resources but also ensures
consistent product quality across the entire product line.
Efficient allocations of experienced engineers: rather than spreading
these valuable resources thinly across multiple projects, they can be
dedicated to the product line (out-of-cycle development), where their
expertise can be fully utilized. This concentration of knowledge leads
to higher-quality outputs and faster development cycles.
These are just some of the advantages that can arise from the shift of
organizations from project-oriented to product-line-based delivery. More
insights are shared in Chapter 21.
References

Design and Management (CSDM), Paris, France: Springer.

management solution with SysM. Prostep Journal, 2017_01.

transportation. University of Twente, Student Theses.
https://essay.utwente.nl/66384/.

OceanofPDF.com
Chapter 6
Digital Continuity and System Life Cycle
Management
6.1 Introduction
Digital continuity, as a key enabler of system life cycle management,
ensures that as the system evolves, all stakeholders – from designers to
operators – can depend on a single source of truth. It serves as the thread
that weaves through the life cycle, connecting disparate pieces of data into a
coherent whole. This chapter explores how digital continuity becomes a
necessity when dealing with variability spread across the multiple assets of
a product line. It shows that without the continuity of engineering data,
developing and managing product lines and their member products would
be impossible.
Section 6.2 reintroduces the concept of complexity and its demand for a
digital continuity of the engineering data. Section 6.3 provides the basic
concepts of the system life cycle and its importance across engineering
domains. The integration of various engineering disciplines and domains –
mechanical, electronic, and software – is no longer optional but required to
build complex systems. Section 6.4 shows how, in this context, the
integration of diverse engineering tools and data presents a significant
challenge, despite the advantages that it brings. The chapter then discusses
the role of frameworks such as the Open Services for Life Cycle
Collaboration (OSLC 2021) and other frameworks like modeling and
simulation information in a collaborative systems engineering context (ISO
2021). Finally, it concludes with Section 6.5, where the need of a digital
continuity becomes even more critical due to the third-dimensional aspect
added by the product line variability in the development artifacts.
6.2 The “Eternal Return” of Complexity
The “Eternal Return” is a Nietzschean philosophical concept that states that
time repeats itself in an infinite loop, with exactly the same events
continuing to occur in exactly the same way, over and over again, for
eternity (Nietzsche 1883). Similarly, the concept of “complexity” is treated
in this book!
Building on the concepts of complexity introduced earlier, it is clear that the
role of engineering has been transformed. While the technical and creative
facets remain valued, they are now part of a larger, more integrated
approach. The integration of advanced information technology solutions –
simplifying tools and tool chains – into every stage of development is not
just a response to complexity but a proactive strategy to cope with it.
In the past, engineers could focus separately on discrete components of a
product. Today, they must consider how these components interact with and
affect each other and the entire system – and even beyond, the system’s
operational environment. In fact, we can say that systems engineering for
today’s systems require a continuous digital fabric as a foundation to
connect every aspect of the system.
6.3 System Life Cycle and Multiple Domains
ISO/IEC/IEEE 15288 (2023) is a standard that defines a set of processes for
systems engineering. This standard is crucial in providing a structured
framework for the life cycle of systems, from their initial concepts through
retirement. The standard defines in detail the technical process that must be
put in place for the successful delivery of systems as well as the life cycle
stages of a system (ISO/IEC/IEEE 2023).
During these stages, a myriad of data is generated, ranging from
requirements and design documents to test reports and maintenance logs.
The integrity and availability of this information are critical for the
continuous progression from one stage to the next.
As we explore the varied domains, disciplines, and techniques involved in
system development – mechanical, electronic, software, modeling, and
simulations, to name a few – it becomes evident that the data produced
must be consistent and managed throughout the entire life cycle of the
system. This requirement extends beyond the confines of individual
disciplines, necessitating an environment where data generated during each
stage is not only reliable but also accessible across all domains (Eigner and
Stelzer 2009). In single-system developments, this leads to the need for
system life cycle management.
At each life cycle stage of the system, several domains are, in fact, involved
in the production of engineering assets. Their consistency and management
become crucial along the system engineering life cycles.
The production of data in each stage from multiple domains is a complex
task. Each domain may use specialized tools and methods to produce and
manage its data, but it is imperative that this data must remain synchronized
along the system development. Inconsistencies or inaccuracies in the data
can lead to errors, delays, and increased costs, which highlights the
importance of digital continuity.
Without it, consistency and efficiency can hardly be ensured, as each stage
builds upon the accuracy and completeness of the information generated in
the previous stages.
As we progress through the ISO/IEC/IEEE 15288 life cycle stages, the
collaborative effort of multiple disciplines contributes to the success of
system development. This collaboration is fueled by the data each domain
generates, relies on, and contributes to. The harmonization of this data
across the life cycle is not just beneficial but essential, forming the
backbone of an integrated, multidisciplinary approach to systems
engineering in the digital era. In addition, data production and exchange
extend beyond the confines of individual enterprises, incorporating
suppliers and other stakeholders into the fold. This extended enterprise
further justifies a digital continuity of data that allows for seamless data
exchange and collaboration, ensuring that all entities contribute effectively
to the product’s key success factors: time-to-market, cost, and quality
(Eigner and Stelzer 2009).
6.4 Digital Continuity and Its Challenges
One of the most pressing challenges in systems engineering today remains
the fragmentation across the engineering tools and data landscape
(International Council on Systems Engineering 2022). As mentioned earlier,
the goal is to integrate tools and data from various domains and vendors,
creating a federation across domain-specific tools to enable collaboration
and analysis.
For instance, the Department of Defense (DoD) has set forth engineering
foundational principles to guide future developments and facilitate data
creation and management (DoD 2023). These principles are the following:
Formalizing the development, integration, and use of models: This
involves using digital models to inform decision-making at both
enterprise and program levels, which is expected to facilitate more
informed decisions, greater insight, and increased transparency.
Providing an enduring, authoritative source of truth: This principle
emphasizes the need for digital threads, which are data-driven
architectures linking information from across the product life cycle.
These threads are envisioned to be the primary data and
communication platforms for a company’s products.
Incorporating technological innovation: The adoption of technological
advancements is crucial to improve engineering practices.
Establishing a supporting infrastructure: This involves creating
infrastructure to support various activities, enable collaboration, and
facilitate communication across stakeholders.
Transforming the workforce: It’s essential to equip and encourage the
workforce to adopt and support digital engineering across the product
life cycle.
However, implementing these principles comes with several challenges:
Integrating life cycle models and heterogeneous environments:
Ensuring consistency and traceability across various models that
describe different aspects of the system at different life cycle stages is
a significant challenge.
Maintaining “authoritative sources” for the entire system: This
involves managing consistency across domain models and tools, and
ensuring that changes in one domain are accurately reflected in related
domains.
Managing consistency across domain models and tools: It is necessary
to facilitate data consistency across life cycle models, which can be
challenging as models and their constituents evolve over time.
Enabling cross-life cycle digital viewpoints for stakeholders: Digital
threads should provide digital continuity, establish digital relationships
across different life cycle models, and create a federated life cycle
information model overlaid on various domain-specific models.
Digital connectivity: Creating effective digital connectivity across
authoritative sources of truth is crucial. This involves creating a web of
links across all data sources, where each link carries certain semantics.
Central to these principles is the concept of the digital thread – a data
architecture that provides a reliable communication platform across the
product life cycle.
The Open Services for Life Cycle Collaboration (OSLC) framework (OSLC
2024) plays a crucial role in facilitating the implementation of data
continuity in engineering. It promotes an open and federated environment
for engineering collaboration, allowing different tools and systems to
interact seamlessly. This interoperability is vital for the engineering data
continuity, as it necessitates the integration of diverse tools and data sources
across various engineering domains.
OSLC’s contribution lies in its standardization of interfaces, which
simplifies the integration of disparate tools by allowing them to share and
exchange data in a predictable manner. This standardization enhances
interoperability and reduces the complexity of integrating various systems.
A significant benefit of OSLC is the support it offers for cross-domain data
integration, which is a key challenge in implementing digital threads.
Furthermore, OSLC lays the groundwork for data continuity by providing a
foundational framework that supports the creation of a data-driven
architecture. This architecture links various datasets, models, and systems
across the product life cycle, offering a comprehensive view of the product
development process.
The integration and interoperability facilitated by OSLC lead to more
informed decision-making and increased efficiency in engineering
processes. Stakeholders gain access to a broader range of data and insights,
reducing redundancy and rework due to improved data consistency and
accessibility.
In essence, OSLC is instrumental in addressing the challenges of
implementing digital threads by promoting open standards, enhancing
cross-domain collaboration, and establishing a unified and interconnected
engineering environment (Gery 2023).
There are several frameworks that can complement or serve as alternatives
to OSLC. For example, MoSSEC (2021) facilitates the sharing of model
context across various domains, overcoming traceability challenges during
the transfer of information from one domain to another within the realm of
modeling and simulation. Similar to OSLC, it provides a framework for
collaboration across distributed datasets. MoSSEC’s primary goal is to
document and convey the who, what, when, where, and why of modeling
and simulation data whenever it is exchanged. This approach offers a more
detailed and encompassing strategy compared to the Functional Mock-up
Interface.
6.5 Digital Continuity for System Variability
Digital continuity, summarized as the integration of life cycle models and
the management of authoritative data sources, presents significant
challenges. These complexities are amplified when dealing with not just a
single complex system but multiple system variants issued from a product
line. Eigner and Stelzer (2009) identify the system life cycle and the
multiple domains as two critical dimensions of system development.
As Figure 6.1 shows, the development of product lines introduces a third
dimension, the system variants – or simply variability. If not managed
correctly, engineering assets can quickly duplicate and grow in an
exponential and inconsistent manner.
Figure 6.1 System domains, life cycle phases, and variants.
The central challenge lies in handling the commonality and variability in
the engineering data of system variants. Each variant, while being a
member of a broader product line, possesses its unique configuration and
set of interdependencies. Managing these effectively calls for a strategic
approach to maintaining a single source of variability and enhanced
configuration management capabilities (as further explained in Chapter 19).
This ensures that all reusable assets within the product line are consistently
managed and their interrelations meticulously overseen, as highlighted by
Forlingieri (2022). Configuration management is the discipline that covers
those important aspects. Chapter 18 offers a complete example of digital
continuity in the MBPLE context, while Chapter 19 explores further into
configuration management for product lines.
Let’s explain this concept with a simple example. Figure 6.2 illustrates an
aircraft product line that utilizes a set of shared assets. These assets are used
in different aircraft variants (aka member products) related to seating
capacities ranging from 150 to 180 or 124 to 156 seats, as an example. We
assume that most of the requirements will be the same, but many will vary
across the member products, impacting also design, testing, and discipline-
specific assets.
Figure 6.2 Example of digital continuity across multiple variants.
The example illustrates that with digital continuity in place, it becomes
possible to monitor and propagate changes across product line assets (aka
shared assets), aircraft variant requirements, and other related assets, rather
than navigating through a collection of disconnected clone-and-own data
sets.
In such a complex environment, the importance of digital continuity goes
beyond being just an optional element; it becomes an essential prerequisite.
Digital continuity plays a critical role in enabling efficient reuse practices,
above all in product line engineering, allowing for accurate product
configurations, and facilitating comprehensive analyses of the impacts of
changes.
