OceanofPDF.com
Part II
Foundations of MBPLE
The second part of this book provides an overview of the foundations of
Model-Based Product Line Engineering (MBPLE). It begins by presenting
a brief history of MBPLE in Chapter 7, including its origins in the object-
oriented methodologies of the end of the twentieth century.
The main foundations of MBPLE are then presented in Chapter 8. Systems
Engineering (SE), Model-Based Systems Engineering (MBSE), and Product
Line Engineering (PLE) define the basic elements that, combined in a
holistic approach, provide the means to improve the efficiency of product
development. Chapter 8 introduces the key MBPLE concepts, which are
further developed in the rest of Part 1.
The main concepts of MBPLE are described in Chapter 9. Concepts such as
features, feature models, shared asset supersets, and member products are
explained in terms that facilitate understanding before moving to the more
formal definitions later in the book. These concepts are based on published
standards, noticeably ISO/IEC, 2021. ISO/IEC 26580:2021 Software and
systems engineering – Methods and tools for the feature-based approach to
software and systems product line engineering.
Chapter 10 focuses on the importance of modeling languages for MBPLE.
These languages support the definition of shared reusable assets of different
nature, as well as the formalization of the variability of product lines
through feature models. Instead of presenting specific modeling solutions,
the chapter outlines the requirements for such modeling languages
(including interoperability requirements) and elaborates on the role of
SysML v2 in the landscape of modeling languages for MBPLE.
The last chapter of Part II (Chapter 11) presents a short analysis of
international standards related to MBPLE. These standards cover the
domains of PLE, SE, MBSE, methods and tools, and data formalization and
exchange. The goal of Chapter 11 is not to dress an exhaustive panorama of
available standards but rather to point to those standards that are central to
MBPLE and to which the reader can refer for further details.
OceanofPDF.com
Chapter 7
Past, Present, and Future of MBPLE
7.1 Introduction
The landscape of software engineering has experienced a remarkable
evolution since the 1960s, a journey marked by significant milestones,
groundbreaking methodologies, and the emergence of key paradigms that
have shaped the modern digital world. As we dive deep into the heart of this
transformative journey in Section 7.2, we will explore the profound changes
initiated by the advent of object-oriented languages and methodologies in
the 1990s. This period not only introduced new programming languages
into real-world projects but also marked the debut of object-oriented design
and analysis, planting the first seed of what would lead to model-based
product line engineering (MBPLE) today.
The narrative then transitions to Section 7.3, drawing a parallel between the
cloning of “Dolly” the sheep and the clone-and-own practices prevalent in
software development at the time. This era underscored the challenges and
limitations of early software product line development, highlighting the
need for more sophisticated and sustainable methodologies.
In Section 7.4, we witness the evolution of product line engineering (PLE),
characterized by the systematic use of feature models and engineering
assets. This section examines the shift toward a more efficient, PLE-
oriented approach to software development, signifying a major
advancement in the discipline.
In Section 7.5, the chapter expands its focus beyond software to encompass
systems engineering. The introduction of model-based systems engineering
(MBSE) and the SysML represents a pivotal shift, indicating the beginning
of a new era where software engineering principles begin to intersect with
systems engineering, leading to the emergence of MBPLE.
Finally, Section 7.6 explores the synergistic relationship between PLE and
MBSE, detailing the efforts to model variability within SysML and the
challenges of integrating PLE and MBSE practices.
7.2 Everything Started with Software
Engineering
Since the software crisis of the 1960s and the NATO Software Engineering
Conferences (Naur and Randell 1969), the field of software engineering has
undergone significant evolution. The late 1980s and early 1990s, in
particular, prefigured a new era of coding and software development
methodologies. During this period, object-oriented design and analysis
made their debut in the software engineering landscape. Early on, there was
considerable debate among experts regarding how design methods, which
had gained popularity in the 1980s, could be adapted to the flourishing
object-oriented paradigm. This period saw a proliferation of methodologies
and notations designed to support object-oriented methods, leading to
intense debates and disagreements among methodologists, a time
humorously named the “Methods War” (Scott and Fowler 1997). A joke
from this era summarizes the atmosphere: “What’s the difference between a
methodologist and a terrorist? You can negotiate with a terrorist!” (Scott
and Fowler 1997). Figure 7.1 epitomizes the mood of the time!
Figure 7.1 A type of methodologist you would have encountered
during the “Methods War.”
Among these debates, three pioneers of object-oriented modeling – Grady
Booch, Jim Rumbaugh, and Ivan Jacobson, collectively known as the
“Three Amigos” – emerged. Their contributions led the object management
group (OMG) to initiate a task force aimed at creating a unified modeling
notation. This effort culminated in the successful development of the
unified modeling language (UML) (OMG 1997), highlighting that despite
variations in methods, the notations employed showed remarkable
consistency.
Concurrently, the 1990s witnessed the emergence of other significant
movements within the software engineering field. Among these, feature-
oriented domain analysis (FODA), conceptualized by Kang et al. (1990),
represented the earliest methodology within the feature model family.
FODA’s primary aim is the identification of prominent or distinguishing
features of software systems. These features, visible to users, delineate the
domain’s scope and give the methodology its name. They encapsulate both
the commonalities within the domain and the distinctions between related
systems, serving to define the domain through a spectrum of mandatory,
optional, or alternative characteristics (Kang et al. 1990).
The introduction of object-oriented languages marked a pivotal moment in
software engineering. It opened avenues for component reuse in software
development. This shift facilitated the creation of configurable software,
with feature models gaining popularity in the software product line
community. This community focused on strategies that enable developers to
create software that could serve multiple products rather than just one.
Central to these methodologies is the use of models to delineate the
variations and similarities among the resulting products or implementation
fragments (Beuche et al. 2004).
The foundation of modern model-based software product line engineering
can be traced back to the innovations of UML and FODA, marking the
genesis of modern software development methodologies and beyond.
7.3 The First Generation of Software Product
Line Development: The “Dolly” Era
The origins of PLE, are often traced back to McIlroy’s (1968) work on
“Mass Produced Software Components.” However, it was again in the
1990s that the discipline started to form consensus and its application
became more visible. During this time, a worldwide event unfolded that
seemed to leap from the pages of science fiction into reality. “Dolly” the
sheep emerged as the first mammal successfully cloned from an adult cell, a
feat that paralleled the cloning practices within the software industry, albeit
with starkly different complexities and implications. In fact, in the software
development landscape, the practice of cloning, characterized by copying
software codes and components for the creation of new applications within
product lines, was still prevalent. This approach was not only limited to the
software code itself but also included other engineering assets such as
requirements and test artifacts.
Beyond investing in technical design and developing domain artifacts,
adopting PLE often requires changing processes, organizational structures,
and tools, leading to significant investments. As discussed in Chapter 3,
instead of using a PLE approach, software systems are frequently created
by “cloning” existing ones. This involves copying and modifying the
requirements, architecture, and code of previous systems. This “clone-and-
own” method became popular because it allows for quick adaptation to new
technologies and meets the demands of fast development cycles.
Despite the reuse and some successes in the industry, this approach presents
several limitations, which are introduced in Chapter 3 and detailed in
Chapter 21. Talking about our “Dolly,” Figure 7.2 exemplifies the result of
uncontrolled clone-and-own practices.
Figure 7.2 Example of a bad result of clone-and-own practices.
These operational challenges inherent in first generation, product-centric
software product line development significantly limit an organization’s
ability to achieve its strategic objectives. The obstacles include difficulties
in meeting deadlines, offering competitive value within controlled costs,
ensuring product quality, and expanding the product portfolio’s breadth and
depth (Lanman et al. 2011).
7.4 The Second Generation of Software
Product Line Development
It was in the 2000s that the systematic use of feature models in combination
with engineering assets led to the first successful realization of PLE,
primarily in automotive (Flores et al. 2012) and defense (Lanman et al.
2011), also called the second generation of software product line
development.
According to Lanman et al. (2011), the significant transformation, known as
the second generation of product line development, lies in the recognition
that it is far more efficient to approach software product line engineering as
the development of a production mechanism – a unified system designed to
automate the production of all products within a product line – instead of as
the creation of numerous interconnected products (similarly to the product-
centric approach). This profound, albeit nuanced, realization of the PLE
breakthrough emphasizes the importance of concentrating on this singular
production method rather than on the extensive array of products.
The second generation of product line development owes much to experts
who established foundational principles for the field. In the US, Northrop
and Clements’ book, Software Product Lines: Practices and Patterns
(Northrop and Clements 2001), laid significant groundwork. In Germany,
Beuche and Papajewski pioneered a PLE software, pure::variants derived as
an outcome of the Consul method (Beuche et al. 2004). Krueger in the US
developed Gears by Big Lever (Krueger 2001), marking, similar to Beuche
and Papajewski, one of the initial PLE solutions.
Meanwhile, Pohl et al. (2005) introduced an alternative approach to
capturing variability through orthogonal variability modeling (OVM), a
concept detailed in their work on software product line engineering. On the
academic front, institutions such as Carnegie Mellon University in the US,
the Waterloo University in Canada, and the Universities of Magdeburg,
Duisburg-Essen, and Braunschweig in Germany emerged as key players in
advancing the field.
This research contributed to establishing the first norm, ISO/IEC 26550
(ISO/IEC 2015). While this standard was a step towards formalizing PLE, it
still lacked a well-defined and formalized PLE approach, which can be
adopted by industry practitioners (more details in Chapter 11). Despite its
limitations, the standard represents a significant milestone in the evolution
of PLE’s. However, the Common Variability Language initiative (Øystein et
al. 2013) did not achieve the same success. This initiative aimed to create a
unified approach to variability modeling but failed due to disagreements
among tool vendors and other key stakeholders. This scenario was similar
to the “methods war” period, where methodologists were at odds. As a
result, a universally accepted variability or feature modeling notation has
not emerged, and after over a decade, the “PLE arena” is still dominated by
tool-specific solutions.
7.5 From Software Product Lines to System
Product Lines
In the early 2000s, the International Council on Systems Engineering
(INCOSE) played a pivotal role in evangelizing and promoting systems
engineering across various industries. This included the development of
ISO 15288, which saw further revisions in 2023 (ISO/IEC/IEEE 15288
2023), and the publication of the Systems Engineering Handbook, now in its
fifth edition (Walden et al. 2023). During this period, system engineers
began recognizing the need for a more formalized approach to architecting
systems consistently. They considered UML (OMG 1997) for its potential
applicability in system analysis and definition. This consideration led OMG
to establish a new task force aimed at creating a modeling language that
could extend UML to support system development. Kobryn et al., along
with a team of experts from various industries, tool vendors, and academic
institutions, introduced the SysML language under OMG in 2007, which
has now reached its 1.7 version (OMG 2022). This marked the beginning of
the MBSE era, a fundamental shift with no point of return.
As we discussed in the previous section, the advancements of PLE in the
software field and the increasing importance of the systems engineering
discipline produced interest in a few experts and companies on the
possibility to apply PLE in systems development. The idea was not
completely new; in fact, the automotive industry had already introduced the
use of “car configurators,” in the early 1990s, though mainly for
manufacturing and not for the engineering of the vehicle. At this stage,
MBSE and SysML were at their beginning, so the primary focus on PLE
was to look at all those engineering assets produced in several domain-
specific repositories and try to identify what varies and map it to those
optional or alternative features. Textual requirements were the first natural
candidates, but the possibility to reuse CAD models and other PLM
artifacts also started to be considered. Bombardier and Alstom in railway
(Chami et al. 2017; Chalé Góngora et al. 2014), Thales (Dubois et al. 2012),
and US DoD (Clements et al. 2014) in aerospace and defense, General
Motors (Flores et al. 2012) in automotive are among the pioneers of the
application of PLE n system development.
At this stage, PLE predominantly relied on a document-based approach, yet
initial proofs of concept (PoC) and feasibility studies for integrating SysML
models were underway. During this period, Hause and Hummell (2015) and
Chalé Góngora et al. (2014) introduced the concept of MBPLE, combining
PLE and MBSE for the first time.
They were among the first to formalize the concept of a 150% model for
SysML assets, presenting initial examples of how to define variation points
within SysML use cases, blocks, and parts, and including textual
requirements as additional assets within the MBPLE scope. Chalé Góngora
et al. (2014), presenting a real-life product family case from the rolling
stock industry, outlined a new way of working for MBSE and PLE. They
showed how variability can be defined in requirements, system use cases,
and system/subsystem architectures (both functional and structural), with
the variability model serving as a central reference for defining variation
points in the assets (Chalé Góngora et al. 2014).
Several similarities are identified in these initial conceptualizations of the
MBPLE approach between those two key references (Hause and Hummell
2015; Chalé Góngora et al. 2014):
Both sets of authors recognized the potential of integrating MBSE and
PLE in response to the increasing complexity of systems.
Both referenced ISO/IEC 26550 as the standard for PLE and SysML as
the language for MBSE.
Both adopted the OVM notation for creating variability models, a
different approach from feature modeling.
Both highlighted the strong dependency of the approach on tools and
tool chains.
At that time, we, the authors of this book, had the chance to actively
contribute to the introduction of MBPLE within our respective
organizations: Forlingieri at Bombardier Transportation, Weilkiens at Oose,
and Chalé at Alstom, recognizing the need to promote this emerging
discipline also within the INCOSE PLE Working Group (INCOSE PLE
2024).
7.6 The Liaison Between PLE and MBSE
The absence of methods and a formalized approach to modeling variability
within SysML prompted Weilkiens (2016) to develop VAMOS, a technical
guide dedicated to modeling and managing variability within SysML
models. Although this approach did not explicitly address PLE, it
established foundational principles for defining variation beyond the
reliance on specific tools.
Forlingieri and his colleagues at Bombardier Transportation addressed a gap
left by VAMOS, specifically the creation of variability models and feature
models to support the reuse of SysML models (Chami et al. 2017). This
development signifies the gradual convergence of essential elements in this
field. At the same time, Chalé and Greugny further elaborated on the
application of MBPLE to support a “composable” approach for architecture
definition in railway rolling stock, providing additional elements for the
business case of MBPLE (Chalé Góngora and Greugny 2017).
Additionally, the work of Young and Clements (2017) deserves recognition
for showcasing the application of MBSE and PLE within Raytheon in the
defense industry. Echoing the endeavors of Hummell and Hause (2015) and
Chalé Góngora et al. (2014), their publication provides an example of tool
application, this time using a feature modeling approach. It illustrates the
model transformation from the comprehensive 150% SysML model to the
specific 100% model, guided by various feature configurations.
The period spanning 2020 to 2022 will go down in history as challenging
years due to the COVID-19 crisis, yet these years were pivotal for MB-
PLE. In 2021, the ISO/IEC 26580 standard (ISO/IEC 26580 2021) was
released, providing better guidance on applying PLE in system
development using a feature-based approach. Concurrently, Airbus
launched its largest digital transformation program in engineering, known
as Digital Design Manufacturing & Services (DDMS) (Forlingieri and
Ferrogalini 2020), which acknowledged MBSE and PLE as key pillars of
this transformation. This initiative spurred progress in the field, leading to
significant publications such as “The Four Dimensions of Variability with
MBPLE at Airbus” (Forlingieri 2022), detailed in Chapter 17. This work
formalizes essential considerations for adopting MBPLE in complex
systems.
This momentum for MBPLE facilitated productive collaboration between
two of us, Weilkiens and Forlingieri, culminating in the establishment of the
first tool-agnostic methodological foundations for MBPLE. They leverage
the recently published ISO/IEC 26580 standard and the insights from
VAMOS, as well as the experiences gained at Airbus, as shown in “Two
Variant Modeling Methods for MBPLE at Airbus” (Forlingieri and
Weilkiens 2022), As elaborated in Chapter 15, the approach introduced two
complementary methods for modeling variation in SysML models,
supported by a feature-based (MB)PLE. Furthermore, the publication
outlines essential guidelines for MBPLE adoption, emphasizing the
significance of implementing a feature-based PLE, enabling variant
modeling in SysML, and separating the methodological discussions from
tool dependencies (Forlingieri and Weilkiens 2022).
The following year, Forlingieri and his previous MBPLE team at Airbus
advanced their efforts by introducing a methodological approach to model
variability, supported by the Airbus MBSE architecture framework, MOFLT
(Madeira, de Sousa Pinto, and Forlingieri 2023). This approach illustrates
the modeling of variability and variation points across different abstraction
layers such as operational, functional, logical, and technical. It emphasizes
the importance of a model-based architecture framework as “the backbone”
of the feature-based PLE approach.
Another significant boost to the discipline was provided by advancements
in the release of SysML v2 (OMG 2023) notation. SysML v1 does not offer
a specific notation to facilitate variability modeling, posing a “challenging
liaison” (Forlingieri and Weilkiens 2023).
In contrast, SysML v2 introduces the capability to define variation and
variants within the model, thereby unlocking multiple opportunities for
reuse. These advancements are discussed in more detail in Chapter 15.
In 2023, the world quickly embraced artificial intelligence (AI) with the
emergence of open foundation models like OpenAI. While AI was not new
to technical audiences, it became popular among non-experts as well.
This technological disruption offers new opportunities in both MBPLE and
systems engineering. Chapter 20 explores potential use cases, highlighting
the synergy between AI and MBPLE (AI4MBPLE).
This book aims to consolidate theory, practical contributions, and real-
world experiences, providing comprehensive guidance on its application.
Three decades after the introduction of object-modeling notations and
feature models, these concepts have merged and evolved beyond their
initial software applications. It is important to recognize the significant
contributions of the pioneers in feature modeling, UML and related fields.
References
Beuche, D., Papajewski, H., and Schröder-Preikschat, W. (2004). Variability
management with feature models. Science of Computer Programming 53
(3): 333–352.
Chalé Góngora, H.G. and Greugny, F. (2017). Where the big bucks (will)
come from–Implementing Product Line Engineering for Railway

Chapter 8
Shifting to a Model-based Approach
8.1 Introduction
Model-based product line engineering (MBPLE) touches a range of
disciplines, including systems engineering, product line engineering (PLE),
and model-based systems engineering (MBSE). It also interfaces with
various other engineering and non-engineering disciplines involved in
developing a product line. The shift from document-based to model-based
engineering, as described in Chapter 4, illustrates the demand for modeling
and the shift from PLE to MBPLE.
Section 8.2 briefly introduces systems engineering, and Section 8.3 adds the
model-based aspect and discusses the shift from systems engineering to
MBSE. Section 8.4 consequently discusses the shift from PLE to MBPLE.
8.2 Systems Engineering
The International Council on Systems Engineering (INCOSE) defines
systems engineering as follows:
“Systems Engineering is a transdisciplinary and integrative approach to
enable the successful realization, use, and retirement of engineered systems,
using systems principles and concepts, and scientific, technological, and
management methods” (INCOSE 2023).
The definition sounds clear! However, if you are not already familiar with
the discipline, you may not know what systems engineering really is or
what a systems engineer does.
The accomplishment of a systems engineer lies in ensuring that everything
results in a successful system. Other engineering disciplines have concrete
outputs like software, mechanical, or electrical components, while systems
engineering has documents and models. The achievement is hard to grasp
and is only truly recognized when it is absent.
Although two decades ago, systems engineering was mainly associated with
the aerospace and defense industries, today it is applied in almost any
company that develops complex systems.
INCOSE drives the systems engineering discipline. Its publication,
“Systems Engineering Handbook,” covers the core topics and tasks of
systems engineering (INCOSE 2023). It is based on the ISO/IEC 15288
standard (ISO/IEC/IEEE 2023), which defines systems engineering
processes in four process groups:
1. Agreement processes: for example, acquisition and supply;
2. Organizational project-enabling processes: for example, infrastructure
management;
3. Technical management processes: for example, risk management;
4. Technical processes: for example, requirements and architecture
definition.
The standard does not have an explicit process for product lines, but several
processes cover the topic. For more details, see Chapter 24.
8.3 Model-based Systems Engineering
In simple terms, MBSE is “systems engineering using models to perform
tasks.” While documents still hold value, they are primarily derived from or
exported from models to present different views of the model information.
Still, documents shall not be the single source of engineering information.
Chapter 4 discusses the need for MBSE in more detail.
INCOSE defines MBSE as “the formalized application of modeling to
support system requirements, design, analysis, verification, and validation
activities beginning in the conceptual design phase and continuing
throughout development and later life cycle phases” (INCOSE 2007).
Although somewhat dated, the definition of MBSE by INCOSE originated
during the early stages of MBSE as a viable alternative to document-
intensive systems engineering. An alternative definition that complements
rather than contradicts it highlights that MBSE is still systems engineering
and emphasizes the purpose of the application of models:
“Model-based systems engineering is systems engineering with the formal
application of models to make engineering information accessible to
machines to support the stakeholders” (Weilkiens 2022).
While document-based approaches can also facilitate structured work and
clear representations, they are not as efficient or effective as model-based
approaches. One of the primary goals of modeling is to allow machines to
access engineering information and provide support throughout the
engineering process. While machines can read text documents, drawings,
and slides, they can only interpret the semantics of the documents. They can
help with formatting, organizing the texts, managing references, and so
forth. The semantics remain unknown to machines. Even with advances in
natural language processing and artificial intelligence, it is difficult for
machines to process the information. In contrast, an MBSE model is based
on a modeling language with systems engineering semantics, enabling
rigorous and unambiguous content to be accessible by machines.
The systems modeling language (SysML) is certainly the most widely used
language in MBSE, but it is not the only one: MBSE is not SysML, and
SysML is not MBSE. SysML is developed by the Object Management
Group. As this book is being written, the language is in the process of
changing from SysML v1 to SysML v2. The book primarily uses SysML v2
but also shows the use of SysML v1 (OMG 2022, 2024).
MBSE is a large topic and not the focus of this book. There are many
publications on the subject. The following books provide a few good
examples to dive deeper into the topic.
Handbook of Model-Based Systems Engineering (Madni et al. 2023):
This is a comprehensive volume on various aspects of MBSE.
SYSMOD: The Systems Modeling Toolbox (Weilkiens 2020):
Description of common MBSE methodology.
A Practical Guide to SysML (Friedenthal et al. 2014): Textbook about
SysML v1.
The Absolute Beginners Guide to SysML v2 (Weilkiens and Muggeo
2024) provides an introduction to the next-generation systems
modeling language SysML v2.
8.4 From PLE to MBPLE
PLE is an approach to software or product development that focuses on
efficiently creating a family of related products with shared characteristics
or features. Instead of developing each product from scratch, PLE
emphasizes the reuse of common assets and processes across the product
line.
PLE refers to products, while systems engineering refers to systems. The
INCOSE handbook states that “product” is equivalent to “system,” “product
line” is equivalent to “system family,” and “product line engineering” is
equivalent to “system family engineering” (INCOSE 2023).
A product is an entity that an organization sells and becomes a system when
integrated and operated in its intended environment. The ISO/IEC 26550
standard explicitly mentions that “product” is equivalent to “system-level
products” (ISO/IEC 2015).
PLE is, therefore, not an alternative to systems engineering but rather an
admixture. Just like systems engineering, PLE is also transdisciplinary. It
must be well integrated into the organization and its engineering activities.
In traditional software or product development, each new product is
typically developed independently, resulting in redundant efforts and
increased time and costs. PLE aims to address these challenges by
establishing a systematic approach to product development, enabling
organizations to manage variability and leverage commonalities within a
product line effectively. Chapter 5 discusses the shift from project-oriented
to product-line-oriented delivery approaches in more detail.
As anticipated in Chapters 3 and 5, some of the key concepts of product line
development include:
1. Variability: Product lines consist of products with common features
and features that exhibit variability. PLE provides mechanisms to
manage this variability, allowing companies to configure and
customize products based on customer requirements or portfolio
planning.
2. Commonality: PLE identifies and promotes reusable assets within the
product line. By maximizing commonality, companies can streamline
development efforts, reduce duplication of effort, and improve overall
efficiency.
3. Domain engineering: Domain engineering involves analyzing the
problem or opportunity space, identifying the commonalities and
differences within the product line, and designing core components
that can be reused across products. It focuses on creating a reusable
foundation for the product line.
4. Application engineering: Application engineering involves developing
specific products within a product line using the reusable assets
created during domain engineering. It focuses on customizing and
configuring the product to meet specific customer requirements.
Although the topic of PLE affects all product areas, most publications,
methods, and tools only deal with software PLE.
A common PLE approach described in the ISO/IEC 26580 standard is
feature-based PLE (ISO/IEC 2021). The terminology in this standard differs
in part from the ISO/IEC 26550 standard (ISO/IEC 2015). The terminology
in this book follows the standards but sometimes uses other terms if we, as
authors, believe this alteration could facilitate their understanding.
Since a product line is more extensive in scope than a single system, to
effectively implement PLE and, in particular, the feature-based PLE
approach, models are essential. The motivation used to “elevate” MBSE as
the way to move forward in systems engineering (abandoning document-
based systems engineering) applies even more strongly to PLE. In other
words, we can define the axiom: If “MBSE will become the norm for
systems engineering” (INCOSE 2014), then “MBPLE will become the
norm for PLE”.
The symbiosis of MBSE and PLE is proving to be a particularly
advantageous strategy (Chalé Góngora et al. 2014). This combination is a
“win–win” for both disciplines. On the one hand, MBSE significantly
contributes to ensuring the consistency of PLE assets. On the other hand,
PLE significantly increases the potential for reusing MBSE models. This
reciprocal relationship increases the efficiency of each approach and
promotes a more robust and scalable development process.
You cannot expect the variability defined in a feature model to be
implemented correctly in all engineering artifacts if these are scattered
across umpteen documents and you hardly get any machine support.
Models can be used to design extremely complex interrelationships more
effectively and reliably.
We can consequently define MBPLE as “the application of feature-based
PLE in combination with MBSE,” where architecture models are central,
but not exclusive for realizing the product line and its member products.
While the feature-based PLE approach ensures a single and independent
source of variability across the engineering assets, the MBSE approach
secures digital continuity and consistency across all the engineering models
in scope.
Chapter 9 presents the concepts of MBPLE in more detail. The glossary in
Annex A defines all PLE and MBPLE terms used in this book.

Chapter 9
MBPLE Concepts and Definitions
9.1 Introduction
This chapter provides an overview of the key concepts of model-based
product line engineering (MBPLE). Feature models are the main source for
formalizing product variability, which is determined by features and feature
configurations. The shared assets supersets contain all elements used to
specify, develop, and validate a concrete physical product. These include
systems modeling language (SysML) models and other engineering
artifacts, such as simulation models, CAD models, and so on. The shared
assets supersets support the variability formalized in the feature model
through “internal” variations.
The definitions are based on standards (ISO/IEC 2021, 2015) but also
contain additions and, in some cases, modified names that we believe are
easier to understand. Figure 9.1 shows the cornerstones of MBPLE. The
product line is the overarching concept that includes variability models,
shared assets supersets, product models, and product portfolios.
Figure 9.1 MBPLE cornerstones: variability, shared assets, products
models, and product portfolio.
Chapter 26 formally defines the terms in an information model, and
Chapters 13–15 present their applications.
9.2 Variability Model
A variability model contains both a feature model and feature
configurations to fully represent the scope of a product line.
The feature model defines the available features and their
interdependencies, establishing the possible variations within the product
line. Meanwhile, feature configurations are specific combinations of these
features that define individual products within the product line. In essence,
feature configurations express the product line’s realized variability. They
detail how the abstract possibilities of the feature model are translated into
concrete products.
In practice, the variability model is often implemented by the project or
workspace of the product line engineering (PLE) tool in which the features
and feature configurations are defined.
In summary, a variability model includes both a feature model and feature
configurations to capture a product line’s intended variability.
ISO/IEC 26580 has a compliant but different view of the concepts (Figure
9.2). The standard distinguishes between domain assets and application
assets, or domain engineering and application engineering. Domain
engineering is about the specification and management of the product line’s
commonalities and variabilities, while application engineering is about the
specification and management of individual products.
Figure 9.2 Domain and application assets.
It is particularly noteworthy, in this view, that the feature model is a domain
asset and the feature configurations are an application asset, while the
concept of the variability model combines both. Both views are valid and
not contradictory.
9.3 Feature Model
A feature model specifies a product line’s variability. It is the “single source
of variability.” A feature model consists of features with feature constraints.
The feature configurations include the selection of features to express the
member product’s needs (Figure 9.3). The ISO/IEC 26580 standard calls the
feature tree the feature catalog, and a feature configuration a bill of features
(ISO/IEC 2021).
Figure 9.3 Definition of feature model.
The features are organized in a hierarchical tree, starting with a root feature
from which the tree spans the subfeatures (Figure 9.4). The view is called a
feature tree. The term feature tree is often used synonymously with feature
model.
Figure 9.4 Example feature tree.
A feature is a product’s characteristic that differentiates it from other
products within the same product line (ISO/IEC 2021). The characteristic is
visible to the product line’s stakeholders.
The definition of a feature also implies that a feature does not specify a
product characteristic without any variability. Therefore, the feature model
does not cover all product characteristics, only the variability. If a
characteristic itself does not indicate variability, the variability is specified
by the subfeatures. However, there are different practices in the industry
and different definitions of the feature concept. For example, the definition
of a feature in feature-oriented domain analysis includes the common
characteristics of the product (Kang et al. 1990). This book follows the
definition given in ISO/IEC (2021), which only considers the variability.
A feature has an identifier, a name, a description, and a binding time. Of
course, further properties can be defined if appropriate. The list mentioned
here includes only the essential properties.
The binding time of a feature indicates in which life cycle phase the feature
is set or decided for a given product. The life cycle stages according to
ISO/IEC/IEEE 15288 are shown in Figure 9.5 (ISO/IEC/IEEE 2023). The
stages “Concept” and “Retirement” are typically irrelevant for the binding
time. The binding time can have one of the values “Development,”
“Production,” or “Operation.” For example, suppose that, in a drone
product line, the binding time of a feature related to the number of engines
is “Development” and that two subfeatures specify an option for four or six
engines. In this case, the feature is integrated into a product during the
design phase, and the production gets a variability-free input specification
for this feature. The decision is made during the design phase, and the
production gets a clear specification for four or six engines. Maybe you
have two different production sites, Factory A builds a quadcopter and
Factory B builds a hexacopter. The variation, including the decision for an
option, is not set during the production stage but indeed affects the
manufacturing system.
Figure 9.5 Life cycle stages.
Source: Adapted from ISO/IEC, 2023.
If the binding time is “Production,” the decision to set a given feature for a
product, such as a drone’s body color, is made during the production phase.
The input for the production phase from the design phase includes unbound
features about the color options such as red, white, blue, and black. The
decision about the concrete color of the drone is made during production,
for example, based on customer orders.
The same principle can be applied to the binding time “Operation.” For
example, if the drone provides a standard and a long-distance battery, the
binding time of the type of battery feature can be “Operation.” The drone’s
user can change the battery type during the operation phase. Consequently,
the procedure for changing the batteries must be provided to the users and
documented in the user manual, and so forth.
Features are organized like a tree, which means a feature can have
subfeatures that are owned or referenced by the parent feature. Additionally,
the subfeature relationship specifies how many of the subfeatures can be
selected in a feature configuration. This is called cardinality, specified by a
lower bound and an upper bound. For example, the cardinality range [1.1]
specifies exactly one possible selection [0.1] specifies none or one, and
[1.4] specifies that one to four features can be part of the product. An upper
bound of “*” means that there is no upper limit. A cardinality range with a
lower bound of 1 specifies a mandatory feature, which means it must be
selected for all products. Mandatory features with cardinality [1.1] have no
variability themselves but can have subfeatures with variability. The
cardinality range with a lower bound of 0 specifies an optional feature.
For example, the feature “batteryKind” is a mandatory subfeature of the
root feature that represents the whole product line and has the cardinality
[1.1]. Subfeatures can be grouped into a set of alternative subfeatures. The
set also has a cardinality that specifies how many subfeatures can be
selected. For example, the feature “batteryKind” has two alternative
subfeatures, “standardBattery” and “longDistanceBattery.” Each subfeature
is optional and has a cardinality of [0.1], but the alternative set has the
cardinality [1.1], meaning exactly one subfeature of the set must be
selected. It is an exclusive-or (XOR) feature group.
A feature can also reference another feature. In that case, the feature is a
proxy for the referenced feature. It can be used to reuse a feature in another
branch of the feature tree. However, it can also be used across feature trees
by referencing a feature from another feature tree or the entire feature tree
itself. In Figure 9.6, “Feature D” is used in two different branches of the
tree, and the feature tree of “Product Y” is put below “Feature E.”
Figure 9.6 Proxy features.
A feature constraint is a Boolean expression about features that must be true
for all member products. It can be any kind of expression. The following
two are commonly used constraints.
Conflicts: If one of the features in a conflicts feature constraint is selected,
none of the other features linked by the constraint may be selected.
Requires: If the source feature of a requires feature constraint is selected,
the target feature shall also be selected.
Any other kind of feature constraint combination can be defined. For
example, if Feature A is selected but not Feature B, then a valid feature
configuration must select Features C and D.
A feature configuration is a valid set of selected features of a feature tree.
Valid means that all feature constraints are met, all cardinality bounds are
met, and all variability decisions are made except for features with binding
times in future life cycle stages. A feature configuration also refers to a life
cycle stage. The ISO/IEC 26580 standard calls the feature configuration the
bill of features (ISO/IEC 2021).
9.4 Shared Assets Supersets
The shared assets supersets are collections of engineering artifacts
implementing a feature model for a given product line. They consist of core
common elements, variation (also known as variation point), and variant
elements that implement variability (Figure 9.7).
Figure 9.7 Definition of shared assets supersets.
The shared assets supersets point to the feature model specifying the
variability. Since the feature model is independent of the supersets, different
supersets could implement it.
The supersets contain all assets required to engineer a product. It includes
the common assets used for all member products and the variants that relate
to features and are only used for some member products. A shared asset is a
digital engineering artifact, such as requirements, architecture descriptions,
CAD models, simulation models, and so forth. A shared assets superset is
only a logical construct. It is not, in reality, a repository in its own right
since it consists of several different artifacts stored in different engineering
repositories.
All engineering artifacts on different levels can be shared assets, which
include, for example, a single architecture description element like an
interface definition and the whole product architecture description. A shared
asset has its own life cycle and can be considered by configuration
management as a configuration item or as part of a configuration item (see
Chapter 19).
Some elements of the shared assets are used for all member products. They
are called core elements. Unlike the feature model, which only considers
variability, the shared assets supersets contain all the elements necessary for
all member products.
A node feature in the feature tree specifies variability. The corresponding
parts that implement variability within the shared assets are called
variations. They represent the point where variation happens, which is why
they are also called variation points. The variations are the glue elements
between features and variant elements in the shared assets supersets. Many
variations can be linked with the same feature because the variability
expressed by a feature can impact many assets. Setting or selecting a feature
“triggers” the variation to which it is linked; this, in turn, results in the
transformation of the appropriate design choice linked to the selected
feature.
A variant also implements a feature. The variation with the linked variants
mirrors the appropriate part of the feature tree (Figure 9.8). The variations
and variants that implement a single feature together define a part of a
corresponding member product. This can be a mixture of SysML elements,
CAD models, simulation models, or any other engineering artifacts.
Variations and variants are not the physical member product parts
themselves.
Figure 9.8 Relationship of feature model and shared assets superset
elements.
9.5 Product Models
A product model is the complete set of variations and variants that
implement the feature configuration for a given member product of the
product line. It can be initially automatically derived from a feature
configuration of the appropriate shared assets, which contain the core
elements and the variations and variants according to the selected features
of the feature configuration. Theoretically, a complete generation of the
product model would be possible. In practice, however, the model is usually
refined even further in an iterative way. For example, if additional variants
that are not managed in the product line for various reasons are specified at
later stages.
Again, the product model consists of various engineering models, such as
SysML system models, CAD models, simulation models, and so on.
9.6 Member Products
Member products are not digital engineering assets. They are actual
physical instances of the product line built from the product model.
Member products are distinct yet interconnected entities within a product
line. They represent the various manifestations of a core product concept,
tailored to cater to different market segments or customer needs.
Each member product, while unique in its specifications and features,
shares a common foundation with the rest of the product line.
This shared foundation typically includes core technologies, design
principles, and operational frameworks. Developing member products is a
strategic process, allowing businesses to diversify their offerings while
maintaining production efficiency and quality consistency. It enables
organizations to explore new market opportunities without deviating
significantly from their established domain of expertise. In essence,
member products embody a product line’s flexibility and adaptability.
They illustrate how a single, cohesive product strategy can create a diverse
yet harmonious portfolio of products catering to a wide range of consumer
needs and preferences.
References
ISO/IEC (2021). ISO/IEC 26580:2021 Software and systems engineering –
methods and tools for the feature-based approach to software and
systems product line engineering.
ISO/IEC (2015). ISO/IEC 26550:2015 Software and systems engineering –
reference model for product line engineering and management.
ISO/IEC/IEEE (2023). ISO/IEC/IEEE 15288:2023 Systems and software
engineering – system life cycle processes.
Kang, K., Cohen, S., Hess, J. et al. (1990). Feature-Oriented Domain
Analysis (FODA) Feasibility Study (No. CMU/SEI-90-TR-021). Software
Engineering Institute, Carnegie Mellon University.
OceanofPDF.com
Chapter 10
MBPLE Modeling Languages
10.1 Introduction
The term “model-based” highlights the need for modeling languages, at
least in the definition of the term “model” defined in this book. This chapter
discusses modeling languages within the context of model-based product
line engineering (MBPLE). Chapter 4 explains the need for modeling and
describes the characteristics of a modeling language. Chapters 13–15
provide examples of how different modeling languages are applied to
specify variability.
Modeling languages formalize features and shared assets. A product
portfolio’s member products are typically outside the scope of models
because they are real-world products. In this book, modeling languages for
feature and variable shared assets are generally called variability modeling
languages.
Section 10.2 defines the terms “model” and “modeling language.” Section
10.3 provides an overview of variability modeling languages, Section 10.4
provides an overview of feature modeling languages, and Section 10.5
provides an overview of shared assets modeling languages. The sections
propose requirements for those languages and discuss the unique role of
systems modeling language (SysML) v2. Finally, Section 10.6 covers the
topic of model interoperability.
10.2 Models and Modeling Languages
Herbert Stachowiak describes in his book Allgemeine Modelltheorie three
fundamental characteristics of a model (Stachowiak 1973):
1. Mapping: Models are representations of things.
2. Reduction/Abstraction: Models are not 1:1 mappings. An illustrative
example is a map. Lewis Caroll, in his book Sylvie and Bruno
Concluded (Carroll 2015), writes about two individuals talking about
the making of a map and its scale. By increasing the scale step-by-step,
they finally agreed to create a map with a scale of 1:1 miles. This
provokes the disappointment of the farmers living in that area, who
object that now the map covers the entire land and blocks the sunlight.
3. Pragmatism: Models are created for a purpose.
Typically, the common definitions of the term “model” are true but, in most
cases, not sufficient to define a model in the engineering context. First, we
require the model to be digital, excluding analog models. Of course, analog
models are also valuable, but they are neither the focus of MBPLE nor
MBSE. This first requirement for model-based engineering (MBE) models
(MBEML-1) refers to Stachowiak’s definition, while the second
requirement (MBEML-2) calls for digital assets:
MBEML-1: Basic Characteristics: An engineering model shall
conform to the three model characteristics defined by Stachowiak:
representation, simplification, and pragmatism, ensuring the model
accurately reflects, simplifies for understanding, and serves the
intended practical purpose within the modeling domain.
MBEML-2: Digital Assets: An engineering model shall exclusively
comprise digital assets.
The third requirement of an engineering model calls for machine
accessibility, whereby “access” means that a machine can read and
write the model and also understand the concepts according to the
domain of the model. For example, a machine accessing an MBSE
model can identify requirements, architectural elements, and the
satisfying relationship between them and can point out gaps in
satisfying traceability. Likewise, a machine accessing MBPLE models
can identify features or variants.
MBEML-3: Machine Accessible: The engineering model shall be
machine accessible, meaning that machines must be capable of reading
and writing it and understanding its concepts in accordance with its
domain.
The requirements summarized led to our definition of an MBE model,
which equally applies to MBSE, MBPLE, and other engineering
disciplines. The definition is based on (Weilkiens 2022).
An MBSE model is a digital model that represents systems and their
environments and is based on a modeling language that covers concepts of
engineering.
A modeling language is a rule-based system of signs. A sign has a meaning
(semantics), a representation (concrete syntax), and a structure (abstract
syntax). The rules constrain the use and combination of the signs. For
example, SysML v2 defines the sign “part definition.” The simple, informal
semantics is that a part definition represents a modular unit of structure. The
formal semantics are quite complex and skipped here. The concrete syntax
specifies a graphical notation (rectangle with different compartments) and a
textual notation (part def). The abstract syntax defines the properties and
relationships of the part definition data element in the model repository.
10.3 Variability Modeling Languages
A language for modeling variability goes beyond simply capturing the
variability of the system; it is a comprehensive modeling language that
includes support for variability among other features, as is the case with
SysML v2.
Around 2012, the OMG tried to develop and standardize the common
variability language (CVL) (OMG 2012). However, as mentioned in
Chapter 7, since the initiative did not materialize, CVL was never finalized.
To date, no common standardized and tool-independent variability language
exists.
The architecture of modeling languages includes three pillars: abstract
syntax, concrete syntax, and semantics. In addition to the engineering
model requirements MBEML-1, MBEML-2, and MBEML-3, the following
lists high-level requirements for each of these pillars for variability
modeling languages.
VML-1: Semantics: The variability modeling language shall cover the
semantics of variability concepts grounded in the standard ISO/IEC
26580:2021 (ISO/IEC 2021).
VML-2: Concrete Syntax: The variability modeling language shall cover
graphical or textual notation elements common in the PLE domain.
VML-3: Abstract Syntax: The variability modeling language shall define
an abstract syntax that makes variability concepts available to machines.
The variability concepts related to MBPLE are defined in Chapters 9 and
26. They are based on common concepts and ISO/IEC standards. However,
they can, of course, differ in different areas of application and do not claim
to be complete or unique.
A model created by a modeling language that satisfies the above
requirements can be understood by human readers familiar with PLE.
Machines can, in turn, read the model data and interpret the semantics.
10.4 Feature Modeling Languages
A feature modeling language is a special variability modeling language and,
therefore, satisfies the requirements in Section 10.2, whereby the variability
concepts are limited to features. The concepts are defined in Chapters 9 and
26.
A feature tree is a common concrete syntax for features (see Figure 14.4),
and a matrix is a common concrete syntax for feature configurations (see
Figure 14.6).
In the MBPLE landscape, there is no standard language for modeling
features. There is an almost common agreement on the concrete syntax of
feature trees, but they differ in the details, for example, how to depict
mandatory features or alternative feature sets.
The feature semantics are also slightly different in the various approaches.
However, the standards ISO/IEC 26550 and ISO/IEC 26580 define a solid
foundation (ISO/IEC 2015, 2021). For example, ISO/IEC 26580 defines a
feature as a characteristic that distinguishes a product from other products.
At the same time, the definition of a feature in feature-oriented domain
analysis (FODA) also includes characteristics common to all products
(Kang et al. 1990).
Feature tree approaches are typically purely graphical and do not specify an
abstract syntax. Without an abstract syntax, they are not considered as
modeling languages according to the definition given above. A proprietary
abstract syntax is probably defined for the implementation of a feature tree
tool, but it is not easily accessible for other tools because there is neither a
documented open abstract syntax nor a standard (see Section 10.5 about
model interoperability).
Examples of feature tree approaches are FODA (Section 14.1.2; Czarnecki
et al. 2005) and implementations in tools like PTC pure::variants (PTC
2024), Gears (BigLever 2024), or FeatureIDE (METOP 2020).
OVM is a variability modeling language (Pohl et al. 2005) often mentioned
as an implementation of ISO/IEC 26550. Their history is related, but OVM
itself is not part of the standard. The feature modeling language OVM is
briefly described in Section 13.4.
10.5 Shared Asset Modeling Languages
Shared assets are digital artifacts required for the development of products.
Modeling languages for shared assets are defined by “native” modeling
languages implemented in the various engineering tools, like SysML or
Modelica, or by tool-specific modeling languages used in simulation,
requirements management, or CAD tools, to mention a few.
Often, these languages do not provide explicit support for modeling
variability and do not satisfy the requirements given in Section 10.2.
However, an exception is SysML v2, which accommodated the great need
for PLE concepts due to its timeliness. The role of SysML v2 in the context
of PLE is presented in Section 10.5.
Many modeling languages and tools for shared assets come from a time
when the specification of product lines did not play a dominant role in
engineering. Therefore, product lines were not explicitly supported by these
languages and tools. Over time, the need for variant modeling has
increased. The direct method is a common approach to implementing
variability afterward in languages and tools that do not explicitly support
variability (Section 15.1.1). This consists of plugins that access a feature
model, manage the variability of shared assets, provide filters, and perform
transformations of the modeled assets. However, variability is so essential
to MBPLE not to be explicitly considered by engineering model languages.
Otherwise, specific engineering tools alone would not have sufficient
knowledge of the overall variability of the product line to provide effective
support and perform validations (e.g. consistency checks).
10.6 SysML v2
The requirements for SysML v2 were developed when there was already a
high demand for modeling variability. Therefore, SysML v2 explicitly
supports variability concepts based on the standard ISO/IEC 26580
(ISO/IEC 2021). However, it does not provide the modeling of features; it
only provides the variability of shared assets. The modeling of shared assets
is covered in detail in Chapter 15.
SysML plays a special role in model-based engineering. In general, a
SysML model describes the overall system and has relationships to many
specific engineering modeling tools. It is usually considered the backbone
of an engineering environment. Therefore, including variability modeling in
SysML is an important step forward. Thanks to this, the variability
modeling in SysML models will no longer rely on non-standard approaches.
10.7 Model Interoperability
Model interoperability is not a specific MBPLE topic. However, by the very
nature of MBPLE, there is a strong need for a broad exchange between
feature and shared asset models. The following example illustrates the core
and challenge of model interoperability (‘Model Interoperability’ 2023).
A variation in a shared asset model has a relationship named R to a feature
in a feature model. The relationship crosses the models’ borders and
requires a model interoperability approach (Figure 10.1).
Figure 10.1 Cross-model relationship between variation and feature.
If the shared asset model owns the relationship, the target element must also
be part of the model as a proxy for the fundamental feature in the feature
model. Technically, the target of the relationship cannot be in another model
repository. Figure 10.2 shows the feature proxy element. The relationship R
is a relationship from the variation. There is another technical relationship
between the feature proxy and the feature element. The proxy must be
consistent with the feature, requiring a technical solution to keep them in
sync.
Figure 10.2 Proxy element for cross-relationships.
The concrete implementation of Figure 10.2 in a model could be, for
example, a link attached to the variation element that points to the feature.
Link management and synchronization with the feature model are
proprietary implementations of the tool in which the shared asset resides.
Examples of possible implementation mechanisms to support
interoperability include:
Open Services for Lifecycle Collaboration is a standardized
interoperability technology that could be used to link shared assets
with features (OASIS 2021).
The variability exchange language was an attempt to standardize a data
format for exchanging variability information (OASIS 2018).
Unfortunately, the project is currently on hold.
If the feature model is created with SysML v2 (see Chapter 13), the
standardized SysML v2 API could be used to provide the feature
information to other engineering tools.

Chapter 11
The MBPLE Foundation in the Standards
11.1 Introduction
Establishing standards for systems engineering concepts, terminology,
processes, and methods is crucial as we explore the complex landscape of
systems engineering (Friedenthal et al. 2015). These standards act as
beacons in the vast sea of systems engineering, guiding professionals
through complexity but requiring them to know how to deal with it and
adapt to specific circumstances. This analogy emphasizes that while
standards provide direction and a foundation, the real skill lies in
interpreting and applying them dynamically to meet the unique needs of
each organization, project, or context.
Section 11.2 begins with the main PLE-specific standards such as ISO/IEC
26550 (ISO/IEC 2015) and the ISO/IEC 26580 (ISO/IEC 2021) standards,
also including the only standard covering the method and tool part for the
discipline ISO/IEC 26560 (ISO/IEC 2019).
They emphasize a paradigm shift in systems engineering to adopt product
line engineering (PLE) for the efficient and effective development of
“system product lines” or “system families.” The focus is no longer on
(single) system engineering but on system family engineering.
Section 11.3 continues by covering the key standard in systems engineering,
ISO/IEC/IEEE 15288 (ISO/IEC/IEEE 2023), and focusing predominantly
on standards for model-based assets and their interoperability.
Figure 11.1 simplifies and highlights the key-related standards.
Figure 11.1 Standards associated with MBPLE.
11.2 PLE Standards
ISO/IEC 26550 (ISO/IEC 2015) was established to formalize the
application of PLE, primarily having a return on experience in the software
domain. Despite being a significant milestone, it remains overly software-
centric and generic to a degree that may lead to interpretational challenges
during its practical application.
Subsequently, ISO/IEC 26580 (ISO/IEC 2021) was introduced as an
extension to its predecessor and addressed the gaps, which frequently
complicate the application of ISO/IEC 26550 (ISO/IEC 2021).
Noteworthy enhancements include the incorporation of feature-based PLE
as a standard methodology for defining and managing variability alongside
a more coherent and practicable PLE mechanism. Figure 11.2 illustrates a
simplification of the standard with its main concepts.
Figure 11.2 Featured-based PLE.
Source: Adapted from ISO/IEC 26580.
Currently, model-based product line engineering (MBPLE) has not been
delineated in any standard, given that the subject is still addressed
separately within PLE and model-based standards. However, we see this
book as a key contribution toward the establishment of a new standard that
could formalize the discipline in the foreseeable future.
11.3 Systems Engineering Foundation,
Model-based and Interoperability Standards
The ISO/IEC/IEEE 15288 (ISO/IEC/IEEE 2023) standard is often regarded
as the cornerstone of systems engineering standards, addressing the
comprehensive needs of the industry and embedding the core principles of
systems engineering. In its latest version and its elaboration within the
INCOSE Systems Engineering Handbook (Walden et al. 2023), it is
highlighted that in many cases, systems engineering is performed in the
context of a product line and that the discipline of PLE fills with processes,
methods, and tools the gaps of systems engineering when it is elevated to
systems family engineering.
In model-based systems engineering (MBSE), the ISO/IEC/IEEE 24641
(ISO/IEC/IEEE 2023) standard focuses on the capabilities and
methodologies of tools for MBSE and software engineering. Although it
does not introduce additional life cycle processes for systems and software,
it specifies an MBSE reference model, including considerations for
cognitive aspects of modeling and model management, thereby supporting
and enhancing system and software life cycle processes.
Concerning standardized language, although there is no specific standard
for feature modeling within PLE, Object Management Group (OMG)’s
ISO/IEC 19514 (ISO/IEC 2017) standard defines the systems modeling
language (SysML), which is on the edge of evolving into SysML v2 (OMG,
2023), currently nearing its final publication phase. This development will
be explored further in subsequent chapters.
As already discussed in Chapter 6, for data representation and exchange,
examples include the OSLC core version 3 by OASIS (OASIS, 2017),
which sets the framework for Open Services for Lifecycle Collaboration
and its domain-specific implementations. Meanwhile, ISO 10303–243
(ISO, 2021) specifies the protocol for modeling and simulation information
within a collaborative systems engineering context.
It is important to note that numerous other standards could influence the
adoption and implementation of MBPLE and that this chapter does not aim
to provide an exhaustive list of all possible standards but to stress the
significance of adopting a multidisciplinary and multistandard approach
when embracing the MBPLE paradigm. The next part of the book (III)
opens with Chapter 12, which is dedicated to the MBPLE paradigm.

Part III
Implementation of MBPLE
Part III is entirely dedicated to the technical aspects of Model-Based
Product Line Engineering (MBPLE). Readers who have started their
MBPLE journey and feel ready to begin the architecture definition and the
formalization of asset supersets of their product lines will find invaluable
information and the core technical elements of MBPLE in this part of the
book.
Chapter 12 offers a general overview of the MBPLE approach, outlining
four key steps to move from single-system, document-based, and siloed
developments to a collaborative, model-based engineering of a family of
systems. Chapter 13 follows up with a presentation of the methods and
basic concepts for creating feature models. The chapter bridges the gap
between the theoretical concepts presented in previous chapters and their
implementation in engineering tools, stressing the central role of feature
models in variability management and in the improvement of model
interoperability. Feature-oriented domain analysis (FODA) and the
orthogonal variability model (OVM) are also briefly discussed.
Chapter 14 continues with an exploration of feature configurations. The
concept of feature constraints is presented as a means to limit the number of
possible feature configurations. The chapter concludes with a discussion
about the integration of feature configurations into large product lines.
Chapter 15 then dwells on the techniques for modeling shared assets. Two
different techniques are proposed: the direct method (in which variants are
explicitly defined in the models) and the clean method (in which variation
points are included within model elements), both illustrated through
practical examples. To link the concepts presented in the two previous
chapters, Chapter 16 provides a comprehensive approach to deriving
product models from feature configurations and shared assets.
Part III continues with the topic of variability management by discussing
the different dimensions of variability in Chapter 17: co-variability (of
products, manufacturing and services), variability in the development
process, variability in different layers of abstraction, and variability in the
levels of the system hierarchy.
Chapter 18 explores digital end-to-end continuity. The chapter stresses the
importance of digital continuity to derive assets in a consistent way and
perform effective impact analysis during the product line life cycle. To
illustrate this, an example showing the introduction of a new feature is
provided. Chapter 19 introduces the critical role of configuration
management (CM) in MBPLE to ensure effective and consistent product
development throughout the product line life cycle. The chapter describes
the specifics of CM for MBPLE and the importance of keeping under
control the evolution of the variability and the assets of the product line,
and mastering the dependencies between the product line assets and those
of the multiple member products.
Chapter 20 closes Part III with an exploration of the application of artificial
intelligence in MBPLE, including opportunities and threats.
OceanofPDF.com
Chapter 12
The MBPLE Paradigm
12.1 Introduction
The Capability Maturity Model Integrated (CMMI) for development
(Software Engineering Institute 2007) extends on the classical definition of
a process provided by the quality management standard (ISO/IEC 2000)
and defines a development process as “a set of activities, methods,
practices, and transformations that people use to develop and maintain
systems and associated products.” If we adopt the wording from describing
the development and maintenance of “systems and associated products” to
“product lines and member products,” we align closely with the product
line engineering (PLE) definition provided in this book’s glossary.
However, when consulting ISO/IEC/IEEE 15288 (ISO/IEC/IEEE 2023), a
cornerstone document for systems engineering processes, there is little
focus on product lines or system product lines, and no direct process
guidance is offered for their development. This gap suggests a necessary
shift in paradigm – a term which, in the context of science as defined by
Kuhn, refers to a “universally recognizable scientific achievement that
temporarily provides model problems and solutions to a community of
practitioners” (Kuhn 1962). While the shift in paradigm is progressing for
model-based systems engineering (MBSE), progressively establishing itself
as the new way of doing systems engineering, the same cannot be said for
PLE yet. MBPLE benefits from the synergies of MBSE and PLE and aims
to become a new paradigm for developing system product lines. After
systems engineering became model-based and digital collaboration
predominant, the last shift remains: the move from MBSE (single) to
model-based system family engineering, that is, MBPLE. As we discussed
in Chapter 11, this paradigm lies at its firm foundation, the PLE, model-
based, and interoperability standards.
This book aims to argue convincingly that MBPLE, with its four main steps
introduced in Sections 12.2–12.5, is the most logical method for developing
product lines and its derived products. It is important to mention that the
steps do not differ between a PLE or MBPLE approach. As introduced in
Chapter 8, the main difference lies in the centrality of the models as main
shared assets to be developed, managed, and reused within a PLE approach.
This chapter is the beginning of the journey into this new paradigm and
presents the four key steps of the MBPLE approach, as shown in Figure
12.1.
Figure 12.1 MBPLE four-step approach.
12.2 Step 1: Define Feature Models
The journey of MBPLE begins with the definition of feature models. These
models formally represent a product line’s variability, capturing the
different features that can be included in a product and how they relate to
each other. Features are organized hierarchically, illustrating parent–child
relationships, and are further defined by constraints that formalize
compatibility or conflict between them. This step is critical as it lays the
groundwork for understanding the scope of variability within the product
line. It facilitates stakeholders’ involvement by aligning the product line’s
capabilities with their needs and constraints. Features play a crucial role in
aligning market needs with how an organization plans to transform these
needs into business opportunities, on the one hand, and with what the
organization is capable of delivering through its product line, on the other
hand (Chalé Gongora et al. 2014; Chalé and Greugny 2017). Features serve
a dual purpose: first, to enhance communication among all involved parties
by making the variations within the product line clear and easy to grasp;
and second, to steer the creation of engineering assets by offering a
definitive reference for understanding these variations and their constraints
(Forlingieri and Weilkiens 2022). Chapter 13 provides a full explanation of
the feature model.
12.3 Step 2: Define Feature Configurations
After establishing a comprehensive representation of the product line
through feature models, the next phase involves defining feature
configurations for individual member products. This process is guided by
the feature models developed in Step 1, allowing for the tailoring of
products to meet specific customer requirements.
Feature models specify the available features and identify incompatible
combinations due to constraints imposed for technical or economic reasons.
Allowing every conceivable configuration could be prohibitively expensive,
making these constraints essential (Forlingieri and Weilkiens 2022). The
activity entails choosing which features will be included in the member
product and setting appropriate parameter values, thereby defining the
product’s unique configuration. This step is instrumental in refining the
product line’s scope for each member product, ensuring that each meets its
intended purpose and market needs. The topic of feature configurations is
further developed in Chapter 14.
12.4 Step 3: Define Shared Assets
The next step focuses on defining the product line shared assets. These
assets encompass a wide range of elements, from requirements and design
models to source codes, each linked to specific product line features
(Forlingieri and Weilkiens 2022). This linkage is crucial to systematically
manage variation points within the assets systematically. The scope of the
shared assets within the MBPLE paradigm extends beyond merely MBSE
models, since it encompasses all engineering assets required to develop the
product line and its member products. The model-based approach ensures
consistency among all those assets, while the feature model guides the
engineers in defining the variation in their specific engineering domain
assets. This process culminates in creating what is often referred to as 150%
assets, from which the specific (100%) assets for each member product are
derived. Chapter 15 illustrates Step 3 with details and examples.
12.5 Step 4: Derive Product Models
The final step in the MBPLE approach is about turning the abstract feature
configuration into a tangible product. This involves deriving the necessary
shared assets, as defined and linked in Step 3, according to the specific
feature configuration selected for the member product. The outcome is the
product models fulfilling the needs of the member product (Forlingieri and
Weilkiens 2022). This ensures that the final product is not only aligned with
the stakeholder’s requirements but also leverages the product line’s full
potential by maximizing the reuse of predefined assets and feature
configurations. In principle, any engineering asset or system can be reused
at least partially to satisfy the future needs of other customers. However, the
efforts required to do this depend on how much “built for reuse” these
assets or systems are. So, anchoring such a paradigm in engineering
processes is central for an organization. Manufacturers in industries such as
automotive, aerospace, defense, rail, and electronics rarely produce just a
single variant of their products. If they do, they could soon go out of
business. The product models are further developed in Chapter 16.
12.6 Extending the Four Steps for a Real-
world Execution of MBPLE
The “MBPLE Factory” is based on the feature-based PLE factory described
in the standard ISO/IEC 26580 (ISO/IEC 2021). The real-world execution
order of the four steps might be different, iterative, and incremental.
To recap, we have the four key steps defined above that can be summarized
as:
1. Define feature models: Create a feature tree to specify the variable
characteristics of the product line.
2. Define feature configurations: The feature configurations define the set
of valid feature combinations for the intended member products of the
product line.
3. Define shared assets: The shared assets supersets are all digital
engineering artifacts required to define the member products.
4. Derive product models: Based on a feature configuration, a product
model could be automatically derived from the shared assets. It can
also be done manually or a mix of both.
However, in practice, a tailoring job that needs to be done to implement
MBPLE within an enterprise setting. For instance, we provide a sequence of
process steps that extend the initial simplified four-step approach:
1. Define the product line scoping
2. Define feature models
3. Define feature configurations
4. Define shared assets
5. Derive product models
6. Verify the product line
7. Customize the product models (if needed)
8. Verify the member products
9. Produce the member products
10. Validate the member products
11. Evolve and decommission the product line
It is important to note that those combined 11 steps are not exhaustive in
representing a complete MBPLE real-world approach. For instance, further
adaptation and tailoring shall be performed to integrate the approach into
the systems engineering processes of an organization willing to embark on
this journey. Chapter 24 provides a description of the MBPLE process and
the steps highlighted here.
