Chapter 13
Define Feature Models
13.1 Introduction
This chapter provides insight into implementing a feature model. Chapter
10 introduces the concepts and Chapter 27 presents the feature information
model. The definition of feature models is the first step of the model-based
product line engineering (MBPLE) four-step approach (Figure 13.1).
Figure 13.1 MBPLE four-step approach “define feature models.”
First, the basic techniques for modeling feature models are described. The
core element is a feature tree. Different types of feature trees and their
relationships are introduced. Subsections provide an overview of feature-
oriented domain analysis (FODA), orthogonal variability model (OVM),
and feature modeling tools.
Section 13.2 covers the basic concepts. Section 13.3 describes how to
specify a feature model with FODA, and Section 13.4 covers use of OVM.
The chapter concludes with overview of feature modeling tools in Section
13.5 and provides another example of a feature model for a drone product
line in Section 13.6.
13.2 Basic Concepts
The basic principle of applying a feature model is first demonstrated with a
simple example to reduce distraction and put the focus on the concepts.
Section 13.6 provides a more real-world example of a drone feature model.
A simple example is a “tiny Product Line Engineering (PLE) wall”
comprising three different bricks: a standard brick (P), a large brick (L), and
a standard dark brick (E). Figure 13.2 is a sketch of the tiny wall product.
Figure 13.2 Product “tiny PLE wall.”
Since customers opt for more variety, two slightly different tiny walls are
developed and offered: a wall with two small bricks instead of the dark one
and a wall with a sandy brick instead of the dark one (Figure 13.3).
Figure 13.3 Products “tiny PLE wall” and “tiny PLESandy wall.”
In addition to the customer requests, the product management plans another
L-brick variation that can be implemented by three very small bricks
instead of the large lower left brick (Figure 13.4).
Figure 13.4 L-brick variations
Product line scoping defines the product line and its member products. It
also includes the definition of features and feature configurations, which are
largely derived from the planned member products. More insights about the
product line scoping are provided in Chapter 24.
The first step of the MBPLE process presented in Chapter 9 is defining the
feature model, typically depicted by a feature tree (Figure 13.5). The
representation is based on the notation for feature trees proposed by
Czarnecki et al. (2005).
Figure 13.5 Feature tree “tiny PLE wall.”
The root feature represents the whole feature tree of the product line. Its
identifier is “PLEW-F1,” the name is “tiny PLE wall,” and the binding time
is “production.” The textual description of the feature states, “the tiny PLE
wall specifies a product line of walls for an illustrative example in the
MBPLE book.”
The lower right brick feature PLEW-F1.2 is an owned mandatory
subfeature of the root feature PLEW-F1 with three owned subfeatures that
are part of an exclusive-or (XOR) feature group – alternative features –
which means exactly one of the features must be selected by a feature
configuration. Another feature is the lower left brick PLEW-F1.1, which is
also an owned mandatory subfeature with two subfeatures in an XOR
feature group.
The feature PLEW-F1.1.2 (three very small bricks) cannot be combined
with the feature PLEW-F1.2.2 (two small bricks). This is specified by the
conflicts feature constraint between the two nodes in the feature tree.
The feature PLEW-F1.1.3 has a parameter property. Instead of having many
features for each valid parameter value, it is only one and the feature
parameter is set when selected for a feature configuration. Different feature
configurations can select different parameter values. The feature sets the
mass of a standard brick. The mass of other brick sizes can be derived
appropriately.
A single feature model specifies the variability for all model layers on the
same level (Figure 13.6). Here, layers are, for example, based on the
common requirements, functional, logical, and physical approach (RFLP).
However, it can also be used in any other layer approach.
Figure 13.6 One feature model for all RFLP layers.
The system levels also play an important role in defining the feature
models. For example, the system, subsystem, and component levels follow
the system’s decomposition. Each element of a decomposition could have
its own feature model: a feature model for subsystem A, one for subsystem
B, and so on (Figure 13.7).
Figure 13.7 Multilevel feature models.
A feature in a feature tree can also be the root element of another feature
tree. For example, the root feature of a subsystem feature model in a system
feature model. In this way, feature trees can be composed of many feature
trees, or a large one can be broken down into several smaller ones.
The feature model on the system level can have feature constraints on
features from different subsystem feature models. To do this, however, the
feature model must have access to all features of the lower feature model. If
another organization owns a feature model on a detailed level, it might not
be fully accessible by the feature model on the higher level. Chapter 14
shows an example of this scenario.
The feature model in Figure 13.5 defines the set of valid member products
of the product line. Typically, not all valid member products are also
planned member products of the product portfolio. The oversimplified
example of the tiny PLE wall has only two features, PLEW-F1.1 and
PLEW-F1.2, with variability represented by their subfeatures. However,
they have already resulted in six unconstrained potential member products.
You get the number of unconstrained potential member products by
multiplying the number of variable options of each feature.
The number of unconstrained potential member products is often cited as an
example of the explosion of possible combinations. It sounds impressive,
but it is irrelevant because nobody would ever build all these combinations.
The set of unconstrained potential member products is only theoretical and
contains undesirable combinations that are invalid due to unfulfilled feature
constraints.
You must consider the feature constraints to get the set of valid member
products. Figure 13.5 depicts one conflicting feature constraint between two
features in the feature tree, which results in a set of valid member products
with five elements. There are two features with two, respectively, three
options (2 × 3 = 6), and the conflicts feature constraint in the feature model
excludes one combination (6 – 1 = 5). If the upper bound of the cardinality
of a feature relationship or feature group is greater than 1, it changes the
number of options. The product of the set of valid member products could
be built.
The set of planned member products includes only those valid member
products that should be engineered and built. The “tiny PLE wall” product
portfolio excludes the combination of three tiny bricks with the sandy brick
from the set of valid member products. The combinations of the planned
member products are specified as feature configurations presented in
Chapter 14. Figure 13.8 shows the different member product sets.
Figure 13.8 Member product sets.
13.3 Feature-Oriented Domain Analysis
A common and old concept of feature modeling is the FODA by Kang et al.
(1990). FODA also defines a notation for feature trees, similar to the
notation used above in Figure 13.5. Since it is not a full modeling language,
it only specifies how to draw feature trees. Tools can add the missing data
structures layer to provide feature tree modeling, but it still remains a tool-
specific proprietary solution.
The FODA feature tree is the mother of the feature trees. The concept is
used in many variant descriptions and modeling approaches. Later, Kang
extended FODA to the feature-oriented reuse method, which incorporates
the marketing perspective in addition to the requirements and architecture
perspective (Kang et al. 1998).
FODA originates from the software engineering discipline but is
independent of software and can also be applied to other disciplines,
including systems engineering.
13.4 Orthogonal Variability Model
The OVM is described by Pohl et al. (2005). An OVM captures the
variability of software artifacts in a separate model. OVM has its roots in
software engineering but could also be applied to systems engineering
models. It also provides a variability notation, as shown in Figure 13.9. The
node with the VP symbol represents a variation point. The nodes with the V
symbol represent variants. The dashed lines depict optional variants. Solid
lines depict mandatory variants.
Figure 13.9 Example of OVM.
OVM uses terms from the shared assets domain (Chapter 14) to model
features such as variants.
13.5 Feature Modeling Tools
Of course, there are commercial tools with which feature models can be
created. For example, pure::variants from PTC (PTC 2024) and Gears from
BigLever (BigLever 2024). Chapter 27 provides more information about
the PLE tools, while Chapter 29 features an interview with PLE pioneers
from the two companies.
The tools follow the basic concept of feature modeling but implement them
proprietarily with their own extensions and in their own format. As
mentioned in Chapter 10, FODA and OVM, for example, have
specifications on what the notation should look like. Still, the so-called
abstract syntax, which means the data format of the feature model, is not
specified or even standardized.
As a proxy for feature modeling tools, we show the open-source tool
FeatureIDE as an example here in order to appear neutral toward the tool
market (METOP 2020). FeatureIDE is not as powerful as the commercial
tools, but it is sufficient to show the use of a PLE tool here.
Typically, PLE tools provide a feature tree view and several dialogs for
specifying the details of a feature or feature relationship. The terms used
and feature tree notation can differ from those provided in Chapter 10, but
the basic concept is the same. Figure 13.10 shows the feature tree from
Figure 13.5 in the tool FeatureIDE.
Figure 13.10 FeatureIDE feature tree.
Applying the feature information to all kinds of shared assets is an
important and challenging task. The cylinders depicted in Figure 13.11 are
different tools, possibly from different vendors, with different repositories.
Exchanging the feature model information to the shared assets superset
models requires interoperability between the tools.
Figure 13.11 Feature model connections.
Technically, the engineering tools must be able to get the feature
information from the feature modeling tool. Additionally, they must be able
to understand the information and appropriately apply it to their engineering
data. Alternatively, a specific PLE tool can do the job independently from
the other tools by retrieving the feature information from the feature model
tool and managing the shared asset variability in the other engineering
tools. Of course, the PLE tool needs access to the tools by an API or plugin
to realize such synergy.
The variability exchange language is an initiative to specify a standardized
format for exchanging feature models (OASIS 2018). However, the project
has not yet been completed. The exchange still takes place proprietarily and
depends on the interfaces offered by the tools used. Often, the feature model
tools offer various plug-ins for the engineering tools to establish the
connection and make the appropriate changes in the engineering model.
Although systems modeling language v2 (SysML v2) does not explicitly
provide feature modeling, it could be used for it, and the SysML v2
Application Programming Interface (API) provides standardized access to
the feature model data.
13.6 Drone Product Line Example
The “tiny PLE wall” is an oversimplified example that demonstrates the
concepts of MBPLE. The “drone product line” example is closer to real-
world projects, although, of course, it omits many details that are outside
the book’s scope.
Imagine an organization that develops various types of drones. They offer
both standard products for sale and customized solutions. Their product
portfolio includes:
Standard consumer drones.
Delivery drones for transporting small goods to hard-to-reach places,
such as islands or ships.
Surveillance drones for tasks like locating and monitoring forest fires.
The feature tree contains one feature constraint: the feature “six engines”
requires the feature “long distance battery.”
The product line for a forest fire detection system (FFDS) also references
the feature model of the drone product line in Figure 13.12. The feature
constraint explicitly defines here that the feature in the FFDS for “long-time
monitoring” missions requires the feature “long-distance battery” in the
included drone feature model (Figure 13.13).
Figure 13.12 Drone product line feature tree.
Figure 13.13 Cross-feature model feature constraint.
After explaining the concept of features and feature models, the next
chapter will shift focus to the other “side of the coin”: feature
configurations.

OceanofPDF.com
Chapter 14
Define Feature Configurations
14.1 Introduction
This chapter presents the modeling of feature configurations, building upon
the foundational concepts introduced in Chapter 9, and the detailed feature
information model presented in Chapter 26. The definition of feature
configurations is the second step of the model-based product line
engineering (MBPLE) four-step approach (Figure 14.1).
Figure 14.1 MBPLE four-step approach “define feature
configurations.”
The chapter outlines the basic techniques in Section 14.2 and covers feature
configurations in product line engineering (PLE) tools in Section 14.3.
14.2 Basic Concepts
Chapter 13 has already shown that the unconstrained potential feature
combinations typically do not correspond to the number of planned
products in the product line. These combinations increase exponentially
with the number of variability. As a simple example, just take 10 features,
each with three selection options. This already leads to 50 049 possible
combinations. Significantly fewer of these are only valid if feature
constraints declare some invalid. However, the number of valid
combinations will certainly still be much higher than the number of
different products that the product line is supposed to create. The planned
set of member products is an outcome of the product line scoping.
Feature configurations specify the planned combinations, which means the
planned products of the product line. A feature configuration is a valid
selection of features.
Feature configurations are often represented within a matrix view, where
the rows depict the features and the columns depict the feature
configurations. The cells show the selection of a feature in a feature
configuration. The root feature is omitted in the matrix, as it is always
selected by all feature configurations of the feature model. Figure 14.2
shows a feature configuration matrix for our simple “tiny PLE wall”
example from Chapter 13.
Figure 14.2 Feature configurations map.
A feature configuration can also be represented by a feature used in another
feature model. Figure 14.3 shows the feature configurations from Figure
14.2, represented by features as part of the feature model. They are an
exclusive-or (XOR) feature group of subfeatures of the root feature and
play a special role in the tree. Selecting a feature configuration implies
selecting the corresponding features.
Figure 14.3 Feature tree with feature configurations.
Let us assume our “tiny PLE wall” is part of another product line, “PLE
house.” The “PLE house” feature tree is depicted in Figure 14.4. The root
feature of the “tiny PLE wall” feature model is referenced and used as a
subfeature of the “PLE house” root feature. The reference is depicted by a
rectangle with a dashed borderline.
Figure 14.4 PLE house feature tree.
All features of the “tiny PLE wall” feature tree could be used here.
However, typically, only the defined feature configurations of the used
feature model are of interest. They represent the planned configurations
created by the experts of the corresponding product line. Therefore, only the
corresponding products are available and can be used within the parent
product line.
A “PLE house” feature configuration can select sub-features of the Door
and Roof (not shown in Figure 14.4) and select one of the “tiny PLE wall”
feature configurations.
Similar to features, feature configurations can also affect all layers of a
level, such as the requirements, functional, logical, and physical layers, and
might include the scope of different system levels.
14.3 Feature Modeling Tools
A feature modeling tool also provides the capability to specify feature
configurations in addition to the feature tree. The usual view and editor for
feature configurations is a matrix. You will find such opinions in
commercial feature modeling tools like pure::variants from PTC (PTC
2024) or Gears from BigLever (BigLever 2024).
Figure 14.5 shows the matrix reproduced from Figure 14.2 in the tool
FeatureIDE (METOP 2020). The parameter settings of the feature “standard
brick mass” are configured in the tool, but not visible in the configuration
matrix.
Figure 14.5 FeatureIDE configuration matrix.
During the features selection, the PLE tools check whether the feature
constraints are fulfilled since only valid combinations can be chosen.
Feature configurations do not have to be always complete. Depending on
the binding time, feature decisions can also be made later. For example, a
feature with a binding time of “production” must not be decided within the
“design” phase. The concept of partial feature configurations is explained in
Chapter 9.
Similar to what was discussed in Chapter 13, the challenge consists of
transferring the information about the feature configuration and individual
features in all affected models and making use of it.
References
BigLever (2024). BigLever Software Gears.
https://biglevehom/solution/gears/ (accessed 25 July 2024).
METOP (2020). FeatureIDE. https://www.featureide.de/ (accessed 1
October 2023).
PTC (2024). PTC pure::variants. https://www.ptc.com/en/products/pure-
variants (accessed 25 July 2024).

Chapter 15
Define Shared Assets
15.1 Introduction
The chapter describes how to model shared assets with Systems Modeling
Language (SysML). The main part uses the latest version of SysML v2, but
SysML v1 and other modeling languages are also presented. Chapters 9 and
27 define the concept of shared assets and the superset. The definition of
shared assets is the third step of the model-based product line engineering
(MBPLE) four-step approach (Figure 15.1).
Figure 15.1 MBPLE four-step approach “define shared assets.”
Section 15.2 presents the basic principle with two different approaches to
model variable shared assets. The direct approach is covered in Section
15.2.1 and the clean approach in Section 15.2.2. Section 15.2 closes by
showing the impact of variability across multiple layers and levels in
Section 15.2.3.
Section 15.3 shows the modeling of shared assets with SysML v2, and
Section 15.4 shows the modeling with SysML v1. Section 15.5 briefly
demonstrates the modeling of shared assets without SysML.
15.2 Basic Principle
There are three basic approaches for modeling shared assets that implement
the variability of the feature model.
The first approach is mentioned here only for the sake of completeness. It is
not a real MBPLE approach, but the one used when there is no established
MBPLE with no explicit management of commonalities between similar
products. Assets are copied and pasted and then further developed (a clone-
and-own approach). Sometimes, this may seem to be the best way for the
project because it is quick and easy, but it can lead to more effort and
missed synergies. The disadvantages of the approach versus the advantages
of using product line engineering (PLE) are described in detail in Part I of
this book (in particular in Chapter 3).
The other two approaches are real MBPLE approaches, presented in
Sections 15.2.1 and 15.2.2. They were first published under these names in
the paper (Forlingieri and Weilkiens 2022).
Methodologies in general and also the MBPLE methodology in this book
follow a logical top-down sequence. In practice, work is usually carried out
simultaneously from top to bottom and from bottom to top.
For example, shared assets are usually already in place when a product line
is set up. Therefore, they exist before the features.
Just as features and feature configurations are identified during product
scoping, the existing shared assets must also be analyzed during asset
scoping to examine their use in the product line.
15.2.1 “Direct Method” to Create Shared Assets
The first real PLE approach is called the “direct method.” All variants are
specified directly at the appropriate point in the model and are labeled as
variants.
If the modeling language does not explicitly cover the concept of
variability, this can lead to inconsistencies. For example, a model of a
vehicle specifies three different engines in the same position within the
vehicle. Without explicit variability modeling, this is a technically invalid
model. Depending on the tool and language, this can lead to problems.
Figure 15.2 shows all variants of “the tiny PLE wall” in a blueprint view of
a fictional wall modeling tool.
Figure 15.2 Direct method shared assets “tiny PLE walls.”
The direct approach is easily applicable. The variant is added like any other
artifact. The bottom positions of the wall now have more than one brick
option. The variation points (also known as variations) are depicted as tags
in Figure 15.2.
In addition to the ease, another advantage of the direct approach is that you
can see everything at a glance. Figure 15.2 shows the common core and all
variant elements. It’s like putting each product’s blueprints on top of each
other. Showing everything at once is practical, but it can also easily have
the disadvantage of making it confusing or hiding aspects.
The blueprint model in Figure 15.2 specifies which different kinds of bricks
are located on the wall’s lower left and right sides. Without processing the
variability information, the model is wrong. If the blueprint model does not
have a built-in capability for variability modeling, it could lead to issues.
For example, a wall modeling tool could complain that placing more than
one brick at the same location is not allowed.
If the tool for the wall specification supports different views of the data, the
specification data could be depicted in different views, each showing only
one or a few variants to make it more overseeable. But the data behind the
views is still squeezed. The shared assets superset created by the direct
approach does not represent real products. You need an additional
technology that manages the variability. Together with the feature model
and selected feature configurations, such technology can transform the
shared assets into a product model, which means a model that specifies only
a single product.
The direct approach is component-oriented rather than systems-
engineering-oriented. However, it is easily applicable and can be a good
choice in the project’s early exploration phases or when variation is added
to existing legacy assets.
15.2.2 “Clean Method” to Create Shared Assets
The second approach is called the “clean method.” The variants are not
directly added to the model like in the direct method. Instead, first, the point
of variation is identified. In the wall example, it is the lower left and right
brick of the wall. Second, the commonality of all variants is explicitly
specified. The common element is a variation element. Here, the
commonality is the concept of a brick with a tag in the middle of the front
and a specific size. The immutable asset and the variations together form
the core of the shared assets superset. The core is part of all member
products. The variants are specified separately and linked with the
appropriate variation in the core (Figure 15.3). The variation links to the
variants and the appropriate feature in the feature model. A variation is also
called “variation point.”
Figure 15.3 Clean method shared assets superset “tiny PLE walls.”
The modeling language and tool must be able to separate common
specifications (the variations) from ad hoc specifications (the variants),
including the relationship between them. Otherwise, the direct method can
be used.
15.2.3 Multilayer and Multidiscipline Shared Assets
We have already discussed the topic of multilevel and multilayer in Chapter
13 when defining the feature models. It also applies to shared assets. The
terms layer and level are used differently. In Figure 15.4, we use the term
layer for the horizontal structure (RFLP) and the term level for the vertical
decomposition level. RFLP and the names of the decomposition levels are
only examples and can be replaced by any other terminology. The concept
remains the same.
Figure 15.4 Multilevel and multilayer shared assets.
In addition to multiple layers and levels, each cell can contain several
models and tools. Overall, it is a multidimensional space with many
different relationships between the individual layers, levels, and models
within a cell. It illustrates the challenge of engineering projects in the real
world.
This could be a challenging engineering space, even for a single system.
But here we are talking about a product line, i.e. a large number of systems.
The variability is defined in the feature model or, as in Chapter 13, in
several feature models and affects all shared assets, which are distributed in
this multidimensional engineering space.
15.3 Modeling of Shared Assets with SysML
v2
An essential capability of SysML v2 (OMG 2024b) is the built-in concept
of variation and variants. Any definition or usage element can be a
variation. The definition and usage elements in SysML v2 cover the most
required kinds of elements used in technical systems engineering processes,
like requirements, parts, ports, interfaces, actions, states, and so forth. A
variation defines a list of design choices, which are the variants. The
SysML v2 concept of a variation misses the link to features since features
are not part of SysML v2 (see Chapter 13). A variant in SysML v2 is always
a usage element because it is a design choice with a context.
Behind the scenes of the SysML v2, the variations and variants are not
model elements on their own but are ends of a variant membership
relationship (Figure 15.5). The relationship connects a definition or usage
element (the variation) with exactly one usage element (the variant). It
denotes the usage element as a design choice for the variation element.
Typically, a variation includes more than one variant. There is one variant
membership relationship for each variant. Finally, it depends on the user
interface of the modeling tool and how we can create variations and
variants. Still, very likely, as modelers, we will not explicitly work with the
variant membership relationship, except if we access the model data using
scripts or other model-data-based approaches.
Figure 15.5 Variant membership relationship.
The variation element is always abstract because, without the choice of one
of its variants, there is no instance specified by the variation. The variants
must conform to the variation. That means if the variation is a definition
element, the variants are implicitly defined by the variation. If the variation
is a usage element, the variants subset the variation. The subsections below
provide concrete examples to illustrate the concept.
Variations and variants need a link to the appropriate features in the feature
model. The feature model is outside of a SysML v2 model, and the link
between variations or variants and the features requires additional
technology to manage the relationship across the models. For example, a
plugin from a feature model tool vendor in a SysML v2 modeling tool could
connect the SysML elements and the features. The plugin can access the
feature model and control the SysML v2 model, for example, display
filtered views based on feature configurations that only show elements that
are valid for the selected features.
Since modeling variations and variants with SysML uses the specialization
mechanism, the clean approach fits very well, and the following sections
use only the clean method. The application of the direct method with
SysML v2 is briefly discussed. Section 15.4 describes applying the clean
and direct method with SysML v1.
The shared assets superset is modeled as a library package in SysML v2. It
contains the shared assets organized in subpackages. It does not cover the
whole shared assets superset, but only the SysML assets. There might be
other shared assets in other models like computer-aided design (CAD),
product life cycle management (PLM), or simulation models.
library package Drone_SharedAssetsSuperset {
package DroneBattery { …
package DroneEngine { …
Section 15.3.1 presents the variability modeling of SysML v2 part
structures. Section 15.3.2 focuses the variability modeling of behavior,
while Section 15.3.3 addresses the variability modeling of requirements.
15.3.1 Modeling of Shared Assets: Parts
Chapter 13 specifies the feature model for the drone product line, which
includes the mandatory feature “batteryKind” with two alternative
subfeatures, “standardBattery” and “longDistanceBattery,” and a mandatory
feature about the number of engines, which can be four or six.
Figure 15.6 depicts an abstract definition of a drone battery that specifies
some properties and two concrete specializations of the abstract definition
for a standard and a long-distance battery. The package
“DroneBattery_Parts” is contained in the package “DroneBattery” in the
shared assets superset library package, which is not shown in Figure 15.5.
The example focuses on a limited set to highlight only the variability
aspect.
Figure 15.6 Shared assets for drone batteries.
The battery definitions are specified by model elements without any explicit
variability. A shared asset is not required to vary. It is just a digital artifact
of the engineering process used for the member products. Implicitly, the
model in Figure 15.6 already contains a touch of variability because it
specifies two different kinds of drone batteries with different capacities. But
it does not specify how they are used. It is only a library of part definitions
like a parts catalog.
Next, variability is added to the shared assets by constraining their usage
according to the feature model. The variations are organized in a separate
package named “DroneBattery_Variations.” The element
“droneBatteryVariation” is a part and a variation. It specifies two design
choices as variants, each of them is linked to the appropriate features in the
feature model (Figure 15.7). The links between the variants and the features
implicitly include the relationship between the variation and the feature.
Therefore, it is not explicitly modeled. Since these relationships are created
between elements in different models and tools, the link cannot be done
directly, but requires a technology that establishes and maintains the
connection.
Figure 15.7 Variation of the drone battery.
The variants in Figure 15.7 depend on the feature model, but the part
definitions of the drone battery in Figure 15.6 have no dependency on
features. Following the principle of separation of concerns, they can be
stored in separate packages in the shared assets superset and can also be
used in other contexts.
The shared assets superset also contains a package “Drone_ProductLine”
with elements specifying the complete SysML part of the drone product
line. The following Figure 15.8 shows only some elements of it. The battery
of the drone product line specializes in the drone battery variation. The top-
level element is the product itself, which includes all variations. Here, it is
the part definition of “Drone.”
Figure 15.8 Definition of the drone product line.
Another feature of the drone product line is the number of engines. The
variation is directly specified inside the drone specification (variation
attribute numberOfEngines). For the sake of simplicity, the
variability is only specified by the number used for the multiplicity of the
engine part (part engine[numberOfEngines] : DroneEngine).
The feature model specifies a feature constraint between the feature
“numberOfEngines” and “batteryKind”: six engines require a long-distance
battery. The constraint can also be considered in the SysML model between
the variants, but it is redundant to the feature model. If possible, the
modeling principle of avoiding redundancy should be followed. Redundant
elements must be kept synchronized to ensure the consistency of the model.
Someone has to do this reliably.
Anyhow, the following textual syntax shows an example of the variability
constraint that mirrors the feature constraint between variants.
abstract part def Drone {
// Battery specializes the variation
part battery :> droneBatteryVariation;
// Engines
variation attribute
numberOfEngines : ScalarValues::Integer {
variant attribute fourEngines = 4;
variant attribute sixEngines = 6;
}
assert constraint {
(numberOfEngines == numberOfEngines::sixEngines) and
(battery == DroneBattery_Variation::droneBatteryVari
ation::longDistanceBattery)
}
part engine[4..numberOfEngines]: DroneEngine;
}
Technically, the number of engines has more impact on the specification of
the drone than a simple change in its multiplicity. For example, the drone
might need different bodies according to the number of engines and
different flight control software. The shared assets superset also contains
the assets for the special bodies and the software (Figure 15.9).
Figure 15.9 Drone body and flight control software variations.
The different bodies and software applications do not appear in the feature
model. They are not selectable product line features but variants
implementing the product feature “numberOfEngines.”
This relationship should be modeled in the constraint so that the constraint
looks as follows. As mentioned above, the relationship to the battery type is
redundant and is already covered by the feature constraint in the feature
model.
assert constraint {
((numberOfEnginesVariation == numberOfEnginesVariation::
sixEngines) and
(battery == DroneBattery::DroneBattery_Parts::DroneBatte
ryVariation::longDistanceBattery) and
(body == DroneBody::DroneBody_Parts::DroneBodyVariation:
:droneBody6Engines) and
(flightControl == DroneFlightControl::FlightControlVaria
tion::flightControl4Engines)) or
((numberOfEnginesVariation == numberOfEnginesVariation::
fourEngines) and
(body ==
DroneBody::DroneBody_Parts::DroneBodyVariation::droneBod
y4Engines) and
(flightControl == DroneFlightControl::FlightControlVaria
tion::flightControl6Engines))
}
So far, the example has been modeled using the clean approach. The
variation element represents the commonality and the variants, the
specializations. The direct approach with SysML means “not using the
variation and variant concepts of SysML v2.” Therefore, it does not make
sense to apply the direct approach if the modeling language explicitly
supports the modeling of variability. Section 15.4 illustrates the direct
approach with SysML v1, which does not support variability modeling in its
notation.
If you were to model the drone with the variability in battery type and
number of engines using the direct method, it would look like in the
example below. The (non-SysML) variation points are not displayed, but
they can still filter out the corresponding parts via an external technology
based on the feature configuration.
part def Drone {
part standardBattery : StandardBattery;
part longDistanceBattery : LongDistanceBattery;
part fourEngine[4] : DroneEngine;
part sixEngine[6] : DroneEngine;
}
15.3.2 Modeling of Shared Assets: Behavior
The behavior elements of SysML have the same foundation as the structural
model elements. Therefore, the basic approach for modeling behavior
variability is the same as for structures.
The drone product line has a feature, “Flight Patterns,” with three
subfeatures: “Fly Observation Patterns,” “Fly Camera Patterns,” and “Fly
Fun Patterns” (Figure 15.10).
Figure 15.10 Feature flight patterns.
Figure 15.11 shows different flight pattern actions corresponding to the
features. The details are omitted here because the flight pattern algorithms
are unimportant for the variability aspect.
Figure 15.11 Flight pattern actions.
The use of variation and variants corresponds to the clean approach again.
This is a good example that shows why the direct approach is not always
possible. The same action modeled according to the direct approach could
lead to a nonvalid SysML v2 model, as the fork node must not have
multiple incoming successions.
The same scenario leads to the analogous formal problem in SysML v1.
Figure 15.12 shows the invalid SysML model.
Figure 15.12 Invalid SysML model scenario usage of the direct
approach.
15.3.3 Modeling of Shared Assets: Requirements
Also, variations and variants of requirements can be modeled similarly to
the modeling of structure and behavior variability. In the example below,
the requirement definition maximum takeoff weight (MTOW) generally
defines a takeoff weight requirement for drones. The two requirement
usages specify concrete weights. The following SysML textual notation
shows parts of the definition of the drone requirement specification,
including the variation of the takeoff weight requirement according to the
product feature “numberOfEngines.”
requirement def <‘D-REQ-22’> MTOW {
subject drone : Drone;
doc /*
* The maximum takeoff weight (MTOW) for a d
rone shall not exceed [X] kilograms.
* This includes the weight of the drone
itself, its battery, payload, and any
* additional attached equipment. [X]
depends on the number of engines.
*/
attribute mtow : ISQ::MassValue;
require constraint {drone.massActual <= mtow}
}
requirement def <‘D-REQ-23’> MTOW_Quadcopter :> MTOW {
doc /*
* The maximum takeoff weight (MTOW) for a q
uadcopter equipped with 4 engines shall
* not exceed 15 kilograms.
*/
:>> mtow = 15[SI::kg];
}
requirement def <‘D-REQ-24’> MTOW_Hexacopter :> MTOW {
doc /*
* The maximum takeoff weight (MTOW) for a h
exacopter equipped with 6 engines shall
* not exceed 25 kilograms.
*/
:>> mtow = 25[SI::kg];
}
requirement droneStakeholderRequirements {
subject drone : Drone;
require droneEngineStakeholderRequirements;
require droneBatteryStakeholderRequirements;
variation requirement maximumTakeoffWeight {
variant requirement mtqw_Quadcopter:
MTOW_Quadcopter;
variant requirement mtqw_Hexacopter:
MTOW_Hexacopter;
}
}
The requirements are linked with features in the feature model. The
requirements are, in turn, satisfied by architectural elements. These also
have corresponding variability and are modeled as variants with
relationships to the corresponding features.
Figure 15.13 shows these relationships between the elements. Conceptually,
the “implements” relationship between the architecture element and the
feature can be omitted. It is sufficient that only those shared assets are
linked with features that are directly derived from them, such as the
requirement. But not the parts that are conceptually derived from the
requirements. If the shared asset model has implemented clean traceability,
it is sufficient to link the source elements with the features to calculate all
derived elements.
Figure 15.13 Feature, requirement, and architecture element.
In this way, if the feature is updated, complete traceability to all impacted
shared assets is secured.
15.4 Clean and Direct Approach with SysML
v1
SysML v1 (OMG 2022a) does not have explicit model elements for the
modeling of variants. However, it is possible to use SysML v1 to model
variability. For example, the variability modeling of software-intensive
systems (VAMOS) approach (Variant Modeling with SysML) provides a set
of stereotypes (profile) for a variability language extension for SysML v1
(Weilkiens 2016).
Figure 15.14 shows variations and variants of the drone example. The
stereotypes “variation” and “variant” are from the VAMOS profile. They
are applied to packages that contain all elements that belong to a variation
or variant. The meaning of variations and variants is slightly different from
their semantics in SysML v2. Since they are attached to a grouping element,
they also play the role of features that are implemented by the elements
inside the packages.
Figure 15.14 Variations and variants with SysML v1.
The core elements are defined in a separate package named “Drone_Core.”
Figure 15.15 depicts some core system architecture breakdown structure
elements with variation points. The VAMOS variation point corresponds to
the variation concept of SysML v2. It marks a single shared asset as an
element with design choices.
Figure 15.15 Drone core system architecture breakdown with
variations.
The variants are specializations of variations which correspond to the
variant concept of SysML v2. Figure 15.16 shows the definition of the
battery variants.
Figure 15.16 Drone battery variants.
The modeling above follows the clean approach. The core and variant
elements are clearly separated. The common parts of the variable elements
are extracted and modeled as abstract core elements, such as the block
DroneBattery.
The direct approach does not require a strict separation of core and variant
elements. Figure 15.17 defines the drone system architecture using the
direct approach. The architecture does not define a valid system without
considering the variability represented by the variation points. It is a drone
with two different batteries and 4 and 6 engines, which means 10 engines,
two bodies, and so forth. The variation points tag the variable elements and
link them with the features. A variability tool could transform the model
into a valid product model based on a feature configuration. However, a
SysML v1 tool cannot interpret the variability out-of-the-box. For example,
if it provides analysis functions such as a mass roll-up pattern, it will
calculate incorrect values because all variant components are considered.
Figure 15.17 Drone system architecture with direct approach.
Even in this simple example, the internal block diagram might look
confusing when all parts and connectors are displayed (Figure 15.18). Of
course, this can be displayed more clearly in several internal block
diagrams, for example, only by showing the parts and connectors of one
feature configuration.
Figure 15.18 Drone internal block diagram with direct approach.
15.5 Non-SysML Shared Assets
A shared assets superset does not contain only systems engineering assets
but also elements from other engineering disciplines.
The following is an extract from the drone product line system requirements
specification. Requirements could also be modeled with SysML, but they
are specified in a text document here, to emphasize their textual form
(similar to a requirements set in a dedicated requirements management
system). The textual syntax of the requirements, and in particular, the
variants and variation points, are not based on a standard but on a project-
specific guideline. The variation points are inside curly brackets and point
to the appropriate feature. The variability is modeled by applying the direct
approach because specifying variability is not explicitly supported by the
tool and language.
Therefore, the specification contains requirements with the same identifier
(REQ-TPW2, REQ-TPW3). In sum, the requirement list shown below is
not valid. It only becomes valid when a feature selection is made, and the
unwanted requirements are removed from the list with the help of the
variation points and feature configurations. Alternatively, the variant
requirements could get special identifiers like REQ-TPW2a and REQ-
TPW2b. However, the complete list of requirements is only valid once the
variants have been transformed.
Requirement Requirement text
ID
REQ-TPW1 The top brick shall be a standard brick with a black tag “P”
in the middle of the front.
REQ-TPW2 The lower left brick shall be an extra-wide brick with a
black tag “L” in the middle of the front {v: F1.1.1}.
REQ-TPW2 The lower left brick shall be three very small bricks with a
black tag “L” in the middle of the front of each brick {v:
F1.1.2}.
REQ-TPW3 The lower right brick shall be a dark standard brick with a
black tag “E” in the middle of the front {v: F1.2.1}.
REQ-TPW3 The lower right brick shall be two small bricks with a
black tag “E” in the middle of the front of each brick {v:
F1.2.2}.
REQ-TPW3 The lower right brick shall be a sandy standard brick with
a white tag “E” in the middle of the front {v: F1.2.3}.
It is also possible to use the clean approach by applying special rules to the
standardized requirement text. In the example below, the rule is as follows:
The text of a variation requirement is enclosed by square brackets (REQ-
TPW2). The deviating requirements are given the same requirement
identifier with a subnumber (REQ-TPW2.1, REQ-TPW2.2). The variation
and the variants reference the feature ({v: F1.1}, …). Without the variant
subrequirements, it is a valid, complete requirement specification. However,
it is abstract because the variation requirements are not specific enough.
Requirement Requirement text
ID
REQ-TPW1 The top brick shall be a standard brick with a black tag “P”
in the middle of the front.
REQ-TPW2 [The lower left brick shall be a composite of one or more
bricks with a total size of an extra-wide brick and a black
tag “L” in the middle of the front of each brick {v: F1.1}].
REQ-TPW2.1 The lower left brick shall be an extra-wide brick with a
black tag “L” in the middle of the front {v: F1.1.1}.
REQ-TPW2.2 The lower left brick shall be three very small bricks with a
black tag “L” in the middle of the front of each brick {v:
F1.1.2}.
REQ-TPW3 [The lower right brick shall be a composite of one or more
bricks with the total size of a standard brick and a tag in the
middle of the front of each brick {v: F1.2}].
REQ-TPW3.1 The lower right brick shall be a dark standard brick with a
black tag “E” in the middle of the front {v: F1.2.1}.
REQ-TPW3.2 The lower right brick shall be two small bricks with a black
tag “E” in the middle of the front of each brick {v: F1.2.2}.
REQ-TPW3.3 The lower right brick shall be a sandy standard brick with a
white tag “E” in the middle of the front {v: F1.2.3}.

Chapter 16
Define Product Models
16.1 Introduction
This chapter completes the description of modeling variability. Chapters 13
and 14 describe the definition of feature models and feature configurations,
Chapter 15 discusses the modeling of shared assets, and also focuses on
how product models for the specification of member products are derived
from them.
The derivation of product models is the fourth step of the model-based
product line engineering (MBPLE) four-step approach (Figure 16.1).
Figure 16.1 MBPLE four-step approach “define product models.”
Section 16.2 presents the basic principles, Section 16.3 describes how to
model a product model with Systems Modeling Language (SysML) v2, and
Section 16.4 presents the modeling with SysML v1.
16.2 Basic Principles
A product model is a product specification based on a feature configuration.
It sets together the shared assets appropriate to the selected features within
the feature configuration. According to ISO/IEC 26580 (ISO/IEC 2021), the
product model is referred to as a “product asset instance.”
A tool could automatically derive a product model: With the selected
features, the feature configuration contains the design decisions for the
variations in the shared assets superset. Depending on the specific shared
asset model, the selected variant can be used and the others discarded. Even
if it is done automatically, in the real world, the product model is often
further developed and completed with elements that are not fully integrated
into the overall product line model.
A product model is an input specification for production and resolves only
those variant choices that belong to the binding time “design” (Figure 16.2).
The choices with binding time “production” are made during production. A
handover artifact for the operation stage, in addition to the product itself,
could consist of a handbook that describes the design choices with binding
time “operation,” for example, the option of how to replace a battery with a
different kind.
Figure 16.2 Handover artifacts.
The shared assets superset of “the tiny PLE wall” example includes two
variations. With the information from the feature configuration
“3VerySmallBricksTinyPLEWall,” a tool could automatically transform the
shared assets into a product model specifying only a single member product
(Figure 16.3).
Figure 16.3 Tiny PLE wall product model.
This requires that the selected variants of a variation are automatically
integrated into the product model, and those not selected are removed to
create a consistent model. It is quite simple in our fictitious wall model
(Figure 16.3). Since our focus here is to facilitate the understanding of the
approach and not of the technical system. Conceptually, the transformation
could be done automatically, but this depends primarily on the capabilities
of the modeling languages and tools used.
Typically, a product model is created in each modeling tool used for shared
assets. For example, a SysML modeling tool, a CAD tool, and a
requirements management tool together form the complete product model
(Figure 16.4).
Figure 16.4 Product model.
SysML models often play the role of the backbone in an engineering
environment. The product model in a SysML model could be the glue that
binds all the others together to a fully traceable specification. The arrows
from the SysML product model to the others indicate that relationship in
Figure 16.4. However, in the implementation of the MBPLE approach, it is
not mandatory to use SysML.
The path from the shared assets to the product assets in the product model is
usually a one-way street. If changes are made in the product model, these
cannot be automatically incorporated into the corresponding shared assets
model. Chapter 18 further details this aspect.
Suppose that whoever is working on the product model decides to change
the bottom left bricks and make the center brick a dark brick as well (Figure
16.5). It is only a minor change, but the transformation that created the
product model from the shared assets is unidirectional and will not
automatically update the shared assets model.
Figure 16.5 Product model change.
If the update does not impact the existing “3VerySmallBricks” variant but
adds another one, “3VerySmallBricksWithADarkOne,” this would also
result in a change in the feature model. Even with a bidirectional
transformation, the decision should be made in advance. An automatic
transformation that adapts the feature model is imaginable but very
challenging.
There are several options:
1. The change can be manually transferred to the shared asset and feature
models. This may require communication and agreement across
departmental boundaries, as other people may be responsible for the
shared assets superset and the feature model than those responsible for
the product model.
In any case, it is important not to skip this step. It is very likely not
necessary for current product development but for future
developments. So, it is a task that could be easily pushed out of the
project plan.
2. The engineering process can prohibit changes to the product model
and require that changes be made only to the source, the shared assets
superset, and the feature model, and that the product model be
regenerated.
This would be a clean approach. It is particularly beneficial for this
approach that the transformation to the product model is as completely
automatic as possible, as otherwise, the effort involved in this iterative
approach can quickly become unreasonably high if several changes
must be made.
3. The shared assets superset is not updated. This could be useful if the
change only affects the specific product and should not become part of
the product line.
4. It could also be a valid approach not to map the complete product
specifications in the product line but only selected areas. The missing
parts are added to the product models outside the product line
development.
16.3 Modeling of Product Models with SysML
v2
In this section, we look at SysML v2 shared assets supersets and the
derivation of a corresponding SysML v2 product model. The product model
can be derived from the shared asset models in many different ways. Two
approaches are shown below:
Generation of a product model that is independent of the shared assets
superset.
Generation of a product model that uses elements of the shared assets
superset and is therefore directly dependent on it.
A product line engineering (PLE) tool could create a product model based
on a feature configuration and shared assets. It looks like a “normal” system
model, for example, the structural model of an XDrone product, which has
a long-distance battery and six engines.
part def ‘Forest Fire Observation Drone’ {
part longDistanceBattery : LongDistanceBattery;
part sixEngine[6] : DroneEngine;
}
If the shared assets superset is created with the direct approach, it is simply
a filtering transformation and probably the setting of some parameters (see
features with parameters in Chapters 13 and 14).
If the shared assets superset is modeled with the clean approach as
described in Chapter 15, the product model can keep links to the shared
assets. Since modeling variability according to ISO/IEC 26580 (ISO/IEC
2021) is part of the language SysML v2 (OMG 2024b), modeling the
product model with variability is relatively simple.
First, the product must be defined. It is a specialization of the product line
element from the shared assets model. In the example of the drone, the
product line element is the “drone” (see in the previous chapter Figure
15.8). In the following, we define the product of a forest fire observation
drone. In SysML v2, the defined by relationship is a kind of specialization.
Figure 16.6 shows the member product “forestFireObservationDrone”
defined by the product line “drone.”
Figure 16.6 Member product “forestFireObservationDrone” defined
by the product line “drone.”
The product model element is linked with the appropriate feature
configuration. As mentioned in previous chapters, the relationship must be
established using a corresponding technology that connects SysML models
with feature models.
For each variation in the shared assets supersets of the product line element,
the corresponding variant must now be selected according to the feature
configuration. The selection is made in the model by redefining the
variation and binding the corresponding variant. The redefinition is
indicated by “:>>“ in the textual notation of SysML v2. The following
Figure 16.7 shows some of these redefinitions in the product model for the
forest fire observation drone.
Figure 16.7 Resolving the variability.
It is easy to imagine that such a product model could also be created
automatically by a PLE tool, at least in this simple example. At the same
time, additional changes that are not created by the transformation could
also be implemented.
16.4 Modeling of Product Models with SysML
v1
Chapter 15.4 describes the modeling of shared assets with SysML v1 (OMG
2024a). Using the clean approach, they are located in core and variation
packages to separate the common core elements from the variable elements.
As described in the previous section for SysML v2, a PLE tool can also
generate a SysML v1 product model from a shared assets superset that is
independent of the shared assets.
It is also possible to create a SysML v1 model that is linked with the shared
assets superset. The VAMOS approach (Weilkiens 2016) describes a “way”
to model it. The product models are inside another package. VAMOS calls
the product model a variant configuration (see terminology comparison
table in Appendix A). Figure 16.8 shows parts of the definition of the forest
fire observation drone variant configuration (product model). At first
glance, the model is more complex than the same situation in the SysML v2
model. This is primarily because the modeling of variability is not explicitly
supported by SysML v1. Variability is primarily modeled using
generalization relationships and redefinition.
Figure 16.8 Forest fire observation drone variant configuration
(product model).
The basic principle is that the root element of the product model (forest fire
observation drone) specializes the root element of the shared assets superset
(done). Selected variants redefine the common features; for example,
battery : LongDistanceBattery redefines battery :
DroneBattery.
References
ISO/IEC (2021). ISO/IEC 2658 0: 2021 Software and systems engineering
– methods and tools for the feature-based approach to software and
systems product line engineering. London: ISO/IEC.
https://www.iso.org/standard/43139.html.
Object Management Group (OMG) (2024a). ‘OMG System Modeling
Language Version 1.7, formal/24-01-07’. Object Management Group.
Object Management Group (OMG) (2024b). ‘OMG System Modeling
Language Version 2.0 beta 2, ptc/24-02-03’. Object Management Group.
https://www.omg.org/spec/SysML.
Weilkiens, T. (2016). Variant Modeling with SysML. MBSE4U.
OceanofPDF.com
Chapter 17
The Four Dimensions of Variability
17.1 Introduction
In this chapter, we show how it is key for companies that aim at
implementing model-based product line engineering (MBPLE) to digitalize,
harmonize, and enhance the end-to-end development of complex systems.
Forlingieri (2022) taking the example of Airbus, shows how such
digitalization initiatives often include manufacturing and after-sales
services, going beyond the development of the system of interest (e.g. the
engineered product) itself. In particular, he identifies four crucial
dimensions to be considered during the modeling and management of
variability within an MBPLE approach (Forlingieri 2022). These include
the following:
Co-variability in product, manufacturing, and services: This
dimension addresses how the variability, in one area, such as product
design, can impact manufacturing processes and services. It requires a
holistic approach to manage the interdependencies effectively (Section
17.2).
Variability in the development life cycle: It refers to the differences
that can arise at various stages of the development process, from initial
design to final production. It emphasizes the need for flexible
strategies for modeling the variability that can adapt to changes and
challenges throughout the system life cycle (Section 17.3).
Variability in layers of abstraction: This dimension focuses on the
different levels of abstraction, from operational concept (OpsCon) to
detailed implementation. Managing variability at each layer ensures
that the overall vision aligns with practical execution (Section 17.4).
Variability in system hierarchy levels: It pertains to the complexity
inherent in systems that are composed of numerous interconnected
parts. Understanding and managing the variability at different levels of
the system hierarchy is essential for ensuring its consistency and
cascading along the entire system (Section 17.5).
Finally, Section 17.6 discusses how, for companies in sectors that develop
complex systems, addressing these dimensions of variability within an
MBPLE approach becomes crucial. The impact is not only on the product
line itself but also the process, method, and tools used to develop it. In
Chapters 13–16, we read how the system life cycle, layers of abstraction,
and system breakdown are interrelated with the four-step approach. This
chapter mainly focuses on their relevance to the aerospace and automotive
industries, but these dimensions can be extended to other industrial contexts
as well. Figure 17.1 illustrates a simplification of those dimensions for an
aircraft development example.
Figure 17.1 Simplified example of the four-variability dimensions.
17.2 From Co-Development to Co-Variability
Forlingieri (2022), using the Airbus example, stresses the importance in the
aerospace industry of integrating the development of aircraft, industrial
systems, and related after-sales services. This approach, called “co-
development” (Michaelis 2013), is essential when adopting platform or
product line strategies. For example, the installation process and industrial
tooling machinery, including working stations and the devices for lifting
and mounting the engine and pylon within an aircraft, can vary significantly
among different models. This variation is a result of the traditional
development approach, where the final assembly line (FAL) adapts its
processes and tooling based on the specific aircraft type being assembled.
Similarly, the development of engine maintenance services often requires
customization to the specific aircraft, with maintenance instructions and
procedures tailored accordingly.
In co-development, the product line’s scope includes the aircraft, its
manufacturing, and the extended services, making it crucial to identify and
assess the “co-variability” from the aircraft, FAL, and related services in the
early phases of development. This early assessment of co-variability is
pivotal for engineering the aircraft efficiently, ensuring standardization
across the FAL, and enabling the reuse of services, thus fostering a more
integrated and efficient development process.
In the automotive industry, the concept of co-variability in products,
manufacturing, and services is equally important. The development of
vehicles, their manufacturing systems, and associated services are brought
together under a unified development strategy. This approach ensures that
vehicles are designed with manufacturing and serviceability in mind from
the outset. For instance, the assembly line processes, tooling, and service
protocols for different models or variants within a vehicle line-up might
require customization to accommodate specific features or technologies. By
applying a co-development strategy, automakers can streamline the
engineering process, standardize production lines, and enhance service
efficiency. This includes developing modular platforms and the related
manufacturing tooling and services. Co-development emphasizes the need
for early identification and management of co-variability across products,
manufacturing processes, and services for those companies that develop
complex systems.
17.3 Variability Along the Development Life
Cycle
In the aerospace industry, the development life cycle of aircraft
encompasses several phases, ranging from the feasibility of launching a
new program to the qualification and delivery of the aircraft to airlines
(Forlingieri 2022). This progression mirrors the general system life cycle
phases defined by ISO/IEC/IEEE 15288 (ISO/IEC 2023). During the
feasibility phase of a new aircraft development program, the evaluation of
new technologies and their interdependencies with the aircraft architecture
is a critical activity. Key variability inputs, such as fuselage type, mileage
range, passenger capacity, and overall weight targets, are collected and
assessed for their impact on the design and performance of the aircraft.
However, other types of variabilities, such as cabin layouts and
configurations, may not be prioritized in the early stages but become crucial
during the concept and design phases. These elements are significant
because of the value they bring to customers, influencing their experience
and satisfaction with the aircraft. Additionally, customer-specific features,
including those related to country-specific qualifications, might be
addressed in later phases.
Similarly, in the automotive industry, the development life cycle of vehicles
follows a phased approach, from initial feasibility studies to the final
delivery to customers. During the early phases, crucial decisions regarding
the vehicle’s design, technology integration, and performance targets are
made. Variability in aspects like engine options, drivetrain configurations,
and vehicle dimensions are key considerations. As the development
progresses, the focus shifts to more customer-centric variabilities, such as
interior design, infotainment systems, and customization options, which
significantly influence buyer preferences.
Understanding the importance of each type of variability at different stages
of the life cycle is vital for the successful development of a complex
system. This approach ensures that all critical factors are considered at the
appropriate times, facilitating a smooth development process and aligning
product offerings with market demands and customer expectations.
17.4 Cascading Variability Along Layers of
Abstraction
In the development of complex systems using a systems engineering
approach, it is unavoidable to consider multiple layers of abstraction or
viewpoints (Wirtz and Bouffaron 2020). For example, defining the OpsCon
is a vital step to comprehend the operational context of the system of
interest (SOI) and its interdependence with other entities. Variability at the
operational layer can have cascading effects on other abstraction layers,
including functional, logical, and technical layers (Forlingieri 2022).
For instance, the adoption of innovative optional features in aerospace, such
as “electrical taxi-in, taxi-out, and pushback operations,” which allow
aircraft to taxi and push back from the gate without using the main engines,
relying instead on auxiliary power (Forlingieri 2022). This feature
introduces variability not only in how aircraft operations are conducted but
also impacts lower abstraction layers. It necessitates additional functions
like “avoid on-ground collision” or “provide auxiliary power for
autonomous pushback,” which must be integrated into the system. These
functions, in turn, can be realized through various system solutions,
introducing further variability at the logical and technical layers.
Similarly, in the automotive industry, the cascading of variability along
different levels of abstraction is key in development. A practical example is
the implementation of an “autonomous emergency braking” (AEB) system.
Depending on the operational scenario (e.g. driving in a high-density area),
the scope of AEB systems may necessitate additional functional
requirements for identifying and responding to pedestrians in various
scenarios, including low-light conditions and unpredictable movements.
This requires the integration of more complex logical and technical
solutions, such as high-definition cameras, infrared sensors, and
sophisticated image processing algorithms, to accurately detect pedestrians
and calculate the necessary response. Effectively managing the variability
introduced by this new system and the different scenarios – from the
operational level, where drivers and vehicles interact with more dynamic
environments, to the technical level, involving advanced sensor technology
and data processing – is critical for developing vehicles that provide
enhanced safety for both occupants and pedestrians, meeting the
comprehensive safety norms.
In both examples, the aircraft manufacturer and the automaker will continue
to develop systems with and without the new innovative features. Thus,
understanding and managing the variability introduced at each layer – from
operational to technical – becomes of primary importance.
17.5 Breaking Down the Systems and Their
Variability
Systems engineering requires an in-depth understanding of system
decomposition or hierarchy levels. The approach involves the breakdown of
the SOI into several systems, which are further decomposed into
subsystems and components (Eisner 2005). Such a hierarchical structure is
crucial for managing variability in the development of systems product
lines. Variability cascades top–down from the upper level to the lower level,
but it can also be generated bottom–up as solutions from subsystem product
lines provided by suppliers.
For example, the integration of advanced propulsion systems in new
generations of aircraft introduces significant variability. This could include
the adoption of more efficient, environmentally friendly propulsion
technologies, such as hybrid-electric engines or fully electric propulsion
systems. At the aircraft level, this introduces operational variability in terms
of range, fuel efficiency, and environmental impact. At the subsystem level,
additional variability must be considered for the propulsion system itself,
which may involve different types of engines (e.g. turbofan, turboprop,
electric motor) and energy sources (e.g. conventional jet fuel, electricity,
hydrogen fuel cells). In turn, in the propulsion system, the variability in
engine technology and energy source options influences product line
decisions at higher levels, including aircraft design, performance
specifications, and market positioning.
Similarly, in the automotive industry, the transition to autonomous driving
technology illustrates a systems engineering challenge that encompasses
multiple levels of system hierarchy. Starting at the vehicle level, the
adoption of autonomous driving introduces operational variability in terms
of vehicle control, safety features, and user interaction. At the subsystem
level, significant variability arises in the sensor suite (e.g. cameras, LiDAR,
radar) and the onboard computing platform, each of which may offer
different levels of performance, resolution, and data processing capabilities.
Those subsystems might allow variability in technology choices and
configurations, which then influences the broader vehicle product line.
In conclusion, the analysis of the impact of variability, during systems
decomposition, facilitates the modeling of it and the development of multi-
level product lines.
17.6 The Four Dimensions and the MBPLE
Implementation
The four dimensions of variability play a crucial role in the development of
product lines, guiding both the elicitation and modeling of variability
through an MBPLE approach. Forlingieri (2022) highlights that these
dimensions also influence the selection of methods and processes in
MBPLE implementation. Some important decisions that are influenced by
the four dimensions of variability include the following:
the use of a single feature model versus multiple feature models for the
development scope;
the use of proxy features to prevent duplication across system levels
and layers of abstraction;
the adoption of partial configurations during the initial stages of
development when making all variability choices is not feasible;
the maintenance of consistent asset modeling across various
abstraction layers and system hierarchy levels.
For additional insights and detailed examples, “The four dimensions of
variability and their impact on MBPLE” (Forlingieri 2022) serves as a
valuable resource.
References
Eisner, H. (2005). Managing Complex Systems. Hoboken: Wiley.
Forlingieri, M. (2022). The four dimensions of variability and their impact
on MBPLE: how to approach variability in the development of aircraft
product lines at Airbus. In: Proceedings of the 16th International
Working Conference on Variability Modelling of Software-Intensive
Systems (VaMoS’22), 23–25 February 2022, Florence, Italy. ACM.
ISO/IEC/IEEE (2023). Systems and software engineering – system life
cycle processes. (ISO/IEC/IEEE 1528 8: 2023).
Michaelis, M. (2013). Co-development of products and manufacturing
systems using integrated platform models. ICED23, 24–28 July 2023,
Bordeaux, France.
Wirtz, J. and Bouffaron, F. (2020). Applying MBSE at airbus helicopters:
the airbus MBSE architecture framework in CATIA no magic based on
SysML. Tag des Systems Engineering 2020 – digital, 18–20 November
2020.
