Chapter 18
Digital End-to-end Continuity with MBPLE
18.1 Introduction
As outlined in Chapter 6, digital continuity is a fundamental requirement
for implementing model-based product line engineering (MBPLE).
Specifically, it serves in defining and reusing shared assets throughout the
development of product lines and facilitates the analyses of change impacts
during the evolution of the product line.
Digital continuity within MBPLE is equivalent to maintaining the
continuity of data or assets, extending from feature models to all relevant
domain assets (e.g., requirements, source codes, CAD models).
Additionally, Chapter 6 introduces standards such as Open Services for
Lifecycle Collaboration (OSLC) and Modelling and Simulation information
in a Collaborative Systems Engineering Context (MoSSEC) that support the
digital linkage and information exchange across different domains.
Section 18.2 explores the significance of digital repositories for a product
line, which houses and manages these shared assets along with their
interdependencies. These repositories are crucial for linking features to
assets and for the consistent transformation of assets from the product line
to the member product scope. Section 18.3 focuses on the centrality of the
Systems Modeling Language (SysML) (Object Management Group [OMG]
2024a,b) models during the entire product line development and
transformation of the shared assets including their digital continuity. Central
is also how such continuity supports the evaluation of changes within the
product line and its subsequent evolution (Section 18.4). Section 18.5
enriches the chapter with an illustrative example that demonstrates the
management of new feature introductions, providing a simple yet effective
visualization of the workflow.
Finally, as reiterated in this book, adopting MBPLE is not an “overnight
activity.” Hence, Section 18.6 shows how in the real world, digital
continuity may not be fully and immediately established, allowing for
temporary manual data exchange methods until full digital continuity is
implemented.
18.2 The Digital Repositories of a Product
Line
As the shared assets are stored across various repositories connected to the
tools (or included with the tools) used for defining or modeling these assets,
interfaces between these digital repositories, based on interoperability
standards, must be developed to ensure complete traceability between
assets, as illustrated in Figure 18.1. This figure provides a simplified
illustration of the connections between different asset repositories,
including the feature model repository.
Figure 18.1 Overview of the feature model and shared assets links
across digital repositories.
MBPLE initially necessitates the definition of the feature model that
captures and formalizes the single source of variability, serving as a
reference for its application across multiple assets in scope. As explained in
the previous chapters, the feature model consists of the formalization of the
product line’s variability. A feature, as part of the feature model, may be
associated with in-scope assets within various repositories, exemplified by
“Feature B” in the figure.
The feature model includes all possible variation points and variants within
the product line, across every level of system decomposition (system,
subsystem, components, etc.), and within all assets in scope (Chalé
Góngora et al. 2014). A fundamental component of this approach is the
establishment of thorough and consistent feature-asset dependencies across
multiple assets, while adhering to the constraints among variants and
preserving the relationships between objects as specified by the information
model utilized. The variability depicted in the feature model might affect a
specific domain or not, which determines whether a feature-asset
dependency exists. For example, a feature might be associated with a
variable requirement or set of requirements, a part, or action of the SysML
v2 model, lines of source code, and test cases up to the three-dimensional
(3D) objects, as demonstrated in Figure 18.1. However, this figure is merely
illustrative; a feature usually does not encompass all these asset
dependencies, but rather, it is more accurate to state that a feature model
might include features with various asset dependencies. Without digital
repositories and their interlinking, consistently defining and managing
variability across the shared assets becomes time-consuming and
unsustainable for the product line development team. This underscores the
crucial role of a configuration management solution that operates in sync
with the product line engineering (PLE) tool. Indeed, while the PLE tool
handles the feature models and their configurations, a configuration
management solution is tasked with controlling, versioning, and
configuring the shared assets for the product line.
18.3 Transforming Shared Assets
Consistently Through the MBSE Model
Due to the extensive range of assets covered by the MBPLE scope, the
maintenance of the assets consistency becomes a central task. This includes
not only managing variability but also ensuring there are no conflicting or
duplicated engineering data. Within MBPLE, the model-based systems
engineering (MBSE) model is central, providing a consistent approach for
transforming assets from the so-called 150% models (product lines) to the
100% models (product variants). Utilizing an MBSE approach to capture
the comprehensive architecture of a product line offers the advantage of
documenting architecture information and design decisions within a unified
model repository. Within this model, different views are established to
deliver relevant information to each stakeholder. Consequently, the model
holds the superset architectural elements, while the varied views represent
the distinct architectural alternatives for each product variant.
Employing SysML to record the comprehensive architecture of a product
line facilitates the capture of architecture information and design decisions
within a central model repository. As previously discussed, all requirements
are stored in a designated repository, which remains synchronized with the
SysML architectural models’ repository. The logical and technical system
architectural models can also serve as a basis for defining a physical
architecture in CAD or for generating a consistent bill of materials. This
also involves linking their dedicated repository to the SysML repository.
It is important to identify which assets should include a variation and which
should not, considering asset-feature dependencies. Sometimes, it’s
preferable not to directly implement variations in the assets to avoid
redundant transformation and effort, which often leads to inconsistencies.
For instance, if the MBSE approach delineates multiple architecture layers
such as operational, functional, logical, etc., it is advisable to avoid
applying variations at the operational layer. Consequently, post-model
transformation, the operational analysis continues to reflect the 150% view,
including all common and variable assets. Despite this drawback, the
superior quality achieved by avoiding inconsistencies in variation
definitions across layers compensates for it (Madeira et al. 2023). Likewise,
as illustrated in Figure 18.2, if requirements are connected to model assets
and both have feature-asset dependencies, a decision must be made on
whether to maintain both links or if an automated mechanism could
indirectly cascade the variability and trigger the transformation. If not, it
must be verified that the linking scheme remains consistent prior to the
transformation.
Figure 18.2 Example of the two types of links, the satisfy link and the
feature-asset across assets.
18.4 Evolving the Product Line
As previously discussed, within the lexicon of PLE, the notion of a 150%
product family frequently arises, denoting a product line scope that is
typically broader than the scope required for a singular product, which is
designated as 100%. Product lines, like individual products, evolve over
time. Establishing digital continuity across all scoped assets significantly
simplifies the product line development team’s tasks. Consider the
simplified drone example presented in Figure 18.3. Initially, a product line
named “Drone Product Line v1” was developed, from which three basic
drone member products were derived, accommodating the variability
demanded by diverse customers. Each member product, while sharing
substantial commonalities, is distinct from the others. Following market
demands for new surveillance capabilities in the drone, the development
team expanded the original product line into “Drone Product Line v2” by
adding new features related to surveillance, thereby broadening the product
line’s scope. Consequently, both Basic and Surveillance drones could be
derived from v2. Alternatively, the team might have opted to maintain
separate product lines, though not in this case. This approach could be
pertinent when a product line becomes too extensive and costly to sustain or
when commonalities are minimal. Typically, during the analysis of gaps
between the new features or requirements to be developed and the existing
product line (also known as the gap analysis phase), the product line
development team determines the path to follow. As illustrated in Figure
18.3, this pattern is reiterated with the inclusion of drone payload
capabilities, aligning all member products under a unified product line and
shared assets. In real-world product line development, member products
might include customizations absent in the product line. While not always
advisable, this practice is sometimes unavoidable. This highlights that a
product line possesses a lifecycle independent of its member products,
although, throughout their existence, multiple synchronization points may
arise where adjustments made at the product line level (as shown in Figure
18.3) or at the member product level are shared and potentially
implemented. Thus, the product line may be enriched by experiences
coming from various member products (Chalé Góngora et al. 2014). Digital
repositories facilitating end-to-end continuity of shared assets and product
models are crucial for conducting gap analyses when new features are
requested, thereby supporting the evolution of the product line while
continually tracking the derivation of member products across different
versions. Chapter 19 covers the topic within “configuration management.”
Figure 18.3 Simplified evolution of a drone product line along the
development time and its related member products.
18.5 The Journey of a New Product Line
Feature
At this point, the benefit of establishing digital continuity for assets within
the MBPLE approach should be clear. The following example focuses on
the impact analysis when new features are integrated into the existing
digital continuity of assets.
Drawing from the earlier drone example, Figure 18.4 illustrates the typical
traceability between a feature and its associated assets that include
variations. As previously discussed, assets such as requirements, SysML
models, and CAD models are often interconnected – a practice highly
recommended for traceability not only in product lines but also in the
development of standalone products.
Figure 18.4 Example of digital continuity between a feature and
other shared assets.
For instance, integrating a “payload feature” in a new generation of drones,
as shown in Figure 18.5, can introduce substantial variability, affecting
everything from the drone’s structure to new software functionalities and
interactions with external systems essential to the overall “delivery”
mission. Introducing a new feature typically necessitates the creation of
new requirements, alterations to SysML models, and the development of
new MCAD and ECAD models. The capacity for such comprehensive
impact analysis and the precise identification of assets affected by the
introduction of new features with variations are made possible by
implementing a system across tools and repositories that ensures such
continuity. Interoperability, based on standards such as OSLC (OSLC 2021)
and MoSSEC (ISO 2021) is an important piece of the “puzzle.”
Figure 18.5 Example of impact of new feature to the existing digital
continuity already in place.
18.6 Manual Workarounds? Only If
Temporary
In Chapter 23, we cover in detail the adoption of MBPLE. Here, we provide
a hint: MBPLE adoption should be incremental within an organization. In
other words, it is not necessary to wait for all asset repositories to be
perfectly linked and synchronized, or for the comprehensive tool chain to
be fully implemented. Although crafting a clear roadmap for MBPLE
adoption is crucial, it is equally important to apply the MBPLE approach to
those assets that are more mature and already have established connections
between repositories. Nevertheless, manual workarounds to bridge the gaps
in repository connections are acceptable as temporary solutions.
For instance, suppose a feature model has dependency links with a SysML
model and a 3D modeling tool, but the connection between the SysML
model repository and the MCAD repository has not yet been established.
How can we ensure that the MCAD model remains consistent with the
SysML architecture model? The dependencies between feature assets alone
do not guarantee such consistency. In this scenario, Figure 18.6 illustrates
how a manual workaround using XML or similar exchange formats can
ensure the consistency of variations implemented for the SysML model and
the MCAD. In the example, the SysML structure, including the variation, is
exported and then imported into a 3D modeling tool. This method also
enhances the quality of the assets transformation from 150 to 100%
instances.
Figure 18.6 Example of data exchange between the SysML v2 model
repository and another domain-specific repository.
To conclude, digital continuity is a critical enabler of a successful
implementation of MBPLE. While it is already a key enabler of success in
single-product development, it becomes essential when the variability
dimension is added, leading to links across variable assets that are used in
different member products. Establishing and controlling those links across
different asset versions, by keeping consistency and implementing the
single source of variability represented by the feature model, without digital
continuity can lead to the worst “nightmares” for the systems engineers
responsible for the product line and its derived member products!
References
Chalé Góngora, H.G., Ferrogalini, M. and Moreau, C. (2014). How to boost
PLE by using MBSE – a case study of a rolling stock product line. In:
Proceedings of the 5th International Conference on Complex Systems
Design and Management CSDM, Paris, France. Paris: Springer.
ISO (2021). Industrial automation systems and integration – product data
representation and exchange – application protocol: For modelling and
simulation information in a collaborative systems engineering context
(MoSSEC). (ISO 10303-243:2021).
Madeira, R.H., De Sousa Pinto, D.H., and Forlingieri, M. (2023).
Variability on system architecture using airbus MBPLE for MOFLT
framework. INCOSE International Symposium, 33, pp: 601–615.

Language Version 1.7, formal/24-01-07’. OMG.

Chapter 19
Configuration and Model Management for
MBPLE
19.1 Introduction
The feature models and shared asset supersets contain many elements, often
numbering in the thousands. Like any single product, a product line and its
member products are governed by configuration management (CM) rules.
This chapter first introduces general CM terms and principles in Section
19.2, laying the groundwork for building CM for product lines described in
Section 19.3. Sometimes, people confuse CM with product line engineering
(PLE). While they share similarities in selecting items to create a valid set,
a valid set of configuration items (CI) and a valid set of features are distinct.
Section 19.4 covers CM in model-based product line engineering
(MBPLE), highlighting key CM aspects for feature models, feature
configurations, shared assets, and product models for the product line and
the resulting member products. While Section 19.5 briefly recalls the
importance of digital continuity and traceability in CM, Section 19.6
provides additional information on the CM consideration of models. This
leads to Section 19.7, which emphasizes the importance of model
organization and management, aiming to organize models and their
elements for effective use by both humans and machines.
The chapter concludes with Section 19.8, which offers an outlook on the
future CM standard, ISO/IEC 26581, and its significance for MBPLE.
19.2 General CM
A project produces many engineering artifacts, each with its own life cycle
from creation to incremental development, freezes, and deletion. CM aims
to ensure the integrity of all artifacts (ISO/IEC/IEEE 2023) by controlling
their changes.
An important CM task is identifying the artifacts and their versions that
compose the whole. The “immutable” temporal set is called a baseline,
which is agreed upon as an officially reviewed version approved by the
team. From a baseline, one or multiple branches can be created. A branch is
an intermediate “piece of work” between two baselines. The versioned
artifacts are CI, the objects under version and control.
Figure 19.1 depicts a simple example. The three technical assets, shown as
a set of requirements, Systems Modeling Language (SysML) models, and
CADs (A1, A2, and A3), have different versions developed over time. The
assets (dotted lines) here are the CI. Baseline B1 is a set of selected
versioned items, including A1 in version v1, A2 in v2, and A3 in v1.
Baseline B2 is another set of versioned items. Branch 1.1 is the temporary
version of the assets created from B1, which, after version control, review,
and approval, becomes B2.
Figure 19.1 CM basic concepts.
The fundamental concepts of CM are simple. However, establishing
effective CM in an enterprise is both an organizational and technical
challenge.
CM affects all engineering artifacts, requiring all stakeholders to agree on a
CM process, including the technical implementation and organizational
procedures such as who can decide about baselines, freezes, changes, etc.
Integrating all areas and artifacts means that different tools must work
together to control the versions. In addition to integrating diverse areas, the
number of artifacts to be versioned can also pose a scaling problem.
19.3 CM of Product Lines
CM comes into play when considering the MBPLE discipline in a life
cycle, as all the assets of a product line need to be managed in terms of
product line evolution, change control, and maintenance.
CM is not a substitute or an alternative to PLE; it is a key enabler for
managing the evolution of the product line and its assets over time. A
product line evolves like a single product does, meaning different baselines
and branches are supported by CM, as with a single product. However,
there are some important differences to highlight.
19.3.1 Variation in Time versus Variation in Space
A product line adds to the typical variation in time, which is controlled by
versioning the CI produced over time and their differences, the variability
of the CI, or variation in space. While variation in time is temporary and
does not translate into the needs of a member product, variation in space
expresses the needs of the different member products derived from the
product line. To summarize, CM focuses on variation in time, while PLE
focuses on variation in space, which we call variability.
19.3.2 Product Line versus Member Product Life Cycle
While CM applied to a single product only covers its life cycle, dealing
with PLE involves managing the CM of the product lines and their member
products. In theory, and ideally (or more easily in software development),
all modifications are performed on shared asset supersets, the feature
model, and/or the feature configurations. This means that product models
should be “read-only” after being derived with a PLE approach.
If avoiding changes and customizations in the product models is possible, it
is desirable but often unrealistic due to specific customer requirements, as
explained in Chapter 24. Hence, CM is usually applied to both the product
line and member product life cycles.
In the next paragraph, we will look at some simple scenarios where CM
works in synergy with MBPLE.
19.3.3 CM in the Development of a Product Line
The CM of a product line during its development stage does not differ from
the CM of a single product’s assets. As long as product models are not
derived from the shared assets supersets, Figure 19.1 is still valid. The
difference lies in the introduction of additional assets like feature models
and feature configurations as CI. The shared assets also represent the
variability of the product line, which is why more elements are managed
under CM than in single product development.
However, it is a common practice for the engineers working on the product
line to start deriving product models from the shared assets based on feature
configurations superset for several reasons. For instance, they might need to
perform simulations or share the product model with other stakeholders.
This can be done through the preview of the product model, supported by
the main PLE tools, without generating a new product model artifact (e.g. to
compare the results of different configurations). In some cases, however,
generating a new product model artifact is necessary (e.g. for the simulation
of the product model).
Figure 19.2 represents the second case, where the product models A3
(CADs) are derived from B1 to perform simulations on the product models.
In this case, we introduce the second dimension, variation in space. At this
temporary stage, it is highly recommended not to change the product
models but to make changes on the shared assets supersets and re-derive the
product models.
Figure 19.2 CM extended concepts for the product line.
It is a good practice to label product models with the ID of the shared assets
baseline they belong to. Since these product models can be kept in the CM
system as “read-only” while other baselines of the same artifact are
produced, it is important to track them back to the “source baseline” (B1).
In Figure 19.2, the product model A3 stands for the type of CI (CAD), PM1
for the product model variant, and B1 for the shared assets superset
baseline.
19.3.4 CM in the Derivation of a Member Product
In this second case, we analyze when CM is applied to the life cycle of a
member product derived from a product line. Once the product line and its
shared assets superset have reached a good maturity for product models to
be generated (e.g. not just for simulation purposes but to start the
development of the member products), CM is applied to the product
models, as illustrated in Figure 19.3 with B2. Similarly to Figure 19.2, the
generated product model for the member product is traceable to its shared
assets supersets baseline (B2 labels). This is like the nationality in our
passport, which shows where we come from.
Figure 19.3 CM extended concepts for the member product.
The key difference is that, in a nonideal world, changes are performed on
the product models, and different baselines are created. Limiting the
changes to only those parts mandated by specific customer needs and
tracking these changes along the life cycle of the product model are key
activities. We strongly recommend evaluating whether features that result
from specific customer requirements can be elevated to the product line
level and added to it (see Section 19.4) instead of treating them by default
as customizations, hence only applicable to the member product.
Once the member product is developed, maintenance is done for the
member product, and any resulting defects must be fixed in both the
member product and the product line, or only in the member product if they
are just related to the customized part of the product model. This is the price
to pay when changes are also performed on the product model.
19.3.5 CM in the Maintenance and Evolution of a Product
Line
New features can be added to a product line to serve new markets or
customer needs. Defects can also be fixed on product lines when identified
in the derived member products. Both scenarios require changes to be
performed on the shared assets supersets, feature models, and feature
configurations.
In the first case, the product line changes will only serve new member
products and will not impact the existing derived member products from the
current product line baseline. The work starts from the latest product line
baseline and follows the same approach described in Sections 19.3.1 and
19.3.2. In the second case, the fixes are the following:
Option 1: Performed only on the shared assets supersets if no
customization was required for the derived product models (read-only)
and updated on all derived member products for the CI in scope.
Option 2: Performed on both shared assets supersets and product
models if customization was originally required for the product
models. As mentioned before, this option leads to double the effort
needed to fix the defects.
19.4 CM for MBPLE
This section covers CM in MBPLE, highlighting key CM’s aspects for
feature models, feature configurations, shared assets, and product models
for the product line and the resulting member products.
19.4.1 Feature Models as CI
Differently than for a single product, product lines developed with MBPLE
include feature models as CI. This is done according to the CM approaches
described in Section 19.3. Each time a feature is added, modified, or
deleted, a new version of the whole model could then be generated. An
update might consist of adding a feature, deleting a feature because it
became obsolete, or changing an existing feature (Lameh et al. 2023). For
instance, instead of changing the whole “feature model 1,” it can be
versioned, according to the CM rules, to a new version, “1.1,” which can
eventually become a new baseline.
19.4.2 Feature Configurations as CI
Feature configurations are also considered CI. New versions of the same
feature configuration can be generated similarly to the feature models. This
can happen when the feature models are updated or when the previous
baseline of the feature configuration is a “partial configuration.” Partial
configurations help derive the product models from the shared assets
supersets in multiple steps. Instead of generating product models based on a
feature selection in one go, you can also add in-between steps. This usually
happens when not all the information on the feature selection is available at
a specific time and life cycle phase.
19.4.3 Shared Assets as CI
Shared assets as CI are the main example used in Section 19.3 and have
already been described in detail. However, it is important to highlight the
challenge posed by the dependency of artifacts on many projects (member
products) compared to a single product development project.
If a core shared asset of the product line changes, this impacts all member
product assets (product models). This is technically challenging, as many
artifacts stored in different repositories must be made dependent on each
other. It is particularly an organizational challenge, as many teams and
managers with sometimes conflicting goals are involved. While the product
line manager primarily focuses on the product line as a whole, a manager of
an individual project prioritizes his/her own product together with its
associated deadlines and requirements. For example, a positive change in
the product line can benefit five member products but negatively affect two
member products of the same portfolio.
19.4.4 Product Models as CI
Product models as CI are also covered in Section 19.3. However, it is key to
complement the topic with some considerations.
First, it is a good practice to keep the product model as “read-only.” When
this is not possible, it is recommended to develop the product model in a
modular way and isolate those parts of the model that will undergo
customization after the product model is derived.
Traceability must be maintained between the product model and the shared
assets to track changes in the product models. In some cases, it is possible
to bring back and implement the changes that were made only in the
member product at a later time in the most suitable baseline of the product
line.
19.5 Maintaining the Digital Continuity
As shown in Figure 19.1, engineers deal with more than one model. In the
context of MBPLE, there is at least a feature model and some engineering
models as shared asset supersets. These models are linked with each other.
For example, a shared assets superset model needs information about
variability from a feature model. This traceability is added to the typical
links that exist between assets in a single product development (e.g.
requirement <<verified by>> test case) as described in Chapter 6.
Another traceability link is between the shared assets superset and the
derived product models. This can be done simply by labeling the product
model with the corresponding baseline ID, as shown in Section 19.3.
However, a better practice involves establishing dependencies between the
shared assets supersets and the product models once these are derived (e.g.
through automatic generation of the dependency).
One challenge is the specific technical connection of the models. Various
approaches exist, including standards such as REST or Open Services for
Lifecycle Collaboration and tool-proprietary solutions (‘Model
Interoperability’ 2023).
The overall architecture of the “Model of Models” should be explicitly
designed and controlled. This is the only way to master the dependencies
and enable a digital thread. Chapter 18 covers the topic of “digital end-to-
end continuity with MBPLE” in detail.
19.6 CM for Models
The paper “Model lifecycle management for MBSE” defines model
lifecycle management (MLM) as “a governance process synchronizing the
create, read, update, and delete (CRUD) operations on heterogeneous
models within the supporting modeling tools and model repositories
throughout the system development life cycle” (Fisher et al. 2014). In
addition to the CRUD operation within a model, MLM should also cover
the management of the model’s life cycle, which includes considering the
versions of the MBSE tools.
Three types of CI can be distinguished. Whether and how the conceptual
distinction is considered depends on the project requirements.
Generally, a model stores information. If the information piece is under
configuration control, it is called an information CI (ICI). A requirement or
a part specification could be an ICI. Independent of the concrete kind of
modeling, it is information that exists in different versions. Depending on
the modeling language, information is represented in a model by one or
more model elements. If you use SysML for the modeling of the
specification of parts, it could be represented by a part definition. The
conceptual ICI is implemented by one or more model CI (MCI) in the
model.
Both the conceptual and model levels have no physical manifestation,
which is located on the repository level. For example, a SysML model can
be stored in a tool-specific database. It could also be exported to an XMI
file, which is a pure text file containing the model in an XML
representation. In addition to the ICI and MCI, we also have a repository
CI. Figure 19.4 shows the three different kinds of CIs in the context of
models.
Figure 19.4 Configuration items of a model.
Assume you create a system architecture model with SysML for a product
line. The products must be in operation for at least 20 years, including
maintenance and further development. This also requires that the system
architecture model still be accessible in 20 years’ time.
The main focus of CM is on the conceptual level. You want to manage the
versions of a feature. The versions of the corresponding model elements go
hand in hand with this. This means all model elements representing the
information get the corresponding version. In practice, the model elements
are versioned, not the information elements, which only exist conceptually.
Typically, a repository contains several model elements and, therefore, has
its own versioning, which is less related to the model element’s versions.
It is important to note the connection, for example, to obtain the
information in the future that a model element in version 37 is stored in the
repository in version 12, and therefore, it is known which tools can be used
to load and read this repository.
Ensure that the information lifecycle requirements are met. For example,
the information in the model can be accessed until the end of its life. It
cannot be taken for granted that models from today can still be opened in,
say, ten years. The associated tool may no longer be available in the version
or be unable to be opened because it has to access a license server of the
tool vendor for this purpose, which is no longer operated, and so forth.
19.7 Model Organization and Management
The elements of a model can be organized by many criteria. For instance,
the model elements of the drone shared assets can be organized by engine
elements, flight-control elements, power management elements, and so
forth.
The organization units of models are, typically, folders. The semantics of a
folder is a container and a namespace for elements. It has a name and
constraints that member elements have distinguishable unique names.
Folders can have subfolders and create a hierarchical structure. Usually,
each level is one-dimensional, and only one criterion can be applied.
An illustrative example of a folder-based organization in the real world is a
cutlery drawer. The drawer is the root folder with many subcompartments,
typically organized by knife, fork, spoon, etc. However, this striking
example already shows a challenge in the organization: all spoons in one
compartment or soup and teaspoons separately? Is expensive silver cutlery
separated from everyday cutlery? Is it sorted first by material and then by
type, or vice versa?
We are sure many people are familiar with this problem and the similar
challenge of organizing files in folders on a hard drive. While there are
often several grouping criteria, folder-based organizations only allow one
per level. This topic is also critical for deriving product models from the
shared assets supersets. For instance, if the model is organized as a large
interdependent chunk each and every time, the product model derivation is
performed for the entire model. If the model is modularized, the product
model can be generated just for the module under scope.
In a SysML model, packages are the folders. The core concept can be found
in almost all modeling languages and tools. In addition to the one-
dimensional organization, there are also other possibilities. For example,
tags can assign several criteria per element on one level and enable a multi-
dimensional organization of the elements.
Organization of shared assets – a SysML example: This example
describes organizing shared assets in a systems engineering model with
SysML. Covering other shared asset models, such as simulation and CAD
models, would exceed the book’s scope.
In SysML, the model organization units are packages. The following
package structure proposal is based on system modeling method
([SYSMOD] Weilkiens 2020). The top-level structure criteria are
engineering-oriented: core elements, variant elements, and product models.
The next level is organized by domain criteria. This pattern can be repeated
on a detailed level. Figure 19.5 shows the package structure pattern with a
simple example. Note the recursive style of the package pattern: the whole
structure repeats for the package battery since the same recursive pattern of
the overarching system is applied.
Figure 19.5 Organization SysML shared assets.
19.8 ISO 26581: A New CM Standard for PLE
ISO/IEC 26580 (ISO/IEC 2021) focused primarily on the PLE factory
elements operating at a fixed point in time, emphasizing variation in space
but not in time. In the previous sections, we discussed how assets, evolving
over time, become CI. Hence, in a PLE approach to derive complete
product models, the versions of all assets must be perfectly aligned at a
specific point in time.
This CM gap in the discipline of PLE necessitates a new standard to
complement the existing ISO/IEC 26580 (ISO/IEC 2021). Therefore, the
INCOSE PLE Working Group (INCOSE PLE WG 2024), under the
responsibility of Dr. Charles Krueger, is leading the development of such a
standard. This new standard aims to facilitate the controlled evolution of
PLE assets over time and guarantee the reproducibility of any previously
configured and released member product.
The future standard will describe the tools and methods that create an
overarching CM solution for the product line and its members, aggregating
and aligning the constituent traditional CM systems.

INCOSE PLE WG (2024). Product Line Engineering Working Group
Webpage. https://www.incose.org/communities/working-groups-
initiatives/product-lines (accessed 24 July 2024).
ISO/IEC (2021). Software and systems engineering – methods and tools for
the feature-based approach to software and systems product line
engineering (ISO/IEC/IEEE 2658 0: 2021).
ISO/IEC/IEEE (2023). Systems and software engineering – system life

Chapter 20
MBPLE and Artificial Intelligence
20.1 Introduction
Artificial intelligence (AI) has significantly influenced various fields,
especially since the release of ChatGPT. This impact extends to systems
engineering and model-based product line engineering (MBPLE).
Artificial intelligence is a broad subject area, and when non-experts discuss
it nowadays, they usually refer to ChatGPT. Section 20.2 first provides an
orientation in the field and explains some key terms related to AI in general.
Section 20.3 shows the application of large language models (LLM), such
as ChatGPT. Section 20.4 presents two types of “AI assistants” for using
LLMs to support MBPLE activities. Section 20.5 combines these two into
one “super” MBPLE assistant and shows its capabilities in a concrete
application use case. Finally, Section 20.6 discusses opportunities and
threats that may arise from using AI in MBPLE.
20.2 AI Foundation
This section explains the numerous abbreviations related to artificial
intelligence: AI, machine learning (ML), deep learning (DL), GenAI, LLM,
and ChatGPT. The focus is on AI for systems engineering (AI4SE) and
LLMs. AI4SE means using AI to support systems engineering activities.
There is also systems engineering for artificial intelligence, which is about
using systems engineering approaches for developing AI systems.
The following subsections explain them briefly.
20.2.1 Artificial Intelligence
The broadest term in Figure 20.1 is AI. Understanding AI begins with
defining what “intelligence” means. Marvin Minsky describes intelligence
as a complex combination of simpler processes (Minsky 1988). Russell and
Norvig propose a two-dimensional definition of AI, considering first
whether AI aims to imitate human behavior or rationality and second
whether it focuses on external actions or internal thought processes (Russell
and Norvig 2020). The Turing Test, formulated by Alan Turing, evaluates
an AI’s ability to exhibit human-like behavior (Turing 1950).
Figure 20.1 AI terminology.
Source: Adapted from Rosenberg et al., 2024 / with permission of Model Based Systems
Engineering 4 You.
Russell and Norvig identify four essential capabilities for an AI: natural
language processing, knowledge representation, automated reasoning, and
machine learning.
20.2.2 Machine Learning and Deep Learning
Machine learning (ML) is a branch of AI that focuses on learning and
making decisions based on data. There are different methods to achieve
this; one of the most popular is the neural network approach.
Neural networks are a type of ML model designed to mimic the way the
human brain works. They consist of interconnected nodes, or “neurons,”
which process and transmit information. When these networks have many
layers of neurons, they are considered as DL models. Deep learning allows
for more complex data analysis and has been particularly successful in tasks
such as image and speech recognition.
20.2.3 Generative AI and LLMs
Generative AI (GenAI) is a subfield of DL. It creates new content, such as
text, images, and audio, by learning from datasets and generating data that
resembles the training data.
Large language models, such as ChatGPT, are a subset of GenAI focused on
text. GPT, or generative pre-trained transformer, is the basis of ChatGPT.
This advanced language model, developed by OpenAI1, is characterized by
natural language processing and text generation.
ChatGPT’s capabilities span multiple domains, including conversational
dynamics, creative and technical writing, educational applications, and code
generation. ChatGPT falls into the “acting humanly” AI category defined by
Russell and Norvig and focuses on human-like interactions and natural
language processing.
ChatGPT is certainly the most famous LLM, but there are also others, such
as Google Gemini2, xAI Grok3, Meta Llama 34, or Mistral5.
20.3 Communication with an LLM
Large language models turn natural languages into an interface between
man and machine. Chatting with an LLM such as ChatGPT is based on
prompts, i.e. a statement that prompts the LLM for a response. Processing a
prompt in an LLM involves tokenizing the text, converting tokens into
numerical values, and generating responses based on these values.
OpenAI has published a guide with six strategies for writing effective
prompts (OpenAI 2024):
1. Write clear instructions: Specify detailed information and provide
examples.
2. Provide reference text: Include references or ask the AI to base its
response on specific texts.
3. Split complex tasks: Break down complex tasks into simpler steps.
4. Give the model time to “think”: Encourage the AI to consider tasks
deeply.
5. Use external tools: Create outputs for external tools that can be fed
back into the AI.
6. Use a test suite: Monitor AI responses over time to ensure consistency
and accuracy.
In most cases, an LLM can also be addressed via an API. In this case, more
parameters, such as the so-called temperature, can typically be set, which
controls how “creative” the responses are.
One aspect of LLM behavior is the phenomenon of “hallucinations,” where
an LLM generates incorrect or fabricated information. This differs from
human lying, which involves conscious intent, whereas AI hallucinations
arise from improperly learned data. However, both phenomena can overlap;
for instance, false knowledge can underpin human lies, and an LLM might
deliberately produce false information under certain conditions. That is why
it is recommended that organizations adopt governance mechanisms to
control and mitigate such risks.
20.4 AI4MBPLE
The application of AI, or more precisely, LLMs, supporting MBPLE
activities can be further categorized as using AI as a domain expert assistant
or as an MBPLE methodology assistant. A similar approach is described for
MBSE in the book “AI Assisted MBSE with SysML” (Rosenberg et al.
2024).
The LLM does not care much about the distinction. Separating concerns
helps focus and write concise prompts.
20.4.1 AI as a Domain Expert Assistant
Using AI as a domain expert assistant means using the broad and deep
knowledge of ChatGPT and similar LLMs to identify additional
engineering artifacts, such as more potential features for a feature model.
This book uses a simple example of a drone product line. The following
prompt provides the existing feature model and asks for additional features
using the LLM’s knowledge. The prompt contains the following important
properties, among others: providing a context, showing an example, and
giving clear instructions.
Prompt:
We are working on a feature model for a drone product line according to
ISO/IEC 26580.
The features’ hierarchical structure is depicted as a list in textual notation,
as in the following example. Constraints about valid feature selections are
given in square brackets.
Feature1 [Constraints: Select exactly one subfeature]
Subfeature1.1
Subfeature1.2
Subfeature 1.2.1
Below is the existing feature model. Propose additional features, including
subfeatures, constraints, and changes to the existing feature model. Use the
same textual notation for the enhanced feature model.
DroneFeature
Number of Engines
Four Engines
Six Engines
Battery Kinds
Standard Battery
Long Distance Battery
Response from ChatGPT:
Here is the enhanced feature model for the drone product line, including
additional features, subfeatures, constraints, and changes to the existing
feature model. I have added some common features and constraints that
might be relevant for a drone product line:
DroneFeature [Constraints: Select at least one subfeature from
Number of Engines, Battery Kinds, Camera Types, and GPS
Capabilities]
Number of Engines [Constraints: Select exactly one subfeature]
Four Engines
Six Engines
Battery Kinds [Constraints: Select exactly one subfeature]
Standard Battery
Long Distance Battery
Camera Types [Constraints: Select exactly one subfeature]
Standard Camera
High-Resolution Camera
Thermal Camera
GPS Capabilities [Constraints: Select at least one subfeature]
Basic GPS
Advanced GPS with Real-Time Tracking
Flight Modes [Constraints: Select at least one subfeature]
Manual Mode
Autonomous Mode
Follow Me Mode
Sensors [Constraints: Select at least one subfeature]
Collision Avoidance Sensors
Altitude Hold Sensors
Obstacle Detection Sensors
Connectivity [Constraints: Select at least one subfeature]
Wi-Fi Connectivity
Cellular Connectivity
Satellite Connectivity
Payload Capacity [Constraints: Select exactly one subfeature]
Light Payload
Medium Payload
Heavy Payload
Control Options [Constraints: Select at least one subfeature]
Remote Control
Mobile App Control
Voice Control
Durability Features [Constraints: Select at least one subfeature]
Water Resistant
Dust Proof
Shock Resistant
We sent the prompt to ChatGPT 4o without any additional training (also
called zero-shot). Currently, a challenge in a real-world project is the limit
of the prompt’s size, which could be a problem for a large feature model.
This is just a simplified example, but it shows how you can use domain
knowledge to identify gaps, generate more ideas, or generate content
quickly. It emphasizes the potential of training the LLM as a domain expert
within an organization (e.g. learning legacy specifications, competitive
solutions, best practices, etc.). We all have experienced the challenge of
chasing the right domain expert, who is always overloaded by support
requests, projects, or on leave.
20.4.2 AI as a MBPLE Methodology Assistant
ChatGPT has also learned methodological knowledge. As there are different
approaches, it is important to clearly describe the context in the prompt and
provide an example.
The following demonstrates the links of a simple system model with the
previous feature model, turning the system model into a shared assets
superset. The prompt has been sent in the same thread as the previous one
for the feature model, which means that the feature model is already known
and does not need to be specified.
The prompt contains an example of modeling variations in Systems
Modeling Language (SysML) v2 and linking them to features. Finally, the
prompt provides a very simple system model and asks for changes and
additions based on the link to the feature model.
Prompt:
Consider the feature model and define the corresponding variations with a
link to the respective feature, similar to the following example for a vehicle:
part def Vehicle{
variation wheel : Wheel {
doc /* Link to Feature */
variant wheelStandard : StandardWheel;
variant wheelX : XWheel;
}
}
part def Wheel;
part def StandardWheel;
part def XWheel;
Change the parts of the given SysML v2 model to variations if the suitable
features exist.
part def Drone {
part battery : Battery;
part engine : Engine;
part frame : Frame;
}
part def Battery;
part def Engine;
part def Frame;
The answer is only given here in a shortened form, as the whole answer is
not important to demonstrate the principle.
Response from ChatGPT:
Here is the revised SysML v2 model incorporating variations linked to the
respective features in the enhanced feature model:
part def Drone {
variation battery: Battery {
doc /* Link to Battery Kinds Feature */
variant standardBattery: StandardBattery;
variant longDistanceBattery: LongDistanceBattery;
}
variation engine: Engine {
doc /* Link to Number of Engines Feature */
variant fourEngines: FourEngines;
variant sixEngines: SixEngines;
}
variation camera: Camera {
doc /* Link to Camera Types Feature */
variant standardCamera: StandardCamera;
variant highResolutionCamera: HighResolutionCamera;
variant thermalCamera: ThermalCamera;
}
variation flightMode: FlightMode {
doc /* Link to Flight Modes Feature */
variant manualMode: ManualMode;
variant autonomousMode: AutonomousMode;
variant followMeMode: FollowMeMode;
}
}

20.5 The “Super” Assistant: Combining
Domain Expert and MBPLE Methodology
Have you ever experienced the excitement of discovering that your team
includes a systems engineer or MBSE expert who is also a domain expert in
your industry? It is a rare and valuable discovery. Now, imagine if your best
domain expert and the most experienced MBPLE expert could train an
LLM to become the ultimate team member you’ve always dreamed of. In
the next sections, we explore the feasibility of such a scenario.
20.5.1 The Creation of a Feature Model for a New Product
Line based on Existing Product Specifications
The use case we present here consists of two activities that can be supported
by a third MBPLE expert, which we call the “super” assistant because it
combines both the system domain expertise and the knowledge of the
methodology. We divide the use case into three parts, answering the
following questions:
1. How to create an initial feature model from an existing specification of
a legacy product that contains some variability but was not originally
intended to serve a product line?
2. How to add new features, necessary to develop the product line?
3. How are the existing requirements impacted by the new features, and
how are the new (added or updated) requirements in the original
specification identified?
Large organizations often start a product line using a brownfield approach,
leveraging existing assets from large legacy systems. This strategy can
benefit from the MBPLE approach (Essoussi et al. 2024).
The example used is a simplified requirements specification of the drone in
PDF format that includes the following requirements:
Req. 1 - The system shall autonomously navigate the drone from the
shore to the designated ship location.
Req. 2 - The system shall ensure safe takeoff and landing of the drone
on the ship’s deck.
Req. 3 - The system shall allow for manual override and control of the
drone by an operator.
Req. 4 - The system shall provide real-time tracking of the drone’s
location during delivery.
Req. 5 - The system shall provide additional continuous monitoring of
the item to be delivered via camera if required by the customer.
Req. 6 - The system shall be capable of carrying payloads up to 10 kg.
Req. 7 - The system shall complete a delivery within a maximum time
of 30 minutes.
Req. 8 - The system shall operate within a range of up to 10 km from
the shore if equipped with 4 engines and within a range of up to 20 km
if equipped with 6 engines.
Req. 9 - The system shall maintain stable flight in winds up to 25
km hour.
Req. 10 - The system shall be able to operate in temperatures ranging
from −10 to 40 °C.
Req. 11 - The system shall ensure a battery life sufficient for at least
one round trip.
Req. 12 - The system shall include collision avoidance technology to
prevent accidents.
Req. 13 - The system shall be equipped with either Radio
communication systems or with 5G to ensure constant contact with the
operator.
Req. 14 - The system shall secure the payload to prevent loss during
flight.
Req. 15 - The system shall comply with all relevant aviation and
maritime safety regulations.
In the next section, we present a step-by-step approach that includes a short
LLM training with prompt engineering (behind the scenes) necessary for
the LLM to execute our use case.
20.5.2 A Step-by-Step Approach
The approach can be summarized in the following steps:
1. Training the LLM with free-text prompts: It consists of teaching the
LLM what a feature model is and how features can be identified and
extracted from a set of requirements. It is usually done through trial
and error.
2. Importing the legacy specification: The requirements contained in a
PDF document specification are uploaded using a model based on
LLM user interface that has been built for the exclusive purpose of this
book.
3. Identifying and exporting the feature model: Features are extracted
from the legacy requirements and organized into a consistent feature
model, which represents the system’s variability. This model is then
exported as a CSV file. The features are labeled with a tool-specific
notation to facilitate import, as a standard variability modeling
language does not yet exist.
4. Importing and visualizing the feature model in a product line
engineering (PLE) tool: The feature model is imported as a CSV file
in the PLE tool. This is because the PLE tool is not connected via API
with the LLM solution.
5. Adding new features and exporting the resulting feature model:
New features are added to the first feature model and exported as a
CSV file.
6. Importing and visualizing the new feature model in the PLE tool:
Same step as before (4), but this time the feature model includes the
new added features.
7. Understanding impact on existing requirements: It focuses on
capturing which requirements are impacted by the introduction of the
new features.
IBM watsonx (IBM watsonx 2024) is the commercial platform used in this
example. It includes several LLMs and prompt labs to facilitate the creation
of AI-driven tasks and services. The Llama 3 70-b instruct LLM is used
here6. Once the feature model is generated as a CSV file, PTC
pure::variants (PTC pure::variants 2024) is used as a PLE tool to import and
visualize the feature model. The solution presented in this book is created
solely to simulate the activities performed by the “super” domain and
MBPLE methodology expert assistant. Integrating the AI solution with the
PLE tool would streamline the entire process.
20.5.3 The Super Domain and MBPLE Expert in Action
Step 1 consists of training the model with free-text prompts, and it is good
practice to refine the prompts until the desired outcome is reached. The
following example shows the first prompt used.
Prompt (zero-shot):
<|begin_of_text|><|start_header_id|>system<|end_header_id|>
You are an MBPLE super assistant working on requirements
specifications. Do not respond in markdown.
Information is provided below, delimited by triple backticks.
...
Shore-to-ship Drone
The system shall provide additional continuous monitoring of the item
to be delivered via camera if required by the customer.
The system shall be capable of carrying payloads up to 5 kg.
The system shall operate within a range of up to 10 km from the shore
if equipped with 2 engines and within a range of up to 20 km if
equipped with 4 engines.
The system shall secure the payload to prevent loss during flight.
...
<|eot_id|>Create a hierarchy of features with a minimum of 3 levels
and a maximum of 5 levels.
The first level is always one feature and is the parent of the features of
the second level. The name of this feature is always the title of the
requirements list (example: Shore_to_shipDrone). The first feature at
level one can only have ps:mandatory (stands for mandatory relation)
or ps:optional (stands for optional) with its children features.
The second level should have around a minimum of 3 and a maximum
of 5 features, either ps:mandatory or ps:optional. The second level
features can have with their children features of level 3 the following
relations ps:mandatory (stands for mandatory relation) or ps:optional
(stands for optional) or ps:or (stands for at least 1 feature within the
group under the same parent must be selected, not mutually exclusive)
or ps:alternative (features under the same parent features are mutually
exclusive).
The third level and eventual fourth and fifth levels follow the same
rules of the second level.
Respond only in a valid CSV format, with 4 columns,
- name of the feature, column title “Unique Name” (all feature names
under this column cannot have spaces between words but only
underscore “_”)
- the parent name of the feature, column title “Parent Unique Name”
- the relation between parent and child feature, column title “variation
Type” (use the variation types starting with “ps:”
- Exact copy of column 1 “Unique Name” but with new title “Visible
Name”.
<|start_header_id|>assistant<|end_header_id|>
First result (invalid):
“Unique Name”,”Parent Unique Name”,”Variation Type”,”Visible
Name”
“Shore_to_shipDrone”,”“,”“,”Shore to ship Drone”
“Delivery_Configuration”,”Shore_to_shipDrone”,”ps:mandatory”,”
Delivery Configuration”
“Engine_Configuration”,”Delivery_Configuration”,”ps:mandatory”,
”Engine Configuration”
“Two_Engines”,”Engine_Configuration”,”ps:alternative”,”2
Engines”
“Four_Engines”,”Engine_Configuration”,”ps:alternative”,”4
Engines”
“Monitoring”,”Delivery_Configuration”,”ps:optional”,”Monitoring”
“Payload_Configuration”,”Delivery_Configuration”,”ps:mandatory”
,”Payload Configuration”
“Payload_Capacity”,”Payload_Configuration”,”ps:mandatory”,”Pa
yload Capacity”
“Payload_Security”,”Payload_Configuration”,”ps:mandatory”,”Pay
load Security”
The training results were unsatisfactory because features like
“Delivery_Configuration,” “Payload_Configuration,” “Payload_Capacity,”
and “Payload_Security” did not reflect any variability; they resembled a
functional breakdown structure instead. After some trial and error, the
second prompt was added to improve the results.
Prompt (after some shots):
(...) Very important: it is not allowed to have a ps:mandatory relation
between a parent and child, when the child feature has no further
children features at the level below.
The feature tree shown as CSV should only focus on those features that
have variation and not covering all the requirements above.
For example: If the requirements have a form like “If required by “
create a ps:optional feature.
For example: “If equipped with” and “If equipped with” = Create two
ps:alternative features for the requirement that have these two clauses.
Example of an invalid and not acceptable output generated below:
“Unique Name”,”Parent Unique Name”,”Variation Type”,”Visible
Name”
“Shore_to_shipDrone”,”“,”“,”Shore to ship Drone”
“Delivery_Configuration”,”Shore_to_shipDrone”,”ps:mandatory”,”
Delivery Configuration”
“Engine_Configuration”,”Delivery_Configuration”,”ps:mandatory”,
”Engine Configuration”
“Two_Engines”,”Engine_Configuration”,”ps:alternative”,”2
Engines”
“Four_Engines”,”Engine_Configuration”,”ps:alternative”,”4
Engines”
“Monitoring”,”Delivery_Configuration”,”ps:optional”,”Monitoring”
“Payload_Configuration”,”Delivery_Configuration”,”ps:mandatory”
,”Payload Configuration”
“Payload_Capacity”,”Payload_Configuration”,”ps:mandatory”,”Pa
yload Capacity”
“Payload_Security”,”Payload_Configuration”,”ps:mandatory”,”Pay
load Security”
The output above is invalid because it does not focus on the features
that have variation and it does not respect the following rule: “a
ps:mandatory relation between a parent and child, when the child
feature has no further children features at the level below is not
allowed.
For example, the following pattern is wrong according to the rule
above:
“Payload_Configuration”,”Delivery_Configuration”,”ps:mandatory”
,”Payload Configuration”
“Payload_Capacity”,”Payload_Configuration”,”ps:mandatory”,”Pa
yload Capacity”
“Payload_Security”,”Payload_Configuration”,”ps:mandatory”,”Pay

In the new prompt, rules are better specified, and the previous
unsatisfactory output of the feature model is added as an invalid example.
Use Case Part 1: How to create an initial feature model from an existing
specification of a legacy product that contains some variability but was not
originally intended to serve a product line?
After refining the prompt, in Step 2, the PDF document containing the
requirements from Section 20.5.1 is imported (Figure 20.2). Based on the
content of the uploaded specification, the feature model is then generated in
CSV format.
Figure 20.2 Import of the legacy specification pdf in the IBM
watsonx application.
Figure 20.3 shows a correct formulation of the feature model based on the
rules provided and document specification of the drone. At this stage, the
feature model is ready to be exported as a CSV file (Step 3).
Figure 20.3 Feature model output in the IBM watsonx application.
The CSV file is then imported in pure::variants and visualized in the tool-
specific feature modeling notation as illustrated in Figure 20.4 and
described in Step 4.
Figure 20.4 Feature model imported in PTC pure::variants.
Use Case Part 2: How to add new features, necessary to develop the
product line?
To complete Step 5, an additional prompt must first be given to the LLM.
This prompt should specify the need to add new features to the existing
feature model and revise the content as shown below.
Prompt:
(...) The user would like to add new features into the existing CSV
output. Based on the user input, add the feature into the CSV.
<|eot_id|>
1) Provide alternative capability to support 4 round trips based on
existing capability already available in the requirements;
2) Provide alternative capability to support a payload of 15 kg.
Provide as additional output also the requirements that are impacted
by these changes.
<|start_header_id|>assistant<|end_header_id|>
The result, in this case, is satisfactory on the first attempt, as illustrated in
Figure 20.5.
Figure 20.5 Revised feature model output in the IBM watsonx
application.
The revised feature model is imported again in PTC pure::variants, and the
previous feature model is updated (Step 6), as shown in Figure 20.6.
Figure 20.6 Feature model updated in PTC pure::variants.
Use Case Part 3: How to identify the existing requirements impacted by
the new features and new requirements to be added or changed in the
original specification?
To perform Step 7, it is also necessary to add an additional prompt that asks
the LLM to provide information about the requirements impacted by the
introduction of new features. This is a key step to understand the impact of
changes on an existing asset and relate the requirement to the correspondent
feature. It helps the identification of variation points in the requirements set.
Prompt:
<|eot_id|>
Provide as additional output the requirements that are impacted by the
change, that consists of the introduction of new features.
Highlight which new feature is related to which requirement and what
is the change. The focus shall be only on those requirements that are
impacted by the new features.
<|start_header_id|>assistant<|end_header_id|>.
The final result provides the requirements that are impacted by the change
of adding new features (Step 7), as depicted in Figure 20.7.
Figure 20.7 Requirements impacted by the new features in the IBM
watsonx application.
Nota bene: The full use case presented here can also be applied to the
“evolution of a product line” that has not been developed following a
feature-based PLE approach. In that case, the document specification or
requirements set is part of the shared assets of an existing product line,
where new features are added to it.
20.6 AI4MBPLE Opportunities and Threats:
Hasta la Vista, Baby!
The previous examples demonstrate how LLMs can be trained to become
the domain expert and methodology expert assistants of your team. These
examples are just a few of the many potential use cases that could be
developed in the MBPLE domain.
For instance, an AI can become “the Terminator” of the unused features,
eliminating unutilized feature configurations in your product line (to avoid
the cost of their maintenance), or it can define the best combinations of
features (feature configurations) to meet new customer requirements. It can
also help determine the right balance of variability and commonality within
your product line and add variation points automatically to your shared
assets superset.
Just like in the movie “Terminator,” where John Connor teaches the
intelligent humanoid to behave more human-like – famous is the line “No,
no, no, no. You gotta listen to the way people talk. You don’t say
‘affirmative’ or something like that. (...) And if you want to shine them on,
it’s ‘hasta la vista, baby’ ” – an LLM, when properly trained, can become
your secret weapon in product line development.
However, while AI can be a valuable companion in developing product
lines, it is important to be aware of potential threats. For example, relying
too much on AI might lead to a loss of understanding of the system
variability during feature model building, a typically creative process that
can spark new features. Another challenge is the risk of AI hallucinations,
where the model might provide false information if no governance
mechanisms are in place, which an inexperienced engineer might
mistakenly accept as valid.
Like any other technology, AI is not inherently good or bad; its impact
depends on how it is used. Do not let your MBPLE AI assistant turn into the
“bad” Terminator!
