Part IV
Adoption of MBPLE
The next-to-last part of this book proposes different aspects to be
considered for the adoption of Model-Based Product Line Engineering
(MBPLE) in organizations. These aspects include the purpose and business
value of MBPLE, an adoption framework and process, the setup and
improvement of methods and tools, an information model, requirements for
end-to-end tool chains, as well as general principles to set up an MBPLE
organization.
Chapter 21 opens Part IV by presenting the elements that can be used to
build a compelling business case for MBPLE based on its value
proposition: cost-effectiveness, lead time reduction, quality improvement,
and return on investment. The chapter demonstrates how MBPLE can
enhance business profitability by leveraging the reuse of digital assets to
improve product and service delivery.
Chapters 22 and 23 are dedicated to the adoption of MBPLE. The former
chapter presents the MBPLE adoption quadrant (MBPLEAQ) tool, which is
useful for identifying the MBPLE adoption route an organization can take
based on its current maturity. The latter chapter complements the
MBPLEAQ with an adoption framework that includes considerations about
processes, methods, information models, tool chains, and organization.
Chapter 23 further stresses the importance of upfront planning and clear
goal definitions when making use of the framework.
Chapter 24 highlights the necessity of tailoring the approach presented in
Chapter 12 to specific organizational contexts, customer requirements and
project needs. Each stage of the approach is mapped to the technical
processes of ISO/IEC 15288 (ISO/IEC 2023). To support the previous
approach, Chapter 25 explores the development and continuous
improvement of organization- and tool-specific methods, highlighting the
importance of adapting standardized approaches to meet the needs of an
organization.
Chapters 26 and 27 are respectively dedicated to the MBPLE Information
Model and to an examination of the MBPLE tool chain that should support
it. Using Systems Modeling Language (SysML) as the primary language,
Chapter 26 details the abstract syntax and semantics of MBPLE concepts.
The notion of information modeling is briefly introduced before the
information models of the feature model, shared assets superset, and
product portfolio concepts are defined. Chapter 27 presents what an
MBPLE tool chain should be in terms of its key components and
importance for the successful adoption of MBPLE in an organization.
Chapter 28 pulls together the concepts presented throughout Section IV and
proposes general principles to set up an MBPLE organization. As an
example of how these principles can be incarnated, the chapter proposes
two generic organizational patterns. The chapter concludes with an analysis
of the application of agile principles in the context of MBPLE, the setup of
an MBPLE adoption team, and a clarification on the concept of “product
lines-of-product lines”.
Part IV concludes with a cross-interview of Dr. Danilo Beuche and Dr.
Charles Krueger, two of the pioneers of the PLE world.
OceanofPDF.com
Chapter 21
A Business Case for MBPLE
21.1 Introduction
From what has been exposed throughout this book, the reader can probably
draw a logical conclusion about the purpose of model-based product line
engineering (MBPLE): it should first and foremost be regarded as a way to
improve the profitability of a business. This is achieved by maximizing the
benefits of applying smart reuse strategies on digital assets to enhance the
delivery of products and services. The path to fulfilling this goal requires
conferring to an organization a clear vision of customer needs in given
target markets and the capabilities to efficiently adapt its products to these
needs. This should naturally lead organizations to higher overall
performances and more successful and sustainable results in the long term.
While the previous arguments might be enticing and sound like sheer
common sense (in particular, and without a doubt, to product line
engineering (PLE) and MBPLE practitioners), they are usually insufficient
to convince decision-makers and drive the transformation of the delivery
approach of an organization. The objective of this chapter is to provide
elements that can help to build an appealing business case for MBPLE.
Whilst Section 21.2 presents the problem of clone-and-own approaches and
the opportunity to shift to MBPLE (by analyzing scalability and
maintenance as well as development cost and lead time), Section 21.3
presents a value proposition of MBPLE adoption that includes cost-
effectiveness, lead time improvement, quality improvement, and return on
investment. The reader must bear in mind, however, that the curves
presented in the rest of this chapter to support the value proposition have
been anonymized for confidentiality reasons.
21.2 The Problem and Opportunity Space
In today’s competitive and global marketplace, it is increasingly difficult to
find organizations and customers who are fully satisfied with the business
performance of traditional reuse approaches to deliver bespoke solutions. In
addition, fast-evolving, disruptive technologies can impose the need for
new system architectures and thus, according to Conway’s law, the need for
a new organization (a huge challenge for many companies). However, still
today, the most frequent reuse approach consists of delivering product
variants that are custom-fit to match the specific needs of individual
customers using “clone-and-own” procedures (see Chapter 3), which
impede more agile and faster delivery of products, in particular, large-scale
products.
In order to keep pace with competition and changing operational contexts,
customers in many sectors are indeed demanding frequent and modular
upgrades, lower development costs, and faster delivery time. Even in the
world of aerospace and defense, customers are now asking for faster,
recurrent modular upgrades, which provide the system capabilities that are
required on the field (INCOSE 2019). These new rules of the game, along
with the growing complexity of systems and business contexts (as
explained in Chapters 2 and 3), have pushed traditional clone-and-own
reuse methods to their limits.
21.2.1 Scalability and Maintenance
Probably the most visible problem of traditional clone-and-own reuse
methods is a well-known difficulty in the software development domain,
usually referred to as “branching hell.” Branching hell arises when an
organization decides to allow for multiple customer-specific developments
or fixes, which are dealt with in separate development “branches.” In a
nutshell, the problem is: “the more branching a product does, the more
overhead for maintenance, validation, and configuration management,” as
discussed in Chapter 19. This state of affairs can become a big issue in the
software world. When transposed to the development of large-scale
complex systems, it can quickly reach gigantic proportions.
Let us take the example of error correction or debugging, an activity where
branching can really become pure hell. First of all, let us assume (and this is
a big conjecture) that when the clone-and-own mechanism is operated to
create a new branch, engineers are able to clearly identify and keep track of
the version that “best suits” their problem among the existing branches of
the product. Since many large-scale systems will remain in operation for
20–40 years, the first issue concerns maintenance over the life cycle of
these systems (debugging notwithstanding). If you are running a successful
business, you may have N operational systems on the field (remember,
these are all independent in a clone-and-own reuse approach). This means
you will have to provide separate maintenance for the N individual systems
over a long period of time.
Now, imagine that at one point in time, a bug is found in one of the systems
in operation. The second issue that arises is that, to perform a proper job,
one would most probably need to have all the customer-specific versions
available (or have N digital twins of the N systems in operation) to
reproduce the error. This brings along a third issue: finding out where the
bug came from in the first place. In which branch was the bug introduced?
Did it exist in the version that was cloned to create the branch in which the
bug was found? This brings, in turn, the fourth and most costly issue:
correcting the error. In the best case, this correction is conducted in a point-
to-point scheme (i.e. between only two branches), meaning that the cost of
correcting errors in a clone-and-own approach is proportional to 2N (Figure
21.1). It might also happen that every branch conducts specific correction
actions on its own. For more details about this matter, see Chapter 18,
where we explore the topic of configuration management.
Figure 21.1 Correcting errors in a clone-and-own approach (without
MBPLE).
Finally, at some other point in time, the organization may decide that the
best option would be to redevelop the product “from scratch” because the
current branches have become utterly unmaintainable. This again implies
huge new investments for the organization and higher costs for its
customers.
As previously explained in Chapter 3, clone-and-own approaches might be
suitable in certain limited cases (like when trying to penetrate a new market
for the first time with a breakthrough solution derived from a previous
product) or for certain types of industries in which a new product instance is
delivered only every other lustrum or decade. But even in those situations,
starting from scratch might be a more effective solution than clone-and-
own. Indeed, three sine qua non prerequisites must be in place for such an
approach to have a chance to succeed. First, teams involved in the
development and delivery of the product must be black belt and eighth dan
in configuration management. Second, the technical debt of the product that
will be used to create the new clone must be estimated and thoroughly
documented. Third, an up-to-date architecture description document along
with its justification file must be properly formalized and available.
Note: Throughout their careers, the authors of this book have very (very)
rarely come across teams that met such high standards.
21.2.2 Development Costs and Lead Time
While the previous section was devoted to maintenance and debugging
costs, we will mainly focus on development costs in the present section.
Figure 21.2 shows a typical evolution of cumulative development costs in a
clone-and-own approach as a function of delivered product instances. As we
can see, cumulated development costs are proportional to the number of
product instances N in a clone-and-own approach (continuous line in Figure
21.2). For completeness sake, Figure 21.2 also shows the total cumulated
costs, including debugging costs, as exposed in the previous section (dotted
line).
Figure 21.2 Cumulated development costs in a clone-and-own
approach (without MBPLE).
The curves in Figure 21.2 can be explained by several factors. On one hand,
verification and validation costs and delays are usually incompressible in a
clone-and-own approach. The reason for this is that, since teams that “own
the clone” do not have, in general, a precise estimation of the technical debt
of the product being cloned, they all tend to perform full verification and
validation campaigns independently of what has been carried out on the
product being cloned or in previous product instances. This is just a normal
human reaction by which teams try to reassure themselves with some level
of confidence in the delivered cloned product.
On the other hand, even though teams are supposed to reuse an existing
product, the modifications that they must perform to adapt the clone to the
specific needs of a given customer usually translate into the development of
fixes, additional debugging activities, and integration work that are very
often underestimated (as explained in the previous section). This
unanticipated work, which naturally translates into higher costs and longer
lead times regarding original project goals, tends to grow exponentially
when the product that is cloned is poorly documented in terms of
architecture, justification of design choices, or technical debt.
21.3 MBPLE Value Proposition
The value proposition of MBPLE is based on a very simple principle:
Transitioning from the management of many independent products, built
through relatively large and siloed development projects, to a single
endeavor (aka Product Line Program) that delivers product instances via
smaller, more cost-effective developments (Navas et al. 2023).
MBPLE enables the management of the resulting portfolio of related
products as one single entity instead of having to manage separately many
single entities. The relations among the shared asset supersets of the product
line, and among those and all of their product models, are indeed
formalized, explicit, tracked, and maintained over time. The MBPLE digital
thread provides a solid foundation for effective decision-making by
enabling, for instance, impact analyses, sensitivity analyses, and
multidisciplinary analyses (see Chapter 18). Once again, to be successful,
this new way of working must become the common delivery approach of
the entire organization as introduced in Chapter 5.
As stated in previous chapters, models also contribute to the cost-
effectiveness improvement brought by MBPLE, thanks to the formal or
semi-formal languages in which models are described. These languages not
only support communication among teams or facilitate interoperability, but
they also support advanced verification activities. Examples of these
activities include checking that assets possess the expected properties or
verifying that assets are correct, complete, and consistent with regard to
other assets.
In the following paragraphs, the benefits of MBPLE are further developed.
They are proposed as different components to build a business case for
MBPLE.
21.3.1 Cost-effectiveness Improvement
The most visible benefit of MBPLE is undoubtedly the cost-effectiveness of
the approach. In this section, we focus on cost avoidance and lead-time
improvements. Section 21.3.3 further dwells on required investments and
the overall return of investments.
Let us start by comparing the cumulated development costs of a given
number of products developed with a clone-and-own approach and with an
MBPLE approach (Figure 21.3). The light gray line in Figure 21.3
represents the cumulated costs in the former approach as a function of the
number of member products, as presented in Section 21.2.2. The dark gray
line represents the cumulated costs in an MBPLE approach for the same
number of member products.
Figure 21.3 Comparison of cumulated development costs with and
without MBPLE (theoretical).
As we can see in the figure, the initial cumulated costs of the MBPLE
approach are higher because of the initial investments required to
implement the approach. Up to the third member product, the cumulated
costs of the MBPLE approach are higher than those of the clone-and-own
approach (i.e. without MBPLE). The point where the two lines cross
represents the payoff point of the MBPLE approach. By experience, this
point is typically reached between the second and fourth member products.
From that point on, the cumulated costs of the clone-and-own approach
become higher than those of the MBPLE approach.
The area between the two lines to the right of the payoff point represents the
benefits obtained with the MBPLE approach. Of course, the higher the
number of member products, the higher the benefits obtained with an
MBPLE approach. The theoretical representation in Figure 21.4 suggests
that the required investments to implement MBPLE are available from the
start, including the first member product. This is not the case for many
large-scale systems or within large organizations, in which investments are
granted gradually on a multi-year basis (we elaborate further on this aspect
in Section 21.3.3).
Figure 21.4 Effort avoidance to perform a task, thanks to MBPLE.
Source: Adapted from INCOSE 2019.
One way of evaluating the cost-effectiveness of MBPLE is to consider the
engineering efforts (thus, engineering costs) that are avoided when
implementing the approach. Figure 21.4 shows the effort avoidance to
perform a development task in an MBPLE approach for a given number of
impacted member products. A development task represents any activity,
work package, or work product that has a clearly defined scope, whose
execution is required to deliver a member product, and whose associated
effort (or workload) can be measured. Examples include issuing a
validation plan, writing product brochures or issuing cost estimations for
calls for tenders, writing a system requirement specification document,
correcting a bug, writing an interface control document, producing the code
of a software module, or drawing mechanical or electrical design diagrams.
Each bar in Figure 21.4 is associated with a given number of member
products that are impacted by the task. The black zone of each bar
represents the “unitary effort” to execute the task (i.e. perform an activity or
produce the expected work product). The dashed zone represents the effort
required to implement MBPLE for the given task (like transforming
artifacts required to execute the task into shareable, reusable assets, setting
up tools, or instantiating assets), which is independent of the number of
impacted member products. The light gray zone represents the effort that
would be required to execute the task for each of the impacted member
products in a clone-and-own approach. For example, the effort required to
execute the task for two member products would be two times the unitary
effort (i.e. the task would be executed once for each member product); the
effort required to execute the task for three product instances would be three
times the unitary effort, and so on and so forth.
The efforts represented by the light gray zones are thus the efforts that do
not need to be provided in an MBPLE approach. Indeed, the efforts to
implement MBPLE for a given task and to execute the task are only
provided once for the benefit of all member products, whatever their
number. If we go back to the example of bug detection and correction
exposed in the previous section, let us assume that an error has been
detected and needs to be corrected. In an MBPLE approach, the correction
would only be performed once on the shared asset supersets of the product
line, then deployed to the member products (this would be done by deriving
the new version of the assets thanks to the automation capabilities of
MBPLE). Of course, the decision to deploy the correction on the derived
products must be done on a member product-by-member product basis,
taking into consideration the criticality of the defect found, the development
stage, and original configuration of each derived member product.
21.3.2 Lead Time Improvement
To establish a relation between engineering efforts and lead time, one needs
to have an estimation of the available workforce in the organization. In our
example, if the organization has enough workforce to parallelize the
correction of each product instance, then the impact on lead time would be
neglectable. However, if that is not the case, the correction of the product
instances would rather occur in a serial mode (i.e. one, two, or three
member products at a time, depending on the available workforce). The
impact on lead time would then be proportional to the number of derived
products.
It is worth underlining that MBPLE also provides the capabilities to
perform quick impact analysis and provide useful information for decision-
making before performing the correction. On the one hand, impact analysis
enabled by the MBPLE digital thread will point out what assets (and what
parts of those assets) would need to be upgraded. Then, automated non-
regression tests would be performed only once, all configurations of the
member products being known. On the other hand, if the results of the
impact analysis show that only a reduced number of member products are
concerned, the correction would only be deployed on those member
products.
Finally, it is easy to imagine that teams that perform their work in a clone-
and-own approach and without the analysis and automation capabilities
provided by MBPLE do so in highly stressful conditions. Such working
environments are naturally more error-prone and have negative impacts on
productivity, thus on lead time. One could thus argue that the inherent
characteristics of MBPLE have a liberating effect on teams and
organizations that adopt it.
21.3.3 Quality Improvement
Figures 21.3 and 21.4 (in addition to Figures 21.5 and 21.6) point to a
simple MBPLE principle: “the more member products you derive, the more
you gain.” This same principle applies to the quality of a product line or, to
be more precise, to the quality of the derived products of the product line.
Figure 21.5 Volume of IVVQ activities and qualification credit as a
function of the number of derived products issued from the same
product configuration.
Figure 21.6 Cumulated development costs with and without MBPLE
considering a gradual investment scheme.
Integration, verification, validation, and qualification (IVVQ) are the first
activities for which gains are obtained when implementing MBPLE in an
organization. These are mainly performed on the derived products of a
product line. When a member product goes through an IVVQ campaign, the
product configuration that was used for its derivation earns what we may
call validation or qualification “credits.” Therefore, the more a product
configuration is used to derive similar products, the higher the credits of
this configuration will be. In some industries, it is even possible to qualify a
product (or parts of a product) via “demonstration” or “certificate” in order
to avoid performing full IVVQ campaigns, as long as it can be proven that
the product configuration and the context in which the product will operate
remain unchanged with respect to previously qualified products.
Figure 21.5 shows the evolution of the volume of IVVQ activities as a
function of the number of derived products. This figure shows that the more
a given product configuration is used, the more it becomes “mature” in
terms of validation and qualification. As a consequence, the volume of
IVVQ activities tends to decrease as the number of instantiated products
issued from the same product configuration grows.
Figure 21.5 also shows that the volume of IVVQ activities eventually
reaches an asymptote, which means that there will always remain a certain
volume of IVVQ activities to be performed. This is particularly true for
safety-critical and mission-critical systems, as detailed in Chapter 24.
As different feature configurations are used to derive different member
products over time, the full scope of a product line is gradually covered.
While performing the IVVQ of the 150% product line is practically
impossible, the more different member products are validated and qualified,
the higher the “credits” of the product line as a whole will be. Finally, it is
worth underlining that validating and qualifying all unconstrained potential
member products of a product line is neither feasible nor desirable. In this
case, defining a reduced number of planned member products that meet the
needs of targeted markets, market segments, or even categories of
customers, as explained in the previous chapters, looks like good practice.
These allowed configurations contribute to reducing the qualification space
and to obtaining the qualification of the product line as a whole at a faster
pace.
21.3.4 The Return on Investment of MBPLE
Challengers of MBPLE and PLE usually advance the arguments of the
“huge” initial investment that is required to set up a product line approach
compared to the traditional clone-and-own approach and the “long” period
of time that is required to reach the payoff point and start obtaining benefits.
This short-sighted view puts aside the fact that, like the money one would
invest in a financial product, MBPLE is indeed an investment. Just like the
money one would put in an investment plan, MBPLE provides higher
dividends in the mid- to long term. If a person decides to go for the
immediate quick win or withdraw the money from the bank before the
investment plan reaches its term, chances are this person will either lose
money or not obtain the maximum benefits in the long term (which in the
end accounts for the same). But the person has indeed the choice.
As stated in Section 21.3.1, the required investments to set up a product line
are not usually granted in their entirety right at the program kick-off.
Investments are more commonly granted gradually on a year-to-year basis
and usually follow some sort of “stairway-shaped” curve, as shown in
Figure 21.6.
There are two notable differences between this figure and the theoretical
curve shown in Figure 21.3. First, we can observe that the payoff point can
be reached “sooner” when investments are done gradually (which is quite
logical because the initial investments are smaller). Second, we can also
observe that, compared to the theoretical curve, cumulated costs for
MBPLE are slightly higher in this type of investment scheme. This is due to
the fact that the cost of instantiation tends to be slightly higher in the latter
case because setting up the “product line factory” would require some
additional preparation work for every instance as the scope of the product
line grows with newly developed features or shared assets of different
nature. Still, the area between the light and dark gray lines to the right of the
payoff point (which, as stated before, represents the benefits obtained with
the MBPLE approach) is worth considering.
In a purely analytical approach, the return on investment of MBPLE could
be estimated as the ratio of the initial investment and the obtained benefits
(savings or avoided costs). A more pertinent estimation of the value and the
return on investment of MBPLE could be obtained by calculating the ratio
of the areas between the two lines to the right (i.e. the benefits of MBPLE
with respect to traditional clone-and-own approaches) and to the left (i.e.
the investment cost of MBPLE with respect to clone-and-own approaches)
of the payoff point in Figure 21.3 or 21.6. Without dwelling further into
numbers, let us just insist on the fact that the higher the number of derived
products, the higher this ratio will be (thus, the higher the return on
investment of MBPLE).
Figure 21.7 shows a comparison of typical nonrecurring costs (NRC) curves
associated with three different delivery approaches: clone-and-own,
delivery using modeling and simulation, and MBPLE-based delivery. The
NRC shown in Figure 21.7 only includes costs related to design,
development, implementation, and initial investments.
Figure 21.7 Nonrecurring cost comparison between three different
approaches (clone-and-own, modeling and simulation alone,
MBPLE).
As stated earlier, the initial cost required to set up the MBPLE approach
usually requires a higher initial investment, which explains why the set of
curves related to a first member product derivation is very similar among
the different approaches (we made the assumption that setting up modeling
and simulation capabilities also requires a higher initial investment
compared to clone-and-own approaches). The curves on the right of Figure
21.7 represent the cumulated nonrecurring costs for a given number of
member products over a period of time (the same number for the three
approaches). This figure provides an example of the type of decision tools
that can be used by a company to simulate different cases and decide on the
level of investments to be allocated to a product line based on risk-to-
benefit estimations.
An interesting literature review of the justification of MBSE is provided in
(Carroll and Malins 2016). Among the many sources analyzed by the
review is a study that drew a comparison between the results obtained by
projects using MBSE, MBPLE, and traditional SE based on the analysis of
several hundreds of projects (Figure 21.8). This work revealed that projects
using an MBSE approach cost 55% less than projects applying traditional
SE. In addition, projects using MBSE deliver on time 62% of the time,
compared to 59% for traditional SE. Finally, it appears that applying
MBPLE translates into even higher benefits: 62% cost reduction and 75%
on-time delivery.
Figure 21.8 Comparison of development costs and on-time delivery
obtained with SE, MBSE, and MBPLE.
Source: Adapted from Carroll and Malins, 2016 / U.S. Department of Energy / Public
domain.
Beyond the numbers, the value of MBPLE relies on its capacity to act as an
enabler of profitable growth for an organization (see Chapter 28). When
engineering teams improve their performance by executing engineering
tasks only once (instead of many times for a plethora of different products)
and are relieved from performing manual, repetitive tasks through
automation, they are enabled to “do more.” The budget and resources that
are made available, thanks to MBPLE, can now be used, for instance, to
explore other business opportunities or work on innovative features to grow
the business.
Therefore, probably the right question a top manager, CTO, or CEO should
ask themselves when trying to decide whether implementing MBPLE is
pertinent or not for the business is not about the return on investment, but
rather, “What should I ask my engineering teams do when they have
finished their week’s job by Wednesday afternoon?”
References
Carroll, E.R. and Malins, J.R. (2016). Systematic Literature Review: How is
Model-Based Systems Engineering Justified.
https://doi.org/10.2172/1561164.
INCOSE (2019). Feature-Based System and Software Product Line
Engineering: A Primer. San Diego: INCOSE.
Navas, J., Bonnet, S., Voirin J.L. and Chalé Gongora, H.G. (2023). A value-
driven, integrated approach to model-based product line engineering.
INCOSE 2023 International Symposium. (Hawaii, USA), San Diego:
INCOSE.

Chapter 22
The MBPLE Adoption Quadrant
22.1 Introduction
It is emphasized throughout this book that adopting model-based product
line engineering (MBPLE) is not an “overnight” effort; it requires time to
be implemented and accepted within a corporate context. Its adoption
depends on several factors, but two main drivers justify its implementation:
the increasing level of system complexity and the variability experienced by
the organization, as introduced in Chapter 2. In theory, a company could
transition from predominantly document-based (single) systems
engineering to model-based development of product lines and their derived
products. However, the practice shows that other transitional development
approaches are usually encountered before MBPLE can be fully adopted.
To simplify and visualize the transition from one stage to another, we
developed the “MBPLE adoption quadrant” (MBPLEAQ), illustrated in
Figure 22.1.
Figure 22.1 MBPLE adoption quadrant (MBPLEAQ).
On the vertical axis, we see the degree of complexity of the system under
development, while on the horizontal axis, we see the degree of variability.
We identified four incremental development approaches depending on the
degree of both variables. The lower-left quadrant is where systems with
relatively low levels of complexity and variability can be found. In this
case, a traditional document-based system engineering approach with a
single-system development scope might not be the best approach for the
company, but it is still sufficient. The upper-left quadrant is associated with
systems that present a higher level of complexity but low variability. An
MBSE approach is the most suitable here. In the lower-right quadrant, we
observe systems with relatively low levels of complexity but a higher
degree of variability. In this case, a system family engineering approach
(aka feature-based product line engineering [PLE]) is sufficient and may not
justify the investment in a model-based approach. Finally, in the upper-right
quadrant, we position systems that present both high degrees of complexity
and variability. These systems will benefit the most from the MBPLE
adoption.
The adoption quadrant also serves to explain the different patterns of the
MBPLE adoption. The transition phases from one quadrant to another are
also important and are the main focus of this chapter. The MBPLEAQ is a
useful tool for companies to understand where they stand and whether the
level of complexity and variability experienced by their products
necessitates an MBPLE approach.
Section 22.2 briefly covers the transition from document-based (single)
systems engineering to model-based systems engineering. Section 22.3
shows the pattern from a document-based (single) system development to a
system family engineering (aka feature-based PLE) approach. Sections 22.4
and 22.5 present the transition to MBPLE from MBSE and from system
family engineering (aka feature-based PLE), respectively.
22.2 From Document-based Systems
Engineering to Model-based Systems
Engineering
The topic of this section is the center of gravity of Chapter 8, where the key
aspects of such transitions are discussed. However, it is important to focus
on the intermediate step that companies adopt while shifting to MBSE. The
transition is well explained by “the evolution of MBSE” represented in the
book Systems Engineering Demystified (Holt 2022).
Companies usually first experience a model-enhanced stage, where textual
requirements are still central but models are linked to them to establish
traceability and enhance their understanding (see arrow “SE + MB” in
Figure 22.1). Only after the stage has reached its limits in dealing with
increasing complexity, the company can implement an MBSE approach
(both approaches model-centric and model-based are considered part of
MBSE).
22.3 From Document-based Systems
Engineering to System Family Engineering
(Aka PLE)
Once the product portfolio of a company increases in variety, the variability
of the systems developed tends also to rise, becoming with the time hard to
manage and control. At this stage, companies start to look at approaches,
such as reuse, modularization, architecture composability, and clone-and-
own (see Chapter 3). The latest technique is usually the most natural and
adopted, even when the organization announces to benefit from a product
line-based approach (see “SE + PL” arrow in Figure 22.1). This trend is
well documented in the literature (Clements et al. 2014; Chalé Góngora et
al. 2014) and it is part of the historical evolution of PLE as a discipline (see
the “Dolly Era” in Chapter 7). We experienced such a transition in the early
phases of our involvement at Renault, Alstom. Bombardier, Thales, and
Airbus as well as the numerous organizations consulted along our
professional careers.
22.4 From Model-based Systems
Engineering to Model-based Product Line
Engineering
In industrial contexts, we often encounter the false myth or
misunderstanding that MBPLE is only centered on the modeling of
variability with systems modeling language (SysML) assets. This is a
wrong statement as discussed already in Chapter 8, when we introduced the
definition of MBPLE as: “the application of feature-based PLE in
combination with MBSE where architecture models are central but not
exclusive for realizing the product line and its member products.” However,
in the transition phase from MBSE to MBPLE, it is often the case that the
PLE approach will be exclusively focused on the SysML assets, especially
when the team or division adopting it has a strong focus on the model-based
discipline (see “MBSE + PLE” arrow in Figure 22.1). In this way, the other
domain models do not benefit from the feature-based PLE approach and
variability inconsistencies may arise. Soon it becomes evident the need to
extend the feature-based PLE approach to other domains, such as
requirements, MCAD, ECAD, etc., and integrate them within an MBPLE
framework. A similar pattern is exemplified by the MBPLE adoption at
Airbus (Madeira et al. 2023).
22.5 From System Family Engineering (Aka
PLE) to Model-based Product Line
Engineering
Several companies managed to successfully implement a PLE approach.
However, in some cases, they do not have MBSE models as central assets
of the PLE factory. This is often the case when organizations migrate from
systems family engineering (not model based) to an MBPLE approach.
In the transition phase, the MBSE domain will be treated as any other
domain to be included within a feature-based approach. However, in this
way, the MBSE models will act as standalone models with low
interdependencies with other assets such as textual requirements, MCAD,
ECAD, SW architecture, test models etc. (see “PLE + MBSE” in Figure
22.1). Soon it will become evident that there is a need to create an MBSE
framework (or even better, an MBPLE framework) that holds the parts
together and brings consistency. This is when the organization transitions
completely to the upper right quadrant (Figure 22.1).
22.6 The MBPLEAQ Applied in the Industrial
Cases
The MBPLEAQ is a simplified “tool” that is primarily used to:
Understand the differences between different systems engineering
approaches when dealing with increasing complexity and variability;
Assess where the organization is in the adoption of MBPLE;
Identify the pattern undertaken in the MBPLE adoption.
In the last part of this book (Part V), we use the MBPLEAQ to visualize the
paths toward the MBPLE adoption of the five organizations featured in the
industrial cases.
References
Chalé Góngora, H.G., Ferrogalini, M. and Moreau, C. (2014). How to boost
PLE by using MBSE – a case study of a rolling stock product line.
Proceedings of the 5th International Conference on Complex Systems
Design and Management (CSDM, Paris, FR). Springer.
Clements, P., Gregg, S.P., Krueger C. et al. (2014). Second Generation
Product Line Engineering Takes Hold in the DoD. 27, 12–18.
Holt, J. (2023). Systems Engineering Demystified: A Practitioners
Handbook for Developing Complex Systems Using a Model-Based
Approach, 2e. Packt Publishing, Limited, UK.
Madeira, R.H., de Sousa Pinto, D.H. and Forlingieri, M. (2023). Variability
on System Architecture using Airbus MBPLE for MOFLT Framework.
Seville, Spain: INCOSE International Symposium, 33, 601–615.
OceanofPDF.com
Chapter 23
The MBPLE Adoption Framework
23.1 Introduction
For most organizations, key advantages such as reducing development costs
and time-to-market, enhancing the quality of design artifacts, managing
complexity, and boosting reusability are central to evaluating the return on
investment. These benefits also serve as fundamental principles that
underscore the utility of model-based product line engineering (MBPLE).
As indicated in Chapter 21, constructing a robust business case for adopting
MBPLE is an essential initial step before proceeding with further activities.
Although the literature extensively details the benefits of MBPLE (Chalé
Góngora and Greugny 2017), each organization must identify specific
advantages that motivate its adoption of MBPLE.
Similar to model-based systems engineering (MBSE), the adoption of
MBPLE necessitates careful planning and clear definition of its essential
pillars and enablers. As demonstrated by the MBPLE mission at Airbus,
adopting the MBPLE approach requires adherence to standards and an
incremental strategy to address product line development challenges while
securing validation from key stakeholders (Forlingieri 2022). This adoption
is not an “overnight” process and demands a deep understanding of the
organizational context. Therefore, there is no universal formula; however,
several critical components can be pinpointed. Reflecting on similarities
with MBSE adoption, organization-driven MBSE methodologies and
frameworks typically comprise three major parts: language, method, and
tool (Delligatti 2013). Yet, the D3 MBSE Adoption Toolbox proposed by
Chami et al. (2018) includes additional components, such as the
“personnel.” According to the MBPLE adoption framework at Airbus, the
key dimensions of the MBPLE framework include process, methods,
information model, tools, and environment.
These examples, among others discussed in the final section of this book as
industrial cases, demonstrate that implementing a digital transformation
where MBPLE is a key pillar necessitates a holistic approach. The MBPLE
approach, in the context of digital transformation, extends beyond mere
technology implementation and considers broader impacts on processes,
people, and the organizational culture or environment (Straub et al. 2021).
Building on these premises, a recommended adoption framework is
introduced here, encompassing the following: process (Section 23.2),
methods (Section 23.3), information model (Section 23.4), tool chain
(Section 23.5), and organization (Section 23.6).
Analogous to the MBSE D3 Adoption Toolbox (Chami et al. 2018), these
pillars are constructed and validated during the definition and development
phases, and then rolled out within the organization and product programs
during the deployment phase (that is why the D3 stands for). While the
MBPLE team plays a critical role in the definition and development phases,
during the deployment phase, programs begin to assume ownership of the
framework. Figure 23.1 summarizes the adoption framework and its
relationship to the three phases.
Figure 23.1 Overview of the MBPLE adoption framework and the
three adoption phases.
23.2 MBPLE Process
Companies that develop complex systems typically maintain extensive
process landscapes to ensure efficient and effective operations. Those
adhering to systems engineering principles, as specified in ISO/IEC 15288
(ISO/IEC/IEEE 2023a), already incorporate these processes as a
fundamental part of their Product Development Process (PDP) (Ulrich and
Eppinger 2004). ISO/IEC 15288 (ISO/IEC/IEEE 2023a) establishes
standards for system lifecycle processes and provides guidance on
integrating these processes into an organization’s workflow. This is based
on the necessity of aligning technical and management processes with the
overarching business strategy.
MBPLE introduces a sophisticated approach to product development,
concentrating on the creation and management of a product line and its
member products. Within the PDP framework, it is crucial that MBPLE
processes align with existing processes to ensure unified and effective
operations.
Integrating MBPLE processes within the existing PDP requires a thoughtful
adaptation of the current process landscape. This adaptation is essential to
preserve the PDP’s flexibility and responsiveness while meeting the unique
demands and objectives of MBPLE. For example, adopting this approach
enables more strategic resource allocation, moving from the single-product
responsibility to the product line scope, enhanced cross-functional
collaboration, and reduced duplications of responsibilities in development.
MBPLE significantly impacts a broad range of processes within an
organization. From the technical management processes and technical
processes (focus of Chapter 24) outlined by ISO/IEC 15288 (ISO/IEC/IEEE
2023a), to the broader business and procurement processes, the influence of
MBPLE is comprehensive. For example, consider the configuration
management processes. The focus here extends beyond a single system to
include a product line and all its member products. This expansion adds
complexity to configuration management practices as the scope of
configuration becomes broader. More details about the role of configuration
management in MBPLE are provided in Chapter 19, while Chapter 24
covers the process topic in depth.
23.3 MBPLE Methods
Methods are extensively utilized in systems engineering. They delineate the
“How” for the “What” established by the process, detailing the steps while
remaining independent of specific tools. It is not accurate to refer to a single
MBPLE method; rather, it is more appropriate to discuss various MBPLE
methods. Indeed, although the method for defining a feature model and
linking features to those parts of the assets that represent a variation should
be one and consistent, multiple methods can be formulated to manage the
variability in each specific domain (e.g. requirements, SysML models,
CADs, etc.). Given the impracticality of developing a standardized,
universal method for MBPLE applicable across multiple domains, we have
identified six takeaways, based primarily on our experience and public
references, to assist methodologists in defining MBPLE methods.
Takeaway 1: The feature model shall be a distinct asset,
serving as the sole source of variability across various
domains
Initially, implementing feature models across diverse digital assets such as
requirements, design models, and CADs is crucial for a comprehensive
MBPLE approach. This practice facilitates uniform variability management
across multiple types of assets, highlighting the significance of an
integrated approach to managing variability. As stipulated by the most
recent feature-based PLE standard ISO/IEC 26580 (ISO/IEC 2021), when
the product line development lifecycle generates multiple assets, it is highly
beneficial to develop and maintain the feature model as an independent
source of variability, separate from any other assets (Malone et al. 2018).
Takeaway 2: MBPLE shall not solely concentrate on
MBSE assets, yet these assets are central in ensuring
consistent implementation of variability
MBSE Architecture frameworks like CAF at Continental (Seidel and
Forlingieri 2023), and R-MOFLT at Airbus (Madeira et al. 2023), which are
examples well known to us authors of this book, incorporate variability
modeling into more traditional system modeling. Due to SysML v1’s
recognized limitations in supporting variability modeling, the representation
of variability and product lines is often neglected in MBSE methods and
frameworks (Madeira et al. 2023). When PLE mechanisms are integrated
with MBSE, the shared assets superset is modeled, and the variation point is
only connected to those model elements that are variable, as they do not
appear in every product of the product line. However, one significant
challenge of this approach is modeling a product line architecture that is
both valid in SysML and conceptually robust according to the system
semantics (Forlingieri and Weilkiens 2022). Since SysML models can
encompass interdependencies ranging from requirements to CAD models,
they help maintain consistency across different assets. For example, a
logical architecture can meet system requirements and form the basis for a
structure that is further developed into a Feature-oriented MCAD model. In
this context, the SysML model ensures consistency among these various
assets. When introducing variability into requirements, SysML models,
MCADs, and so forth, the feature model acts as the single source of
variability to be implemented across the assets, and the SysML model
ensures a consistent implementation across these assets. This principle
forms the fundamental assumption of MBPLE: it does not imply focusing
exclusively on SysML models, but rather on the model-based approach to
ensure a consistent implementation of variability.
Takeaway 3: Variability is inherently multidimensional,
and this characteristic should be reflected in the
definition of MBPLE methods
Chapter 17 of this book introduces and develops the four dimensions of
variability: co-variability in product, manufacturing, and services;
variability in development lifecycle; variability in layers of abstraction; and
variability in system hierarchy levels. These dimensions, initially identified
at Airbus (Forlingieri 2022), provided the organization with crucial insights
for developing MBPLE methods. For example, considering an aircraft, the
variability of the system of interest (SoI) – the aircraft in the Airbus case –
must account for variability inputs from the final assembly line (FAL) and
vice versa, if the SoI is the FAL. The second dimension emphasizes that
variability must be addressed throughout the entire lifecycle, from concept
design to after-delivery customization. Variability then cascades
hierarchically along systems levels top-down from the market but can also
be infused bottom-up by legacy solutions and suppliers. Last but not least,
variability may be pertinent at one abstraction layer (e.g. the physical layer)
but irrelevant at another (e.g., the functional layer). This
multidimensionality of variability in complex systems makes the model-
based approach more necessary than a choice, when implementing feature-
based PLE.
Takeaway 4: MBPLE methods should be developed
incrementally while maintaining a focus on the broader
picture
Given that MBPLE methods are applicable to all domains involved in a
product line’s development, it is impractical to comprehensively cover all
domains at once before beginning to implement and deploy MBPLE
methods. This statement should not be misinterpreted. It is essential to
develop an information model that captures all assets within the scope and
their interdependencies, but this does not necessitate having a fully detailed
method developed for each asset. Capitalizing from lessons learned at
Alstom (Chalé Góngora et al. 2014), Airbus (Madeira et al. 2023), and
Continental (Seidel and Forlingieri 2023) across three distinct industries, a
practical starting point for defining methods is to initially focus on textual
requirements and SysML models. Given the complexity of such systems, it
is advisable to first establish a method that might involve modeling
variation in a SysML model or defining a feature model, initially at one
hierarchical level before expanding to multiple levels. Another example,
limited to the SysML model, is to start with modeling variation in SysML
by focusing on structural views before progressing to behavioral ones, once
sufficient experience has been gained.
Takeaway 5: Tools and tool chains shall not dictate the
definition of MBPLE methods
This statement does not imply that tools are unnecessary or irrelevant.
Rather, it stresses the importance of defining methods independently of any
specific tool or tool chain initially (Forlingieri and Weilkiens 2022). It is
crucial, after establishing this foundation, to assess how the methods align
with what existing tools can provide and identify any discrepancies. Tools
are designed to help organizations address challenges and should not dictate
the standards or direction of method development activities. This principle
applies not only to the development of MBPLE methods but also to any
methodological definition.
Takeaway 6: MBPLE methods shall consistently serve
and facilitate the needs of the end-users
A fundamental objective of MBPLE methods is to enhance the modeling
process and the end-user experience by minimizing effort and potential
discrepancies. It is essential to collaborate closely with principal end-users
to pinpoint their specific needs for variability management and to
implement the methods across diverse domains, while welcoming feedback
for enhancements. This focus on the user ensures that MBPLE methods
remain effective and flexible in various organizational environments
(Madeira et al. 2023).
Chapter 25 covers this topic more extensively, providing some more
examples of methods.
23.4 MBPLE Information Model
In Chapter 7, we revisit the historical development of feature modeling
languages and the creation of SysML. While SysML benefits from a
standardized language (ISO/IEC 2017), this is not the case for feature
modeling or variability languages. The common variability language
(Haugen et al. 2012) represented the most significant effort to define a
standard for variability modeling, drawing from the Feature oriented
domain analysis language (Kang et al. 1990). However, it ultimately failed,
and not due to technical reasons alone!
Instead of focusing solely on the language, it is more appropriate to discuss
the information model. This is because MBPLE encompasses more than
just the modeling language; it requires an information model that spans all
the domains involved in MBPLE. Such an information model enables the
mapping of elements to method outputs and maintains tool independence
across methods. A notable example is the Systems Engineering Common
Architecture Model (SECAM) from Airbus (Wirtz and Bouffaron, 2020),
which also incorporates the variability aspect. Ideally, an information model
should address the components of a feature model and their connections to
other assets. Chapter 26 digs deeper into this subject.
23.5 MBPLE Tool Chain
In the preceding section, we emphasize an essential principle for defining
MBPLE methods: “Tools and tool chains should not determine the
definition of MBPLE methods.” There should be a clear separation between
method formalization and its implementation using a specific tool or tool
chain. MBPLE methods should be supported by an information model, as
previously mentioned, to facilitate the mapping of method activities to
different tools. This approach is crucial as it helps mitigate lock-in effects
and enhances the interoperability of assets among various internal and
external stakeholders.
A definition of an MBPLE tool chain, like any tool chain, needs to
incorporate an As-Is topology that reflects the existing tools based on
current methods and a To-be topology that plans for the future integration of
tools in a subsequent phase. This strategy should be paired with a roadmap,
providing visibility into what tools will be available and when, which
assists the MBPLE team in prioritizing their solutions’ definition and
development. It is unrealistic to initially define a comprehensive MBPLE
tool chain and then implement it.
Figure 23.2 displays a basic MBPLE tool chain (Chalé Góngora et al.
2014), which includes:
a. PLE tool
b. Requirements management repository
c. System modeling tool and repository
d. Overarching configuration management
e. Interoperability solution for tools to exchange data, queries
Figure 23.2 Simplified basic MBPLE tool chain.
The diagram illustrates the PLE tool (a) as a distinct entity, not integrated
within other solutions. It facilitates the definition and management of
feature models and configurations, linking variable assets to features, and
enabling the transformation of assets from other domain-specific tools. The
requirements management repository (b) contains sets of textual
requirements with variations and interfaces with the PLE tool. Similarly, the
system modeling tool and repository (c) should also connect with the PLE
tool. A configuration management solution (d) sits atop the other domain
applications, managing and controlling the configuration of assets. The
interoperability (e) among the applications in scope shall be built on
existing standards such as OSLC (OSLC 2021) and MoSSEC (ISO 2021).
Such a basic tool chain already offers robust MBPLE capabilities and
supports its initial deployment.
However, the MBPLE team should also envision a more extensive solution
by outlining and working towards the definition and deployment of a
broader tool chain (Chalé Góngora et al. 2014), as depicted in Figure 23.3,
which might include:
f. Software repository
g. Test repository
h. BOM tool and repository
i. CAD tool and repository
j. Others
Figure 23.3 More comprehensive MBPLE tool chain.
We advise that the additional applications shown in light gray in Figure
23.3 be implemented in a second phase, following a successful initial
deployment of the basic MBPLE tool chain.
Additional considerations during the tool chain definition are summarized
as follows:
PLE transformation mechanism: While primary PLE tools enable asset
transformation within several domains, this process typically occurs
separately within each tool.
ALM and PLM integration: The PLE tool shall facilitate communication
with both application lifecycle management (ALM) and product lifecycle
management (PLM) domains, though significant integration challenges
remain, particularly regarding configuration management.
Feature-assets linking: This capability is vital for filtering and
transforming variability across different domain assets, but each link should
be maintained carefully to avoid duplications. For instance, if a system
requirement and a SysML model element are already linked, should the
feature also link to both asset types for the same purpose?
Interoperability standards: It is essential to rely on interoperability
standards rather than isolated API solutions or tool-specific options. This
will promote the adoption of MBPLE and the inclusion of tools from
various vendors or open-source solutions.
Chapter 27 will explore this topic in greater detail.
23.6 MBPLE Organization
In large-scale or high-tech systems, developing a product line typically
involves multiple customer projects, unlike mass-market commercial
products. This process begins with comprehensive risk and opportunity
analyses, which consider factors such as leveraging knowledge from past
projects, evaluating technical scope similarities, identifying competitive
advantages, securing funding, and addressing knowledge gaps.
In an MBPLE approach, a company’s business strategy is outlined in
strategic plans and roadmaps for its product lines. These roadmaps define
market requirements, target positioning, expected features, investments,
revenues, and sales opportunities, and are regularly reviewed by strategic
teams and product line heads. The roadmap is reflected in the product line’s
architecture and supported by technical roadmaps of its subsystems and
components.
Supporting a product line’s development and maintenance often involves
hybrid funding schemes that combine self-funded and external budgets.
Initial funding might come from major customers or self-funding stages,
evolving over time to include customer contributions and centralized
financial flows managed by the product line development program.
A best practice in the industry is to manage the product line as a single
entity, allowing flexibility in development plans. Organizing the product
line strategy and roadmap within a coordinated development program
ensures consistent strategic planning, governance, and operations. This
programmatic approach helps balance long-term strategic objectives with
short-term financial goals, effectively aligning company strategy, product
policy, management, engineering, and financial aspects.
Understanding the relationship between agile methodologies and product
line development is also crucial within the organization, being the two
practices complementary as explained in Chapter 28.
Successfully adopting MBPLE requires a dedicated team that goes beyond
the organization’s traditional structure. This includes the core MBPLE team
responsible for defining and developing MBPLE, the MBPLE personnel
outside the core team who validate and use the MBPLE framework in
various projects and programs, and the MBPLE environment where the
framework can be developed and validated independently of development
programs. Chapter 28 explores these aspects to provide readers with a
clearer understanding of MBPLE’s role within an organization.

Chapter 24
MBPLE Process
24.1 Introduction
There are various ways to address process tailoring and adaptation when implementing model-based product line
engineering (MBPLE). Although MBPLE does not fundamentally alter the engineering processes in an
organization, it does extend them in specific ways. For instance, requirements engineers will continue to work on
requirements as usual, but now they will handle a superset and create variation points. This also applies to software
engineers, test engineers, architects who build design models, and others. Governance remains crucial, with change
control boards continuing to function as before. However, changes now involve shared asset supersets and feature
models, which are reviewed and vetted from the perspective of the entire product line. These and other changes
must be reflected into the organization’s processes.
This chapter extends the four MBPLE steps from Chapter 12, highlighting the need to tailor the approach for real-
world MBPLE execution, as well as specific organizational and systems engineering processes. Each section of the
chapter, from 24.2 to 24.12, presents one of the 11 MBPLE extended steps and their mapping to the technical
processes of ISO/IEC 15288 (ISO/IEC 2023). The final Section 24.13, briefly covers the nontechnical processes
(ISO/IEC 2023) and emphasizes their importance in rolling out MBPLE successfully.
While familiarity with ISO 15288 (ISO/IEC 2023) aids in understanding the content presented here, we do not
cover all possible mappings between MBPLE steps and the system life cycle processes. Instead, we highlight their
key interdependencies, particularly between MBPLE and the technical processes (ISO/IEC 15288).
The Matrix 24.1 shows the mapping of MBPLE extended steps with the systems life cycle processes.

24.2 Define the Product Line Scoping
The step precedes the MBPLE four-step approach and contributes to the definition of feature models, feature
configurations, shared assets, and product models. It describes which member products and shared assets shall be
part of the product line, including the identification of their commonality and variability. This requires as inputs
market analysis, stakeholder needs analysis, legacy specifications and models and the identification of the main
drivers of variability for the product line. The Product Line Scoping activity is well described in the ISO/IEC
26550 (ISO/IEC 2015) and adapted in ISO/IEC 26580 (ISO/IEC 2021).
Examples of questions that shall be answered during this phase are:
1. Which products shall the product line support?
For instance, based on market needs and enterprise objectives, the products to be developed are identified.
2. Who are the stakeholders?
The key stakeholders for the product line development and those that benefit from its derived member
products are identified here.
3. What are the market’s needs?
These are essential inputs to define the product line and member products to be developed.
4. What are the legacy products to be used as a references?
Usually, new products have resemblance to previous ones, which can be used as reference.
5. What assets are common and variable among the member products?
Identification of those shared assets that shall be kept variable and those that should be kept common across
the member products.
Those questions help the definition of a feasible product line, avoiding overstretching of the member products,
requirements and cost. Planning must start with a clear definition of the product line and its scope that is a joint
effort of product line management, PLE teams, marketing, and other key functions.
Mapping to the ISO/IEC 15288 Systems Life Cycle Processes: Define product line scoping is a step that
necessitates several inputs coming from nontechnical life cycle processes. However, here we focus on the technical
processes of ISO/IEC 15288 (2023). The most important process for this process step is the “business or mission
analysis,” since a product line scoping consists of a mix of business inputs and technical inputs. The product line
scoping can also be further refined in the “stakeholder needs and requirements definition.” While these steps are
usually applied to the single-system life cycle, with the introduction of MBPLE, the focus shifts to the product line
life cycle and the member products life cycle, once derived.
24.3 Define Feature Models
An entire chapter is dedicated to this process step (Chapter 13) so we do not cover it here further. What it is
important to highlight is that feature models are an output of the product line scoping and serve to capture the
variability in a formalized way.
Mapping to the ISO/IEC 15288 Systems Life Cycle Processes: “Define feature models” is an iterative activity
that starts in the “business or analysis mission” process and continues until the “design definition.” Market-related
features and high-level features are likely to be identified in the early life cycle process, including also the
“stakeholder needs and requirements definition” and “systems requirements definition.” More detailed features
such as technical features and domain-specific features are defined in the later “system architecture definition” and
“design definition” processes. The definition of features is done in parallel with the traditional activities of the life
cycle process (e.g. requirements elicitation and definition, architecture definition etc.), but the focus is here
centered on the product line system and not on the single system.
24.4 Define Feature Configurations
Chapter 14 covers this process step. Similarly, for the previous step, they are an output of the product line scoping.
The focus here is on the mapping with the system life cycle process.
Mapping to the ISO/IEC 15288 Systems Life Cycle Processes: Feature Configurations follow the same
definition pattern of the feature models (see Section 24.3). The most important aspects (features) for the market
and customer are taken into account in the early process, while the selection of technical features, which are
hidden to the customers, are usually defined in the later processes.
24.5 Define Shared Assets
Chapter 15 covers this process step. Similarly for the previous steps, the initial stage of the identification of shared
assets is also an output of the product line scoping. This section focuses on the mapping with the system life cycle
process.
Mapping to the ISO/IEC 15288 Systems Life Cycle Processes: The definition of the shared assets, including the
modeling of variation and linking with the features, starts with the “stakeholder needs and requirements definition”
and continues until the “design definition,” where the activity is performed in the specific domains. For instance,
the definition of requirements as shared assets is mainly defined in the “stakeholder needs and requirements
definition” and “system requirements definition.” The definition of variation in the system architectures and
related architecture models are defined in the “system architecture definition,” while SW models or CAD models
in the “design definition” process.
24.6 Define Product Models
Like for Sections 24.3–24.5, this step is discussed in a dedicated chapter (Chapter 16). We stress here the fact that
this step together with “define feature configurations” consist of the bridge between the product line development
and the member products derivation. While feature configurations and product models are defined during the
development of a product line, they are used along the derivation of member products. They are the main inputs
and references for the development of member products.
Mapping to the ISO/IEC 15288 Systems Life Cycle Processes: “Define product models” start with derivation of
requirements assets (“stakeholder needs and requirements definition” and “system requirements definition”). It
continues with the derivation of system architecture models (“system architecture definition”) and the domain-
specific models such as SW, MCAD, ECAD, etc. (“design definition”).
24.7 Verify the Product Line
This is the first process step that follows the four-step approach. According to the definition of the IS0/IEC 15288,
“the purpose of the Verification process is to provide objective evidence that a system, system element, or artifact
fulfills its specified requirements and characteristics” (ISO/IEC 2023). In our case, we refer to a system product
line, and we apply the process to features, feature configurations and shared assets developed for the product line.
Verification artifacts and their traceability with requirements can be developed in the previous step for the product
line and used in this process. As the variability expressed by the product line may (potentially) fulfill an infinitive
number of configurations, it is only possible to verify the system product line for the feature configurations in
scope. This process step is also iterative and can start as early as the first shared assets are defined.
Mapping to the ISO/IEC 15288 Systems Life Cycle Processes: Although verification assets are typically
established early in the life cycle, concurrent with the requirements definition, this step is predominantly aligned
with the ISO/IEC 15288 “Verification” process. It is important to note that while the system product line can be
verified for member products in scope (based on feature configurations and related product models) using the
shared assets available, validation cannot be performed. This is because a product line, as a final implemented
system, does not exist. This aspect is further elaborated in Section 24.9.
24.8 Customize the Product Models (If Needed)
This step is directly related to “define product models” and has the primary scope to develop specificities and
customizations to the derived product models. This happens because in the real world a product line hardly meets
all the specific customer requirements. This is usually a management decision that opted for developing extra
specific features that are not part of the product line. The project handles these features as customization and
member product specificities, because it does not meet the business case elaborated for the product line (e.g. it is
unlikely that other member products will require these features). Sometimes, in a second stage, those specific
features are added within the product line scope, impacting its evolution (see Section 24.11). A good strategy in
handling these member product-specific adaptations is to limit them to part of the system, where their impact is
contained in terms of changes and engineering re-work. A system modular architecture and building-block
approach may facilitate the introduction of such specificities.
Mapping to the ISO/IEC 15288 Systems Life Cycle Processes: This step necessitates rework and modifications
in the derived product models. Typically occurring after the product line is delivered, it involves rerunning the
activities performed for the system product line, from “stakeholder needs and requirements definition” to “design
definition,” but only for the specific Δ (delta) added to the member product. Establishing digital continuity among
the assets helps identify changes and minimize the re-work effort, as explained in Chapter 18.
24.9 Verify the Member Products
After the eventual product model specificities are developed in the “customize the product models,” the new
resulting product models should be verified for the Δ (delta) developed outside the product line scope.
Mapping to the ISO/IEC 15288 Systems Life Cycle Processes: The key life cycle process involved here is again
the “verification” process, but only for the Δ (delta) developed for the member product (specificities).
24.10 Produce the Member Product
The production step only applies to the member products and not to the product line. It occurs when the system is
finally integrated, and built. The variability here of the components is finally visible in the set of member products
derived by the product line.
Mapping to the ISO/IEC 15288 Systems Life Cycle Processes: The process step is performed in both the life
cycle process “implementation” and “integration” as defined by the ISO/IEC 15288 (ISO/IEC 2023).
24.11 Validate the Member Products
Once the member product is produced, either partially or completely, the “validation” process must be fully
executed for each individual member product derived from the product line. According to ISO/IEC 15288, “the
purpose of the Validation process is to provide objective evidence that the system, when in use, fulfills its business
or mission objectives and stakeholder needs and requirements, achieving its intended use in its operational
environment” (ISO/IEC 2023). Since a product line, being an abstract concept rather than a finite product, cannot
be observed “achieving its intended use in its operational environment,” it is impossible to validate the product line
itself. Validation is only possible for the derived member products. Therefore, the product line can be considered
validated only for the in-scope member products that have been validated.
Mapping to the ISO/IEC 15288 Systems Life Cycle Processes: After discussing the application of verification
and validation activities for both the product line and its derived member products, it may initially seem like
redundant effort, but it actually results in a significant reduction of overall effort. Validation assets, similar to
verification assets, are often predefined as part of shared asset supersets for the product line, but they are only
executed during this step and in the “verify the product line” phase. Specifically, the “verification” step occurs
once, not for each member product. Only changes (Δ delta) resulting from adaptations in the product models
require additional verification. This approach translates to lower nonrecurring engineering costs for the
organization, improved system quality due to early detection and resolution of defects across all member products,
and shorter program development times, thanks to reduced time spent on redundant activities.
24.12 Evolve and Decommission the Product Line
In Chapter 18, we explored the evolution of product lines, particularly when new features necessitate changes in
the existing product line. Product lines commonly evolve over time and eventually become obsolete. It is possible
for member products to be phased out while the product line continues to be maintained and used for deriving new
member products. Conversely, a product line may be decommissioned even before its derived member products
are phased out.
When discussing concepts like maintenance and decommissioning of product lines, we are not referring to the
operational maintenance or retirement of a specific system. Product lines are not finite products; therefore, our
focus is on managing all assets that contribute to the development and sustainability of a product line.
Mapping to the ISO/IEC 15288 Systems Life Cycle Processes: In ISO/IEC 15288 (ISO/IEC 2023), we
encounter the “maintenance” and “disposal” processes, which may seem similar to the idea of maintaining (and
evolving) and disposing of a product line. However, it is crucial to distinguish that these processes are intended for
systems that are in operation, which is not the case for a product line. It is more appropriate to align this process
step with all the technical processes described earlier in ISO/IEC 15288 (ISO/IEC 2023). This is because the
evolution of a product line involves revising various assets, but only for the delta (changes) that are implemented.
24.13 MBPLE Mapping with the Non-Technical Processes
The preceding sections outline the mapping of MBPLE steps with the technical processes detailed in ISO/IEC
15288 (ISO/IEC 2023). This chapter does not aim to comprehensively document all interdependencies between
MBPLE extended steps and the processes outlined in the standard. Instead, it emphasizes the most critical
relationships. Furthermore, it does not provide a complete adaptation of the MBPLE approach to a specific
enterprise context, as systems engineering processes must be tailored to meet the unique requirements of each
organization.
Given the focus on the technical aspects of the life cycle processes, detailed coverage of the linkages with
organizational project-enabling processes and technical management processes has not been included. The next
paragraphs provide a short discussion on the impact of MBPLE on nontechnical processes.
Organizational Project-enabling Processes: This process category enables the technical management processes
and technical processes. The MBPLE implementation shall also consider their adaptation within the enterprise. For
instance, the “life cycle model management” process should be adapted to describe the life cycle models for both
the product line and its member products. Other processes in this category, such as “infrastructure management,”
“portfolio management,” “human resources management,” “Quality Management,” and “knowledge
management,” do not require specific adaptation of the steps to be performed but should be expanded in scope to
cover the product line and its member products. For example, “human resources management” must supply key
personnel with MBPLE competencies for product line development, “quality management” ensures adherence to
internal guidelines and quality standards, “portfolio management” provides inputs to the product line scoping,
“infrastructure management” provides the MBPLE tool chain, and “knowledge management” facilitates the reuse
of feature models, feature configurations, and shared assets, as well as the collection and sharing of learning
materials and lessons learned for the product line development.
Technical Management Processes: This category covers the management aspects of the technical processes,
which are also impacted by MBPLE. For example, “project planning” must distinguish between planning for a
product line project and its member products. Similarly, “project assessment and control,” “measurement,” “risk
management,” and “quality assurance” must differentiate between the product line and its derived member
products. The “measurement” process shall define new KPIs specific to product line development, such as the
percentage of commonality across member products. The most impacted processes by MBPLE are “information
management” – distinguishing between shared assets information and product model information- “configuration
management” – impacting all technical processes through the management of changes and configurations of
features, feature configurations, shared assets, and product models- and “decision making” – applied in all MBPLE
steps, such as deciding the best product line architecture, whether to develop specificities for member products, or
when to phase out the product line.
If you are reading this book with the goal of adapting your enterprise processes to an MBPLE approach, we
strongly suggest the readers first to determine whether your organization has already implemented system life
cycle processes. Then, proceed with the guidance provided here, tailoring it to your specific enterprise context. For
those who have not done it yet, we highly recommend reading ISO 15288 (ISO/IEC 2023) and the International
Council on Systems Engineering Handbook (Walden et al. 2023) to gain a comprehensive understanding. As a
famous model-based systems engineering (MBSE) podcast acknowledges at the end of each episode: “Trust us, we
are systems engineers!” (The MBSE Podcast 2024).
References

management.
ISO/IEC (26580: 2021). Software and systems engineering – methods and tools for the feature-based approach to
software and systems product line engineering.
ISO/IEC/IEEE (15288: 2023). Systems and software engineering – system life cycle processes.
