OceanofPDF.com
Chapter 25
MBPLE Methods
25.1 Introduction
Chapter 23 has already addressed the topic of model-based product line
engineering (MBPLE) methods and presented takeaways. In this chapter,
we will explore the topic in more depth.
First, Section 25.2 defines the concept of method as it is understood in this
book and distinguishes it from other concepts such as process and
methodology. The following Section 25.3 introduces the distinction
between general and specific methods based on OMG’s model-driven
architecture (MDA) (OMG 2014). Finally, Section 25.4 shows how
organization- and tool-specific methods can be developed using an MBPLE
method’s task example.
25.2 Methodology, Process, Method, and
Tool
Methods describe how to perform a task. For example, a description of how
to model a feature tree. They should not be confused with processes that
describe “what” needs to be done but not “how.” For instance, an MBPLE
process can describe that a feature model shall be developed to specify the
variability of the product line but not how the feature model is defined.
Tools help to carry out the methods efficiently. While you could draw the
feature tree on a piece of paper, being considered a sort of “tools,” they are
probably not the best choice for this engineering task. You should use a
feature modeling tool that is much more effective, particularly in managing
features and connecting them with shared assets.
Methodologies are collections of processes, methods, and tools. The
definitions of the terms process, method, tool, and methodology are based
on Estefan and Weilkiens (2020), illustrated in the concept model in Figure
25.1. Not shown, but not to be forgotten, are the “actors” who execute the
process methods and use the tools. A methodology references one to many
processes. Each process references one to many methods and a method is
linked with one to many tools. While processes are rather independent of
methodologies, and methods are rather independent of processes, tool can
be specific for methods. Therefore, the relationship between method and
tool is bidirectional.
Figure 25.1 Concept model methodology, process, method, and tool.
25.3 Independent and Specific Methods
This book describes the MBPLE process and methods, particularly in Part
III. Of course, this is helpful, but it is insufficient to implement in a
concrete organization or project. Part V presents various industrial MBPLE
cases from different organizations. As you read, you will revisit many
concepts covered in other book chapters. However, these concepts have
been adapted and supplemented, with some parts omitted. The book, along
with many other publications, remains independent of any specific
organization’s needs or tool features.
In line with the MDA (OMG 2014), methods can be divided into
organization-independent methods (OIM) and organization-specific
methods (OSM), as well as tool-independent methods (TIM), and tool-
specific methods (TSM).
Figure 25.2 provides an overview and lists the combinations of method
kinds. OIM and TIM are found in this book and other books and standards.
OIM and TSM are, for example, described in handbooks provided by
(MB)PLE tool vendors. They are tool-specific but organization-independent
methods.
Figure 25.2 Independent and specific methods.
In some organizations, OSMs are defined in cross-organizational method
descriptions and developed and maintained, most of the time, by
engineering methodology departments. Besides expertise, this type of
organization unit also needs the power to define cross-organizational
methods. They are typically responsible for the organization’s processes and
tools landscape to ensure their harmonization and governance within the
organization. It is a balancing act between standardization, individual needs,
and freedom for the engineers and projects.
Organizations should customize the processes and methods from books,
standards, and other sources to their specific needs. The primary goal of an
organization is not to perfectly implement the published methods but to
develop successful products. The adaptation affects the methods rather than
the processes. The how (methods) varies more in organizations than the
what (processes).
Developing OSMs and TSMs is not a one-off task but a continuous process.
Changes in requirements triggered by various entities such as organizations,
markets, new technologies, and project feedback must be regularly
incorporated into the OSMs/TSMs (Figure 25.3). If necessary, variants of
the methods shall also be considered in different areas of the organization.
The MBPLE concepts can also be used here to implement a “method
product line.” For example, the specific “member methods” can be
documented in a project cookbook. This is where the OSMs and TSMs
come together (Figure 25.2). Of course, it is not a clear-cut distinction, and
TSMs can also be developed organization-wide.
Figure 25.3 Continuous methods improvement.
25.4 Method Development
Methods can be developed similarly to a systems engineering project: there
are, besides others, requirements, a number of external stakeholders, use
cases with inputs and outputs, and a solution architecture or collection of
methods derived from these. The OIM, as described in this book, is a
reference architecture to which the solution architecture is aligned. The
required tools can then be derived, and the TSMs can be developed based
on the OSMs. However, the tools are often available in practice before the
OSMs are developed.
Figure 25.4 shows a context diagram providing some sketches as an
example for OSM/TSMs for MBPLE. Of course, many more roles and
external systems should have been listed, but the scope is kept short for the
sake of simplicity.
Figure 25.4 MBPLE OSMs/TSMs context.
Based on the identified actors in the context, the use cases of the actors can
now be identified and described. This is typically an iterative process. The
use case analysis leads to further actors, who in turn lead to further use
cases, and so on.
The actor “product management” has the use case “show impact of a
feature change,” among others (Figure 25.5). In the interview with product
management, the following is outlined: the feature model is displayed in the
web-based PLE dashboard. The product management marks a feature that is
to be changed, which can also mean adding a new sub-feature or deleting a
feature. The product management then receives a list of all affected shared
assets, including their status, to estimate the scope of the change and see
which departments should be involved in the planning.
Figure 25.5 MBPLE use case “show impact of a feature change.”
The scenario implies that the engineering models and the modeling
environment are set in a way that the question of impact can also be
answered. As described in Part III, the features are linked to shared assets,
including systems modeling language (SysML) models. One SysML model
represents the backbone of the product line and specifies how all the assets
are linked together, including the references to more specific engineering
tools like Mechanical Computer-Aided Design (MCAD), Electronic
Computer-Aided Design (ECAD), or software engineering tools.
The product management request can be addressed as follows: the feature in
question is linked to shared assets in the backbone SysML model. From
there, further linked shared assets, including references in other models, can
be identified. The devil is then in the details, requiring clarification of
which traceability paths to follow and to what extent.
This primarily concerns OSMs. TSMs describe how the features and shared
assets in different modeling tools are technically connected with each other.
The output of the impact analysis is a list of impacted assets. The
organization-specific description of the output includes properties like
name, location, responsible organization unit, and status. This implies that
methods exist to create this information. In particular, a shared asset must
be linked with an organization unit and status. Shared assets are stored
using different engineering tools, but all should use the same set of
organization units and status kinds. You need an agreement across the
organization on these sets and a technical solution to store them in a single
location that all shared assets engineering tools can access.
This simple example shows what is involved in a simple product
management use case. It is important to specify the methods well and to
master their implementation, as they minimize the many small hurdles of
day-to-day project work and make the processes more effective. Otherwise,
improvisation occurs, many meetings are called to overcome the hurdles,
things are done twice, misunderstandings lead to conflicts, and so on.
References
Estefan, J.A. and Weilkiens, T. (2020). MBSE methodologies. In:
Handbook of Model-Based Systems Engineering (eds. A.M. Madni, N.
Augustine and M. Sievers). Cham: Springer International Publishing. 1–
40. Available at: doi: 10.1007/978-3-030-27486-3_12-1.
OMG (2014). ‘Model Driven Architecture (MDA) MDA Guide rev. 20.0,
ormsc/2014-06-01’. Object Management Group.
OceanofPDF.com
Chapter 26
MBPLE Information Model
26.1 Introduction
This chapter dives into the detailed structure and semantics of variability
concepts within the model-based product line engineering (MBPLE)
context. Building on the concepts presented in Chapter 9, the abstract
syntax of these concepts is formulated in this chapter. It is intended for
advanced users who want to dive deeper into the definition of the concepts,
for example, to implement them themselves in modeling languages or tools.
The information model is defined by using a subset of Systems Modeling
Language (SysML) v1 modeling of blocks. Other modeling languages (e.g.
UML) can also be used to specify concepts. As we primarily use SysML in
this book, we also use it for the information model.
The chapter begins with an introduction to information modeling in Section
26.2, explaining the model elements used to define the semantics and
abstract syntax of domain-specific terms. It then aligns its structure with the
one presented in Chapter 9, illustrating the MBPLE information model
coherently and sequentially (from Sections 26.2 to 26.10).
Key elements include detailed explanations and visual representations of
the MBPLE information model, encompassing feature models and the
details of features, including their identifiers, descriptions, and binding
times. It explores feature constraints, explaining how they govern the
relationships and conditions among features within a model.
Additionally, this chapter specifies the concept of feature configurations,
emphasizing their role in ensuring compliance with feature constraints
within a selected feature set. It also explores the use of the Object
Constraint Language (OCL) for the formal expression and validation of
these constraints (OMG 2014).
The chapter discusses shared assets within the MBPLE information model,
outlining their roles and classifications and differentiating between core
elements, variants, and variations. It also addresses the concept of variant
configurations, highlighting them as a unique type of shared asset.
Finally, the information model covers the product portfolio and member
products of a product line.
26.2 How to Read an Information Model with
SysML
An information model – also known as a domain, concept, or domain
knowledge model (Weilkiens 2020) – defines the semantics and abstract
syntax of terms in a specific domain. For example, the Systems Engineering
Book of Knowledge provides an example of a systems engineering concept
model (SECM) about high-level systems engineering concepts (SEBoK
Authors 2021).
If you are already familiar with SysML, you can skip this section. The
following briefly explains some SysML basics used for the information
model.
The semantics of a term is typically informally specified by text in natural
language. The information model specifies the structure of a term and its
relationships to other terms. Figure 26.1 uses a subset of SysML v1 block
model elements with a simple example. A concept is represented by a
SysML block. The properties of the concept, if any, are modeled by value
properties and listed in a separate compartment with the textual syntax
“name: Type,” whereby the type specification is optional. The type is a
value type like Boolean, string, or an enumeration.
Figure 26.1 Example information model.
In SysML, a “generalization relationship” describes a connection between a
more detailed, specific concept and a broad, general concept. The specific
concept inherits all the characteristics of the general concept and may also
have its own or change some of the inherited ones. The generalization
relationship is noted as an arrow with a closed arrowhead.
In SysML, a composite relationship means that a concept consists of several
parts that are defined by another concept. In contrast, a reference
relationship is similar but implies a reference to another concept rather than
ownership of it. At the end of this relationship is a property name, and the
number of objects involved is specified by a range, written as
[lowerBound..upperBound], which defines the minimum and maximum
number of objects that can be contained in this relationship. The composite
relationship is an arrow with an open tip and a black diamond at the other
end. If it is a reference relationship, the diamond is missing.
You can read the information model in Figure 26.1 as follows:
A Car and an unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV) are kinds of a vehicle.
The Car owns four wheels specified by the concept Wheel and has a
horsepower value property.
The Car also references an optional driver specified by the concept
Person.
The UAV has a value property maxFlightDuration typed by the value
type Integer.
The Quadcopter is a special kind of UAV. It owns four engines defined
by the concept Engine.
The Engine has a value property specifying the rotating direction of
the propeller. The type of the value property is an enumeration.
The Quadcopter also references an optional remote pilot defined by the
Person concept.
The Quadcopter has a constraint that ensures the remote pilot is
present if the quadcopter is in the air. The constraint is specified using
a natural language expression. It can also be specified more formally
with the OCL (OMG 2014).
The Quadcopter’s value property totalMass has a “/” prefix, which tags
it as a derived property. Its value can be derived from other
information in the model, which is not shown here.
In the following section, we will use this kind of modeling to specify the
MBPLE information model.
26.3 Product Line
The product line is the overarching element representing a family of
products, which includes a variability model, shared assets supersets,
product models, and member products (within a product portfolio; Figure
26.2).
Figure 26.2 Information model: product line.
26.4 Variability Model
The variability model is the overarching concept containing a feature model
and a set of feature configurations (Figure 26.3). It, therefore, contains all
the information about variability in the product line, including the feature
selections for the planned member products of the product portfolio.
Figure 26.3 Information model: variability model.
26.5 Feature Model
The feature model specifies a product line’s variability. It has a name and
owns the root feature of the set of features, which is also referenced as a
derived union (Figure 26.4). The features are organized in a hierarchical
tree structure. The root feature is the feature from which the tree spans the
subfeatures. The derived property “features” is the set of all features
contained in the feature tree. It is derived from the subfeature relationships
explained further as follows.
Figure 26.4 Information model: feature model.
Feature models are also often called feature trees. The terms can be used
synonymously. Strictly speaking, the feature model is the repository (i.e. the
model data), and the feature tree is a view of the model.
A feature is a product’s characteristic that differentiates it from other
products within the same product line. It has an identifier, a name, a
description, and a binding time. The identifier shall be unique within the
feature model. The binding time is a value from the enumeration value type
“LifeCycleKind.”
A feature can reference another feature. It can bind features about the same
variability in different areas of a feature tree or reuse a feature in another
context. The referenced feature can also be a feature or the complete tree of
a different feature model.
A feature can have subfeatures specified by a subfeature relationship in the
information model (Figure 26.5). The derived Boolean property “isEnd”
indicates if subfeatures are defined or if this feature is a leaf.
Figure 26.5 Information model: subfeatures.
The subfeature relationship connects a feature (the master) with one
subfeature. The property “subFeature” is derived and represents an owned
or referenced subfeature. Exactly one of these two kinds must be set. The
derived properties “isOwned” and “isReferenced” directly show the kind of
subfeature relationship. A feature can have many subfeature relationships.
The relationship specifies the lower and upper cardinality of the subfeature.
The default is a lower and upper cardinality of 1. If the lower cardinality is
zero, the subfeature is optional. If it is greater than zero, the subfeature is
mandatory. The properties “isOptional” and “isMandatory” are derived
from the cardinality values. If the upper cardinality is greater than 1, more
than one of the subfeatures can be selected by a feature configuration.
The derived properties “subFeatures” and “parent” are derived from the
property “subFeatureRelationships.”
26.6 Feature Constraint
A feature constraint specifies an expression about features that must always
be true in the context of a feature configuration. The expression is specified
by the operation “constraintExpression(),” which returns true if the
expression is true for a given feature configuration (Figure 26.6). The
feature constraint involves at least two features whereby one could be
specified as the source element if the expression is directed. The source
element is the first feature in the ordered set of referenced features.
Figure 26.6 Information model: feature constraint.
The “conflicts” constraint is a special feature constraint that redefines the
operation “constraintExpression().” It specifies that the features are in
conflict with each other and that only one of them can be a member of the
given feature configuration. The constraint is specified by using the OCL
(OCL v2.4, 2014). OCL is a formal language used to describe expressions
on models. The OCL expression for the conflict feature constraint is as
follows.
features->select( f | fCfg.selectedFeatures->includes(f)
)->size() <= 1
In natural language, the expression means checking all features in the set
“features” and selecting those features that are a member of the set
“selectedFeatures“ of the given feature configuration. There may be only
zero or one feature in the selection.
The “requires” constraint is a special feature constraint that also redefines
the operation “constraintExpression(),” specifying that a feature
configuration must select all other features if the source feature is part of
the feature configuration. The OCL statement is as follows:
if fCfg.selectedFeatures->includes(features-
>first()) then
fCfg->includesAll(features)
else
false
Endif
26.7 Feature Group
A feature can have a group of alternative subfeatures. The feature group
constrains the cardinality of subfeatures (Figure 26.7). The feature group
specifies a lower and upper cardinality. The OCL statement of the
constraintExpression() operation is defined as follows.
lowerCardinality <= subFeatureRelationships-
>collect(feature | subFeature)->select(
f | fCfg.selectedFeatures->includes(f))->size() <=
upperCardinality
Figure 26.7 Information model: alternative feature group.
If the upper cardinality is 1, it is an XOR group – also known as an
alternative. That means exactly one of the group’s features can be selected.
If the upper cardinality is unlimited, it is an OR group. That means you can
select any of the features. The lower cardinality controls that at least one
and the maximum allowed number of features are selected within the group.
26.8 Feature Configuration
A feature configuration is part of a variability model and specifies a valid
set of selected features of a feature model for a member product. A feature
configuration has a name and references a set of selected features (Figure
26.8). The constraint of the feature configuration assures that all feature
constraints that cover selected features are met.
The constraint expression given in OCL is as follows.
selectedFeatures->forAll( f |
f.bindingTime.isEqualOrBefore(lifeCycleStage) a
nd f.featureConstraints-
>forAll(fc.constraintExpression(self)) and
f.parentRelationships->forAll(
sfr | sfr.lowerCardinality <=
selectedFe
atures->including(f)->size()
<= sfr.upp
erCardinality)))
Figure 26.8 Information model: feature configuration.
26.9 Shared Assets
A shared asset is a digital artifact that contributes to the architecting, design,
fabrication, operation, maintenance, and retirement of a product (and its
components). It can be a member of one or more shared assets supersets.
The shared assets superset can have a name and own shared assets that
specify the products (Figure 26.9).
Figure 26.9 Information model: shared assets superset.
A shared asset can also have a name. All other properties are outside the
scope of MBPLE and are defined by the specific engineering language of
the shared asset, such as SysML, CAD, and so forth.
The shared assets are distinguished between core elements and variants. A
core element is used for all product line products, whereas a variant is only
used for specific products.
Variation is a special core element, also known as a variation point. It is
where variability happens in the system. The nature of this variation is
inherently abstract, requiring a specific design decision to be made
concrete. The design decisions are represented by the variants related to the
variation (Figure 26.9).
A variation implements the feature that specifies the variability, and the
variants implement the appropriate subfeatures (Figure 26.10). This
scenario is also depicted in Chapter 9 in Figure 9.7.
Figure 26.10 Information model: shared assets/features relationships.
26.10 Product Model and Member Product
A product model is a specification of a member product. It refers to the
corresponding feature configuration. It is derived from shared assets,
including the core elements and the variation and variant elements, which
can be derived from the relationship to the feature configuration. The
product model can still contain variability. Variations with binding time
“Production” or “Operation” have not yet been resolved. Therefore, many
member products can refer to a product model (also known as product asset
instance [ISO/IEC 2021]).
A member product is a concrete physical product specified by a product
model that is part of an organization’s product portfolio. Figure 26.11 shows
the definition of the “Product Model” concept.
Figure 26.11 Information model: member product.

ISO/IEC (2021). ISO/IEC 26580: 2021 Software and systems engineering –
methods and tools for the feature-based approach to software and
systems product line engineering. ISO/IEC:
https://www.iso.org/standard/43139.html.
Object Management Group (OMG) (2014). Object Constraint Language
Version 2.4, formal/2014–02–03. Object Management Group.
SEBoK Authors (2021). Systems engineering core concepts:
https://sebokwiki.org/w/index.php?
title=Systems_Engineering_Core_Concepts&oldid=67939 (accessed 15
June 2023).
OceanofPDF.com
Chapter 27
MBPLE Tool Chain
27.1 Introduction
In this chapter, we explore the concept of model-based product line
engineering tool chain, focusing on its various components and their roles
in the adoption of MBPLE. Figure 27.1 illustrates five distinct categories of
MBPLE tool chain components, providing clarity, particularly for those
new to this subject. Additionally, for each solution category, the key
capabilities related to MBPLE, the existing commercial and open-source
tools, as well as the gaps, are presented.
Figure 27.1 MBPLE tool chain categories.
In Section 27.2, we begin with the product line engineering (PLE) solution,
a cornerstone of any MBPLE tool chain. This component allows teams to
define and manage feature models and configurations, ensuring consistency
across the many variations of a member product. It acts as the “engine” of
the MBPLE tool chain.
Next, in Section 27.3, we shift our attention to the model-based systems
engineering (MBSE) solution, particularly highlighting SysML v2 (Object
Management Group [OMG] 2024). This transformative standard introduces
enhanced variability modeling capabilities into system modeling. While the
full transition to SysML v2 is ongoing, it is important to discuss the current
implementation of SysML, its integration, and its limitations concerning
MBPLE.
In Section 27.4, our discussion then moves to the application lifecycle
management (ALM) solution, detailing how it has evolved to integrate with
both PLE and MBSE tools across different tool vendors. This integration is
crucial for managing the life cycle of software-intensive components within
product lines, covering everything from initial design to their continuous
evolution.
In Section 27.5, we explore the product lifecycle management (PLM)
solution, which is central to the digital transformation of engineering and
manufacturing. Integrating PLE solutions into the PLM domain facilitates
the management and continuity of complex product data and variability.
However, there are still overlaps with legacy variant management solutions
that are embedded in PLM systems.
In Section 27.6, the conversation turns to the configuration management
(CM) solution, which plays a vital role in tracking and managing changes
and variations across the product line. This segment highlights the necessity
of a unified configuration management strategy to ensure coherence and
integrity across all product line components.
We conclude the chapter with Section 27.7 by looking forward to the future
evolution of the MBPLE tool chain and its landscape, anticipating how
current trends and new solutions might shape its development.
27.2 PLE Solution
The PLE solution is the “engine” of an MBPLE tool chain since without it
there will not be an MBPLE approach. It shall provide four main basic
capabilities to the users:
1. Defining and managing feature models.
2. Defining and managing feature configurations.
3. Linking mechanism between variable assets and features.
4. Transforming the shared assets consistently in the domain-specific
tools.
A PLE tool usually distinguishes between feature models and shared assets.
Since the definition of variability and its capture into feature models is an
iterative process and includes multiple system hierarchy levels, PLE tools
also offer two additional capabilities:
5. Creating partial feature configurations, assuming that the user has not
chosen all features at a specific development stage but only part of
them.
6. Structuring and managing feature models and configurations at
different hierarchy levels (e.g. system, subsystem, components, etc.)
PLE solutions automatically check if the chosen features are allowed in the
feature configurations. This means checking if the selected features meet all
the required relationships and constraints defined in the feature model.
Since PLE tools shall be accessed by all the stakeholders who contribute
into the development of the product line, going from the marketing team to
the system developers, it is beneficial to provide a web interface with
limited functionalities and a client interface for those that are working hand-
on with features and shared assets.
Finally, the integration with other engineering domains shall be facilitated
by open API or support of standards like OSLC (OSLC 2021) and MoSSEC
(ISO 2021). This is a prerequisite for the realization of an end-to-end
MBPLE tool chain.
PLE tools: Today there are mainly two PLE alternative commercial
solutions in the market, pure::variants (PTC pure::variants 2024), recently
acquired by PTC and Gears (BigLever 2024) by BigLever Software. Due to
the lack of a standard variability language and variability exchange
language, those two solutions set their own standards and guidelines to
adopt a PLE approach within several industries. While Figure 27.2 shows
an example of a feature model in PTC pure::variants, Figure 27.3 shows the
same example in Gears. In Chapter 13, the open-source solution called
FeatureIDE (METOP 2020) was introduced. Similar to pure::variants,
which is based on Eclipse, FeatureIDE offers capabilities more oriented
toward research usage rather than full-scale organizational adoption. More
information about these tools can be found on their websites or in several
existing publications.
Figure 27.2 Example of feature model in PTC pure::variants.
Figure 27.3 Example of feature model in Gears BigLever Software.
Gaps: PLE tools are advanced and set the standard for the discipline.
Without them we would have not probably had the industrial application of
PLE. However, one of the gaps that those solutions have is the lack of a full
modeling experience. This does not mean that feature models are not
models, since they resemble the three main characteristics highlighted in
Chapter 10 (MBEML-1, MBEML-2, and MBEML-3) and provide the
capability to link feature and model elements, including the generation of a
product model from the shared assets supersets. The gap mainly concerns,
for instance, the act of modeling features similarly to SysML or CAD tools
as well as the possibility to create different views of the same feature
model.
This is probably due to the fact that those solutions were conceived to
support PLE mainly in software development and still strongly rely on their
original technology. What they provide is more a graphical representation
of the feature model with limited modeling capability.
This gap was first identified by Chami et al. (2017) in their work “Model-
based variability solution with SysML” where they used SysML and a
specific profile extension to model the features and their configurations.
Forlingieri and Weilkiens (2022) proposed a similar approach, named
System Feature Modeling (SysFM) illustrated in Figure 27.4, in
continuation with the approach started by Forlingieri at Bombardier
Transportation, but exploring its coexistence with the main PLE tools. It is
important to highlight, once more, that SysML tools are not PLE tools. We
strongly recommend limiting the usage of SysML only for capturing the
variability in feature models and defining the feature configurations in those
organizations where MBSE is predominant to facilitate user acceptance.
Those organizations shall enable in a second stage the critical capability to
import or, even better, synchronize the feature models and configurations in
a PLE tool. In fact, in both Bombardier Transportation and Airbus’s cases,
feature models and configuration, after being defined in the SysML tool,
were imported into a dedicated PLE tool.
Figure 27.4 SysFM example with SysML.
Source: Adapted from Forlingieri et al., 2022 / John Wiley & Sons.
Another gap is more on the consistency and management of the
transformation from the shared assed into the product model across
different domains. Due to the multiple commercial solutions that can
integrate with PLE tools, it is hard to achieve a consistent and standard way
to manage the transformation. This challenge can be partially addressed by
implementing integration solutions based on interoperability standards
rather than open or proprietary APIs, as discussed in Chapters 6 and 18.
27.3 MBSE Solution
Without an MBSE approach, the “MB” part of MBPLE is unachievable. As
noted, MBPLE does not only handle variations in system models; it
integrates a broad range of engineering assets holistically. Yet, SysML
models are crucial for ensuring the consistent implementation of variability
across different engineering domains. These models essentially define how
variability is structured across shared assets. An MBSE solution for
MBPLE should at least include:
1. Establishing dependencies and constraints between Feature-SysML
models.
2. Linking with PLE tools to display feature models and their
configurations.
3. Filtering and previewing the outcomes of feature configurations in the
SysML model.
4. Transforming SysML shared assets into specific product models.
Such capabilities are also mentioned for the PLE solution. However, the
MBSE solution should be able to mirror these capabilities to allow the
interoperability on the MBSE side, such as, for instance, creating
dependencies from the MBSE between a model element and a feature. A
key additional capabilities that is not only relevant to MBPLE is:
5. Versioning and control of the SysML models (also part of the CM
solution)
It is important to note that SysML was not designed for modeling product
lines but for systems, which makes it unsuitable for direct application to
product lines without adaptations. Although SysML v1 supports a wide
array of systems engineering tasks, it lacks elements for modeling the
product lines, such as features (Forlingieri and Weilkiens 2022). SysML v2
Notation (OMG 2024) bridges this gap.
MBSE tools: Since the advent of SysML, SysML v1 tools like Rhapsody
by IBM (IBM Rhapsody 2024), MagicDraw (also known as Cameo System
Modeler) by Dassault Systemes (Cameo 2024), Enterprise Architect by
Sparx Systems (Sparx 2024), and Integrity Modeller (now PTC Modeller)
by PTC (PTC Modeler 2024) have added SysML capabilities to their UML
modeling features. Except for Enterprise Architect, the other three are also
embedded as part of the ALM solution of IBM and PTC and the PLM
solution of Dassault Systemes and PTC, respectively. All the presented
MBSE solutions are connected with PLE tools and allow the synergy
between PLE and MBSE. Several documents and publications about each
solution and their SysML-specific capabilities exist, so this is not the place
to further detail them (tool examples are not shown on purpose). The open-
source scene is mainly dominated by Capella by Obeo (Capella 2024),
which is not based on the SysML language. It also includes other tools like
Papyrus (Papyrus 2024), which is SysML-based.
Gaps: As mentioned in the previous paragraph, SysML v1 and its tools
were not developed with the intention of modeling variability, which
presents challenges such as creating a product line architecture that is both
valid in SysML and conceptually well-formed according to system
semantics (Forlingieri and Weilkiens 2022). However, to address this
challenge, the latest version of SysML, SysML v2, introduces specific
concepts for modeling variability, as shown in Chapter 15. This provides a
consistent foundation for developing tools and methods to model variability
in the system model.
In Section 27.2, an important gap is identified: the lack of a full modeling
experience for feature and configuration definition. This has pushed some
MBSE tool vendors to integrate PLE functionalities within their system
modeling tools. While this may appear as a solution for projects with a
limited scope and few engineering assets, it is difficult to scale for large
organizations where the PLE tool should not depend on other domains, but
rather being holistic. We should avoid the paradox where MBSE, intended
to break down silos in engineering and enhance collaboration, instead
creates a silo for MBPLE!
27.4 ALM Solution
PLE solutions started to be used in software development already at the
beginning of the 2000s. This means that since their birth, they had to be
integrated with ALM solutions, originally used only in developing software.
This includes, but it is not limited to, requirements management, test
management, and source code management disciplines.
As mentioned before, MBSE solutions can be also considered as part of an
ALM solution if integrated. Interoperability standards like OSLC support
primarily the links between the solutions that are part of the ALM domain
and also the connection with the PLE solution.
The ALM solution shall provide the following minimal capabilities to
support MBPLE:
1. Linking with PLE tools to display feature models and their
configurations.
2. Filtering and previewing the outcomes of feature configurations in the
ALM assets such as requirements, test cases, and source codes.
3. Transforming ALM shared assets into specific product models
consistently.
Additional capabilities that are not only relevant to MBPLE are:
4. Integrating with an MBSE solution
5. Integration with PLM tool chain
6. Versioning and control of the ALM assets (also part of the CM
solution).
ALM tool chains: In the last decades, several solutions entered the ALM
arena; however, for simplicity we mention here just the most popular that,
we believe, cover around 90% of the market. The Engineering Lifecycle
Management by IBM (IBM ELM 2024) offers traditional ALM capabilities
and integration with the main PLE solutions. The same for Codebeamer by
PTC (PTC Codebeamer 2024), which since 2022 owns one of the two PLE
solutions, pure::variants, and provides similar capabilities. Polarion by
Siemens (Siemens Polarion 2024) also covers the same capability span. All
three ALM tool chains also have integration with one or multiple MBSE
solutions and provide similar version and control functionalities. As an
open-source solution, it is worth mentioning the combination of the
Atlassian family (Atlassian 2024) with Jira, Confluence, XRay, and the
GitHub (GitHub 2024) flavors that offer “light” ALM capabilities. We do
not show any examples of the mentioned tools since the specific ALM tools
are not central to this book.
Gaps: Common gaps are usually related to the limited model-based
capabilities and the complexity of their usage. For instance, even if they
integrate with MBSE solutions, their integration is often based on heavy
synchronization approaches between the MBSE assets and other ALM
assets, or it is not as smooth as linking a requirement element with a system
element inside a SysML tool, being both part of the same model. Even
when the integration is built on OSLC, it is not always fluid and user-
friendly. Surprisingly the ALM integration is often more straightforward
and functional with the PLE solutions, even if provided by other vendors.
The main limitation for both ALM and PLM solutions, as it will be
discussed in the next paragraph, consists of the tool vendor lock-in effects.
In fact, due to the complex architecture and integration challenges among
ALM tool chains, it is in theory possible to have ALM tools from different
vendors, but in practice hard to realize.
27.5 PLM Solution
PLM tool chains have rapidly become a fundamental pillar of engineering
and manufacturing as well as a central component of digital transformation
across various industries. Serving as the critical collaboration platform,
PLM ensures digital continuity and links all related product data, including
MCAD, ECAD, BOMs, simulations, and more.
With the integration of PLE solutions into the PLM domain, it has become
easier to define and manage the variability of bills of materials, MCAD
models, and ECAD models.
Historically, PLM systems typically utilize built-in variant management
solutions that are not feature-based or PLE-based.
The modern PLM solution shall provide the following minimal capabilities
to support MBPLE:
1. Linking with PLE tools to display feature models and their
configurations, similar to the ALM solution.
2. Filtering and previewing the outcomes of feature configurations in the
PLM assets such as MCAD, ECAD, and BOMs.
3. Transforming PLM shared assets into specific product models
consistently, similar to the ALM solution.
Additional capabilities that are not only relevant to MBPLE are:
4. Integrating with an MBSE solution
5. Integration with PLM tool chain.
6. Versioning and control of the PLM assets (also part of the CM
solution).
PLM tool chains: It is possible to list three main commercial tool chains
that, we believe, cover a big part of the PLM market. These are Windchill
by PTC (PTC Windchill 2024), Teamcenter by Siemens (Siemens
Teamcenter 2024), and 3DExperience (Dassault Systemes 2024) by
Dassault Systemes. All three platforms provide the aforementioned PLM
capabilities and have integration with the main PLE tools. As mentioned
before, sometimes they provide overlapping or conflicting functionalities
with the PLE tools, but except for a few cases this trend is changing since
the main vendors support more and more the PLE-based approach to
develop product lines. A clear example of it is the recent acquisition of
pure::variants by PTC. Due to the complexity of PLM tool chains and the
capability to scale to full enterprise, no open-source holistic PLM solutions
exist. In line with the previous sections, we do not display any examples of
the mentioned tools.
Gaps: Two main gaps are worth mentioning here. The first is the lack of
PLM and ALM tool chain integration. Even if more and more organizations
aim at achieving digital continuity along the entire development lifecycle,
the definition and management of variability for a product line that includes
ALM and PLM assets still remains a challenge. Sometimes partial
integration exists. Alternatively, companies can still organize their shared
assets in the two distinctive tool chains. Having the PLE solution and the
MBSE solution as integration points between the two.
The second main gap is related to the overlapping variant management
legacy capabilities of PLM solutions that sometimes conflict with the PLE
mechanism. In fact, having variant management capability in the PLE
solution does not mean that the solution follows a PLE-based approach
guided by the IS0 26580 (ISO/IEC 2021).
27.6 Configuration Management Solution
As shown in Chapter 19, CM provides the capability to reuse engineering
assets of different domains with different lifecycles status. All engineering
assets, ALM or PLM, can be reused as well as configured from a single
source of truth for different versions and variants. Each of these information
sources possesses its own configuration identification specific to its domain
and lifecycle. One of the primary challenges is integrating all these different
assets of the product line into common and consistent shared assets
configurations. This is the consequence of the coexistence of several
domain-specific CM systems.
A CM solution shall support MBPLE with the following capabilities:
1. Integrating with a PLE solution and working in synergy with it.
2. Accessing the repositories where the MBSE, ALM, and PLM shared
assets are stored and querying the data in the configurations, keeping
the authoring tools responsible for changes in the assets.
3. Identifying which is the common and variant part of the product model
within the configuration of each member product.
4. Creating product models from the shared assets that are consistent and
in line with the feature configurations and model transformation.
A CM solution is not limited to the capabilities mentioned earlier, but these
are key for the implementation of the MBPLE tool chain.
Gaps: Configuration management solutions, often referred to as version
control solutions, commonly utilize a method known as branching. This
involves creating a copy of a set of assets that then evolves independently
from the original. While branching has several appropriate uses, it is
unsuitable as the primary method for managing variations within a set of
reusable assets (known as variation in space, see Chapter 19 for more
details).
When variations are managed by initially creating branches, it usually
results in unnecessary workload, because the branched asset (e.g. consider a
requirement set) is not entirely modified; a significant portion often remains
unchanged between the original and the branched copy. Unless the
granularity of the versioned assets closely matches the granularity of the
variations, branching in version management is not effective alone for
MBPLE. In fact, version and control management, essential for tracking
changes to the shared assets throughout their lifecycle, should not be
confused with variability and variant management. Hence, often a
configuration management solution is proposed by tool vendors as a way to
manage variability. This is usually due to the lack of understanding of the
MBPLE approach and to the lack of synergies and integration with the PLE
tools that are often seen by organizations as unnecessary if a configuration
and control management solution is already in place. It is key to stress the
fact that product lines similarly to single products require a configuration
management approach, and one is not a substitute for the other. Chapter 19
focuses entirely on the CM topic.
27.7 The Future of the MBPLE Tool Chain
On one hand, it is challenging to predict how companies will embrace the
MBPLE too lchain in the future, as the inherent “variability” makes
predictions nearly impossible. On the other hand, we can identify some
critical factors that will significantly influence the adoption of the MBPLE
tool chain.
First, in recent years, we have observed two main trends that are somewhat
contradictory. There has been the development of more interoperability
standards to facilitate collaboration across tools from different vendors,
such as OSLC and MoSSEC. Concurrently, several acquisitions by large
PLM or ALM vendors of independent PLE and MBSE solutions occurred.
This can lead to a “crossroads” where companies must choose whether to
adopt a multi-vendor approach and focus on integration or to opt for a
single-vendor solution. In an ideal world, the multi-vendor approach would
likely be more advantageous for organizations wanting to implement
MBPLE, but in practice, it can be challenging to execute. We already see
companies opting for a “third way,” where they depend on a primary tool-
vendor platform but maintain some flexibility to integrate third-party
solutions.
Secondly, SysML v2 is considered to be a game-changer not only for
MBSE but also for MBPLE. This is not only because it introduces
variability elements into its language, but primarily due to its open API,
which facilitates the easier integration of the SysML model with variability.
If all vendors adhere to the API specified by the new standard, achieving
digital end-to-end continuity will be simpler. However, due to the novelty
of the language, several open points remain. For instance, it is not yet clear
how SysML v2 asset transformation will be supported by PLE tools after
the introduction of the variant notation. The INCOSE PLE Working Group
(INCOSE PLE WG 2024) is coordinating the establishment of a common
understanding among PLE and MBSE tool vendors for the implementation
of important SysML v2 aspects related to PLE.
Lastly, we hope that the PLE community of experts, driven by the INCOSE
PLE Working Group, will establish a standard language for defining and
exchanging variability data. Similar to SysML v2, a standardized variability
language would enhance the current integration landscape and lead to
greater flexibility in selecting PLE tools within a company’s existing tool
chain.
