Chapter 28
MBPLE Organization
28.1 Introduction
As stated throughout this book, implementing model-based product line
engineering (MBPLE) in an organization entails aligning all sectors,
divisions, and functions around a common delivery approach. While
Chapter 23 presents an overall adoption framework for MBPLE, and
Chapter 24 explains the necessity of adapting the organization to the
“MBPLE way of working” and the need to tailor MBPLE processes to the
context and needs of the organization, this chapter details key
organizational aspects. Section 28.2 presents guidelines to set up and
operate the organization supporting an MBPLE approach. Section 28.3
provides two examples of generic organizational patterns to operate the
model-based product line in different contexts.
The organizational patterns presented below should be considered as
implementation models. In most cases, the organizational changes required
to reach a specific target must be planned and be considered as, or
integrated into, an enterprise transformation program. As a reminder, a
commonsensical approach would be to align the organization and the
architecting approach of the product line, which in turn should be dictated
by the business context and constraints (INCOSE 2019). The chapter closes
with brief presentations of the synergies between agile principles and
MBPLE (Section 28.4) and of the MBPLE adoption team (Section 28.5)
and concludes with an important clarification of the concept of product line
of product lines in Section 28.6.
28.2 Guidelines for Setting Up and Operating
an MBPLE Enterprise
The birth of a product line: In the field of large-scale or high-tech
systems, the development of a product line is generally not performed
through a single customer project, but rather through the execution of
several customer projects (AFIS 2015). The situation is quite different for
commercial products in mass markets, of course. But, in fact, how is a
product line born?
In the general case, the decision to develop a product line is taken after a
series of opportunities versus risks analyses that take into consideration
several aspects, such as:
The feasibility of accessing and capitalizing knowledge from past
customer projects or from customer projects being enforced.
The technical scope covered by these projects to evaluate the similarity
of the delivered solutions.
The identification of achievable competitive advantages or a
groundbreaking concept from data obtained through market analyses
and business intelligence.
The possibility of allocating budget and resources to finance the
development of the product line, which is generally segmented over
time.
The identification of “unknowns” or knowledge gaps in the current
product offer.
The possibility of gathering data and information to support key
decisions.
Giving a reason to live to the product line: In a MBPLE approach, the
business strategy of a company should be formalized in strategic plans and
roadmaps of its product lines, or in similar documents. Roadmaps define
what is required or expected from each product line, for what markets or
customers, and at which point in time. These elements are usually defined
by teams in charge of the strategy and the product policy of the company;
they are reviewed regularly (at the pace of relevance) and are typically
owned by the “head of the product line.” Examples of information
contained in product line strategic plans and roadmaps include the
following:
The current and target positioning of the products in specific markets.
The expected features over time, differentiators to outplay the
competition.
The market segmentation.
The required investments and expected revenues, and foreseeable
volumes of sales and opportunities.
The roadmap of a product line is incarnated in its architecture, captured in
great measure in the MBPLE digital assets (predominantly models). The
roadmap is also supported by the technical roadmaps and policies of the
subsystems and components that constitute the product line. These elements
are usually defined by the architects or the technical authorities of the
product line, and must be managed in a coordinated way.
Feeding the product line: To support the development, deployment,
evolution, and maintenance of the product line, most companies put in place
hybrid funding schemes that combine self-funded and external budgets in a
staged approach. External budgets come from customers that buy (or intend
to buy) products issued from the product line. Even in fully self-funded
developments, investments are generally granted in several stages. So, the
development of the product line is performed thanks to a series of customer
projects and self-funding stages that must be managed in a coordinated way
(that is, within a program). These schemes are very common in market-pull
sectors that are driven by call-for-tender mechanisms.
It must be pointed out that funding mechanisms can evolve over time. The
initial funding sources to launch the development of the product line may
differ from those that will eventually be in place at later life cycle stages.
Typical funding mechanisms include the following:
Initial funding (product line launch):
Full or gradual self-funding for upfront development of the product
line.
Funding by a major customer within a product line “launch”
project.
Joint self- and customer-funded development.
Maintenance and upgrade funding (product line “run” mode)
Pro-rata contribution by customer projects to the evolution and
maintenance of the product line.
One single financial flow centralized and managed by the product
line development program.
Raising the product line: Whatever the organizational pattern, one of the
best practices in the industry is to organize the development of the product
line as a whole, as if it were a single entity, despite the fact that the product
line roadmap might contain unknown aspects, and to provide enough
degrees of freedom to allow the evolution of the development plan over
time. For this, it is highly appropriate to organize the implementation of the
strategy of the product line and the execution of the product line roadmap
within a coordinated, single endeavor. This endeavor is usually referred to
as the “product line development program,” which is accountable for the
overall profit and loss statements of the product line (in the mid- and long
terms) and is usually managed by the “head of the product line program.”
Such a programmatic approach is paramount for managing the life cycle of
the product line and for coordinating in a consistent manner the delivery of
the products of the product line.
It provides indeed a consistent framework for strategic planning,
organizational setup, governance, and day-to-day operations.
A product line development program is the most appropriate structure to
address diligently the potential conflicts between long-term strategic
objectives (i.e. the expected profitability achieved through the realization of
the roadmap of the product line) and short-term objectives (i.e. the
revenues, cash, and profit and loss achieved through the execution of
customer contracts). In fact, this programmatic approach allows to align and
connect in an effective manner a company’s strategy, product policy,
program management, offers and projects management, architecting and
engineering, as well as the financial aspects of the product line, as covered
in Chapter 21.
28.3 Examples of Organizational Patterns
The guidelines exposed above should allow a company to define the
principles that will frame the definition and governance of its MBPLE-
based enterprise. However, since the number of different possible MBPLE
organizations can be huge, in this section we propose two generic
organizational patterns to which the reader can refer to, depending on the
reader’s particular business context.
In order to better understand the organizational patterns presented below, let
us first take a slightly different look at an MBPLE enterprise based on the
principles exposed in the previous section. Figure 28.1 shows the
dependencies of different categories of actors in the MBPLE enterprise.
Figure 28.1 A different look at an MBPLE-oriented enterprise.
Actors at the business level can be considered as a circle of investors who
have an interest in the product line; they are the internal customers of the
product line program. Actors in the product line program and in the
development teams coordinated by the program can be considered as the
entrepreneurs of the product line; they develop, promote, and require
sponsorship and financing from internal customers. Finally, the
development of the product line must be supported by capabilities provided
by an MBPLE adoption team, like the one presented in Section 28.5
hereafter.
Figure 28.2 below shows the first generic organizational pattern for rather
vertical businesses and highly integrated product line teams. This figure
covers only the highest level of the product line. In this pattern, the overall
authority of the product line is in the hands of the “head of the product
line,” who is supported by the “chief architect of the product line” and the
head of the product line program. This line of business is accountable for
the overall profit and loss of the product line and reports directly to the
business authorities, normally at the level of the executive committee of the
company.
Figure 28.2 Organizational pattern at product line level.
The “head of the product line program” oversees the different disciplines
that work within customer projects to deliver products. He also supervises
customer project managers and coordinates the deliveries expected by
customer projects. The head of the product line program is accountable for
the operating gross margin of the product line, which can be seen as the
integration or consolidation of the operating margin of each customer
project.
The second generic organizational pattern shown in Figure 28.3 below
concerns those businesses that put in place a “composable” approach to
deliver the products (or building blocks) of the product line (the approach is
explained in Chapter 3). The figure represents only one of the subsystems
of the product line. In this pattern, “subsystem 1” contributes to three
different product lines and can be seen as an “internal” product line.
Figure 28.3 Organizational pattern at the subsystem level.
The head of Subsystem 1 is accountable for the delivery of the expected
versions of Subsystem 1 (including all necessary assets and documentation)
to the different customer projects within each product line. To compose a
product, the other subsystems of the product line would follow the same
principle and provide the deliveries required by customer projects. In this
pattern, the role of the “product line architects” is paramount to guarantee
that the composed products will possess the emergent properties expected
by the customers. In certain cases, a “chief product line architect” might be
necessary to carry out this work consistently across the different product
lines.
In order to guarantee the consistency of the overall product line
configuration, the MBPLE enterprise requires managing variability at
different levels, across domains, and across disciplines, whatever the
organizational pattern. The deployment of MBPLE into different levels of
the system breakdown structure must be planned carefully and its
implementation must be carried out consistently. The methods and concepts
presented in Chapters 13–16 provide the capabilities to manage
multilevel/multilayer variability in a consistent manner.
28.4 So, What About Agile?
To explain how agile principles can be applied in an MBPLE organization,
we first attempt to draw a simplified picture of the different elements that
allow the MBPLE enterprise to conduct and operate its business.
The application of agile methods is particularly appropriate for the early
stages of the product line life cycle (setting up the scope and architecting
the product line). Agile methods can be applied to define and review the
product line plan and the product line roadmap. Methods and tools
dedicated to value identification and tracking (like value canvasses,
empathy maps, value-pulled developments or key decisions and knowledge
gaps) are extremely useful to identify product line differentiators, rank
architecture concepts of the product line, prioritize product line features and
support arbitration and decision-making.
Agile methods can also be applied during the early design activities of the
product line, like architecture definition or operational analysis (Gregg et al.
2016). But most importantly, agile methods can (should!) be applied to
conduct the development of the very first member products of the product
line. Besides the methods and tools mentioned above, approaches like
iterative developments (not necessarily incremental), product backlog and
sprints, and integrated product teams – in which Integration verification
validation and qualification teams are integrated from the very beginning –
are particularly effective to develop the first member products of the
product line in a consistent manner. The application of agile methods in this
context is also instrumental in “rolling out” and fine-tuning the MBPLE
organization’s methods, tools and way-of-working, and anchoring healthy
agile practices in the genes of MBPLE teams.
At later stages, once the product line is established, agile methods can be
applied to operate the product line in an effective and efficient way. As
explained earlier in the book, in MBPLE product models are “derived”
from the shared asset supersets greatly improving the speed (and quality) of
the development. Here again, value-based agile methods can be used to
perform very efficient gap analyses between new customer needs and the
feature model of the product line, and define the appropriate member
product configuration. Agile methods can of course be used for product line
upgrades or the development of a new product line if required.
There are, however, some points that require particular attention when
applying agile methods in an MBPLE context. First, for well-established
product lines, the emphasis of agile methods should be more on supporting
smart reuse and regular reviews of the value proposition of the product line,
so there is practically no need to do agile “from scratch.” Second, the term
feature must be clarified from the beginning: features have different
meanings in agile development (features are related to services of
functionalities that drive business value and are implemented in sprints) and
in MBPLE (features are differentiating characteristics of a product that are
used to formalize variability).
28.5 MBPLE Adoption Team Setup
One of the best practices in industry to support the adoption of MBPLE
(and later the operation of the MBPLE enterprise) is setting up an MBPLE
adoption team. This adoption team is composed of the following elements:
1. MBPLE team, that is responsible for the definition and development of
MBPLE.
2. MBPLE personnel that are outside the MBPLE team and validates the
MBPLE framework. Personnel include engineers and managers.
3. MBPLE environment, as a space where the MBPLE framework can be
defined, developed and validated outside the development programs.
MBPLE Team: The MBPLE team owns the definition and development of
the overall MBPLE framework and supports the deployment in the product
line development program (see Figure 28.4). The MBPLE team can be part
of a digital transformation program, like (Digital Design, Manufacture and
Services (DDMS) in Airbus (Forlingieri and Weilkiens 2022), if its
adoption is an enabler of the transformation. Alternatively, the MBPLE
team can be included within the Method and Tool department of the
organization or as part of a Center of Competencies (CoC). While the
MBPLE team should work synergistically with the model-based systems
engineering (MBSE) team, we recommend that it remains a separate entity
rather than an extension of MBSE. This is because MBSE is just one of
several domains with which MBPLE interfaces. The teams shall include the
following:
Figure 28.4 Interdependencies among MBPLE team, personnel and
environment.
Process experts, method experts, responsible also for the information
model, tool experts, and coaches that have experience in deployment and
can support the MBPLE personnel in validating the solution as well as in
deploying it. MBPLE coaches and the MBPLE personnel guide the
transition of the organization in deploying MBPLE within product line
programs. For instance, the MBPLE team can vary from a few members of
three or four people up to a bigger team of 15 or 20 people depending on
the scope of the adoption and the size of the organization.
MBPLE Personnel: As illustrated in Figure 28.4, it serves as the bridges
between the MBPLE team and the programs, facilitating the
implementation of MBPLE within an organization. Often referred to as
MBPLE champions, their role involves validating components such as
processes, methods, the information model, and the tool chain, while also
providing essential feedback. Their insights are invaluable due to their
development experience.
This validation ensures that the MBPLE framework does not remain
theoretical – an “ivory tower” development – and does not suffer from low
user acceptance upon its rollout. The MBPLE personnel also play a key role
in identifying early adopter programs willing to implement the solution
with the support of both the MBPLE personnel and the MBPLE team.
It is paramount to recognize that the “human factor” is pivotal in any
adoption process. Organizational motivation is important, but individual
motivation is equally vital. For example, engineers need to understand how
MBPLE can improve their daily tasks. Clear answers to such questions
should be provided to them. Awareness needs to be raised among managers
and engineers, with managers also needing to see a compelling business
case to back the initiative as sponsors, as explained in Chapter 21. Regular
training and coaching sessions are essential to facilitate this process.
Adopting new methodologies often faces significant resistance, making one
of the greatest challenges not technical, but related to people’s acceptance
of change. It’s crucial to tailor the understanding of MBPLE to different
levels within the organization. While managers need to grasp the aspects
relevant to their roles, engineers require a more in-depth knowledge. Not
everyone needs to understand every detail, but clear, role-specific
communication is essential for successful adoption.
MBPLE Environment: The core concept of the MBPLE environment is to
establish a “safe” space where the comprehensive MBPLE framework can
be defined, developed, and validated prior to the deployment in programs
(see Figure 28.4). This environment also facilitates the understanding of the
future organizational structure needed to develop the product line with
MBPLE. It incorporates all resources and feedback from proofs of concept
and early adopters, as well as lessons learned, contributing to the MBPLE
“knowledge base.” This knowledge base includes detailed descriptions of
processes, norms, training materials, demonstrators, tool guidelines, and
access to a pre-production tool environment, among other resources.
MBPLE knowledge should be readily accessible, user-friendly, and actively
promoted by the MBPLE team to progressively engage more stakeholders.
Essentially, the MBPLE environment acts as a rehearsal for the actual
MBPLE deployment, laying the groundwork for its success.
28.6 Product Lines-of-product Lines
We conclude this chapter with a short clarification note, with the intention
of helping readers who might come across the barbaric term of “product
lines-of-product lines,” often introduced in the discussion about the product
line’s organization. Let us set aside right away two of the more common
misunderstandings associated with this concept. First, product lines-of-
product lines have nothing to do with systems of systems. Second, the term
product lines-of-product lines is often erroneously used to refer to
multilevel or multilayer feature models.
Having made these clarifications, let us come back to the basic definition of
a product line as the group of products that a company sells to sustain its
business. Strictly speaking, very few examples of product lines-of-product
lines exist in the industry – see, for example, Gregg et al. (2016). This
practice is present in companies that sell products issued from a product
line (pl) to external customers while integrating these same products into a
higher-level product line (PL) whose products are also sold to external
customers. The governance of both product lines, pl and PL, lies within the
scope of the same company, albeit probably allocated to different teams.
Examples of this type of business include airspace surveillance systems,
earth observation systems, or combat management systems, which might
integrate products (e.g. radars, satellites, ground stations) that can be sold
separately to other customers.
Let us consider now an original equipment manufacturer (OEM) who buys
components from providers that apply a product line approach to deliver
their products to different customers (as typically done in automotive,
aerospace, or railway). The OEM integrates these sourced components into
the higher-level products of its own product lines. Here, however, there is
no overarching instance that governs the product lines of the OEM and its
providers. Even though they are closely related, these are two different
businesses.
It is also important to note that a company can adopt a product line
approach not only to deliver the products it sells but it can also apply
product line principles to develop all or some of the components of its
products. These components can implement different reuse strategies and
apply specific MBPLE techniques. In general, though, the business cases of
these components are justified by the business case of the product lines to
which they contribute. In the end, all the investments and resources required
to operate the MBPLE enterprise are financed by the income generated by
the sale of the member products of its product lines.
References
AFIS (2015). Systems Product Line Engineering Handbook. Cepaduès.
France.
Gregg, S., Scharadin, R., and Clements, P.C. (2016). The best of both
worlds: agile development meets product line engineering at Lockheed
Martin, INCOSE 2016 International Symposium (Edinburgh, Scotland).
INCOSE: San Diego (US).26 (1): 951–965.
INCOSE (2019) Feature-based system and Software Product Line
Engineering: A Primer. INCOSE, San Diego.
OceanofPDF.com
Chapter 29
PLE Pioneers’ Perspectives: Beyond Tools
and Tool Vendors
29.1 Introduction
The authors of this book have been dealing with product line engineering
(PLE) and more recently with model-based product line engineering
(MBPLE) from the late 2000s, however, as covered in Chapter 7, the PLE
discipline sees its birth at least a decade before. This is mainly thanks to
two of the pioneers of the discipline, Dr. Charles Krueger and Dr. Danilo
Beuche. Both, one in the United States and the other in Germany, had the
intuition to work on a more structured and efficient way to develop software
by introducing the concept of software product lines, that then evolved in
feature-based PLE. The key aspect of how everything stated is that, in both
cases the idea of developing software product lines came with the intent to
solve their daily academic projects that required extensive coding.
Differently than in the other chapters, this is structured as dialog between
one of the authors, Marco Forlingieri, and the two eminent guests. To avoid
repetitions and facilitate a smooth read, some questions are repeated for
both of our guests; some others are not. It is worth highlighting that, while
the answers and definitions given in the interview do not always align with
our perspectives as the authors, the fundamental principles and essence of
PLE are widely accepted and shared. Let’s get started!
29.2 Presentation of the PLE Pioneers
Marco Forlingieri: Welcome Charlie and Danilo to this open dialog
among PLE experts. Tim, Guillermo and I are grateful to have you here,
since we strongly believe that no book about PLE could be written without
your contribution. How would you like to introduce yourself to our readers?
Dr. Charles Krueger: Thanks Marco! I am the founder and CEO of
BigLever Software (BigLever 2024), which for over two decades has helped
organizations adopt and benefit from feature-based PLE. With more than 30
years of experience in systems and software engineering practice, I am an
acknowledged leader in the PLE arena, with many publications in the field
of PLE. I am passionate to bring innovative PLE concepts, state-of-the-art
methodologies, and success stories to the forefront of the systems
engineering community. Aside from the leading role at BigLever, I was the
lead editor for the ISO/IEC 26580 (ISO/IEC 2021) standard on feature-
based PLE and past co-chair for the INCOSE PLE International Working
Group. I am also a current member of the Forbes Business Council.
Everything started back in the days, when I received my PhD in computer
science from Carnegie Mellon University and helped to pioneer the field of
systems and software product lines.
Dr. Danilo Beuche: I am Vice President of Strategy and Go-to-Market at
PTC, former CEO and co-founder of pure systems. The software company
created pure::variants (PTC pure::variants 2024), a tool developed for the
implementation of product line technologies in embedded software systems.
Puresystems was acquired by PTC in 2023. I began my career in the field of
embedded operating systems and software families in the mid-90s. The
research on tool development for feature-based software development at the
University of Magdeburg ultimately led to the establishment of pure systems
in 2001. Additionally, since 2016, I have served as an honorary professor at
the Institute for Information Systems at the University of Leipzig.
29.3 The Early Encounter with the PLE
Discipline
Marco Forlingieri: I would say you are both an authority in the PLE field.
Now let’s go back to the beginning. Could you share the story of your first
encounter with PLE or variant management? What captured your interest
in this field?
Dr. Charles Krueger: My Master’s project at Carnegie Mellon was to
author the ACM Computing Surveys issue in June 1992, simply titled
Software Reuse, which became the most widely referenced publication in
the field of software reuse. The taxonomy used there identified application
generators that automatically produce large-scale software systems from
high-level abstractions. This was the most effective and promising form of
software reuse out of the seven different approaches being explored at the
time.
The powerful and inspirational implications from that study led to my 1997
PhD thesis at Carnegie Mellon, entitled “Modeling and Simulating a
Software Architecture Design Space” (Krueger 1997), which further
elaborated how generative reuse could be applied to large-scale software
architectures rather than just small-scale software components.
In my first commercial job, I and another engineer spent several months
putting together “Pearl scripts” that allowed a team of 120 engineers to
build eight different “flavors” of a complex mulmillion-line
telecommunications software system from a shared collection of software
files, without any clone-and-own. With less than a year worth of effort
invested, we measurably tripled the output of the team of 120 engineers.
Insights from this inspirational experience provided the seeds of the feature-
based PLE field as we know it today.
Dr. Danilo Beuche: I was first exposed to the challenges and benefits of
product lines in a university course on object-oriented design of high-
performance operating system families – based on the ideas of David
Parnas about program families from the 70’s (Parnas 1976) for massively
parallel computers by Schröder-Preikschat in 1994 (Schröder-Preikschat
1994).
I joined his research group the following year and began working on
porting the project to a new embedded platform. Initially, the idea of having
a highly configurable operating system with numerous C++ defines seemed
promising, as it allowed for various configurations. However, in practice,
creating new working configurations was challenging due to the extensive
C++ preprocessor defines that needed to be set and the significant
background knowledge required about dependencies and code structure.
This complexity deterred frequent configuration changes. To address this
issue, we aimed to simplify the process and improve the usability of
program families, also known as product lines. This goal garnered the
interest of several research group members, including myself.
However, this work on configuration approaches was intended to be only
the “tool” to gain more time for the fancy stuff, i.e. developing highly
customizable operating systems for special-purpose applications. Only a
couple of years later, it turned into being the subject of main research with
embedded operating systems as the “demonstrator” application. Working
with the automotive industry in Germany in the late 90’s in joint research
projects, we learned that the software in this domain was becoming the
perfect application for these ideas, and no off-the-shelf industry solution
was available at that point in time.
Doing what good researchers do, when they see that their ideas can turn
into a commercially “exploitable” solution, we (Holger Papajewski,
Friedrich Schön, Wolfgang Schröder-Preikschat, and I) decided to create a
start-up to do so under the name “pure::systems.” The rest is history.
29.4 The Primary Benefit of PLE
Marco Forlingieri: Charlie, Danilo, I can see that for both of you the
benefits of PLE were the drivers for deepening the topic and making it at
the center of your professional life. However, if you could select one
primary benefit to emphasize for PLE, what would it be, and why is it
significant?
Dr. Charles Krueger: Modern feature-based PLE has one simple opus of
magic. It allows the full lifecycle of systems engineering assets to be
automatically generated from clear and concise high-level feature-based
abstractions for a family of products or systems, thereby eliminating the
conventional approaches of clone-and-own and tool-specific variant
management, as well as the associated manual inefficiencies. This
mechanism enables engineers to shift away from the low-value, labor-
intensive, replicative, error-prone engineering effort that deprives them of
the time and energy better spent on high-value innovative work that
advances business objectives.
Dr. Danilo Beuche: The efficiency gains (i.e. the time of engineers not
spent on repeating work others already have done before) when developing
and maintaining a product line. These efficiency gains are so pervasively
embedded in the PLE practice. For instance, a reduced asset base and size
to maintain saves time in its maintenance; the typically better test coverage
leads to better quality, resulting in less time spent on issue fixing. This often
turns into better revenue opportunities by having new product features
delivered to multiple products more easily. It must not be overlooked that
building a product line is inherently more complex compared to building a
single product instance. But all statistics and our own experience show that
it already starts paying off with a few product instances to be built and
maintained.
29.5 Differences in PLE Application Between
Software, Systems, and Across Industries
Marco Forlingieri: Now I have a question only for Charlie, since I know
your strong statement on the following matter, which some of the readers
may disagree with. Do any notable disparities exist in applying PLE
principles to software development versus system development?
Dr. Charles Krueger: In 2006, I noticed that the same feature-based PLE
principles applied to software could be applied equally well to model-based
systems engineering (MBSE) and other systems engineering assets and
disciplines. We filed for US patents on this concept in 2007 and released the
industry’s first commercial “PLE bridge” integrations into Systems
Engineering tools shortly after that, specifically for IBM Rhapsody (IBM
Rhapsody 2024).
From the ISO/IEC 26580 perspective and from my industry experience,
there are no meaningful differences in the principles and strategies for
implementing and deploying feature-based PLE to software engineering
versus systems development.
Marco Forlingieri: For you, Danilo, I have another question, always
focusing on differences. What are the differences in applying PLE in
various industries such as automotive, railway, aerospace, and consumer
goods? How do these industries differ in their approach and challenges
faced in adopting PLE?
Dr. Danilo Beuche: Clearly, certain industries are far ahead in MBSE
adoption than others, and that also relates to the use of PLE. Aviation and
Defense are leading industries in those disciplines. There are clear
differences among systems of various nature. Obviously, a home appliance,
like a dishwasher, is not as complex as a car or an airplane. And this
impacts the specific PLE approach like many other factors: number of
variations, number of product variants, product line lifecycle duration vs.
product lifecycle duration, regulations, to name a few. However, I am not
able to pinpoint major differences when it comes specifically to the PLE
approach across industries. The approach is quite straightforward and
industry-independent.
29.6 PLE Standards and Their Development
Marco Forlingieri: From your previous answers, it is clear that you
strongly support the application of feature-based PLE across software,
systems, and heterogeneous industries. PLE standards become essential to
regulate the discipline and set shared guidelines. What role did you play in
the development of norms such as ISO/IEC 26550 (ISO/IEC 2015) 26580,
Variability Exchange Language (VEL) (OASIS 2018)– still not published –
etc.? Could you provide some insights into your contributions to these
standards?
Dr. Charles Krueger: In 2015, the INCOSE standards group asked me to
become their liaison in the ISO/IEC Software and Systems Tools and
Methods working group. At my first meeting with the working group, I saw
a clear opportunity to create a new standard on the modern feature-based
PLE tools and methods since the existing early-generation academic PLE
standard at the time – ISO/IEC 26550 – did not adequately reflect the
commercial advances in the field.
This led to the ISO/IEC 26580 on the feature-based approach to PLE. I was
the lead editor on that effort and recruited Paul Clements, another of the
PLE pioneers, to be the co-editor. At that time, I leveraged my role as co-
chair on the INCOSE PLE working group (INCOSE PLE WG 2024) to
collaborate with both INCOSE membership and ISO membership to gain a
broad industry consensus on ISO/IEC 26580.
When the OMG SysML v2 (OMG 2024) standard initiative began to work
on the variant modeling portion of that standard, the initiative leader,
Sanford Friedenthal, asked me to lead the variant modeling working group
to assure that SysML v2 and ISO/IEC 26580 were mutually aligned and
compliant. Their plan at the time was to embed a full PLE solution into
SysML v2, but this would have been an MBSE-only PLE solution rather
than an orthogonal PLE solution for the full systems engineering life cycle
and therefore not ISO/IEC 26580 compliant. Furthermore, this would have
been an enormous undertaking by the SysML v2 team, having a scope of
effort that they did not yet fully appreciate.
I helped them separate the two concerns of MBSE and feature-based PLE,
showing how the addition of a concise variation point construct into the
SysML v2 language would be sufficient for an ISO/IEC 26580 compliant
MBSE solution that allowed the feature-based PLE concern to be managed
by a separate ISO/IEC 26580 feature-based PLE compliant tool, with the
PLE feature catalog, Bill-of-Features, and PLE Factory Configurator all
outside of the SysML v2 scope.
Dr. Danilo Beuche: I am a member of the ISO working group, which is
responsible for the ISO 265xx family of standards. While not being the main
editor of ISO/IEC 26580 (credits must go to Charles Krueger and Paul
Clements), I was part of the core team who worked on this standard for a
couple of years. And I am quite happy with the impact this standard has
been able to achieve. As part of the ISO WG, I am also reviewing other
related PLE standards, namely some of the newer members of the
2655x/2656x family standards.
The VEL is an “indirect” result of a larger publicly funded industry
research project my company pure systems participated in. The core idea
and language prototypes for this language came out of this project, and
pure systems with other interested parties started the standardization effort
by establishing a dedicated technical committee in OASIS (OASIS 2018).
Personally, I would consider myself rather the business sponsor of pure
systems’ VEL activities. The main work was done by other pure systems
folks – Michael Schulze and Uwe Ryssel – on the language side; the
development team built libraries to make this language an integral part of
pure::variants. Today, we use this language actively in our product to
communicate with other products, such as Catia Magic (Cameo 2024).
Nevertheless, it is not yet a final standard out of OASIS. But there is more,
pure systems also contributed to AUTOSAR’s effort to include variability
into their standards (AUTOSAR 2024).
And, not to forget, about a decade ago I was working together with many
contributors from other companies and organizations on the OMG CVL –
Common Variability Language (Øystein et al. 2013) concept, which sadly
was never finalized. Even earlier we initiated an Eclipse project around
building a feature modeling language, but this never got enough traction,
so we stopped it many years ago.
29.7 Synergies and Challenges of the Liaison
PLE and MBSE
Marco Forlingieri: As you know, all three authors of this book have been
promoting the adoption of PLE in combination with MBSE (aka MBPLE).
How do MBSE and PLE complement each other? Can you elaborate on the
synergies they offer and the challenges they pose when integrated?
Dr. Charles Krueger: MBSE and PLE are fully complementary and
orthogonal concerns. MBSE’s concern is on the structures and behaviors of
a system. PLE’s concern is on the distinguishing characteristics among the
members of a family of similar systems. At the intersection of the MBSE and
PLE concerns is the variation point, which allows the crosscutting concern
of PLE to be reflected as feature-based PLE variability within the concern
of MBSE model elements.
By applying feature-based PLE to MBSE, a single MBSE superset model
with variation points can represent the model for an entire system family
portfolio. While managing the superset MBSE model and feature-based
PLE model is more complex than managing the MBSE model for a single
system, it is much simpler and more efficient than managing a collection of
clone-and-own copies of the MBSE model for each member of the system
family portfolio.
Dr. Danilo Beuche: Before answering these questions, let me point out that
I (and not only I, see the INCOSE Handbooks statements on PLE and
systems engineering) consider SE (without MB prefix) and PLE as having a
symbiotic relationship. The concept of describing variability through
variation points is a mandatory part of the SE when being applied to
families of systems (which many complex systems to which “proper” SE is
applied to, are).
With that being said, the relation between MBSE and PLE is quite
interesting. Theoretically, a perfect match, as in most approaches for PLE,
some kind of variability model(s) is an essential element. However, before
the advent of SysML v2, none of the OMG standards, such as SysML (v1) or
UML, had any construct to support high-level variability modeling and,
even more critically, supported variation points in their languages out of
the box, which in turn meant that expressing variations in models was
difficult without building individual extensions. This lack of direct support
was and is, in my opinion a showstopper. Especially the representation of
shared superset assets can cause grief when tools or languages have no
native understanding of the concept of a product configuration.
Therefore, it has always been necessary to develop language and tool
extensions to effectively integrate these aspects. Notable examples include
Tim Weilkiens’ VAMOS approach (Weilkiens 2016) and the integration of
the orthogonal variability model, based on Klaus Pohl’s ideas (Pohl,
Böckle, and Linden 2005), into PTC Modeler (PTC Modeler 2024). Both
approaches are fully implemented within the respective modeling language
meta-models. To address this challenge, some MBSE tools have developed
proprietary language extensions and tool integrations. These extensions, for
example, connect to feature-based PLE tools such as Gears or
pure::variants.
29.8 The Protagonist Role of the Feature
Model in PLE
Marco Forlingieri: As illustrated extensively in this book, feature models
play a crucial role in PLE. Why are feature models essential in PLE, and
how do they facilitate the management of product variability?
Dr. Charles Krueger: Feature models (or the feature catalog) are
orthogonal to MBSE models and other models in other engineering
artifacts such as mechanical BOMs. For example, MBSE models describe
structures and behaviors for the system(s) of interest, whereas the PLE
Feature Catalog describes the high-level abstractions for the distinguishing
characteristics among the different products in a product line.
Abstraction is the key to the value of feature models. There will likely be
two or three orders of magnitude fewer elements in a feature model than
there are variation points in the engineering assets across the engineering
lifecycle. For example, a feature in a feature model might be the optional
capability for pedestrian detection in a car that comes in four different
flavors: (1) none, (2) pedestrian warning that sounds a warning alarm, (3)
pedestrian braking that automatically applies brakes, and (4) pedestrian
avoidance that applies brakes and steering. These four abstract variants in
the feature model will control thousands of complex and interdependent
variation points in requirements, architecture, design, mechanical BOMs,
electrical controllers, software, calibration data, user manual
documentation, test cases, certification documents, manufacturing plans,
service documents, sales brochures, and much more. Having concise high-
level abstractions in the feature models that drive the precise, repeatable,
automated behaviors of variation points provides powerful benefits such as
engineering complexity reduction, reduction in errors compared to manual
approaches, and overall reduction in engineering effort.
Dr. Danilo Beuche: Let me rephrase the question: Why is a dedicated
variability model essential in PLE, and why are feature models, in my
opinion, a particularly good choice for variability modeling?
When we look at the variability model, it is at the heart of a product line, it
allows one to focus on what differentiates products without having to
understand how the system achieves that differentiation technically. As
variability is a main driver; before even building the solution, it must be
representable in a solution-independent way. In many cases, PLE
variability must be referenced when working on various other assets, such
as source code and test plans. Therefore, it should be easy to use and free
from excessive “unnecessary noise” from the perspective of most users, of
which we have many in product lines. A clear separation of concerns is
crucial here. There are many ways to model variability explicitly, such as
decision diagrams, orthogonal variability models, and propositional logic.
Feature models are just one approach. Now, let’s discuss why feature
models are the “perfect” choice for this purpose.
As someone who fell in love with feature models almost three decades ago, I
have a fairly biased view here. Around 1998, when I was trying to find a
solution to the problem of making complex configuration logic easily
accessible to (software) engineers, I came across an article from Kryzsztof
Czarnecki. In this chapter, he introduced the idea of feature models, and it
immediately clicked in my brain. This combination of a few language
constructs was immensely powerful yet easy to explain and remember. The
original FODA (Kang et al. 1990) ideas were in many aspects an
inspiration yet not sufficient for real-world modeling when I tried to capture
the logic of the operating system product line we were working at that time.
So, I built a “new” feature modeling language, which is now manifested in
pure::variants. All the enhancements and developments done were intended
to preserve key properties of the nature of feature models. Easy to grasp
and use even for the “casual” user when writing and configuring, yet
powerful enough to cover a majority of PLE use cases.
Due to the many stakeholders being exposed to the PLE variability, having
an easy to read and learn language is paramount for acceptance and
usability. This is the “superpower” of feature models; they just have the
right balance. If we look at it from a “mathematical” perspective, for
instance, the OVM language is basically identical to what you can express
with feature models, yet, to me, reading and grasping the relationship of
multiple OVM variation points is much harder, at least for my brain, than
reading the equivalent feature model. Similarly, if we look over the fence
into the world of PLM tools such as Siemens Teamcenter (Siemens
Teamcenter 2024), PTC Windchill (PTC Windchill 2024), or Dassault
Enovia (Dassault Systèmes 2024), to name a few, there is configuration
logic usually expressed in “flat” lists of variation points connected via
logic rules in the background. This is almost identical to the OVM
approach. But as refinement relationships must be expressed through
“invisible” rules. These complex dependencies are much more difficult to
grasp for the casual user.
29.9 Common Misconceptions About PLE
and Usage of a PLE Tool
Marco Forlingieri: There are several myths or misunderstandings about
MBSE, such as the fact that “MBSE wants to replace SE” or that “it wants
to eliminate requirements.” Regarding PLE, Charlie, what are, according
to your experience, some prevalent misconceptions that people have about
PLE, and how do these misunderstandings impact its adoption and
implementation?
Dr. Charles Krueger: Early-generation PLE methods focused on reuse,
where reusable artifacts in a reuse library could be leveraged to create
member products in a product line of similar systems. Unfortunately, this
simple reuse concept is the “obvious” one that people intuitively gravitate
to. Sadly, the recommended methodology in books, papers, and even in
standards like ISO/IEC 26550 has clone-and-own style reuse at the
foundation.
Many organizations have gone down this “obvious” path, only to be
overwhelmed by the complexity of managing all the nearly identical but
distinct clones in their portfolio. These organizations tend to abandon their
PLE initiative and then spread the message that PLE doesn’t work. I
characterize this phenomenon as “the things about PLE that are obvious do
not work and the things about PLE that do work (i.e. ISO/IEC 2680) are not
obvious.” This is why in ISO/IEC 26580 we use the term “sharing” rather
than “reuse.” The common meaning of reuse is a cloning event, whereas
sharing connotes joining together for a long-term collaborative journey
with supersets.
Another prevalent misconception is that adding a PLE tool into an
engineering practice will provide the desired benefits of efficiency, quality,
cost reduction, complexity reduction, and scale. However, feature-based
PLE requires organizational change and engineering workflow change to
be successful. This is articulated in ISO/IEC 26580, but organizations
sometimes believe they can ignore this reality and still be successful by
installing a modern PLE tool while avoiding the more challenging people-
oriented issues of modernizing organization structures and processes. I
characterize this phenomenon as changing technology is easy, but hanging
people is hard.
Marco Forlingieri: I often hear the statement: “Why do we need a PLE
tool if we can do everything in the systems modeling tool?” Why is there a
need for specialized tools to manage variability in product lines? What
limitations exist in relying solely on system modeling tools for this purpose?
Dr. Danilo Beuche: The answer to this question is kind of a summary of
what I already expressed before: There is a need for an explicit dedicated
variability modeling language with properties making it easy to use for
specifically that purpose. The variability model is at the core of PLE and
referenced from many other data assets throughout the engineering flow. If
this kind of model is being defined in a dedicated tool like pure::variants or
in a systems modeling tool makes no difference from a theoretical point of
view.
Practically speaking, with MBSE not being as widespread as it could and
should be, for a lot of users, a dedicated tool without the “overhead” of
MBSE is quite attractive. It also helps to get started with PLE without
having to introduce MBSE, too, to benefit from this. And with no formally
defined variability language built into SysML, all approaches are currently
“proprietary.” Whereas there is a solid offering for dedicated product line
factory configurators (as tools like pure::variants or Gears are classified
according to ISO 26580) for commercial customers as well as open-source
research tools like FeatureIDE with similar capabilities.
This picture may (or may not) change with the success of SysML v2, as the
approach does not preclude anyone from building explicit variability
modeling into their SysML v2 approach as a language extension and/or to
add this to a revised standard at some point in time.
As a side note, one practical limitation of many MBSE tools available today
is the challenge of collaboration and evolution of models. Graphical
notations are inherently difficult to compare and nicely merge when
concurrent changes are made in different branches. This limits the use of
models in software-development-like workflows (e.g. using feature
branches). However, this kind of workflow is paramount for modern, agile
development (and not only there). We designed and built pure::variants in a
way that things like three-way compare and merge are nicely supported by
the modeling approach and in the tools UI. This was not an easy task for
feature models, but it is so much harder for MBSE languages.
29.10 SysML v2 as Potential “Game-
Changer” for MBPLE
Marco Forlingieri: Along this book and ultimately in the last answer of
Danilo, we heard about the advent of SysML v2. As PLE pioneers and tool
vendors, do you perceive SysML v2 primarily as a threat or an opportunity
for PLE? How do you think it will influence the landscape of PLE
methodologies?
Dr. Charles Krueger: Variant modeling in SysML v2 was intentionally
designed to be compliant with ISO/IEC 26580 and aligned to industry best
practices in feature-based PLE. The synergy between these two highly
visible standards validates the role of PLE in the future of MBSE and
systems engineering. The key to success will be how well MBSE tool
vendors comply with both ISO/IEC 26580 and SysML v2. If these vendors
do not understand or do not comply with the good guidance provided in
these standards, then they might do more damage than good to the
landscape of PLE and MBSE.
Dr. Danilo Beuche: SysML v2 introduces embedded variation and variants
as native elements of the language, which is a significant improvement and
will greatly facilitate the integration of MBSE and PLE. In my opinion, the
separation of concerns is well managed. This approach allows SysML v2 to
seamlessly connect with both existing and newly developed PLE variability
modeling concepts and tools, reflecting the orthogonality of the driving
variability models in PLE. I believe this will make MBSE in combination
with PLE much more attractive. I also think that it is a huge opportunity for
MBSE to become more accessible and integrated in companies’ engineering
approaches beyond those domains where the use of MBSE is kind of
mandated. And thus, it will be a relevant element of PLE methodologies of
the future.
Yet, I see SysML v2 only as one element of many in the successful PLE/SE
approach. I don’t see all product lines being built using SysML v2, while
being appropriate for many. It also depends on how the SysML v2 tool
vendors will implement the integration of the SysML v2 variability concepts
into their tools. We have some interesting times ahead of us with SysML v2.
Looking forward to them!
29.11 The Key Absent in the PLE Arena: a
Variability Modeling Standard
Marco Forlingieri: While we see SysML already advancing to a second
new life with SysML v2, why is there not a widely adopted standard for
feature modeling or variability modeling languages? What challenges do
you foresee in establishing such a standard?
Dr. Charles Krueger: The feature ontology required for an effective
feature-based PLE feature catalog in complex, commercial, large-scale
system families is very sophisticated, with modularity, hierarchical
composition, layers of abstraction, mixing instantiation with generalization,
and more. Early efforts to specify PLE feature modeling standards were
mostly academic and suitable for small-scale demonstrations but not
suitable for large-scale commercial experience. Creating a standard
requires a lot of time, money, and effort. While feature-based PLE has been
in the early stages of the technology adoption curve, PLE vendors have
been laser focused on creating the tools and methods that enable repeatable
success stories. As feature-based PLE “crosses the chasm” into the broader
adoption by the mainstream, pressures to create industry standards will
increase and benefit those involved.
Dr. Danilo Beuche: As a tool vendor, one has always a very ambivalent
relationship to standards. On one hand, good standards create better
understanding and accessibility of concepts, but you are always concerned
about commoditizing away your competitive advantage by making it easier
for others to build similar tools based on your know-how being exposed
through good standards. And even worse, bad and/or too restrictive
standards may kill innovation. From a user’s point of view, this is different;
here, interoperability and choice of multiple tools for the same purpose are
key benefits. From my point of view, standards like VEL and SysML V2 are
balancing this nicely. Over time, with growing knowledge and expertise,
with feature-based product lines being a widely accepted PLE approach
supported by multiple tools, we will probably see more formal language
standards emerge.
29.12 The Future of MBPLE with the Rise of
AI and Large End-to-End Proprietary
Solutions
Marco Forlingieri: We are heading to the last part of our discussion, and I
would like to have your opinion, Charlie, on a “hot” topic nowadays: What
are the potential advantages and disadvantages of integrating artificial
intelligence (AI) technologies into (MB) PLE processes? How might AI
enhance or disrupt current practices?
Dr. Charles Krueger: The combinatorics in PLE is staggering. With only 33
independent optional feature choices, you can specify a unique product
configuration for every human on planet Earth. With only 280 independent
optional feature choices, you can specify a unique product configuration for
every atom in the known universe. Complex product lines may have
thousands of unique feature choices. Although advanced PLE tools provide
capabilities to dramatically reduce combinatorics, the numbers can still
remain astronomical. Product portfolio managers face the challenge of
guessing how many products for how many feature combinations will be
economically desirable and optimal to the market and their corporate
bottom line. A good role for AI is to explore market trends and projections
with the PLE feature combinations to plan the perfect product line portfolio.
Marco Forlingieri: Danilo, in 2023 we assisted in the acquisition of pure
systems by the ALM and PLM vendor PTC (PTC News 2024). How do you
envision the future of PLE tools? Will they evolve to become tool chain
independent, or will they remain closely tied to specific PLM or ALM tool
vendors?
Dr. Danilo Beuche: That is a good question to which I have no definitive
answer. Let me break it down into areas where I am certain and areas
where the future remains uncertain.
I strongly believe that PLE will become more widespread in many
industries. For companies and teams deciding about tools, it will become a
critical point to see with which tools PLE can be done in an efficient
manner. For instance, PLE integration is already part of many RfP
(requests for proposals) of customers, addressing ALM and MBSE vendors.
I believe we will see a new wave of competing approaches and solutions
coming to the market in the near future. But for customers, the main
challenge is how to integrate this in their entire tool chain. Fully closed
one-vendor ecosystems will only have limited acceptance. Hence now, tools
with the special focus on PLE like pure::variants are quite an attractive
choice. If enough tool vendors decide or are forced (e.g. by the way of
industry standards) to integrate standard APIs in a nice way, then tools like
pure::variants can become more tool chain independent. But based on our
experience, there is one critical element: Easy access to variability models
in all connected tools; for instance, when working with test plans, engineers
must be able to interact seamlessly with PLE models from within the test
plan. They should be able to review the test plan for a specific product
instance easily and add variations to a test case or test plan without being
forced to switch tools and contexts. Future PLE tools must deliver this
capability to remain appealing for most use cases.
For now, where basically each combination of a PLE tool and engineering
authoring tools requires some kind of proprietary work to achieve this level
of integration, it is not possible for a PLE tool to be tool chain independent.
A wide adoption of VEL would be a step closer to tool-independence and
more choice for the users, but VEL is not sufficient as it does not cover the
technology to directly expose variability models to the users in the
authoring tools. Here surely, a widely accepted standard for PLE variability
models and the availability of rendering components could make the job
easier.
To summarize, sooner or later, PLE concepts will be pervasive. But I do not
know if this will be through an open collaborating ecosystem of engineering
tools, including a standalone PLE factory configurator with a standardized
API or through many point-to-point integrations. Or if MBSE and SysML
v2++ will take over the world and become the hub for PLE.
29.13 Conclusion
During this friendly talk, Dr. Charles Krueger and Dr. Danilo Beuche gave
us insightful and not obvious answers on the PLE topic, including its often
troublesome “marriage” with MBSE. What fascinates us, the authors of this
book, is their passion and entire professional life dedicated to PLE. After
almost 30 years, they are still at the forefront of this powerful formula and
the magic of sharing commonality and managing variability to deliver
customer satisfaction. This is ultimately the essence of feature-based PLE.
