Part V
MBPLE Industrial Cases
To close this book, five real-world cases of model-based product line
engineering (MBPLE) adoption in three different industries are proposed:
aviation, defense and heat, ventilation and air-conditioning (HVAC)
equipment, represented by Airbus, MBDA, Thales, Raytheon and Belimo.
To select the industrial cases, different criteria were identified. These
criteria are independent of the modeling language and tools used by the
different contributors:
Adoption of systems engineering processes as defined by ISO/IEC
15288.
Implementation of product line-based development.
Application of ISO/IEC 25550 and 25580 standards.
Use of features to represent and manage variability.
The approach to implement MBPLE can be mapped to the 4 key steps
of Chapter 12.
Adoption of a model-based approach.
At least two types of assets are controlled by the feature model (e.g.
requirements, MBSE, CAD).
While there might certainly be more than five cases that comply with those
criteria –like some of the works referenced throughout the book– the
intention of this last part is to provide a “fresh picture” of the current state
of adoption and deployment of MBPLE conscious (and hoping) that this
picture is fast evolving in today’s world.
Each case presented in Part V proposes an overview of the adoption of
MBPLE and zooms in on a specific topic of interest. The case presented by
Airbus focuses on the unique characteristics of the aviation industry and
explores the concept of “variability cascading” across several levels of the
system hierarchy and across different abstraction layers (the reader can refer
to Chapter 18 for key concepts).
The case presented by MBDA is centered on the implementation of a
model-based approach with Systems Modeling Language (SysML) and its
integration with feature-based product line engineering and provides
detailed examples of feature models and other shared assets, such as
electrical design artifacts.
Taking a different point of view, the case presented by Thales reflects on a
15-year journey of product line development, evolving from document-
based and clone-and-own approaches to a very mature, organization-wide
delivery approach based on MBPLE.
Raytheon’s case, the sole non-European example, highlights the US
Department of Defense’s (DoD) concept of the “digital trinity.” It
demonstrates how modern system family engineering enhances this
concept, enabling the DoD and its defense contractors to innovate rapidly
and maintain relevance. This approach extends beyond the traditional
model-based application of PLE by integrating DevOps practices and a
Modular Open Systems Approach (MOSA) architecture.
In the last industrial case, Belimo highlights the market-driven context that
prompted the company to adopt MBPLE. The case focuses on variability
management, stressing the differentiated application of variability during
two phases: an early development stage, which includes concept and system
architecture definition, and a later stage, including system design.
Part V concludes with a brief analysis of the five industrial cases,
highlighting the similarities and differences in MBPLE adoption among the
cases.
OceanofPDF.com
Chapter 30
Airbus: MBPLE in Commercial Aviation
Davi Henrique de Sousa Pinto
30.1 Introduction
Digital era: The digital and artificial intelligence (AI) era brings challenges
to all industries, who need to adapt to survive the increasing competition
and quick change of environment (Knaster and Leffingwell 2020). The
challenges go across all layers of an enterprise, from business to
technology, manufacturing, operations, and services. The aerospace
industry, historically spearheading innovation and driving changes due to its
complex needs and products (Walden et al. 2023), has been facing the
growing and fast needs to digitalize itself to remain competitive.
Rise of product line engineering: In this environment, the product line
engineering (PLE) discipline rose from highly variable and customizable
software products in the nineties, which required more sophisticated
techniques to manage the variability and reuse (Kang et al. 2009).
Thousands of features, driving both static and dynamic perspectives of
software source code and runtime, meant more sophisticated and formal
descriptions of variability, processes, and techniques to harvest, understand,
and automate development.
Requirements and systems engineering at Airbus: At the same time,
systems engineering penetration increased in all stages of the life cycle of
aircraft development. The perceived need to tackle more complex aircraft
from manufacturers, such as Airbus, and certification authorities enforced
standards such as the ARP 4754A (Society of Automotive Engineers
Aerospace 2010). Although focusing mostly on requirements engineering, it
functioned as a crucial step in shaping both processes and culture to
systems engineering, laying the foundations which enable the current top–
down push from the enterprise of model-based approaches, data-centric
processes, digital continuity, and PLE.
Next generation of Airbus aircraft: These advances are mandatory for the
next generation of aircraft, such as the zero-emission, which aims at
targeting not only business but also societal demands regarding
sustainability. Recent technologies, such as AI and future iterations, also
rely on strong digitalization and change readiness efforts to be implemented
in a timely manner. They are enablers for more optimized products in both
development, manufacturing, and operations.
Increasing complexity: One of the ways to measure complexity is the
amount of source lines of code (SLOC) within a system. Airbus and Boeing
data shows that the SLOC is doubling roughly every four years (Feiler et al.
2009). SLOC cannot grow indefinitely, as it impacts affordability (Feiler et
al. 2009). Approaches such as PLE help reduce the costs of such extensive
source code by better managing variability and reuse. Figure 30.1 shows the
exponential growth in Airbus aircraft lines of code.
Figure 30.1 Software complexity in Airbus’ commercial aircraft.
30.2 MBPLE at Airbus
Digital design, manufacturing and services: To tackle the previously
described challenges, Airbus has launched in 2019 a group-wide program,
called Digital Design, Manufacturing and Services (DDMS) (Ferrogalini
2019). This program, with its significant budget and resources, aims at
preparing the whole enterprise for the next generation of products while
upskilling, capitalizing, maturing, and battle-hardening key capabilities on
current successful product iterations.
Capitalizing on existing aircraft: The A220, A320, A330, and A350
families are still thriving in the current market (Airbus 2024a,b), with huge
backlogs and constantly submitted to new updates and modernizations. This
is a perfect playground and environment for ironing new capabilities and
processes.
30.2.1 Model-Based Systems Engineering
Complex environment: Deploying wide group changes in complex
environments such as the Airbus Group is not simple. Solid foundations in
systems engineering are necessary before any attempt at widening the scope
to proposed initiatives such as model-based systems engineering (MBSE),
multidisciplinary analysis and optimization (MDAO), or PLE.
Requirements-based engineering: The transition to a requirements-based
engineering approach was the first step taken. The sheer volume of
requirements that needed to be formalized in a complex configuration
management environment, while having to remain robust and airworthy,
made the transition take its time. A cultural change was also vital to
achieve, along with the development and maturation of IT tools that
supported this effort.
Expansion to systems engineering: The next logical step was the adoption
of a broader systems engineering processes scope. Changes of this caliber
are fundamentally distinct amongst Airbus Divisions. The business size,
airworthiness, and certification requirements make Airbus commercial
adoption slower and denser than the Airbus helicopters (AH) and Airbus
Defence and Space (ADS). On the other hand, faster advances from AH and
ADS bring much-needed development and feedback to any group-wide
transformation, such as DDMS’s envisioned systems engineering
transformation. Nevertheless, initiatives in the Commercial Aircraft
Systems department go back to almost a decade.
R-MOFLT architecture framework: Several initiatives within the group
in the last two decades, along with MBSE academic and industrial
developments, led to the creation of a group-wide MBSE architecture
framework based on the Systems Modeling Language (SysML):
Requirements, Mission, Operational, Functional, Logical, Technical
Analysis (R-MOFLT) (Ducamp et al. 2022). As seen in Figure 30.2, the R-
MOFLT framework covers both problem and solution spaces, supporting
the Airbus Systems Engineering Referential processes.
Figure 30.2 Airbus R-MOFLT architecture framework.
30.2.2 Product Line Engineering
Origins at Airbus: Although several decentralized initiatives were taken
within the group, the first more organized PLE developments were in the
Airbus Defence and Space division. It started by focusing on requirements
management and applicability. Later, MBSE assets were included, and a
formal model-based product line engineering (MBPLE) was established
within DDMS, expanding to all Airbus divisions.
Local optimum: Product lines are one of the pillars of the DDMS program.
The principles of reusability and overall optimization are core to the DNA
of the Airbus organization. Due to the sensitivity of product profitability
related to its performance, localized techniques, along with several
successful programs spanning for decades, created several “silos” of local
processes. Those share many of the important concepts seen in product
lines, although not standardized or managed as such. Those include
modularization, reusability (from cross-program in clone-and-own styles, to
jointly managed, such as in lower-level components).
30.2.3 Focusing on Aviation
Safety first: For civil aircraft, the main driver for the development and
operation is safety (ICAO 2023), as a single accident can profoundly harm
the manufacturer’s trust and cost millions or billions in financial damage,
on top of the human cost in injuries and deaths.
Slim profit margin (IATA 2024): Aviation’s profitability is fragile and low
(Figure 30.3). Jet fuel, with its volatile price linked to oil prices, makes up
around 30% of aviation operating costs. On top, the increasing costs of
climate change and decarbonization put increasing pressure on the
industry’s already slim margin. With alternative sustainable aviation fuel
costing more than twice as much as aviation fuel, existing solutions to
reduce dependency on fossil energy are not economically viable yet.
Figure 30.3 Global airline net profit and EBIT margin.
To optimize operational profitability and retain a strong market position,
Airbus has historically invested large amounts of cash on weight reduction,
manufacturing efficiency, and other initiatives.
Role of PLE: In this environment, PLE is one of the levers to increase
performance on all axes of development (products, manufacturing, and
services) and operation. Although the applied approach might not be the
same as the ones used for other industries, the core principles remain the
mastering of variability and reuse while increasing quality.
PLE is also a key element in optimizing the production ramping up, which
has been highlighting inefficiencies and bottlenecks on processes.
30.2.4 Model-Based Product Line Engineering
Beyond product lines: Airbus has chosen to integrate into its DDMS
transformation project, a MBPLE initiative. Its mission is to cover not only
product line development but also variability management and trade-off
analysis. The scope spans across business units, and it aims at
implementing, tailoring, and expanding the ISO 26550:2015 (ISO/IEC
2015) family.
Feature-based approach: The Airbus MBPLE framework has its
foundations in the feature-based approach described in the ISO 26580:2021
(ISO/IEC 2021). As previously mentioned, the framework emphasis is not
only on product lines, which has its own organization within DDMS. The
focus lies on variability management, and how variability models and PLE
configurators can be used in different disciplines.
Modeling approaches: From a SysML modeling perspective, the
framework is versatile and covers both “direct and clean” modeling
approaches described by Forlingieri and Weilkiens (2022).
Configuration management: The product line life cycle added a
considerable complexity to configuration management, as both phases
(domain and application engineering) shall be managed in tandem, with
traceability not only within but also between them. The Airbus
implementation targets at performing configuration management in the
variability models. The other asset classes are in general covered by their
own domain (requirements, MBSE models, etc.).
Customization: From offer to delivery, variability is a key aspect for
customization. Here the challenge is a transversal variability management
approach to maximize profitability through quicker anticipation of market
needs, and faster feasibility studies and time to market.
Trades: Variability management is at the heart of trades. From a technical
perspective, a trade can be seen as a provisional product line, with the
trades’ candidates seen as product line variants. Provisional here means an
architectural trade that can be run in hours but also a longer pre-program
phases spanning for years, while concepts and studies are performed before
architectural freeze. From that point, development can go back to a single
product or remain a product line covering a family of products.
Feature-based variability on trades: The end-to-end trade flowchart
below (Figure 30.4) exemplifies this principle, which uses the powerful
PLE techniques of variability management to manage not only architecture
and parameters, but also the simulation model.
Figure 30.4 Simplified trade flow using PLE techniques.
Powering multidisciplinary analysis and optimization: Many of the
existing trades are already performed with effective and specialized tools,
which are not meant to be replaced by PLE techniques. Where PLE can
place a fundamental role is on MDAO, where the multimodel,
multidisciplinary nature of the analysis benefits from having a more
standard variability description and possibly configurators on model
instantiation.
30.3 Challenges for MBPLE Implementation
Heterogeneity of variability management: Without the existence nor the
application of standards, variability has been historically managed in
diverse ways: spreadsheets, scripts, macros, matrices, templates, filters, and
customized systems. This means that processes and organizations have
invested and adapted themselves to variability management and not all have
pain points related to it.
Obstacles to a holistic approach: This heterogeneity is a barrier to
achieving a more holistic view and management of variability, a pillar of
product lines. It requires strong sponsorship and business case to prove the
benefits on wider scope outside of existing silos.
30.4 Variability Cascading
This section presents and discusses valuable principles related to variability
cascading. Although focusing on SysML (Object Management Group
[OMG] 2024) models and textual requirements, the principles can be
extended to other similar languages, such as UML (OMG 2017), and other
classes of assets, such as 3D elements.
At Airbus, pure::variants (PTC pure::variants 2024) is mainly used for
feature modeling and as a PLE configurator. For SysML modeling, Cameo
Systems Modeler (Cameo 2024) is the tool used. Pure::variants has a direct
connector to Cameo Systems Modeler, and these tools are used in this
section to illustrate the concepts. Figure 30.5 shows the current tool chain.
Airbus tooling never dictate the framework, but it is the opposite. The
principles are centered at a holistic and agnostic framework, based on data
products, ontology formalization and data continuity.
Figure 30.5 Airbus MBPLE tool chain.
30.4.1 Dimensions of Variability
The principles and techniques here can be applied to any of the four
dimensions of variability Figure 30.6 (Forlingieri 2022).
Figure 30.6 Four dimensions of variability.
From a technical perspective, though, the four dimensions can be split into
two categories:
1. SysML variability cascading (requirements and MBSE) – intra-
system, mainly covering the “variability in layers of abstraction”
dimension. This focuses on how to cascade variability in a consistent
manner along the system engineering processes (ISO/IEC/IEEE 15288
2023). In our context, this means cascading variability in textual
requirements and MBSE models for a given system of interest (SOI).
The MOFLT systems architecture framework has been expanded to
include variability and support PLE architecting through the
MBPLE4MOFLT method (Madeira et al.2023).
2. Variability model (feature models) cascading – inter-system,
mainly covering the “co-variability in product, manufacturing, and
services,” “variability in development lifecycle,” and “variability in
system hierarchy levels” dimensions. The focus here is on cascading
variability between different SoI. Most of this consists of multimodel
and multilevel feature models. If different SOI models are linked, the
techniques from “intra system” can be applied.
Figure 30.7 illustrates how both kinds of cascading (“intra-system” and
“inter-system”) work together in a “co-variability in product,
manufacturing, and services” context.
Figure 30.7 Variability cascading overview.
SysML: Figure 30.8 illustrates how the SysML covers both structural and
behavioral views from a system while supported by requirements.
Moreover, all views are linked together, maintaining continuity and
consistency. Naturally, variability can impact all those views, and cascading
of variability both inside the same views and cross-views needs to be taken
into consideration.
Figure 30.8 MBSE model views.
In this section we consider the cascading in the following SysML diagrams:
1. Structural: Block definition diagrams (BDD) and internal block
diagrams (IBD).
2. Behavior: Activity diagrams (ACT) and state machine diagrams.
3. Requirements: Requirements diagrams.
30.4.2 Variability Cascading Main Principles
The cascading principles are illustrated by a simplified aircraft example,
which proposes a new optional capability to improve fuel consumption and
reduce emissions. This capability aims at delaying engine start by using a
wheel powered engine in the landing gear for ground operations. The
example is inspired by the e-Taxi aircraft concept (Madeira et al. 2023),
with its feature model described in Figure 30.9.
Figure 30.9 e-Taxi simplified feature model.
Focus on core elements driving variability: The use of variation points by
constraining assets to features shall be the minimum possible and reserved
for core and root elements driving variability. Usage of variation points for
cleaning of models or convenience shall be avoided. Remember that
variation points are expensive to maintain from a consistency and semantic
perspective.
Example: One of the functions used by the system to perform an electric
wheel-powered taxi is called “Produce AC Movement on LG from electric
power.” Figure 30.10 shows the function represented by a SysML block and
its owned elements. This is a variable function linked to the feature
“Electric.”
Figure 30.10 Function “Produce AC Movement on LG from electric
power.”
This function is part of the functional architecture (as a SysML part), as
shown in Figure 30.11.
Figure 30.11 Extract of the functional architecture highlighting the
studied function usage as a SysML Part.
Also, its behavior is part of an activity diagram, as shown in Figure 30.12.
Figure 30.12 Extract of an activity highlighting the behavior of the
studied function within the context of Taxiing-In.
This example shows the power of analyzing and identifying the true root
elements driving the variability. By applying a single variation point in the
block representing the function, all its owned elements inherit it (meaning
they also get deleted together with its owner during a model
transformation). Not only that, but most tools will cascade by default the
removal of elements to their usages in diagrams (for example, all parts of
the aforementioned block also get removed from BDDs and IBDs, as well
as the activity from ACT diagrams).
The result of this minimalistic approach to variation points will not always
result in clean models and leaving hanging elements, such as diagram ports
(Figure 30.13). This cleaning can be either done manually or automatically.
The temptation here shall be resisted, as creating nonessential variation
points can create a nightmare later, when trying to manage their
consistency.
Figure 30.13 Port left hanging after model transformation which
removed a function. Clean-up is required in this case.
30.4.3 Integrate Local and Global Variability
The variability being defined in a system element rarely lives on its own.
External interactions normally exist and can impact the definition of
variation points. External variability can come in the form of other
variability models driving variation point’s definition (aka using features
from other feature models) or existing variation points in other
models/assets, which should be cascaded to the working model – for
example, a variation point in a system being cascaded to a subsystem.
Example: During the definition of an industrial process (behavior view) on
a SysML model, several variation points are needed. Some of them derived
from variability in the product definition (structural view). In this case, the
product structure variability, including requirements, is cascaded to the
industrial behavior variability.
30.4.4 Use Validation Rules and Automation
To achieve the full potential of variability cascading, automation and
validation checks are essential. They are responsible for the integrity of
complex models and interactions between different elements.
Example: A variable requirement is created to fulfill the feature “wheel
powered” (Figure 30.14). At the functional level, two different functions
were defined to satisfy this requirement, fulfilling the two alternative
features “hydraulic” and “electric.” After the model transformation, if either
“electric” or “hydraulic” were selected, a function would still have a
requirement to satisfy, as the “Wheel Powered” feature will be selected in
either case. This means that those variation points are compatible, and no
inconsistency will be created in this case.
Figure 30.14 Variation points must be created in a consistent manner.
Automation and validation rules are essential to manage this
complexity.
Moreover, the “satisfy” link could be used by an automation to
automatically cascade variation points. In fact, Airbus has developed a
plugin to support MBPLE SysML model variation points cascading and
validation checks to guarantee consistency as described.
30.4.5 Master Variability Path
One of the challenges of variability cascading is the consistency. This is
even harder when working in multimodal environments. In those scenarios,
the variability path shall be mastered. What is meant here is that variability
should be identified at its root, and all the branches of cascading shall be
identified, and the best technique to manage this cascading selected.
30.5 Conclusion: Ready for Take-Off?
Not everything that shines is gold: There is no “silver bullet” when it
comes to larger changes like the ones of PLE and MBPLE. It requires
investments not only in PMT (process, method, tool), but also in
organizational changes. Several pillars are required, such as solid
requirements management, systems engineering, digitalization, and data
management, among others. While the principles of PLE are universal,
aviation has its own specificities.
Variability management: It is at the heart of PLE. It is an extra layer of
information that can be attached to any digital asset. Its traceability shall be
mastered. Consistency here remains the primary challenge.
Variability cascading: It is key to achieving consistency in 150% models.
Cascading is not only applicable to a single context (like an MBSE model
or a 3D model), but also between them. Automation is key here.
Cascading principles: Focus on the core of variability, the correct level of
abstraction, while mastering the cascading path. All of that by using the
minimum amount of variation points, which should not be used for cleaning
or convenience only. Remember that variation point consistency
management can be very hard.
Keeping ahead of the pack: Airbus is a market leader in commercial
aviation. Keeping itself ahead of the pack is no simple task. Maintaining the
current fleet efficiently while innovating for future aircraft is a big
challenge that requires strong digitalization efforts. Initiatives focused on
reuse/optimization, such as MBPLE, are essential to achieving future
business and societal objectives.

Chapter 31
MBDA: Building an End-to-end Model-based
Framework for Product Lines
Dieter Wagner
31.1 Introduction
This chapter introduces how model-based product line engineering
(MBPLE) has been implemented at MBDA. Given the high complexity of
MBDA’s products, their development typically requires more than a decade
of time. An instance of this complexity can be seen in the Command-and-
Control Tactical Operation Center, which is partially covered in the next
sections. Higher complexity, if not properly managed, leads to higher
development costs and time. Consequently, MBDA has initiated an
improvement program with the objective of halving the lead time and
reducing nonrecurring costs by 25%.
As part of the program, a model-based end-to-end development process
known as the “end-to-end model-based engineering (E2EMBE)” process
has been launched. A crucial component of this novel E2EMBE process is
feature-based product line engineering (PLE) in line with the ISO/IEC
26580 (ISO/IEC 2021).
The primary focus of this chapter is “the conjunction” between PLE and
MBSE. To illustrate it, the “Command-and-Control International Product
Line (C2IntlPL)” is used as an example.
31.2 MBDA Motivation for MBPLE’s Adoption
The MBDA Group, a European defense organization, is one of the world’s
three leading missile systems companies and has a significant presence in
Germany, France, England, and Italy. In 2022, MBDA had an employee
base of over 14 000 employees, generating a significant turnover of 4.2
billion euros (MBDA 2024). MBDA’s aim always revolves around
developing products that aptly meet the requirements expressed by MBDA’s
customers. At the same time, the main concern is to produce systems that
meet the different requirements of different military areas, which differ
significantly in terms of their practical application. The MBDA’s product
portfolio includes missiles for applications such as “Meteor,” an air-to-air
missile; “Taurus,” an air-to-ground missile; and “Enforcer,” a hand-held
surface-to-surface missile.
As MBDA’s systems grow increasingly complex, development times have
correspondingly lengthened, often exceeding 10 years on average. In light
of recent geopolitical changes, the urgency for faster product development
has become paramount. Despite this, MBDA’s development process
remains rooted in a document-centric and waterfall approach until 2022.
In fact, in 2010, MBSE was introduced in the company as a revolutionary
improvement since the document-based approach reached saturation, as
illustrated in Figure 31.1. Unfortunately, the MBSE adoption without top–
down sponsorship and support quickly reached its limits. As a result, a new
transformation program was launched in the MBDA in 2022 with the aim of
finally introducing consistent model-based development. E2EMBE
approach lays the foundation of the subsequent model-based product line
introduction, the end-to-end product line engineering ([E2EPLE] is the
MBDA name for MBPLE).
Figure 31.1 MBDA evolution/revolution.
The E2EMBE approach focuses on the key following objectives:
1. Enabling digital continuity: The methodology E2EMBE is based on
different engineering models. The end-to-end indicates that the method
includes the entire product development lifecycle process. SysML
models are predominantly used for MBSE, while UML models are for
software development, MCAD (Dassault Systèms 3DEXPERIENCE
2024) for mechanical designs, ECAD (Siemens 2024) for electrical
and electronic circuit diagrams, and an assortment of other models
created using specialized tools in their respective domains. To ensure
that those individual models are not managed in silos, they interoperate
with each other, thereby establishing what is commonly referred to as a
digital thread. A prominent example of suitable interoperability
standards used at MBDA for this purpose is the Open Services for
Lifecycle Collaboration (OSLC 2023). The connection of all models
through a digital thread enhances the overall coherence and integration
of the product line development process, fostering better
communication and collaboration among the various domains involved
in the project.
2. Enabling a single source of truth: The fundamental principle of
establishing a single source of truth holds outstanding importance
within the E2EMBE methodology, embracing digital continuity. This
procedure is based on the consistent use of the latest references to
assets located in the E2EMBE information model.
3. Enabling controlled reuse: Enabling controlled reuse is a necessity at
MBDA. New programs should be constructed with modular and
reusable frameworks, ensuring flexibility and efficiency in
development. In order to proficiently supervise the reuse process and
monitor the utilization of particular modules across various projects
and iterations, the adoption of a tool-supported methodology becomes
imperative. The emergence of E2EPLE presents itself as a practical
and effective solution for overseeing and regulating controlled reuse
within the organizational framework. E2EPLE offers a comprehensive
approach to managing and enforcing the reuse of modules, thereby
enhancing the overall sustainability and scalability of projects within
the MBDA.
4. Enabling variability: When reusing development resources, a
significant challenge is the need to comply with customer
requirements, which are not fully met by existing implementations. To
tackle this particular challenge effectively, it is key to deploy a well-
thought-out strategy that focuses on enabling variability within the
development process. This method involves adapting existing
functionality by introducing a new variant that is specifically designed
to meet a customer’s individual needs. The efficient management of
these diverse variants is governed by the use of the E2EPLE method.
5. Managing configuration: E2EPLE emerges as a central component in
facilitating the administration of different models and versions,
guaranteeing a structured and effective configuration process. By
leveraging E2EPLE, organizations can enhance their ability to manage
the complexity inherent in E2EMBE, thus optimizing operational
efficiency and ensuring consistency and integration of diverse models
and versions.
31.3 The MBSE Framework as MBDA
Foundation for MBPLE
All the above principles were considered when the E2EPLE methodology
was defined and integrated into the E2EMBE process. To understand how
the MBPLE approach works, it is important to first review the MBSE
portion of the E2EMBE.
Figure 31.2 shows the structure of the MBDA’s MBSE framework. In the
following section, a brief description of the rows and columns of Figure
31.2 is introduced.
Figure 31.2 MBDA MBSE framework.
Operational architecture: It explains the system from the perspective of
the end user. Essentially, it describes how operators interact with the system
by presenting various use cases. It represents the system as a black box
whose external interfaces serve as points of interaction with the outside
world. It provides a comprehensive understanding of the operational aspects
of the system and how users interact with it.
Functional architecture: It outlines the system’s functions in a manner that
is agnostic to any solution within the boundary of the product context. This
approach ensures that there are no constraints imposed by specific solutions
or design parameters. This architectural blueprint also illustrates the
interplay of functions through functional chains, depicting how they
collaborate to achieve desired outcomes. The functions are linked to the
corresponding use cases, creating a cohesive structure that guides the
system’s behavior.
Structural architecture: It covers the realization of one or multiple
possible configurations that meet the requirements set forth by the
functional architecture. This layer presents a spectrum of physical
architectures that can manifest the envisioned functionalities. The functions
outlined in the functional architecture are assigned to specific elements
within the physical architecture, which serve as the building blocks for
implementing the desired functionalities. This allocation ensures a clear
mapping between the abstract functions and their concrete realizations
within the system.
Needs and requirements: They consist of the essential specifications for
each architectural component, guiding the assignment of model elements to
ensure alignment with stakeholder expectations and project objectives.
States: They hold varying significance across the three different
architectural structures due to the unique ways in which data is processed
and interpreted within each system. Each architecture brings forth distinct
methods and algorithms that influence the interpretation and utilization of
the statistics presented in the column.
Core elements: They include a comprehensive list of essential components
tailored to each specific type of architecture, serving as a key reference
point for understanding the fundamental building blocks required for the
development and implementation of the corresponding architectural
structure.
Scenarios: They provide a detailed representation of the chronological
sequence of activities and functions required to implement the various
activities derived from use cases and functional chains, thus offering a
comprehensive overview of the temporal progression within the system
architecture.
The above-described MBDA MBSE framework can be applied recursively
on any level of the system, as shown in Figure 31.3. This means that the
system-level model is decomposed into subsystems, and, in turn, the
subsystem-level models are broken down into equipment.
Figure 31.3 MBDA MBSE framework hierarchy.
31.4 The E2EPLE Methodology
To facilitate the implementation of the PLE combined with MBSE, it is
important to establish a precise scope for the product line. An illustrative
example of this implementation is demonstrated in the upcoming sections,
through a simplified subset of the “C2IntlPL.”
Figure 31.4 shows the hierarchical structure of the product lines,
showcasing a collection of distinct product lines that are interconnected and
interdependent. As each product line offers a range of products, these
member products are used as “building blocks” for the parent product line.
Figure 31.4 Product line hierarchy.
The product line model, realized with PTC pure::variants (PTC
pure::variants 2024) shown in Figure 31.5 is organized into folders to make
navigation within the model easier. The structure of the model is designed
per level to facilitate the exploration of the product line model, and it is
explained below.
Figure 31.5 Product line model.
Assets folder: It contains references to the E2E models. These references
indicate the exact location of the shared asset superset and unshareable asset
in their respective versions.
Feature catalog folder: It contains at least one feature catalog (aka feature
model). Depending on the project’s needs, some features from the main
feature catalog can also be outsourced to a separate feature catalog.
Feature catalog: The feature catalog in Figure 31.6 contains an additional
semantic to support the MBDA MBSE framework.
Figure 31.6 MBDA feature catalog.
The additional semantic is based on specialized features, which are
described in the following section.
Package feature: The inclusion of a package feature is essential for
enhancing the organization of models within a product line. The package
feature serves the purpose of structuring models in a more efficient manner.
It is important to note that package features are assigned to any shared
assets superset elements. Therefore, the recommendation is to align the
MBDA MBSE framework model with the feature catalog structure as
closely as possible. This alignment facilitates a more coherent and
systematic approach to model organization, thereby enhancing the overall
orientation within the system.
Capability feature: It plays a crucial role in capturing the diverse
capabilities of the system. These capabilities are essentially the fundamental
building blocks of the system’s functionalities. The primary objective here
is to develop a set of capabilities owned by MBDA, enabling reusability
across various projects. However, it is also important to highlight that there
are customer-specific capabilities that are not intended to be shared across
different projects.
Use case feature: Within the MBDA MBSE framework, the use case
feature represents a traditional method of delineating the system’s usage
capabilities from the user’s perspective. For each capability, at least a use
case is provided to articulate MBDA’s intended utilization of the
capabilities. In practice, customers often have unique requirements
regarding how they utilize these capabilities, leading to multiple use cases
associated with each capability. This results in a diverse collection of use
cases, with some owned by MBDA and others by the customers,
showcasing a high level of variability in the system.
Constraint feature: It serves the purpose of capturing various constraints
originating from customers, national legislation, standards, or MBDA’s
product strategy. These constraints play a critical role in narrowing down
the solution space within the system. For instance, the feature catalog
example showcases a constraint from MBDA’s product strategy, limiting
the supported communication formats to three options (Link15, ADatP-3,
USMTF) for any given product.
Functionality feature: It comprises the atomic building blocks that form
the basis for constructing capabilities within the system. These
functionalities are interconnected to define the behavior of capabilities,
forming functional chains that start with an incoming event and conclude
with an outgoing event.
Design choice feature: To cater to the varying needs of different customers,
functional chains may exhibit differences in functionality or fidelity levels.
As a result, the design choice feature is introduced to capture the variability
present in functions and functional chains.
Table 31.1 indicates which specialized feature type is used in the different
architectures.
TABLE 31.1
Cross-reference feature type versus architecture layer.
Architecture Operational Functional Structural
Feature type
Package feature X X X
Capability feature X
Use case feature X
Constraint feature X X X
Functionality feature X
Design choice feature X X
The selection is based on best practices resulting from various internal
proof-of-concepts.
In addition to the elements described above, it is key to mention other
components that form the so-called product line model. These are:
Provided products folder: It contains the product portfolio of the product
line. Each product in the portfolio is defined by its own bill of features.
Bill of features: It contains product specifications for this product. The bill
of features (aka feature configuration) is the selection of features needed to
fulfill the customer’s needs.
Questionnaire folder: It contains an optional questionnaire designed to
capture customer needs and preferences, fostering a customer-centric
approach to product development. The questionnaire aims to gather
valuable insights and feedback from customers. The answers to the
questionnaire are integrated into the corresponding Bill of Features for
further analysis and decision-making processes. The inclusion of customer
feedback in the product line development cycle enhances customer
satisfaction and ensures that products align with market demands and
preferences.
It is important to note that, due to contractual obligations, not all assets
within the model are shareable, highlighting the need for secure and
controlled access to certain elements of the product line model. This
restriction ensures compliance with contractual agreements for handling
sensitive information within the product line model and highlights the
importance of data security and confidentiality in PLE.
To implement the variability described above in the product line model, two
additional elements that are at the core of the MBPLE approach are
introduced as follows:
Shared assets superset: It contains all development assets needed during
the product development cycle. The MBSE model is part of the shared
assets superset. All assets need to be connected to the features or their
variations. These connection points are called variation points.
Variation points: To enable PLE for MBSE models, it is essential to
incorporate variability as an integral part of the MBSE model. The
specific location within the MBSE model where variability is
implemented and referred to as the “variation point.” Each variation
point is precisely linked to the corresponding feature of a feature
model, ensuring a systematic approach to managing variability.
At MBDA we defined three distinct types of variation points:
“atomic,” marked as (1), “starting,” marked as (2), and “propagated,”
marked as (V) variation point, as illustrated in Figure 31.7.
Figure 31.7 “Atomic,” “starting” and “propagated” variation points.
The figure illustrates a simplified (collapsed hierarchy) perspective of the
different variation points within the MBSE framework. This visual
representation aids in understanding the strategic placement and
relationship of variation points in the overall model structure. By
categorizing variation points into specific types, such as atomic, starting,
and propagated, modelers can effectively capture and represent the
variability. Leveraging these variation points facilitates the creation of
adaptable and customizable MBSE framework models that can
accommodate diverse requirements and configurations. Understanding the
nuances of each variation point type is essential for implementing PLE
successfully in MBSE models and ensuring the efficient management of
variability throughout the system development process.
Atomic variation point: The utilization of the atomic variation point is
specifically targeted toward the management of variability for functional
and structural architecture exclusively. The term “atomic” signifies the
absence of any dependent child model elements, a characteristic that is
uniquely viable within the structural architecture domain. This concept
holds significance because the structural architecture will be populated by
the resulting products of the child product, thus making the “atomic”
variation point a crucial design decision in constructing the building blocks
of the system. As illustrated in Figure 31.7, the “safety” feature is explicitly
linked to a hardware element in the structural architecture, serving as a
prime illustration of a design choice feature within the architecture. The
design choice feature and constraint feature are effectively implemented
through the utilization of “atomic” variation points, highlighting a
sophisticated approach to architectural design and decision-making.
Starting variation point: The variation point, known as starting, consists
of a model element that is linked to other model elements and is either
within the same architectural level or spans the hierarchical structure of
architectural levels.
The starting variation point is strategically positioned within the MBSE
model, typically associated with other elements like capabilities, use cases,
or functional chains. This strategic placement ensures that the foundational
aspects of the variations are well-defined and aligned with the overarching
objectives of the project.
In the illustrative scenario shown in Figure 31.7, the presence of two
distinct “starting” variation points (marked as 2) is highlighted, each linked
to specific features within the operational architecture. One use case is
linked to the feature “planning,” while the other use case is linked to the
feature “aircraft,” a feature variant of the feature “control.”
Essential to the insertion of the starting variation point is the absence of any
break in the model dependency, the meta-model, which extends from the
operational architecture to the structural architecture. This crucial aspect
ensures that a capability or use case can be easily extracted from a 150%
MBSE model. By conducting a model transformation (extraction), it is a
prerequisite that all model elements essential for the advancement of a
capability or use case are retained.
Propagated variation points: To facilitate the PLE factory in identifying
the appropriate elements, the concept of “propagated” variation points is
assigned to all associated model elements pertaining to a capability or use
case. This strategy ensures that the necessary components are effectively
transmitted and preserved throughout the transformation process.
31.5 Not Only MBSE Assets: The ECAD
Variability Management Within MBPLE
In the E2EMBE method, the models for software, mechanical, electrical,
and electronic domains also contribute to the E2EMBE information model.
As already mentioned, the MBDA framework focuses on the realization of
a central MBSE model with connections to the other engineering models. It
should be noted that the MBDA framework model contains equipment-level
software and hardware that are modeled as low-level components in the
structural architecture. These low-level blocks must be then replaced with
other domain-specific engineering models. For this purpose, the MBDA
E2EMBE method offers a collection of connectors that enable this
interchange.
To enable the reuse of software and hardware, variability management is
required. The E2EPLE method offers two different approaches based on the
availability of PLE connectors:
If a PLE connector (e.g., E3Cabel) is available, the management of
variability within the ECAD tools is done by adding the variation
points directly in the corresponding models. The structural architecture
is populated from the ECAD using the MBDA E2EMBE interchange
functions.
If no PLE connector (e.g., Mentor and Matlab/Simulink) is available,
the management of variability is done by adding the variation points
directly in the structure architecture. The engineering domain models
are populated with the contents of the structural architecture using the
MBDA E2EMBE exchange functions and not in the domain-specific
model.
31.6 E2EPLE Tool Chain
In the MBDA environment, two MBSE tools, IBM Rhapsody (IBM 2024)
and the open-source Capella (Capella 2024), are used for system analysis
and design. The application of the MBDA MBSE framework is the same for
both tools, ensuring a consistent approach and methodology. PTC
pure::variants is used as a PLE tool to realize the MBDA E2EPLE method.
PTC pure::variants support both IBM Rhapsody and Capella, enabling a
similar application of the same PLE techniques and strategies. PTC
Pure::variants linked to the MBSE tools via individual bridges. The bridge
provides functions for adding features to model elements, previewing
resulting models based on a concrete bill of features, and transforming or
generating the final MBSE framework model.
While in Capella, feature propagation occurs automatically through an
internal dependency tree that links elements from the higher-level model to
those in the lower-level model; IBM Rhapsody does not support automatic
feature propagation. To address this limitation, MBDA has developed an
IBM Rhapsody “add-on” that enables feature propagation within the tool.
Once a higher-level parent model element is linked to a feature, the add-on
initiates the propagation process and assigns the same feature as
“propagated variation points” to all lower-level dependent model elements.
31.7 A Real-Life Example for the E2EPLE
The following two figures show an example using both IBM Rhapsody and
PTC pure::variants.
In Figure 31.8, two specific use cases, namely “Plan Mission” and “Plan
Communication,” are presented. Each of those individual use cases is
linked to its respective constraints through the utilization of the “use case”
features “PlanMission” and “PlanCommunication,” which are shown in
Figure 31.9. For the sake of clarity and simplicity in the illustration, the use
cases have been associated with two generalized functions. The use case
labeled as “Plan Mission” is connected to both “FunctionA” and
“FunctionB.” The other use case, “Plan Communication” is solely linked to
“FunctionB.” Upon the propagation of the use case “Plan Mission” using
the IBM Rhapsody add-on, the resulting “propagated” variation point
labeled as “PlanMission” is attached to both functions. Similarly, if the use
case “Plan Communication” undergoes propagation, the function
“FunctionB” will then acquire the “propagated” variation point associated
with “PlanCommunication.” It is important to note that the underlying logic
here operates under the principle of logical disjunction, denoted by the OR
operator. Consequently, the ultimate “propagated” variation point is
determined as “(PlanMission) OR (PlanCommunication).” This implies that
both use cases, being propagated, can use the inclusion of the corresponding
variation point within the functions.
Figure 31.8 Propagation logic “starting” variation point.
Figure 31.9 Propagation logic “starting” variation point.
In Figure 31.10, there are two functional chains, namely “Functional Chain
Variant A” and “Functional Chain Variant B,” both linked to their respective
design choice features, “SafetyVariantA” and “SafetyVariantB.” Upon
propagating the use case “Protect Air Field” to both functional chains, it
exhibits a propagated variation point denoted as “ProtectAirField.”
Figure 31.10 Propagation logic “atomic” variation point.
The add-on establishes a PTC pure::variants’ constraint for “Functional
Chain Variant A” as (ProtectAirField) AND (SafetyVariantA) and for
“Functional Chain Variant B” as (ProtectAirField) AND (SafetyVariantB).
This logical framework empowers PTC pure::variants to derive a MBSE
model that exclusively incorporates one of the two functional chains.
Figure 31.11 provides a visual depiction of a product preview wherein the
design choice feature selected is SafetyVariantB, resulting in the graying
out of SafetyVariantA and its corresponding anchored constraint.
Figure 31.11 Preview of PLE factory configured MBSE framework
model.
Since MBPLE does not focus only on MBSE assets, the following part
shows a simple extension of the presented approach for an ECAD model.
To import an ECAD model into IBM Rhapsody, an additional IBM
Rhapsody add-on was developed. It reads an XML file generated by
Xpedition Designer, which describes the electrical hardware. The XML file
is partitioned into three sections:
Signal group definition: It defines all relevant electrical signals used in the
ECAD design. The RhGUID is an IBM Rhapsody unique identifier to
recognize the corresponding MBSE model element. In addition, all signals
may contain attributes such as tolerances to define the signal.
Here is an extraction of the XML file section:
<signalgroup RhGUID="GUID" name="Power">
<signal RhGUID="GUID" name="V240" type="Power"/>
<attribute RhGUID="GUID"
name="Tolerance" value="+-10V"
<signal RhGUID="GUID" name="V400" type="Power"/>
<signal RhGUID="GUID" name="V15" type="Power"/>
</signalgroup>
Assembly definition: It defines the collection of assemblies for the
electrical boards.
In the example below, the device “TOC” contains two variations of
mainboards and two variations of IOBoards. The device and each assembly
contain their connectors called “ports.”
The Ports are SysML proxy ports, which allow the nesting of other proxy
ports.
Note: Due to the variability, the boards of the same type have the same set
of connectors. The variability is based on signal groups and signals defined
by the netlist.
Here is an extraction of the XML file section:
<device RhGUID="GUID" name="TOC">
<assembly RhGUID="GUID" name="MainboardA">
<port RhGUID="GUID" name="X1"/>
<port RhGUID="GUID" name="J1"/>
</assembly>
<assembly RhGUID="GUID" name="MainboardB">
<port RhGUID="GUID" name="X1"/>
<port RhGUID="GUID" name="J1"/>
</assembly>
<assembly RhGUID="GUID" name="IOBoradA">
<port RhGUID="GUID" name="J1"/>
<port RhGUID="GUID" name="X2"/>
</assembly>
<assembly RhGUID="GUID" name="IOBoradB">
<port RhGUID="GUID" name="J1"/>
<port RhGUID="GUID" name="X2"/>
</assembly>
<port RhGUID="GUID" name="X1"/>
<port RhGUID="GUID" name="X2"/>
</device>
Netlist: It contains the individual connections of signals between pins on
connectors. In the example, the external connector of the device “TOC” has
the variability of two different power signals, “V240 and V400”. Depending
on the capability of the mainboards, the power is connected to the right
voltage signal.
Here is an extraction of the XML file section:
<netlist>
<net fromDevice="TOC" fromPin="1" fromPort="X1"
signal="V240" signalgroup="Power"
toAssembly="MainboardA" toPin="1" toPort="X1"/>
<net fromDevice="TOC" fromPin="1" fromPort="X1"
signal="V400" signalgroup="Power"
toAssembly="MainboardB" toPin="1" toPort="X1"/>
</netlist>
The import IBM Rhapsody add-on reads the XML and automatically
creates the corresponding SysML elements for the structural architecture.
Figure 31.12 shows an ECAD-imported structural architecture model. The
architecture was enriched by constraints that contain the relevant features of
the feature catalog. For demonstration, the preview of PTC pure::variants is
activated to show a product that contains only MotherboarsB and
IOBoardA. After using the factory configurator of PTC pure::variants, all
unused connectors and blocks are removed.
Figure 31.12 Preview of PLE factory configured ECAD imported
structural architecture model.
31.8 The Journey of E2EPLE at MBDA
Continues
Since 2022, MBDA has embarked on a transformation toward establishing
an E2EPLE and E2EMBE integrated approach to support the next
generation of missiles and weapon systems. The presented approach based
on MBPLE showed significant benefits, and it is a central pillar of MBDA’s
improvement program. Examples of capabilities developed within the
E2EPLE framework, such as capturing and organizing consistently the
variability within a product line model or connecting the feature catalog
with the MBSE model and the ECAD model, are essential in developing
multiple variants of MBDA’s systems.
The MBPLE approach is currently in the rollout phase and has been
recently adopted for the first products. Due to nondisclosure agreements
between MBDA and its customers, no further information can be provided
regarding its adoption.
Along with the successful outcomes of MBPLE at MBDA, three main
challenges arose during its rollout and are highlighted as follows:
Introducing MBPLE to the folks: Introducing MBPLE into a non-PLE
organization requires big efforts and leads to problems in the distribution of
responsibility. For example, the project departments within MBDA have
always acted as the end-to-end developers of finished products. Following
this novel approach, their responsibility is mainly limited to the integration,
verification, and validation of the products. To better address this challenge,
MBDA is working on a new business case to transform MBDA into a PLE-
enabled company in the long term. As part of the business case, other
development areas such as electrical, electronic, mechanical, and software
will be introduced to MBPLE.
Using variation in MBSE models: Understanding where and how a
variation should be implemented in the MBSE model using the variation
points presented with limited efforts.
Validating the product line model: The validation of a product line model
can only be carried out on a transformed model (product model), which in
turn means additional work. This area requires more investigation and
necessitates better support from the existing tools landscape.
Annex 1: MBPLE Terms at MBDA.
Term Description
Variation point Location within the shared assed superset where the
variation is located.
End-to-end The end-to-end model-based engineering (E2EMBE)
model-based provides a digital continuity of a single source of truth
engineering development assets for the whole development
lifecycle.
E2EMBE The E2EMBE information is a set of connected models
information model produced by different development domains.
Lower-level A lower-level product line is a stand-alone product line
product line that continues its product to the direct upper-level
product line.
Analysis on upper-level product line is a stand-alone
Upper-level
product line that provides high-level features and
product line
consumes products of lower-level product lines as
Parent product building blocks.
