Chapter 32
Thales: A Long Road to MBPLE – from Initial
Conception to Completion
Agnes Guiblin
32.1 Introduction
In 2023, Thales Group is present in 68 countries worldwide and employs 77
000 people. Thales Group is composed of three core business segments:
aerospace and space, cybersecurity and digital identity, and defense and
security. Thales surface radar (SRA) is part of the latter segment and is a
global player in the naval, ground, and air radar surveillance domains. SRA
has an internal footprint with nine sites in six countries. Our radar sensors
provide our customers with the right information on all flying, driving,
walking, or sailing objects, allowing them to take the right decision at any
critical moment. From the ground and ocean surface to the stratosphere, we
help our customers make the lives of their citizens and soldiers safer and
ensure the protection of critical sites, borders, coastal areas, oceans, and
airspace. More than 4000 Thales radars are in operation in over 30 countries
worldwide (Thales 2024).
At SRA, product line engineering (PLE) has been deployed for over fifteen
years on ground master radars (GMR). Our radars are military products
designed mainly for air surveillance, but they can also be used for
operational engagement. They detect and ensure the tracking of highly
maneuverable targets (aircraft, helicopter, etc.) in order to provide an air
situation to a remote or local command and control center. Our radars can
be installed on a dedicated site or on mobile carriers (Thales 2024).
Using PLE concepts to implement a market-driven product policy and
strategic reuse was both an opportunity and a challenge. PLE relies heavily
on the development of shared elements, gradually adapted to new projects.
It requires finding the right balance among operational capability,
development complexity (e.g. processes, human interactions, architecture
for reuse and implementation), radar quality, and increased productivity. A
central role differentiating PLE engineering concepts from other reuse
concepts is the explicit management of variability and product instances as
an integral part of the approach. This aligns reuse with the reality that “as
is” reuse is often very difficult. For instance, requirements for new products
often result in additional or slightly different needs compared to previous
products. Knowing and taking into account these new requirements from
the start is almost impossible. The primary capability of PLE is to account
for the variability of engineering elements that are subject to continuous
evolution in the radar life cycle. This practice allows us to remain
competitive in our market segment.
At the beginning of our PLE transformation, Thales decided to renew our
product portfolio and invested in the development of a platform based on a
composable product architecture using building blocks (BBs). One of the
platform’s objectives was to reduce nonrecurring costs by massively using
software or hardware BBs in our radars and also reducing the number of
development activities. The development of our radars is still realized today
following this composable approach and shared across all military surface
radars that have similar architectures. In 2011, the Ground Master 400 radar
(GM 400) was already a commercial success with four large contracts.
However, the team was still organized in “project mode.” Ground Master
200 radar (GM 200) was in the development phase, and Ground Master 60
radar (GM 60) was in the prospect phase. At that time, the SRA economic
results were not so good, leading to two main issues:
“How to secure the reuse at the level of the Ground Master product
line”?
“How to ensure the qualification achievement when using a BBs
platform for a radar with high variability and many parameters”?
32.2 Transitioning to PLE: Dealing with
Different Reuse Approaches
To address those challenges, the team defined six “product reuse
categories” (A–F) for the Thales Group as a means to describe the level of
reuse of a product with respect to the related market needs. Categories A
and B are more related to the project than to the product nature. Categories
C and D are based on the product’s characteristics and its modular design.
Categories E and F are implemented with product variability to a managed
set of features. These categories are described as follows:
A: “Full-custom development.” It corresponds to developing each
project as a new one, and it applies to markets with a single instance.
B: “Copy and modify.” It corresponds to cloning an existing project
baseline to create a new one (no relation is maintained between these
two), dedicated to markets with few instances, mostly specific.
C: “Reuse BBs.” It corresponds to defining a product baseline with a
list of BBs (each having its own baseline). The project baseline
implements a set of BBs. This is dedicated to markets with a medium
number of instances with a combination of specificities and reuse.
D: “Reference architecture and commonalities.” It corresponds to
implementing at the project baseline the reference architecture and
instantiating a set of BBs. This is dedicated to markets with a medium
number of instances with a combination of specifics and reuse.
E: “Managed variability and derivation process.” It corresponds to
feature modeling implementation with explicit variability management
and derivation process. This is dedicated to markets with a large
number of instances marginally specific. It also allows customer-
specific developments.
F: “Managed variability and derivation process.” It corresponds to
the direct reuse of products in project solutions. This is dedicated to
rather stable markets with a large number of instances with no specific
requirements. It does not allow any customer specifics.
The transition from reuse category C to D with the surface radar 3D (SR3D)
Platform was difficult due to the flexibility required. The main objective
was to develop a large, scalable product, but it ultimately led to an increase
in the number of parameters in our radars. For GM radars, the increased
complexity led to a radar setting with over 3000 parameters, complicating
the SR3D platform qualification and reuse of generics BBs qualified was
not efficient at the radar level.
At SRA, the positioning of the GM radar market segment was clear: “We
needed to manage the product variabilities and allow specific
developments.” However, at that time, no “internal best practices” existed at
Thales, so we looked at practices coming from the automotive industry,
even if the GM radar production part was not comparable to a traditional
car (e.g. over twenty different radars are produced per year). We were also
interested in academic publications, especially from Carnegie Mellon
University (Kang, Cohen, and Hess et al. 1990), and we started
collaborating with other Thales units. The transition to product reuse
category E was underway, and it generated important cultural and
organizational changes, which affected several practices within our way of
doing systems engineering. For instance, we developed a GM radar
roadmap with a model-based approach and with a top–down thinking; we
shared the models with all the stakeholders; we deployed the derivation
process from product assets to projects. However, even if the model-based
dimension was already there, we decided to focus first on the PLE
implementation.
32.3 The Formalization of the Variability
Management Approach
During the multistep transition to PLE, we worked on the formalization of
the new engineering approach. We started by identifying the variation
points of the GM400 surveillance radar with the product line manager, the
product line architect, and the project engineering managers. We analyzed
and refined the high-level feature descriptions of our product variability in
order to obtain an engineering feature model able to express the variability
impacts at the “domain engineering” level, according to the ISO/IEC 26550
(ISO/IEC 2015). For example, in the GM radar missions, we identified
variability in the surveillance modes of air defense: “long range,” “short
range,” and “extended air defense” for the detection of tactical missiles. For
GM radar functions, we identified the functionalities related to mobility: a
radar can be “fixed” or “mobile.” We also identified and classified radar for
aircraft types, such as IFF-type secondary antennas, which could be added
to specific models only. At the interface level, we identified the
functionalities related to communication with “remote,” “local command,”
and “control centers,” as well as the predisposition for the customer’s
equipment in the radar shelter. Proceeding this way allowed us to establish
our first GM radar variability analysis. Figure 32.1 summarizes an example
of the type of elements that were at the center of the variability analysis.
Figure 32.1 Partial scope of the variability analysis.
First, we formalized this analysis by collecting the so-called “variabilities.”
We introduced variability representations that allowed us to organize them –
through parent-child relations-, characterize them – mandatory, alternative,
option, OR features – and add dependencies between variabilities –
requires, conflicts, and other constraints. This resulted in a GM radar
feature tree.
In the second stage, following the internal product roadmap and the first
customer contracts, we selected the variability for each project. At that
stage, we had not adopted a specific PLE tool, so we focused on
consolidating the approach and worked essentially with two types of
representations (in table form in Excel and graphic form). Even if it was not
tool-supported yet, such formalization produced several improvements.
For example, we used to spend a lot of time in meetings discussing the
concepts of variability, mainly because people shared different points of
view. By publishing the GM radar feature model, we shared the same
reference with all the stakeholders, and communication became less
conflictual. It brought consistency – same feature vocabulary, same
roadmap, etc. – between the different stakeholders at offers, solutions,
software, hardware development, industry, and service levels, as well as
across projects. The feature model became a common reference to organize
and monitor the GM product line, an engineering concept validated by the
product line architect.
Before the new standard ISO/IEC 26580 (ISO/IEC 2021), the SRA team
understood the key capability of a feature-based PLE approach. Since many
variations simultaneously impact many assets and are subject to ongoing
evolution, the feature model is an efficient way to share within the whole
organization “what” the product line variability is and “how” it will or
should evolve.
After introducing the foundation, it is time to have a look at the process we
adopted. At the beginning of the transition, we were organized in what we
call “reuse project mode.” This means that when a new contract was signed,
we looked for the project that best fitted the purpose of the contract
specification and then proceeded by copying, pasting, and modifying the
original (i.e. clone-and-own). This was often a source of error since the
corrections on a project are rarely transferred to the others. This approach
was “doing the job” with a few projects running in parallel, but it became
unsustainable as soon as many more projects started running in parallel. The
management part was also a challenge for the team. This led to the
definition of a five-step variability management process. The process is
summarized below.
Step 1: It consists of “the variability analysis.” Features – equivalent
to a functional characteristic of the product and that represents a viable
unit of variability – are identified and organized hierarchically with
variability types as mandatory, optional, alternative, OR, including
feature dependencies as relations or constraints. This consists of the
feature model, a visual representation of permissible variability
amongst properties of a product within a product line.
Step 2: It consists of what we call “the project configuration.”
Features from the feature model are selected to specify a product
variant.
Step 3: It consists of “variability mapping,” which is where the
variation points are identified into the product asset and linked to the
related features with the support of logical equations and also
calculations.
Step 4: It consists of the “derivation process.” The derivation is
triggered by applying the features selected for the project to the
product assets to obtain the project instantiation results. Project-
specific requests will be developed at the level of the project branch.
Step 5: It consists of the “specificity integration in the feature model.”
This means that if any specifics, developed within the customer
project, present a potential for the product line then these will be
integrated into the feature model after a favorable decision of the
Configuration Control Board (CCB).
32.4 The Advent of a PLE-driven
Organization
To implement the PLE approach described above, the “engineering
development environments” should also be based on PLE principles. The
PLE reference model emphasizes the key differences between product (aka
product line) and project engineering and the need for two development
cycles:
First, the product engineering scope has the objective to define and
implement commonalities and variability of the product line. This –
domain engineering – is where the variability at the asset level is
introduced (with feature model, requirements, modeling, radar
standard configuration, etc.).
Second, the project engineering scope has the purpose of meeting the
customer’s needs by exploiting the variability and assets of the product
line via derivation – application engineering – as well as by creating
specifics if necessary, as shown in Figure 32.2.
Figure 32.2 Thales SRA level orchestrated development environment.
Based on the assets of the private configuration space at the domain level, a
set of integrated tools, internally named “Orchestra suite,” has been defined
at Thales Group level to allow users to initiate the derivation process. A
private system engineering data organization has been established to allow
the teams to apply the rules of the SRA development “Orchestra suite,”
usage. Examples of the major documents and assets mapped in the PLE
scope include:
The technical description document is used by bid managers to frame
the offer. If the customer requests a specific need in relation to the
feature model, the bid manager identifies that there will be an
additional cost in terms of development and/or qualification.
The specification documents and the functional configuration audit,
used by the engineering delivery manager frame the radar
performances and control the impacts on the qualification of the
product and projects.
The definition breakdown structure, used by the industry, generates the
applicable configuration of the project already in the early project
phase.
The test plan and test sheets, used by integration, verification,
validation, and qualification management, generate the evidence that
the requirement is met by the product – qualification done for a
standard configuration in a radar state⇔versioning –if a specific
requirement exists for the project a qualification complement must be
done.
The implementation of variability management within the asset scope
presented above has saved us time on the radar schedule. For instance,
during the orientation review phase, the project documents are now
automatically generated from the instantiated product documentation.
32.5 No PLE Exists Without a Solid
Governance
In support of this new way of developing products, we also put in place the
incremental standard configuration strategy. This allows us to validate
perimeter and performance improvement, obsolescence management, and
product financing from cumulative project taxes and self-funding budgets.
The engineering team relies on the configuration produced by the derivation
process to “freeze” the scope and perform the radar qualification.
Consequently, the bid manager’s act within a specific framework to come
up with an increased reuse rate in the radar division. After several difficult
years, we optimized the project lead time and finally realized savings,
allowing also budget reduction in the call-for-tender phase. Nevertheless,
competition has also come to our market segment, so this triggered the
necessity to further improve the standard product and, at the same time, to
limit the product options. This led to the introduction of the concept of
“activable” options in standard product configuration for sales negotiation
and export authorization. These are radar-integrated options that give the
bid manager the flexibility to negotiate with the customer without
impacting product development or qualification.
To enforce this new process, a new business line, the GMR, is created. Its
primary responsibility is to establish a governance framework that can be
synthesized with the following expression: “The anchorage to product leads
the business, the standard configurations lead the radar qualification, and
the projects follow.”
Some of the governance rules are here summarized:
The product line cannot be implemented without the support and
involvement of the management whose objective is the product.
One manager for products and projects is appointed.
The product team leads the developments. New product features
introduced into the feature model are approved in the CCB.
The product team’s budgets come from projects and self-funding.
Communication and lobbying are necessary. A dedicated resource to
deploy this PLE process is appointed to support engineering teams.
Project teams focus on customer interface and specific deliveries.
32.6 Toward MBPLE: The Improvement of the
PLE Approach Continues
During the transition to the PLE approach, we always sought to increase
productivity. One enabler was to revise the existing radar parameterization
scope. The complexity we faced consisted of managing over 3000
parameters that belonged to 40 – too large- software BBs. We applied PLE
concepts and looked for variation points. One might think that the
complexity of variability – the number of different product instances
possible – grows more or less exponentially – each new optional element
doubles the number of possible instances –, which quickly leads to a
situation where testing all possible instances is impossible. Even the
configuration of the variation points gets very complex due to all the
dependencies to control. However, the misconception that variation points
create additional complexity can be easily proved. A huge reduction in
complexity can be achieved since several dozens of features may control
thousands of technical variations. This reduced complexity, in our case,
resulted in simpler parameterization, less complex testing of reusable BBs,
and consequently simpler maintenance. We managed to control those 3000
parameters with only 70 key features that were part of the feature model.
Radar’s parameterization cost has been reduced from 120 to 4 hours, and
consistency between projects has been increased, without mentioning that
no dedicated radar experts are needed any longer to manage the right setting
of parameters. This is a simple example of how a well-established PLE
approach can make a difference.
We have also established a solid engineering data model structure,
containing the specification reference, the PBS CI (configuration item
structure of the product) and PBS FCH (functional chain structure), the IVV
(integration, verification, and validation) reference, and the changes
reference. This engineering data model is composed of different levels:
system needs, logical architecture and physical architecture with embedded
variability, and follows the Arcadia methodology (Voirin 2023). The
increasing focus on the model-based approach – the shift toward (model-
based product line engineering) MBPLE – and the introduction of a shared
information model allow us to better control the changes of the functional
chains and to better manage the technical debt. Since the GMR is a product
line that has almost 15 years of existence, it is key to share the radar
knowledge and to promote the development in co-engineering while being
independent of the tools used at system, hardware, or software levels. A
good publication that describes the synergy between PLE and MBSE at
Thales is “A value-driven, integrated approach to model-based product line
engineering” (Navas et al. 2021).
On the tools side, we also sought for improvements. For instance, we
replaced Gears (BigLever 2024) with PTC pure::variants (PTC
pure::variants 2024) to adhere to the decision taken by Thales Group and
benefit from the one “Orchestra suite.” Pure::variants, in fact, better meet
the needs of Thales Group and secured the new GM AESA development
while ensuring the delivery of parameter files for different GM radar
products of GMR product lines.
This also involves setting up team coordination meetings to take into
account the needs of the projects, the applicable versions of the BBs, and
changes approved in the CCB. The feature model facilitates the entire
collaboration process. To address the complex models of other engineering
teams, we put in place the use of multiple interdependent and consistent
feature models. For example, our radar solution is decomposed into several
BBs, and some BBs have their own “local” feature model. As part of the
composition of the radar, local feature models are connected to the higher-
level radar feature model using dependencies and calculations. These
reusable and configurable BBs are used in multiple product lines. In order
to use a BB in a product line, the configuration of the BB is connected and
linked at least to the radar-level feature model by some means. There are
various approaches to achieve this outcome.
These include:
The use of constraints and relations to connect features of different
models.
The use of preconfigured instances of the respective BB – providing a
set of named alternative configurations or even just a single BB
configuration – to be included in the product line assembly.
A combination of both, by defining preconfigurations that provide a set
of feature selections, etc. but are still leaving the final configuration of
the BBs open to either manual selection or additional constraints and
relations.
We also changed our product life cycle management tool and enterprise
resources planner (ERP). We looked for variation points in the breakdown
structures, and the feature model allowed us to define a standard applicable
configuration as early as possible, not only to improve productivity but also
to simplify the generic product definition breakdown structure.
The project’s definition breakdown structure is now automatically
generated from the product definition breakdown structure derivation. This
is enabled by the connection of both product and asset lifecycle
management (PALM) (Thales PALM 2024) and ERP-integrated tools with
PTC pure::variants (PTC pure::variants 2024).
The final transition to an MBPLE approach allowed us to work on over 20
000 existing data modules.
We saw several improvements brought by the introduction of a feature-
based PLE approach. However, it is worth mentioning that using feature
models across product lines calls for proper change management and well-
defined responsibilities regarding the evolution of the product line. The
“price to pay” is the maintenance effort and potential duplication of features
in different feature models, since independent reuse in multiple scenarios
demands large amounts of independence of the configuration of the BBs.
On the organizational side, the main improvement focused on the
interactions between the product engineering activities (aka PLE) and the
Project engineering activities in line with their respective Lifecycles, which
are usually not synchronized. Figure 32.3 outlines the respective contents of
product engineering (“domain”) and project engineering (“application”) in a
dual DDQS (define, develop, qualify the solution) process representation.
Each step (orient, design, etc…) refers to activities either within the product
development lifecycle or within the project development lifecycle (like the
derivation process). Those activities cover the PLE practices. We also
leveraged the approach to support the “customer service,” for instance, by
linking the feature model to our customer service tool environment in order
to automatically generate the customer’s user documentation for the project.
Figure 32.3 Product domain engineering and project application
engineering.
32.7 (MB)PLE Benefits and
Recommendations Based on SRA’s
Experience
The PLE and most recent MBPLE deployment’s benefits and lessons
learned on GMR are considerable and helped the SRA team to develop
competitive, attractive, and sustainable radars. Some of the experienced
benefits are listed as follows:
1. Lead time reduction: This covers the proposals, the launch of the
projects – automatic derivation of generic documents – and the radar
manufacturing – thanks to the introduction of standard configurations.
2. Quality improvement: Thanks to variability management, better
coherence between projects exists and a reuse of qualified
configurations is in place.
3. Cost reduction: In both recurring costs – thanks to BBs and standard
subassemblies reuse – and nonrecurring costs – thanks to variability
management.
4. Attractiveness improvement: Better features and higher
performances at the product level and variability management allow to
meet customer needs more easily – the engineering efforts of projects
are mainly focused on customer specifics.
To achieve such benefits it is important to take into consideration some key
recommendations that are grouped below for MBPLE adoption, feature
models usage, product development vs project development, and
architecture and BBs.
MBPLE adoption recommendations can be summarized as follows:
Do not underestimate the weight of the architecture in the MBPLE
success. For instance, choose mature architects and do not consider
that architecting is only a matter of method or process; in fact,
architecture models are not going to solve the project challenges alone.
Do not consider MBPLE as an engineering issue only, moving from
project development to product-based development involves the entire
organization and requires cultural changes and reallocation of
responsibilities and budget, as well as management support.
Do not be too optimistic on actual skills and practices to jump from
“no products” to “nothing but the product.” It is a long journey!
Do not believe that when the feature modeling is defined correctly and
the architecture models are developed, then the product line is fully
designed.
The feature models usage primary recommendations are:
They shall be used to manage product lines over the entire product life
cycle and not only in the initial stages.
They are the result of an iterative process, but the product line design
shall remain stable and sufficiently adaptable to meet changing market
needs.
They shall be used as the primary means of communication between
all the project stakeholders.
They shall be kept as an important knowledge source and preserved
because almost no one can have a complete overview of complex
systems like our GM radars. They can also be used to facilitate the
training of new people.
They shall include only variable features in order to remain simple and
focus on variability and defined as a clear hierarchical structure for all
stakeholders.
They shall be reviewed by stakeholders regularly (e.g. obsolete feature
removal, new feature introduction after product CCB’s approval, bid
and project configurations updating, generic assets impacts tracking,
product line roadmap upgrading, etc..).
They shall introduce an engineering concept that must be validated
with project architects and the product line architect.
The key product development vs. project development recommendations
are:
A product line is not a project; a defect can spread to multiple
applications in mass production. Hence, it is necessary to make in-
depth qualifications of the product line before using it in projects; if
possible, mature the product with long-lasting tests as soon as possible.
Pay special attention to product configuration management and not
only to the process. The permanent goal shall be to stick to the limited
number of variants of the product decided by the product line manager.
Avoid “wild and radical improvement.” Once a product is stabilized,
the major evolution costs are in the qualification; group and plan the
improvements through batches at a low pace.
Finally, the major architecture and BBs recommendations are:
Work for the technical success of the architecture. This happens when
the reference architecture is reliable, resilient, and fully compliant with
the technical challenge of the system under development; this is a real
business differentiator. Consequently, some stable BBs can be used
without change within multiple products, specifically hardware and
low-level software BBs, on a larger perimeter than originally targeted.
Harmonize, generalize and share modeling, simulation, IVV tools, test
benches, and processes.
Harmonize and generalize the product line and BBs architecture as the
reference technical solution for the engineering community.
Avoid the development of software BBs that are too large and require
an excess usage of parameterization.
Define clear roles between the BBs teams and product line teams (e.g.
define who is responsible for the architecture implementation
qualification).
Increase awareness and establish a common terminology and
semantics. For instance, in the GMR team since the beginning, there
has been confusion and mix-up between variability and
modularity/scalability/parameterization.
To conclude, the PLE and its consequent model-based deployment at GMR
have been a long journey with many challenges that did not stop its
adoption but, once solved, reinforced the necessity of establishing a
consolidated model-based and improved version of it: the MBPLE
approach. Thanks to the powerful feature-based PLE concepts and their
adaptation to our context, we managed to show continuous improvement
such as aligning bid data and project, engineering, and service phases,
improving quality, reducing the cost and lead time of radars, and above all,
remaining attractive in our competitive market segment. The GMR product
line has succeeded in defining a “delivery” approach based on a product
line that has become the common way of conducting business for all
functions at Thales SRA.

Chapter 33
Raytheon: System Family Engineering –
Innovating at the Speed of Relevance
James Teaff
33.1 Introduction
At Raytheon, an RTX business, the foundation of everything we do is
rooted in our values and a higher calling – to help our nation and allies
defend freedoms and deter aggression. We bring the strength of more than
100 years of experience and renowned engineering expertise to meet the
needs of today’s mission and stay ahead of tomorrow’s threat. Our team
solves tough, meaningful problems that create a safer, more secure world
(Raytheon 2024). In response to Department of Defense (DoD) Instruction
5000.97 digital engineering (DE) (DoD 2023), Raytheon is adopting the
augmented system and method of systems family engineering (SFE), digital
engineering, modeling and simulation (DEM&S) plus development
operations (Dev*Ops) plus modular open systems approach (MOSA) open
system architecture (OSA).
At Raytheon, the value of SFE using feature-based product line engineering
(FbPLE) is compelling, with measured improvements across numerous key
metrics including safety, security, quality, cost, time-to-field new features,
technology upgrades, hardware and software maintenance deliveries, and
similar. The use of FbPLE on US DoD contracts has resulted in aggregate
superlinear cost avoidance for the DoD measured in hundreds of millions of
US dollars and for the associated defense contracting teams equally
impressive top and bottom-line improvement, enabling the DoD to move at
the speed of relevance and contractors to innovate at the speed of relevance
(Greg et al. 2015; Clements and Krueger 2019; Defense Innovation Board
2019; Clements and Krueger 2021). The ISO/IEC 265xx set of standards
defines systems and software product line engineering as a general
approach to engineer a family of technically similar systems in an efficient
manner, taking advantage of the systems’ similarities while respecting and
managing their differences. ISO/IEC standard 26580:2021 Software and
systems engineering – methods and tools for the feature-based approach to
software and systems product line engineering, or more simply FbPLE, is a
specific form of system family engineering that uses product feature models
as the authoritative source of truth (ASoT) for defining and managing
product variation across the system families of interest (SFoI) full life
cycle; and automation in the form of a digital engineering ecosystem (DEE)
comprising a daisy-chained product line engineering (PLE) factory and
Dev*Ops software factory (ISO/IEC 2021; Defense Innovation Board
2019).
Some benefits of the FbPLE can be measured using the following:
Delivering 3x more capability within budget than non-FbPLE
contracts
Providing continuous delivery of value within weeks of contract
award, and extending across the contract period of performance
New and modified software and hardware modules are securely
delivered per contracted production and maintenance rhythm;
including daily, weekly, monthly, and quarterly
Enabling rule-based, automated, and semi-automated enforcement of
International Traffic in Arms Regulations (ITAR) and similar, ensuring
no product variant contains any content that is not allowed to be
exported
Significantly reducing contract execution risk
Realizing an eightfold improvement in verification and validation time
per production cycle
The key concepts of FbPLE are developed across this book and simplified
in the INCOSE PLE Primer (INCOSE 2019).
In essence, engineers do not carry out development work on the product
asset instances. Instead, they work on the shared asset supersets, the feature
catalog, and the composition matrices – that is, they work in the digital
asset factory. The automated factory configurator applies their work to each
product variant instance that it affects. Change and evolution are handled
systematically through well-defined governance processes. A factory rule
base (not shown) automatically verifies the product asset instances are fit
for use.
Feature-based PLE enables automated production of hardware with
embedded software systems, software-only systems, or daisy-chained
digital asset production lines integrated with hardware fabrication. It works
in multiple security zones, enabling cost-efficient assembly of modules at
differing classification levels, from unclassified through secret and top
secret, efficiently leveraging an organization’s global workforce (Teaff et al.
2019).
33.2 Introduction to the Digital Trinity
The digital trinity of DEM&S plus Dev*Ops plus MOSA OSA is depicted
in Figure 33.1 (DoD 2018, 2020, 2022; Roper 2020, 2021). Let’s take each
concept of the digital trinity one at a time.
Figure 33.1 The digital trinity.
DE is defined as an integrated digital approach that uses authoritative
sources of system data and models as a continuum across disciplines to
support lifecycle activities from concept through disposal (DoD 2018).
There is a lot going on in the definition, but the key concept is the use of
digital models as ASoTs. Take your vehicle’s navigation system – you plug
in your destination, analyze different routes to find the one you like, and
start driving. The display shows your current location, when and in which
direction to turn, and more. The navigation system uses a digital model –
data that represents reality – that has been certified as accurate – it is a
source of truth – by an authority – a company such as Google LLC. For the
digital trinity, once you have certified digital models, you can do what-if
analysis, high-fidelity simulations, and more. The Air Force calls this
“simulate before you aviate” (DoD 2020).
Development-operations “plus” or Dev*Ops is defined as an agile method
for fabricating, operating, and sustaining a family of systems that eliminates
the historic divide between development (Dev) and operations (Ops), i.e.
the infamous “push it over to operations each year and hope nothing breaks
too bad” paradigm. The wildcard character “*” or star represents adding
important system quality attributes to DevOps such as cybersecurity
(DevSecOps); safety (DevSecSafetyOps); and more, resulting in Dev*Ops,
sometimes referred to as DevStar (DoD 2022). Once you unpack Dev*Ops
and abstract away the technical details, what appears is its key characteristic
– the continuous delivery of value to the warfighter, with minimum viable
product variants fielded in weeks to months after contract award and
incrementally updated with new product features and technology day-by-
day across the contract period of performance. Contrast this with the
historic one-and-done program where a product is acquired and fielded
years after contract award. As noted in the Defense Innovation Board’s
2019 report, software is never done, and the internet of things (IoT) has
demonstrated software is increasingly embedded in the physical devices –
the hardware – we use daily (Defense Innovation Board 2019). Out of
necessity, management practices at commercial companies have been
transformed to product-based management rather than project-based
management; that is, managing a family of products with continuous
innovation, technology refresh, and similar.
The MOSA is the DoD’s business and technology strategy for acquiring,
operating, and sustaining families of systems composed of severable,
reusable modules that are conformant to OSA (DoD 2020). An OSA is an
architecture that separates the system into major functions and elements,
which work together across interfaces in conformance with widely
supported, consensus-based standards. Figure 33.2 depicts the key system
quality attributes, of which severability, reusability, composability, and, of
course, modularity are most frequently mentioned in literature.
Figure 33.2 MOSA OSA key system quality attributes.
Jargon aside, MOSA and OSAs enable the acquisition of assemble-to-order
systems from best-of-breed system modules that, once fielded, can be
effectively sustained throughout their life cycle, from installing new product
features to swapping out system modules as part of a technology refresh –
all in the operational environment.
33.3 A SFE’s Example:The Hypothetical
Intelligence Gathering System
Using the hypothetical intelligence gathering system depicted in Figure
33.3, let’s apply FbPLE plus DEM&S plus Dev*Ops plus MOSA OSAs,
concentrating on the FbPLE aspects.
Figure 33.3 Hypothetical intelligence gathering network (H-IGN).
The H-IGN system’s mission is to support the intelligence analyst via
capturing and processing sensor data collected by sensor platforms. The H-
IGN system comprises one or more unmanned vehicles (UxV), a ground
control segment, and an intelligence analytics and dissemination segment.
The H-IGN product portfolio supports three primary variations:
Small area of interest (AOI): line of sight UxV control and one analyst
such as covering a city block
Medium AOI: less than 200 square miles and up to five analysts such
as covering a city
Large AOI: less than 125 000 square miles and up to 20 analysts
Simply stated, to apply FbPLE plus DEM&S plus Dev*Ops plus MOSA
OSA, we take what we typically do for a product and add an engineering
analysis step – feature-oriented scope, commonality, and variability
analysis, or feature-oriented scope, commonality, and variability (FO-SCV),
as depicted in Figure 33.4.
Figure 33.4 FO-SCV analysis:Part of digital engineering.
FO-SCV analysis begins with eliciting what product features are in scope
for the product line, followed by determining what features can be common
(invariant); and what features need to be variant (different form/fit/function)
(Kang et al. 1990). The information from the FO-SCV analysis is captured
in a product feature model and associated product feature profiles which
together comprise the feature catalog (Clements and Krueger 2017). The
product feature model and associated data from the digital engineering
ASoT for the definition and management of product variation across the
entire digital ecosystem. DE with FbPLE is continuous with stepwise
refinement. Also known as top-down analysis, engineers iteratively create a
set of architecture, design, and parametric analysis models at different
levels of abstraction, and with different viewpoints and perspectives
addressing the concerns of different categories of stakeholders and model
consumers. The end result is a set of linked models comprising a digital
thread.
Let’s do a quick technical deep dive into a small aspect of our H-IGN
system to illustrate. First up is to identify variations, model them, and create
a set of factory product rules forming the authoritative source of truth for
variation across the SFoI.
The FO-SCV analysis identified the following variations in sensor
platforms:
One or more unmanned aerial vehicles
One or more unmanned ground vehicles
One or more maritime unmanned surface vehicles
The H-IGN system supports the following sensor platform control options:
Continuous active ground control
Autonomous vehicle control
Moreover, the system capabilities and total lifecycle cost for each product
variant in the portfolio differ based on its product specification (its factory
bill-of-features).
Time to capture the FO-SCV analysis in a feature mode. Product variant
features are abstractions of the variant behaviors (behavioral features) and
structure (structural features) – ultimately the form, fit, and function (FFF)
– of system elements. A feature model represents the behavioral features
and structural features in a hierarchical graph (Clements and Krueger 2017),
which for our H-IGN sensor platform control modes results in the feature
model in Figure 33.5.
Figure 33.5 H-IGN sensor platform control modes feature model.
For the security aspect of the digital trinity, the engineering analysis
includes:
Continuously analyzing the cyber-attack surface for each system
variant in the product portfolio.
Creating or amending cyber resiliency capabilities and cybersecurity
controls for each system variant addressing each identified attack
vector.
Digitizing cyber resiliency and cybersecurity verification rules forming
a living rule base.
Using the factory’s living rule base ensures each system variant
automatically generated by the factory contains the requisite cyber
resiliency capabilities and cybersecurity controls.
The cyber-attack surface analysis for the H-IGN system identifies the
ground control of UxV communications system (COMMS) as an attack
vector. Potential attacks include:
Passive data interception
Covert data modification
COMMS disruption
The communications system feature’s engineering ensures ongoing human
and/or autonomous sensor platform control for each system variant, each
differing in capabilities and cost. Features include:
Wireless COMMS: radio frequency (RF) and microwave radio
Encryption algorithms
Frequency hopping algorithms
Line of sight COMMS
Wired COMMS: essentially the UxV is tethered to a ground operator
Modeling the COMMS capability set results in a variant feature model,
depicted in Figure 33.6, comprising the wireless COMMS feature and the
line of sight COMMS feature.
Figure 33.6 Subset of H-IGN COMMS capabilities.
The H-IGN subject matter experts provide the following specifications:
All product variants shall have redundant COMMS capabilities for
greater cyber resiliency
All product variants shall encrypt data to ensure cybersecurity
RF COMMS shall use a frequency hopping algorithm to ensure
cybersecurity
Additionally, for each AOI variant, the subject matter experts provide the
following specifications:
The small AOI variant
Shall have both wireless radio frequency and line of sight COMMS
for greater cyber resiliency
Customers may additionally choose to add “basic” or “enhanced”
wired COMMS, but not both (this drives capability and cost
variation)
Shall not support autonomous vehicle control (this drives
capability and cost variation)
The medium AOI variant
Shall have both wireless radio frequency and microwave radio
COMMS for greater cyber resiliency
Customers may additionally choose to add line of sight COMMS
for greater cyber resiliency
Shall not support wired COMMS
Shall support autonomous vehicle control for greater cyber
resiliency
The large AOI variant
Shall have both wireless radio frequency and microwave radio
COMMS for greater cyber resiliency
Shall not support line of sight COMMS
Shall not support wired COMMS
Shall support autonomous vehicle control for greater cyber
resiliency
Each subject matter expert specification digitizes into a product rule. The
product rules exist in the PLE factory rule base as assertions – Boolean
expressions that evaluate to either true or false. For example, the subject
matter expert specification All product variants shall have redundant
COMMS capabilities for greater resiliency results in the product rule:
Assert ((COMMS.COMMSTypes > {WirelessCOMMS}) OR
((COMMS.COMMSTypes > {LineOfSightCOMMS}) OR
(COMMS.COMMSTypes > {WiredCOMMS})));
While the specifications All product variants shall encrypt data, and RF
COMMS shall use a frequency hopping algorithm, results in the product
rule set:
Assert ((COMMS.COMMSTypes.WirelessCOMMS.RF >=
{BasicRFCOMMS}) REQUIRES (COMMS.COMMSCybersecurity.
EncryptionAlgorithm == {EncryptionAlgo_Alpha}));
Assert ((COMMS.COMMSTypes.WirelessCOMMS.RF >=
{BasicRFCOMMS})
REQUIRES (COMMS.COMMSCybersecurity.
FrequencyHoppingAlgorithm == {FreqHopAlgo_Bravo}));
Adding these assertions to the PLE factory’s product rule base results in the
initial rule base for COMMS (Figure 33.7).
Figure 33.7 PLE factory’s product rule base results.
Once the digitized product rules are in place, the PLE factory checks the
assertions in the rule base and issues an error to the systems engineer for
every rule violation as they work. For example, if a digital engineer
attempts to design a system variant with basic RF COMMS without a
frequency hopping algorithm, the factory throws an assertion failed when
evaluating the product rule base:
Assertion failed: Assert ((COMMS.COMMSTypes.Wireless-
COMMS.RF >= {BasicRFCOMMS}) REQUIRES (COMMS.
COMMSCybersecurity.FrequencyHoppingAlgorithm ==
{FreqHopAlgo_Bravo}))
Just a couple of more steps, and we are done. Feature profiles are named
selections of features in a feature model used by the factory configurator to
tailor a digital asset – or more concisely, a feature profile is a composition
specification. Figure 33.8 depicts a feature profile named Medium AOI
COMMS Variant Omega. The checkmarks in the model indicate the set of
features selected for the composition specification – Basic RF Comms,
encryption algorithm alpha, etc. Boxes that are not checked indicate the set
of features deselected for the composition specification.
Figure 33.8 Example feature profile.
The final step is to create digital assets, add variation points – planned for,
controlled points of variability in assets to accommodate a specific
operational context – and create the logic for the PLE factory configurator
to exercise the variation points; that is, to compose the assets using one or
more feature profiles in a bill-of-features. The Java snippet below depicts a
simple software module that includes points of variability (sections of
software code tagged with “///* BeginGearsBlock Java <tagName>”) that
the factory configurator will either include or exclude when tailoring the
module (Figure 33.9).
Figure 33.9 Simple Java SW module.
And the accompanying asset logic for the factory configurator to delete
tagged sections of the software module depending on the feature profiles in
the composition specification:
When (true)
Select “NonsensicalCOMMSModule.java”
{
If (NOT (COMMS.COMMSTypes.WiredCOMMS >=
{BasicWiredCOMMS})) {
DeleteBlock Java “FooTag”;
}
If (NOT (COMMS.COMMSTypes.WiredCOMMS >=
{EnhancedWiredCOMMS})) {
DeleteBlock Java “BarTag”;
}
StripBlockDelimiters;
}
Returning from the depths of our analysis to our FbPLE plus digital trinity
method, the increasingly rich feature model and digitized product rules are
subsequently used to define and manage variations across all categories of
models and associated digital assets, such as enterprise architecture models,
system models, software modules, technical data packages used by
hardware fabrication; bills-of-materials used by the supply chain to
purchase commercial off-the-shelf hardware and software, and more.
Finally, the resulting digital thread – linked models and data – is verified
against the contractually requisite set of MOSA open system architectures
(OSAs), referred to as a government reference architecture.
The digital ecosystem of engineering tools forming the PLE factory, the
high-fidelity simulation environments, the analytics using digital twins
reflecting the operational state of fielded systems, plus the hardware
factories, comprise a factory-of-factories realizing a “Matrix movie”-like
virtual environment. Adding a new variant to the system family is now as
straightforward as creating a new bill-of-features that will assemble to order
the system variant from the digital asset supersets. Innovation is as quick as
adding variation points – or small sets of new digital assets – to the
supersets of assets, reusing preexisting assets to automatically compose,
certify, and field the new system variant in hours to days rather than months
to years.
And there you have it – FbPLE plus DEM&S plus Dev*Ops plus MOSA
OSAs applied to a hypothetical system family. At this point, you may be
asking, where is the bang for the buck … that comes during the operational
lifespan of the system family when the digital asset factory (including the
software factory), daisy-chained with the physical asset factory (hardware
fabrication), daisy-chained with supply chains, and more, comprising a
factory-of-factories spanning government and contractors created and
certified to field the minimum viable product variants, is used hour-by-hour,
day-by-day, to innovate and deliver value to the warfighter.
33.4 Challenges and Solutions
As with any paradigm change, adopting the FbPLE plus digital trinity
method has a number of challenges, with the technical challenges relatively
easy compared to the requisite organizational changes. Fortunately,
industry, government, academia, and standards consortia have collaborated
for 20+ years, culminating in a set of enablers in the form of standards and
regulation-complying best practices to lower the risk of organizational
change, including:
Publication of ISO/IEC standard 26580
Enhanced variation modeling mechanisms in the OMG SysML v2
standard
Augmenting the systems engineering body of knowledge (SEBoK)
with product line engineering addressing the engineering of SFoI
Augmenting the INCOSE Systems Engineering Handbook addressing
the engineering of SFoI
Methods to pay for activities that benefit more than one program,
using an approach that is compliant with federal acquisition
regulations (FAR)
Methods to enable export to countries where ITAR or export control
restrictions apply, ensuring that no system variant contains any content
that is not allowed to be exported
Methods to enable secure and efficient production and maintenance
where the system comprises unclassified and classified – at varying
levels – content
Model-based systems engineering best practices
Two decades of software product line engineering best practices at the
Software Engineering Institute
Methods for efficient configuration management that scales to the
small and the large
Methods for verification and validation where human safety is
involved; and/or where the fielded system cannot be easily maintained
e.g. a satellite in orbit
Fiduciary due diligence requires we use two key enablers – a value forecast
model and a set of metrics to measure achievement against the forecasted
values. A number of publications are available addressing the metrics
(Clements and Krueger 2019). For the value forecast model, we started with
the structured intuitive model for product line economics (SIMPLE),
amended with FbPLE concepts as described in Greg, Scharadin, and
Clements (2015). Augmenting SIMPLE with value estimation techniques
from agile DevOps resulted in a multivariable value forecast model we
termed SIMPLE-plus-plus, or more simply SIMPLE++.
Historically, business value models for strategic reuse of digital assets were
simple linear cost saving economic models. These economic models
forecast savings – primarily as cost avoidance as a result of improved
production efficiency – as a linear function of the number of products in the
SFoI. These models have a number of characteristics, including:
The models do not account for important customer and contractor
value factors unrelated to cost avoidance, such as rapid adaptation to
changing market or battlefield conditions (aka agility), and similar.
Many of these are frequently referred to as intangible benefits, and
their exclusion leads to incorrect, low valuations.
The models do not adequately express near-term value gains vs. long-
term value results.
The models do not adequately handle aspects common to defense
acquisitions, including varying, and at times lengthy acquisition
cycles, low volume and periodicity of acquisitions, technology
readiness level (TRL) requirements, and similar.
The models do not account for the observed superlinear cost avoidance
effect organizations – such as the US Navy – are experiencing as
described in Greg, Scharadin, and Clements (2015).
These characteristics led to SIMPLE++, a modern, comprehensive value-
based model. Key characteristics of the SIMPLE++ model include:
Inclusion of both cost avoidance and high-value customer and
contractor benefits, including parameterized versions of benefits
historically viewed as intangibles.
Defense acquisitions aspects tailored into the model, e.g. export/import
(EX/IM) compliance, US Federal Acquisition Requirement (FAR)
compliance, MOSA conformance, and more.
Inclusion of the superlinear cost avoidance effect.
Fine-grained temporal view capable of expressing near-term value
gains as well as long-term value gains.
SIMPLE++ extends the concept of value beyond solely monetary values to
include other forms of value, including warfighter value, DoD acquisition
value, defense contractor value, societal value, and more. The resultant
enhanced value model uses a tailored value language to describe these
facets. Business value points (BVPs) are used to quantify the intangibles as
a reflection of their relative importance to decision-makers. Decisions are
made using the BVPs, or the BVPs can be transformed into economic terms
based on either industry-reported measures; or based on expert opinion. For
example, values for end-user satisfaction can be gathered by an anonymous
survey and then transformed into dollar amounts relevant to decision-
makers, e.g. acquisition dollar spend versus value received, or for a
contractor’s positive top-line and bottom-line improvement. In combination
with traditional economic parameters, the models provide a holistic return
on investment (ROI) calculation as depicted in Figure 33.10.
Figure 33.10 Multivariable tangible and parameterized intangible
ROI calculation using SIMPLE++.
33.5 Conclusions
This chapter explored the digital trinity plus modern system family
engineering using FbPLE for SFoIs, a holistic solution enabling the DoD
and defense contractors to innovate at the speed of relevance. Each aspect
of the digital trinity – DEM&S plus Dev*Ops plus MOSA OSAs – was
defined, and corresponding aspects of system family engineering as
codified in ISO/IEC standard 26580:2021 feature-based PLE were defined.
A technical deep dive using a hypothetical system family described adding
FO-SCV analysis to standard stepwise refinement engineering analysis. The
output of the FO-SCV analysis was captured in a product feature model
comprising the ASoT for product variation definition and management
across the digital engineering ecosystem.
Cybersecurity and cyber resiliency system quality attributes of the digital
trinity were analyzed, and a rule base was created and used by the factory-
of-factories to continuously verify fielded system variants. Finally, common
challenges and solutions were discussed, including the augmented
structured intuitive model for product line economics-plus, plus
(SIMPLE++) value forecast model.
The digital trinity plus modern PLE system and method has matured over
the past 20+ years; is founded on numerous consortia-driven standards and
regulation-compliant industry best practices; and has been successfully
piloted in US DoD contracts, including the Navy’s AEGIS product lines and
the Army’s live training transformation product lines. Application of this
system and method has realized continuous delivery of value to the
warfighter concurrent with superlinear cost avoidance for the US DoD.
