Chapter 34
Belimo: Innovation in Comfort, Energy
Efficiency, and Safety Solutions with Product
Lines
Manuel Pijorr, Markus Hüppi, Daniel Messmer, Alba Pennisi, Mariana
Reyes Perez, Mitko Tanevski, and Simon Hoffman
34.1 Introduction
Belimo is the global market leader in the development, production, and
sales of field devices for the energy-efficient control of heating, ventilation,
and air-conditioning systems. The focus of its core business is on damper
actuators, control valves, sensors, and meters. Belimo values creativity and
craftsmanship, continually setting market standards. Founded in 1975, the
company employs over 2300 people, operates in over 80 countries, and has
been listed on the Swiss Exchange (SIX) since 1995 (Belimo 2024).
Belimo’s product portfolio has grown significantly over the years, driven by
continuous innovation and market demands. Some of the key products are
highlighted below.
Damper actuators: In 1976, Belimo made a significant breakthrough in the
HVAC industry by introducing the first direct-coupled motorized damper
actuator. This innovative technology was able to reduce installation time by
two-thirds. With investments of approximately 7% of its revenue into
research and development (R&D), Belimo has repeatedly achieved such
breakthroughs. The advanced technology allows for seamless integration
with building automation systems, providing precise control and
optimization of airflows and meeting the growing demand for sustainability
and energy efficiency.
Control valves: Since the beginning, Belimo has set new market standards
with its control valves, for example, with its cutting-edge pressure-
independent valves, the development of characterized control valves, or the
energy valve, which maximizes energy usage within HVAC system.
Sensors and meters: Adding HVAC sensors and meters to its lineup
allowed Belimo to offer comprehensive solutions to provide reliable and
accurate measurement and monitoring with seamless building automation
system integration.
Digital ecosystem: Belimo is dedicated to delivering superior products with
the right digital connection now and in the future. The ecosystem of
Belimo’s smart devices enables flexible system integration through all
common building automation communication protocols and Building IoT
platforms. With the help of the additional internet of things (IoT)
connection, buildings can be easily optimized, managed, and maintained.
Belimo’s success is rooted in its commitment to solution leadership and the
creation of customer value. Significant investments in R&D have led to
technological breakthroughs and proprietary technologies, anticipating
market trends, and developing cutting-edge solutions.
As a key player in the HVAC sector, Belimo has experienced significant
market growth, which brings substantial challenges in managing its wide
products’ portfolio while delivering customized products cost-effectively.
To support this expansion and maintain high standards, Belimo is
transitioning from product-oriented to product-line-oriented engineering.
The main drivers behind this strategic shift are listed below.
Increased product’s complexity: Belimo initially expanded its portfolio by
transitioning from solely mechatronic devices with reduced complexity to
embedded devices. Compared to mechatronic devices, an embedded device
is more complex due to the integration of software with the hardware,
which requires specialized knowledge in electronics and software
development. Additional complexity arises from ensuring reliable
communication, rigorous testing, regular software updates, and seamless
system integration. In contrast, mechatronic devices do not involve these
additional layers, making them simpler to design, manufacture, and
maintain. Besides that, the dynamics of change regarding requirements and
new technologies are higher in software development than in pure
mechatronics development.
The introduction of IoT-enhanced products created new challenges: the
increase of the level of “smart connectivity” through the IoT. This evolution
has led to the development of more complex systems.
The integration of smart, connected devices requires advanced capabilities
for interoperability and sophisticated control mechanisms, which further
increase the complexity of both product development and deployment
processes. Additionally, the dynamic of changing requirements and
technologies keeps increasing.
Such a shift is also driven by new regulations in regard to cybersecurity
compliance and by the increasing number of functional requirements as a
response to limit the growing solution space caused by other interacting
systems, e.g. smartphone applications or cloud infrastructure (see Figure
34.1).
Figure 34.1 Increased product’s complexity at Belimo.
Increased variability: Belimo operates in a highly variable context, as
each building’s HVAC system demands tailored solutions to meet specific
requirements. With a product portfolio exceeding 15 000 items, managing
this extensive range necessitates significant effort. To ensure a homogenous
portfolio and unique user experience, commonalities across different
products need to be properly managed. Besides that, a significant amount of
turnover is generated with customized solutions for an Original Equipment
Manufacturer (OEM) market. Therefore, a certain speed and flexibility are
needed to enhance our existing solutions.
Organizational growth: Due to a well-defined growth strategy, Belimo
grew not just in revenue but in size as well. Its organizational growth asks
for more systematic approaches, which can be scaled up globally to ensure
seamless collaboration and solid know-how management. Belimo has
recognized the limitations of a single product-oriented engineering
approach in addressing these complexities. Key motivations for
transitioning to a product line engineering (PLE) approach included:
Enhanced reusability: By focusing on reusable engineering artifacts,
PLE allows Belimo to leverage common components across multiple
products, reducing development time and costs. Based on this, it
allows to reduce the technical variance of the product portfolio
keeping the market flexibility.
Improved variability management: PLE provides a structured method
to manage product variations systematically, ensuring that
customization does not lead to inefficiencies. This is very important to
enable proper configuration management across several product lines.
Ensured consistency and quality: A product line approach ensures
consistency in product design and quality, as standardized components
and processes are used across the product portfolios.
Even though a PLE approach as such delivers more systematic possibilities
to address the challenges mentioned, Belimo reached the limits regarding
artifact handling, specifically in regard to reuse and configuration
management across different products. Therefore, Belimo has decided to
invest in a model-based product line engineering (MBPLE) approach. The
basis for this improvement lies in the models, which describe the necessary
information of a product line and can be referenced and maintained across
their life cycle. Adopting such an MBPLE approach allows Belimo to
effectively manage complexity, ensure consistency, improve efficiency,
scale operations, and maintain robust documentation, all of which are
crucial in implementing PLE.
However, transitioning from a product-oriented to a product-line-oriented
approach supported by MBPLE presents several challenges, mainly related
to organizational change management:
Organizational resistance: Shifting to MBPLE requires significant
changes in organizational culture and mindset. Employees accustomed
to product-oriented methods may resist the new approach.
Process overhaul: Implementing MBPLE necessitates a comprehensive
overhaul of existing engineering processes, including the adoption of
new tools and methodologies.
Initial investment: The transition involves an initial investment in
training, process development, and tool acquisition. This investment
can be substantial, but the long-term benefits justify the costs.
34.2 Transitioning to MBPLE: Target and
Definitions
The transition to MBPLE requires Belimo to adopt a more systematic
approach to manage its extensive product portfolio. Following a feature-
based PLE approach as described in the ISO/IEC 26580 (ISO/IEC 2021),
Belimo is shifting to model-based and, therefore, to MBPLE to bring
consistency and a single source of truth along all the reused artifacts. This
also includes a comprehensive configuration management for the necessary
assets.
To keep certain flexibility regarding specific customer requests, the
development projects at Belimo still focus on the development of single
applications. Nevertheless, to be able to keep the product line definitions
consistent and leverage them in the development projects, the modeling of
artifacts (e.g. features, requirements, system elements) is always done in the
context of the product portfolio, where the products under development
belong to. By doing so, the overview across the whole portfolio is ensured,
as well as reuse of artifacts is simplified.
This approach also allows comprehensive, up-to-date views about the used
models across the whole portfolio. The project can be seen as a “vehicle” to
create new versions of the product portfolio and of the product line-related
models (see Figure 34.2).
Figure 34.2 Portfolio mindset at Belimo.
In Belimo, the following concepts together with the related definitions have
been introduced to ensure a common understanding within the organization.
Product family: A cluster of products sharing a common set of features,
marketed similarly, and often sharing similar applications (see Figure 34.3).
Figure 34.3 Market and technical perspective.
Product line: A cluster of products and/or services sharing explicitly
defined and managed system elements (both common and variable), built
on the same core architecture, ensuring consistency and reuse inside. The
same core architecture focuses here mainly on functional distribution on
modules or system elements as well as the proper reuse of Belimo’s core
technology elements – e.g. the Application-Specific Integrated Circuit
(ASIC), which consists of a custom-designed integrated circuit for a
particular task or application. The product line and product family
definition in Belimo diverges slightly from the definition of the ISO/IEC
26580 (ISO/IEC 2021).
Product platform: It consists of Belimo’s core technology elements –
system elements – which are shared across the majority of Belimo products,
like an ASIC, the onboard software, or the smartphone application. Besides
the core technology elements, features, feature options, and requirements
are also reused for multiple products and are therefore considered elements
of the product platform. The product platform shall ensure that the
functional behavior of the devices is aligned and leads to a proper user
experience across Belimo’s complete product portfolio (see Figure 34.3).
Feature: A specific aspect or capability of a product designed to meet a
particular market or user need. Features are variant drivers, cluster
requirements (stakeholder or system), and use cases.
Feature option: A specific refinement or setting available to the customer,
providing additional functionality or personalization (e.g. color options or
connectivity alternatives). The feature and feature options definition in
Belimo also diverges from the feature definition of the ISO/IEC 26580
(ISO/IEC 2021).
System element: A discrete part of a system that realizes specified System
Requirements, forming components, modules, products, or products of
products.
By looking at its product portfolio, Belimo has identified two different
perspectives, as illustrated in Figure 34.3: The “market perspective” and the
“technical perspective.”
Market perspective: Perspective on the product portfolio, focusing on
product families. This perspective is related to the problem space. The
market perspective shows commonalities across product families with
elements from the product platform (e.g. features, feature options).
Technical perspective: Perspective on the product portfolio, focusing on
product lines. This perspective is related to the solution space. The technical
perspective shows commonalities across product lines with elements from
the product platform (e.g. system elements).
Belimo’s vision is to streamline the product creation process by simplifying
product configuration from both market and technical perspectives. By
examining both perspectives, Belimo identifies product families and
product lines based on shared features and architectural commonalities.
Belimo’s approach recognizes the orthogonal nature of the feature-based
and architectural-based portfolios (product families and product lines). The
intersection of these portfolios is represented by so-called market products,
which are abstract representations of real products from Belimo’s product
portfolio. Besides this, the split allows proper communication about the
market products to different stakeholders with different perspectives.
34.3 A Glance of the MBPLE Approach:
Asset’s Definition Across Different
Perspectives
As stated in ISO/IEC 26580 (ISO/IEC 2021), domain assets focus on
reusable components and models that implement the commonalities and
variabilities across a product line, while application assets are tailored from
these domain assets to create specific products, incorporating
customizations and specific configurations necessary to meet individual
product requirements. Depending on the life cycle of the product, different
assets are considered. The main domain asset, considered during the
modeling definition in the “concept” phase of the product development are
features, feature options, modules, and system elements (see Figure 34.4).
Figure 34.4 Domain and application assets across market and
technical perspective.
Features and feature options: Customizable aspects of the products that
represent specific capabilities or characteristics desired by the customer
specified with requirements (market perspective). Additionally, verification
procedures are linked to the requirements so that they can be reused based
on the features and their options. These assets are defined and managed in
PTC Codebeamer (PTC 2024).
Modules and system elements: Discrete, reusable parts of the system
fulfilling specific requirements and functions covered with MBSE models.
These models are defined and managed in iQUAVIS (iQUAVIS 2024), a
MBSE tool (not SysML-based).
Considering the product platform, domain assets can be shared across
product lines instead of being specific to one product line. All domain assets
are structured within libraries, enhancing their reusability and traceability in
a modeling context across different product families and product lines.
The market products are considered “application assets” in Belimo’s
product portfolio. They are bridging the gap between customer needs and
technical capabilities, providing a clear and coherent framework for product
development as well as properly connecting the different perspectives.
Within product-oriented development projects, additional product-specific
application assets like feature and feature options or modules and system
elements can be developed. Within these projects, it is possible as well to
enrich the domain assets of the product families and product lines to
increase the scope of their definition.
During the industrialization phase of the product development, additional
domain assets are considered for reuse – e.g. mechanical CAD models for
automated generation of product CAD models, BOMs for automated
generation of product BOMs, SW source code, and parameterization files.
All these assets, including their combinatorial and automated generation of
applications, are handled in SAP VC (SAP 2024).
Depending on the phase, the assets can have different levels of abstraction.
For example, during the concept phase of a development project, the
following abstractions are considered:
Features and feature options represent variance on the requirements
level which are maintained in Codebeamer (PTC 2024).
Modules and system elements describe the structure of a product on a
more logical level of abstraction covered by MBSE models in
iQUAVIS (iQUAVIS 2024).
A market product is still configurable in a range that has no influence
on the product’s architecture. These market products are binding
elements that are covered in Codebeamer and iQUAVIS.
Symmetrically, during the industrialization phase of a development project,
the following abstractions are in focus:
Features and feature options represent variance on the sales level,
which is represented by sales characteristics in a product configurator
(SAP VC).
Modules and system elements describe the structure of a product on a
physical level of abstraction considering mainly supply chain relevant
aspects. This is represented in the BOM inside the PLM system.
A market product is a so-called finished good that has no variance
anymore. This is covered as a “material” in the PLM system.
Belimo’s MBPLE journey is ongoing. In fact, the company is in the process
of defining a streamlined and comprehensive methodology that would allow
to integrate process and tool wise the more abstract view provided during
the concept phase with the view on the assets created during the
industrialization phase. This would allow an improved end-to-end workflow
as well as traceability across the entire development. For instance, the
current approach does not have a consistent 1:1 relationship between the
features in different systems (e.g. Codebeamer and SAP VC).
34.4 Variability Management at the Core of
MBPLE
Managing variability of the assets is crucial for Belimo to efficiently define
customer-relevant products compared to the past, where highly specific
projects were developing customized solutions with limited reuse capability
across the portfolio. With this, diverse market needs of product families can
be fulfilled while maintaining high standards of quality and performance
within a product line.
Different key elements are defined to describe the valid combinatorial
across the domain and application assets (see Figure 34.5).
Figure 34.5 Conceptual model of Belimo’s variability management.
Feature model: This consists of a feature-to-feature matrix that maps and
defines the relationships between different features and their options. It
identifies dependencies, conflicts, and synergies among features. Based on
this matrix, a set of allowed feature-based product descriptions is derived.
Variant model: This consists of a system element-to-system element matrix
that maps and defines the technical compatibility across different system
elements. It identifies dependencies, conflicts, and synergies among system
elements. Based on this matrix, a set of allowed system element-based
product descriptions is derived.
Configuration model: This model maps features and feature options with
system elements, showing which system element or combinations thereof
can fulfill a specific feature. It is the basis for the definition of the system
element-based product description based on feature or feature option inputs.
For instance, when a customer selects a set of features for their product, the
configuration matrix indicates the system element combinations that will
meet those requirements. The final product results from assembling those
system elements.
Feature-based product descriptions: This is a simplified representation of
a product, based on the combination of feature options. In simple terms, a
feature-based product description is a collection of feature options. These
descriptions outline the combinations of features that can be offered to
customers, ensuring that only marketable product configurations are
developed – similar to the bill-of-features.
System element-based product descriptions: This is a simplified
representation of a product, based on the combination of system elements.
In simple terms, a system element-based product description is a collection
of system elements. These descriptions specify products created using
technical compatible combinations of system elements. This ensures that all
product configurations offered to customers can be operated.
The elements are used, depending on the different levels of abstraction of
the assets mentioned in Section 34.4 and the development phase of the
project.
For instance, during the concept phase of a development project (see Figure
34.6):
The feature model is created to clarify the proper scope of the
development project, showing the requirements variance by reusing
requirement assets based on features and options from the feature
model.
The feature-based product descriptions are created based on the feature
model to define the necessary market products (application assets) and
collect the requirement assets from the domain. Additionally,
application-specific requirements assets might be added.
The system element-based product descriptions are defined for the
market products based on the MBSE model assets from the domain,
which consider reusable architectures, system elements, and module
models from the domain.
A variant model and configuration model are not explicitly defined
during the concept phase. The logic is implicitly modeled with the
other three models. This simplification is used because the scope of the
market variance described with the feature-based product descriptions
is considered the same as the technical variance described with the
system element-based product descriptions.
Figure 34.6 Variability management during the concept phase of a
development project.
As Figure 34.7 summarizes, during the industrialization phase of a
development project:
The feature model is created to define the market variance offered to
the customer. This is done in SAP VC (SAP 2024) using domain assets
that describe the sales characteristics of the offered products.
The configuration model is created as the basis for the variant creation
during the sales process. Based on the sales characteristics required by
the customer, the configuration model describes the system-element-
based product description of a finished good. Since this logic needs to
be executed during the sales process, the configuration model, as well
as the feature and the variant model, are implemented in a product
configurator (SAP VC).
The system element-based product description is automatically derived
by the product configurator, which has the configuration matrix
implemented as well as the system elements and modules from the
physical perspective. This is the basis for the bill of material of a
production order for a dedicated finished product, including
mechanical CAD models as well as other engineering documentation
assets.
The variant model can be implemented additionally to avoid the
complex rule sets in the configuration model.
The feature-based product description is provided in the product
configurator as a so-called market description as an entry point of the
configuration process of a finished product. This entry point predefines
some sales characteristics to provide a certain preconfiguration of a
finished product to the customer.
Figure 34.7 Variability management during the industrialization
phase of a development project.
Since both variability management approaches fulfill different purposes
(variability management definition for development vs. variant
configuration during the sales process), the involved mechanisms and
processes are different. Belimo is currently developing a comprehensive
methodology to address these gaps within the domain engineering models.
The to-be approach aims to enhance the end-to-end workflow and improve
traceability throughout the development process.
To sum up, variability management offers several benefits for Belimo. The
main ones are listed below:
Consistency: Adherence to predefined rules prevents invalid or
incompatible feature and system element configuration combinations,
ensuring reliable product configurations.
Flexibility: The variability management tools allow for diverse product
configurations, accommodating various customer preferences and
technical constraints.
Efficiency: With increased traceability through the variability
management tools, during the different project phases the product
development can be sped up and the likelihood of errors can be
reduced.
By leveraging variability management, Belimo can efficiently manage
product variability, delivering customized HVAC solutions that meet
diverse market demands while maintaining consistency, flexibility, and
efficiency.
34.5 Organizational Shift and Cultural
Changes
Adopting MBPLE requires significant organizational and cultural changes.
This section highlights the key shifts and processes that are implemented to
ensure a smooth transition within our organization.
To tackle the general complexity’s increase mentioned in Section 34.2, the
organizational structure of the R&D function is adapted to facilitate
simplified coordination and more efficient communication among different
departments. This adaptation mainly focuses on a line organization structure
aligned with technical processes of systems engineering, according to
ISO/IEC 15288 (ISO/IEC 2023). In this structure, every department takes
over clear responsibilities for executing the activities of their technical
process scope. These responsibilities are carried out by defined roles, in
cross-functional teams that realize the development of the product lines. A
team that has methodological governance oversees the implementation and
continuous improvement of the MBPLE approach in general. This setup of
clear roles and responsibilities ensures that roles, process responsibilities,
and line responsibilities are conflict-free among the employees.
Belimo focused on adapting the stage-gate development process for
MBPLE. This ensures that the methodology can be properly executed
within the adapted organization. The strong link between the stage-gate
development process and the MBPLE methodology emphasizes
collaboration, standardization, and continuous improvement, aligning with
the company’s commitment to innovation along an iterative and recursive
mindset.
As usual, the organizational and process adaptations require a cultural
change within the organization. To support this, training programs and
workshops are conducted to equip the team with the necessary skills and
knowledge. Additionally, on-the-job coaching takes place to support during
the daily business. With those actions, Belimo fosters a culture of
innovation and adaptability, encouraging employees to embrace new
processes, methodologies, and technologies.
34.6 Case Study and Practical Insights
To illustrate the practical benefits of MBPLE, this section presents a case
study from Belimo’s MBPLE implementation for a “Damper Actuators
product line,” focusing on the concept phase of its development project.
This example highlights how MBPLE can facilitate the development of
configurable products, enhancing flexibility and responsiveness to market
demands.
The referenced project aims to modularize and streamline a big part of the
“Damper Actuators product portfolio” at Belimo. By applying MBPLE,
Belimo effectively addressed various features for the development to
incorporate communication protocols, movement types, torque/force
requirements, cabling, and fail-safe mechanisms within proper product
variants.
The “damper actuators product line” modularizes key aspects of market
products from different product families using similar MBSE models for
modules and system elements based on a set of reusable features and
feature options. It includes the construction of a library for reusable
requirements assets. In the past, these aspects were addressed through
dedicated single product-oriented development projects with limited
realization of synergies. The goal was to utilize a modularization approach
to manage the commonalities more effectively, allowing for the creation of
diverse products with shared assets.
The following steps were concluded during the concept phase of the
development project in regard to the MBPLE approach:
Features, feature options, as well as requirements, are elicited and
included in the requirements domain assets pool in Codebeamer.
The relevant features and feature options are addressed to the relevant
product families of the “damper actuators product portfolio” in
Codebeamer. Application-specific features and feature options as well
as requirements are defined.
Features and feature options relevant for the variant definition during
the concept phase are defined and transferred to the product family
projects of iQUAVIS. These relevant feature options do not consider
things like housing color, cable length, or other aspects that have no
influence on the architecture of the product line. During the
industrialization phase of the development project, these aspects need
to be considered for the feature model (see Figure 34.8).
Figure 34.8 Overview of features and feature options belonging
to a product family in iQUAVIS (simplified content).
Nota bene: The transfer of information from Codebeamer to iQUAVIS
is currently manually realized. In 2025, an automated interface
between both tools will be established.
The feature model is created per product family in iQUAVIS.
Therefore, the variability definition is done along the feature tree
branches (Figure 34.9), and additional exclude or mandatory rules
between the branches are included in a DSM. (Figure 34.10).
Figure 34.9 Views on feature model in iQUAVIS (simplified content).
Figure 34.10 Feature model DSM in iQUAVIS (simplified content).
With the built-in “variant manager” in iQUAVIS, a variant analysis per
product family is done with the support of the tool. Based on this, the
relevant market product configurations are automatically created and
linked with the feature options to create the feature-based product
descriptions (see Figure 34.11).
Figure 34.11 Feature-based product descriptions in iQUAVIS
(simplified and limited content).
The market products are created in the product families and linked
with the feature options accordingly (Codebeamer). This allows the
reuse of the requirements, which are referenced to the features and
feature options.
Nota bene: The transfer of information from iQUAVIS to Codebeamer
is currently manually realized. In 2025, an automated interface
between both tools will be established.
The product line scope is defined by referencing the relevant market
product configurations from the product family projects in iQUAVIS
(Figure 34.12).
Figure 34.12 System element-based product descriptions in
iQUAVIS (simplified and limited content).
The market product configurations are linked to reusable system
elements and module models – variant model – to create the system
element-based product descriptions. (Figure 34.12). Based on this
information, interface specifications, and functional allocation are
reused in iQUAVIS.
As described in Section 34.4, this simplified approach is chosen because
there is no difference in market and technical variance considered to keep
the development effort under control. This might limit the flexibility of later
scope extensions of the product line. Additionally, this setup works quite
well because it is suitable for both a brownfield approach, where we already
know the market products of the product families, and a greenfield
approach, where we analyze the feature model from scratch to define
completely new market product configurations.
The application of MBPLE brought several key advantages:
Focused development: By reusing communication, gearbox, and actuator
modules defined with MBSE models, as well as design assumptions from
the prototype, the focus can be kept on optimizing the modular components
and integrating them efficiently.
Simplified customization: The modular approach enables quick and easy
build of applications by reusing requirements, MBSE models for
customization to meet specific customer requirements, such as different
communication protocols, torque ranges, and fail-safe mechanisms.
Extended product portfolio: The modularization allows for future
extensions of the product portfolio. A new communication module can be
developed according to customer demand and integrated with limited effort
together with the existing modules.
Broader use case realization: The platform approach enables the
integration of actuators and other products emerging from the platform
project into a broader context, facilitating more complex use cases at the
product-of-products level.
Standardization of interfaces: To ensure a seamless integration of
modules, standardized interfaces are defined, which cover a broader
application context, such that similar modularization principles can be
applied also in other product line development projects. These interfaces are
managed within MBSE models in iQUAVIS to ensure the proper reuse of
these assets during the development of the architectures.
Those advantages led to several projects notable successes, including:
Enhanced product quality: Improved actuator performance due to
focused development efforts and leveraging modular design principles.
Software reuse: With streamlined hardware, the software development
benefited from a platform approach, allowing for the direct reuse of
software components, libraries, and functions across different
products.
Cross-application flexibility: Although this project focused on air
applications, the same actuators can also be adapted with limited effort
for water applications, demonstrating the versatility of the modular
approach.
Integration capabilities: The modular design with dedicated sensor
interfaces allows for easy integration of sensors, enhancing the
functionality and customizability of the actuators.
The modularization of the “damper actuators product line” through MBPLE
has significantly transformed Belimo’s approach to product development.
By leveraging a modular architecture with standardized interfaces and
reusable assets for MBSE models and requirements, Belimo achieved
higher efficiency in development processes, further enhanced product
quality, and greater flexibility to meet diverse customer requirements. The
standardized interfaces facilitate the integration and reuse of modules across
different product line projects, maximizing the utility of developed
components and reducing redundant efforts.
The ability to reuse assets and integrate new technologies seamlessly has
allowed Belimo to dynamically extend the product portfolio, ensuring a
swift response to market demand and technological advancements.
Moreover, the platform approach has opened new possibilities for
integrating actuators with other products, enabling complex use cases and
providing a robust foundation for future innovations. This case study
demonstrates the strategic advantage of MBPLE in the context of large-
scale product line projects with limited reuse of existing artifacts.

Chapter 35
MBPLE in Action: What We Can Learn from
the Five Industrial Cases?
35.1 Introduction
As industries evolve, so too must our approaches to managing complexity
and variability within systems. This chapter analyzes how those five
organizations adapt and implement model-based product line engineering
(MBPLE) in response to their unique challenges.
The five cases presented in this chapter – Airbus, MBDA, Thales,
Raytheon, and Belimo – highlight the diverse facets of MBPLE adoption
and the necessity of adapting practices to fit specific industry needs. Each
case sheds light on how these organizations address the complexity and
variability inherent in their systems, illustrating that while MBPLE offers a
robust framework, its application must be tailored to the unique demands of
each sector.
Section 35.2 explains the selection and significance of these five cases as
representative examples of MBPLE adoption in different industrial
contexts.
Section 35.3 utilizes the MBPLE adoption quadrant introduced in Chapter
22. It applies it to the five cases to understand and analyze their
relationships with complexity and variability and to derive the adoption
patterns they followed. This section presents three alternative adoption
paths.
Section 35.4 highlights the similarities and divergences among the five
cases as well as how they adapted from the product line engineering (PLE)
standards for the adoption of MBPLE.
We chose to include various perspectives in this section of the book, even
those that do not entirely align with our understanding of MBPLE or fully
conform to the standards. For example, we feature organizations that use
model-based systems engineering (MBSE) tools not solely based on
systems modeling language (SysML) or that explore alternative
implementations of feature models and variation points. Our main
motivation is to share diverse MBPLE practices unified by the core
principles defined in the PLE standards. As MBPLE continues to evolve as
an engineering approach, lessons learned from multiple industries are
invaluable. Paraphrasing the joke from Chapter 7: “We may be
methodologists, but you can still negotiate with us!”
35.2 Why Do These Five Cases Matter?
Throughout this book, we have emphasized that while we propose best
practices for MBPLE learning, implementation, and adoption, starting from
common motivation drivers, a one-size-fits-all approach is not feasible.
Different industries require adaptations based on their unique needs,
constraints, and contingencies. These adaptations may be influenced by
technical factors as well as socio-economic and political considerations.
All five cases meet the minimum criteria described in the Part V
Introduction and come from three distinct industries: aviation, defense, and
heating, ventilation, and air control.
Naturally, one might ask: Why focus on these specific industries and
organizations? The reasons for our choices are outlined below.
Defense – MBDA, Thales, and Raytheon: The defense industry is a
pioneering sector in the application of systems engineering, MBSE
(Ducamp et al. 2022), and PLE (Lanman et al. 2011; Clements et al. 2014).
Despite being considered a relatively new discipline, MBPLE has achieved
a certain level of maturity within this field. The complexity and variability
of systems such as MBDA, Raytheon missiles, and Thales radars are
notably high, reflecting the complex nature of defense technologies.
Aviation – Airbus: The aviation industry shares several characteristics with
the defense industry, such as large-scale program development, maturity in
systems engineering and MBSE, high complexity, and the development of
safety-critical systems. However, it also has its own unique aspects. For
example, certain aircraft areas, such as cabin modules and systems, have a
higher degree of customization. The industry also deals with multiple
commercial clients, whose ultimate users are the passengers, and has a
strong interdependence with the manufacturing and service dimensions
(Forlingieri 2022).
Heating, ventilation, and air control – Belimo: This industry has great
potential for the end-to-end application of system engineering practices due
to the relatively short system life cycle phases. The same applies to MBPLE
as a discipline of systems engineering. We chose to include this real-world
example to illustrate the diverse applications and adoption strategies of
MBPLE. Belimo, a market leader in this sector, produces a wide range of
components such as sensing, control, and actuation solutions, catering to the
high variability and diverse needs of numerous customers across several
industries. While the products themselves are not as complex as those in the
defense and aviation sectors, this relative “simplicity” allows for the
observation of the MBPLE application along the entire product life cycle,
from ideation to production, within the same team.
One limitation of this last part of the book is the absence of the automotive
and railway, also among the first forerunners of (MB) PLE. Although the
authors reached out to some of the key companies in these sectors that have
adopted the MBPLE approach, time constraints and the priority of bringing
this book to publication led to the decision to limit this edition to only five
cases. We hope to welcome more industrial cases and update the existing
ones in future editions.
The authors have also integrated their own experiences to address these
limitations throughout the book. They have worked on MBPLE in Europe,
Canada, and the Asia–Pacific region, supporting the automotive (e.g.
Renault, Continental) and railway industries (e.g. Bombardier
Transportation, Alstom).
35.3 The MBPLE Adoption Quadrant:
Understanding Paths and Trajectories
Toward MBPLE
This section describes the five systems of interest – aircraft and its systems,
missiles, radars, and sensing, control, and actuation solutions – of the five
organizations, using as metrics the level of complexity and variability, as
introduced in Chapter 2. This premise is necessary to understand their paths
and trajectories to adopt MBPLE.
Complexity: Aircraft and their systems, as well as missiles, exhibit a high
level of complexity. This complexity arises not only from the advanced
technologies and large scopes involved but also from the highly regulated
environment in which they operate. These factors result in the development
of large, complex systems within extensive, long-lasting programs, making
the management of complexity crucial. This applies to both MBDA and
Raytheon.
Radars, while still complex, have a lower order of magnitude compared to
aircraft and missiles. Their complexity lies primarily in software
parameterization and the capability to serve multiple contexts and scenarios
despite being integrated into large mission programs.
Belimo’s products, on the other hand, are the least complex among the
cases discussed. While the scope-driven complexity is not as critical here,
the integration of new technological advancements poses challenges in
managing the complexity of these components.
Figure 35.1 illustrates how these products relate to varying degrees of
complexity.
Figure 35.1 Complexity and variability degrees of aircraft, missile,
radar, and HVAC solutions.
Variability: Aircraft are unique systems. They operate in standardized
environments (airports, airspace, etc.) that limit and control the “external
variability” – coming from the environment –, resulting in lower variation
in components such as wings, landing gear, and cockpit. On the contrary,
the cabin exhibits a high degree of variability due to its exposure to final
customers – the passengers – and the diverse requirements of various
airlines (see Chapter 2).
Missiles typically have less variability, but the portfolio and diverse mission
capabilities of the systems can quickly increase their variation. Radars may
exhibit higher levels of variability than missiles, particularly in software
parameterization and due to the numerous scenarios they serve.
Finally, sensors, actuators, and control solutions demonstrate high levels of
variability because they are integrated into numerous products across
various industries.
Figure 35.1 also depicts the position of these products in relation to their
degree of variability.
MBPLE adoption paths: To understand this section, it is key to read
Chapter 22, where the MBPLE adoption quadrant is explained. We use the
“MBPLEAQ” to analyze and compare commonalities and variabilities in
the MBPLE adoption paths of those five organizations. Three distinct
patterns arise.
Figure 35.2 shows the first path that we call “complexity-triggered
adoption.” This applies similarly to the Airbus, MBDA, and Raytheon cases
described in Chapters 30 and 31.
Figure 35.2 Path of “complexity-triggered adoption” of MBPLE at
Airbus and MBDA.
The three organizations started the adoption of MBSE first within a digital
transformation that helped them move away from a document-based
approach (introducing first SE + MB) while still focusing on single-system
development.
The second phase of this transformation consisted of integrating some PLE
concepts and mechanisms first into the SysML assets only (MBSE + PLE)
to finally land into a broader adoption of MBPLE. The high level of
complexity of their products and the large amount of documents posed the
MBSE adoption as a priority. However, the increase in variability (e.g.
cabin monuments and systems) and the need to reuse models – to justify the
large investment in MBSE – pushed the organizations to slowly introduce
the PLE component into the MBSE adoption and then to extend it to a more
holistic model-based PLE approach (MBPLE). Although Raytheon is
considered among the pioneers in adopting PLE in combination with MBSE
(Young and Clements 2017), they do not adopt the naming “MBPLE” but
refer just to feature-based PLE in combination with the “digital trinity” as
shown in Chapter 33.
Figure 35.3 presents an opposite pattern that we call “variability-triggered
adoption” and that applies to Thales, described in Chapter 32.
Figure 35.3 Path of “variability-triggered adoption” of MBPLE at
Thales.
In this case, the increase of the radar portfolio, including the related
variability within their systems and engineering artifacts (e.g. requirements,
source codes, parameters, etc.), pushed Thales to move from single-system
and document-based development to a system family engineering approach,
passing first through a clone-and-own approach (SE + PL), both still relying
during the first stage on documents. Slowly, the introduction of MBSE at
Thales Group to respond to the increasing complexity convinced the radar
teams to experiment with MBSE but treat it at the same levels as other
documents and assets (PLE + MBSE). Finally, with the full adoption of the
Arcadia MBSE methodology and Thales in-house “orchestra suite” (Navas
et al. 2021), the organization shifted to a model-centric PLE approach that
we know as MBPLE.
Figure 35.4 illustrates the final pattern identified in our fifth industrial case,
which we call the “Divide et Impera” (Latin for “divide and conquer”)
adoption, or more simply, “differentiated adoption.” Considering Belimo’s
portfolio and its significant variability, the initial direction was to transition
from a document-based, single-system approach to a system-family
engineering approach (dotted lines).
Figure 35.4 Path of “differentiated adoption” of MBPLE at Belimo.
This involved gradually introducing the concept of product lines and
variability, relying on clone-and-own first, before implementing the PLE
approach with a focus on the BOMs, requirements, and other models (PLE
+ MBSE), but without a unifying system architecture principle. This
strategy aimed to better manage variability in the late stages of design,
where quick wins could be achieved.
In a subsequent stage, the same team at Belimo recognized the necessity to
invest more in system architecture and in a model-based approach to
facilitate monitoring system evolutions and improve consistency, often
hindered by the multitude of documents. This team focused on the concept
and earlier design phases, moving from a document-based approach
(through SE + MB first) – starting first with a few diagrams – to an MBSE
approach (continuous lines). Gradually, the team introduced PLE elements
– features and variation points – to control logical system architectures and
other assets, primarily in the architecture models (MBSE + PLE).
The final “mile,” which is still in progress, involves adopting an MBPLE
approach from both the lower-right and upper-left quadrants. However, it is
important to recognize that due to the relatively low complexity of Belimo’s
components, the business case for a fully integrated Application Lifecycle
Management (ALM) and Product Lifecycle Management (PLM) model-
centric strategy is not as compelling compared to the other three major
organizations and their more complex systems.
35.4 Commonalities and Divergences in the
MBPLE Adoption
The five industrial cases, while covering the MBPLE adoption and
providing a general overview of it, focus on diverse facets, going into the
details of specific aspects of the adoption.
We opted for this approach to make them more attractive to the readers
because a comprehensive treatment of each case within the limited space of
this book, or a simple comparison between them (between apples and
oranges), would not have done justice to the distinct ways those companies
have implemented MBPLE. However, it is key to highlight some
similarities and differences in the adoption. We use the MBPLE adoption
framework described in Chapter 22 to ease this cross-case analysis. The
information provided below is a synthesis of what can be found in the
previous chapters about those five organizations and additional knowledge
provided by the guest authors in the form of a questionnaire.
MBPLE process: At Airbus, while the approach itself is based on the
ISO/IEC 26550 (ISO/IEC 2015) and 26580 (ISO/IEC 2021) standard
family, some tailoring is performed to adapt to Airbus’ needs and culture, as
well as aviation-specific business processes. MBPLE is part of a larger
transformation initiative called Digital Design Manufacturing and Services
(DDMS) with a strong focus on company-wide systems engineering and
product line-oriented practices.
Similarly, MBDA uses the well-known PLE standards as the basis for the
MBPLE approach. The company implements MBPLE in an improvement
program, named the “Greater Engineering Transformation Program,”
focusing on the technical management and engineering (MB)SE processes.
At Thales, MBPLE is integrated into define, develop, and qualify the
solution (DDQS) processes that are accompanied by PLE instructions,
primarily based on ISO/IEC 26550. In addition, the PLE approach is also
applied to the bid management process and to customer service processes to
deliver the customer’s user documentation for the project, based on the
features and instantiated assets.
RTX (parent company of Raytheon) provides a comprehensive “OneRTX
Digital Engineering” solution using Digital Threads – including a mature
MBSE solution – enabling engineering lifecycle activities for products in
the RTX product portfolios from concept through retirement and disposal in
line with the ISO/IEC 15288 (ISO/IEC 2023). OneRTX solution is based on
a multi-year initiative and comprises a set of common, tailorable MBSE and
feature-based PLE processes and guidelines with accompanying staff
training boot camps. The OneRTX solution has been successfully used on
numerous DoD contracts, compressing life cycles, enabling informed
decisions using high-fidelity analysis models and simulations, and
accelerating time-to-field for both new products and adding new product
features to fielded systems.
Finally, Belimo, Innovation, and R&D processes are highly influenced by
ISO/IEC 15288 (ISO/IEC 2023). In addition to that, to enable the reuse of
artifacts and foster the definition of its modular architectures, the
configuration management process is also central in the adoption of
MBPLE – also based on ISO/IEC 26580.
The current focus at Belimo is only on the engineering processes, in
particular, the “concept definition” and “system architecture definition
processes” and the later “design processes,” because of the differentiated
application of MBPLE, described in Section 35.3 of this chapter and, more
in detail, in Chapter 33.
MBPLE method: The MBPLE methodological approach used at Airbus
focuses on a holistic 4-step approach – using a single variability model to
drive different types of assets, in line with the MBPLE paradigm presented
in Chapter 12. The shared assets currently in scope are: SysML models,
governed within the Airbus’ MBSE framework, requirements (textual and
SysML-based), and 3D elements.
At MBDA, the SysML model is the master for any type of architecture,
from operational to structural. For example, structural architecture provides
the framework for mechanical and electrical/electronic architecture
development. Since the structural architectures are variable, they are all
controlled by a feature model and its feature configuration within the
MBPLE approach.
Thales promotes a holistic PLE approach where the feature model is central
and gives more emphasis to it than to the MBSE part. For instance, the
company focused on formalizing the method for the extraction of features
in an iterative way, where instead of creating digital feature models at first,
spreadsheets and/or mind maps are often used to capture the initial
variability, eliminating the concern for structure and hierarchy amongst
variation points. As an example of another method formalization, Thales
also separates the “need level” features and the “solution level” features
(Navas et al. 2021) within the feature model.
Raytheon primarily creates and delivers artifacts pursuant to US DoD
contract deliverables, which in turn are based on US DoD instructions,
handbooks, etc.
For several years the US DoD has launched multiple digital transformation
initiatives, deprecating document-based systems engineering, and with the
publication of DoD instruction 5000.97 (DOD 2023), Raytheon transitioned
to model-based engineering (MBE) comprising MBSE methods, plus
model-based hardware and software design methods, model-based
simulations, etc. for systems, systems of systems, and system families, by
applying a feature-based PLE approach. The example provided in Chapter
33, “the hypothetical intelligence gathering system,” shows a simplified
application of Raytheon’s approach in dealing with system family
engineering and the so-called “digital trinity.”
Belimo slightly differs from a traditional MBPLE method application.
While still having the ISO/IEC 26580 as a reference, Belimo’s features
consist of “common” features (e.g. a cluster of common requirements) and
feature options that express the variability and “rule” it in the architecture
models. Feature models and architecture models are differentiated into two
levels: a more abstract one, including features, requirements, and MBSE
models, and a more detailed one, where features and BOMs are integrated
within the product data management part.
MBPLE information model: Airbus integrates the current MBPLE
framework elements into its internal data model for systems engineering
(SECAM), which includes a meta-model for supported assets and employs
SysML notation (Ducamp et al. 2022).
MBDA encompasses various features in its “E2EMBE information model,”
ranging from use cases to design choices, and includes different types of
variation points such as atomic, starting, or propagated.
Thales has developed an “engineering data model” at the solution
engineering level that includes specification references, product breakdown
structure configuration items, functional chains structuration, integration,
verification and validation references, and changes references. This model
is structured into different levels: system needs, logical architecture, and
physical architecture, incorporating variability and adhering to the “Arcadia
methodology” (Voirin 2023). It is designed to promote co-engineering
developments while remaining tool-independent and is not based on SysML
but on a tool-specific notation.
The OneRTX digital engineering solution includes the definition of the
components of the digital threads required to support engineering activities
for a product, including capability allocation, requirements traceability, and
blended virtual and physical integration, verification, and validation
(IV&V). For each product in the RTX product portfolio, a digital thread
definition activity is performed identifying the authoritative source of truth
(ASoT) for each category of digital artifact used for the products. These
tailored digital threads comprise the enduring, bidirectionally linked,
authoritative sources of truth used by customers and RTX engineers to
make informed decisions.
Belimo documents its methods in an ontology description. Unlike the other
cases, Belimo’s feature tree does not follow the recommended FODA-like
(Kang et al. 1990) hierarchical notation for variability. Additionally,
similarly to Thales, their MBSE models are not based on SysML.
MBPLE tool chain: The current tool chain at Airbus focuses on creating a
holistic approach and an end-to-end agnostic flow of variability data across
the organization and tools. PTC pure::variants (PTC 2024b) is used as the
main hub, connecting to Cameo Systems Modeler (Cameo 2024) and IBM
DOORS Classic (IBM 2024a). The 3DEXPERIENCE solution (Dassault
Systèmes 2024) has its own variability management (not in a feature tree-
like), with a mapping between PTC pure::variants and the 3DX references.
As data is treated in the form of agnostic ontologies, the tool chain does not
drive the implementation of the framework.
MBDA has a heterogeneous development environment with different tools.
For this reason, an MBPLE tool that supports an open ecosystem was
selected, and the choice fell on pure::variants from PTC. MBDA’s tool
chain includes IBM Rhapsody (IBM 2024b) and Capella (2024) as MBSE
tools, Xpedition Designer (Siemens 2024) for ECAD, and the
3DEXPERIENCE platform for MCAD.
Thales uses the “orchestra suite,” an internal set of integrated tools that
support MBPLE methods, including PTC pure::variants, Capella, and
Product and Asset Lifecycle Management (PALM) for the PLM and
Enterprise Resource Planning (ERP) parts (Thales 2024). A holistic
MBPLE approach is enabled by connecting both PLM and ERP-integrated
tools with the inputs of the project variant coming from the feature model.
Raytheon’s OneRTX enterprise integrated digital environment, consisting
of integrated, best-of-breed engineering tools support engineering activities
including: model-based acquisition, model-based mission engineering,
model-based requirements engineering, model-based architecture, model-
based product line engineering, model-based multidisciplinary analysis and
optimization (MDAO), model-based hardware design (mechanical and
electrical); and software design, model-based emulations, model-based low
and high-fidelity simulations, as well as blended virtual and physical IV&V.
Due to the impossibility of listing all the solutions used, we refer here
mainly to IBM Rhapsody (IBM 2024b) used for the MBSE domain, and
gears (BigLever 2024) as an integrated PLE tool.
Belimo has foreseen a roadmap for introducing MBPLE and related tools in
the earlier stages of development. The main tools involved are Codebeamer
(PTC 2024a) for requirements management, iQuavis (iQUAVIS 2024) for
high-level feature modeling and architectural modeling, and SAP VC (SAP
2024) for later-stage development. SAP VC includes the product structure
and related finished configurations, as well as separate features and assets
from PLM.
MBPLE organization: At Airbus, the MBPLE framework does not lead
organizational changes, but it serves as an important contact point for end-
users, aiding acceptance and accelerating adoption. It is part of the DDMS
program, a temporary organization, and works in synergy with the MBSE
team and the team responsible for adapting Airbus to become a product
line-based organization.
Similar to Airbus, MBPLE at MBDA is part of the “Greater Engineering
Transformation Program” and within the MBSE team. At this stage, MBDA
has deliberately decided to focus only on engineering management
processes, and the E2EMBSE and E2EPLE teams primarily support the
functions that perform such processes. Due to confidentiality, no other
information could be shared.
At Thales SRA, we saw how the process puts the product at the center of
the organization and how the product leads the business. The company
formed two product teams for two different product lines, collaborating
with project teams. For over 10 years in the Radar division, a dedicated
resource was appointed to deploy MBPLE and support engineering teams.
Finally, in 2023, this resource will be integrated into the “SE method and
tool” department. This service will bring support for all SRA SE and is
composed of ten persons. Additionally, a new organization will be put in
place at the end of 2024 with the creation of a Center of Competences,
including around 50 persons.
Raytheon is on a digital transformation journey that will support business
growth, drive operational excellence, and equip employees to perform their
best. The digital transformation strategy is driven by leading-edge digital
capabilities that will redefine how the company works, optimize its
operations, and transform its products and services. A key digital
transformation initiative enhances the product lifecycle by becoming a
model-based enterprise (including MBPLE), connecting its product data
from design through sustainment. Due to confidentiality, no additional or
specific information concerning the MBPLE organization can be shared.
At Belimo, as stated before, MBPLE is applied to two main life cycle
stages. At the earlier stage (conceptual), the innovation system architecture
(IAS) team is responsible for the methodology-wide implementation of
MBSE/MBPLE from a method and tool perspective. This team provides
training on the subject, including requirements, features, and architecture
modeling. Comprising six people, the team handles methodological aspects
with two dedicated members. At the later stage (more design-focused),
product structure and related variability management are handled in SAP
VC rather than from the IAP (product data management). Both teams
belong to the same department, IA (innovation architecture).
Table 35.1 shows the key terms of the MBPLE 4-step approach used by the
five organizations and compares them with our terms (the book’s authors)
and the ISO/IEC 26580’s terminology.

This chapter summarizes the key commonalities and differences among the
five cases. Among the divergences, is the centrality of the ISO/IEC 26580
standard in shaping the adoption of MBPLE in diverse organizations and
industries.

Chapter 36
Conclusion
This book has provided a comprehensive overview of the fundamental
concepts, available technology, technical implementation, and strategic and
organizational aspects of model-based product line engineering (MBPLE).
These elements, along with the industrial case studies presented in Part V,
provide useful guidance to understand and implement MBPLE in an
organization.
Several key elements for the development and deployment of MBPLE were
identified and addressed. These elements were first envisioned in the
introductory chapters of the book, which presented the benefits of
transitioning from a single-product to a product-line-based delivery
approach and the necessary enablers (strategic, cultural, technological, and
organizational) for the deployment of MBPLE. Subsequently, each element
was further developed in later chapters of the book (in particular, Parts III
and IV) to support this first view. The last part of the book also establishes
how these different key elements can fit together and function as a whole
within an MBPLE-oriented organization.
The first conclusion of this book is that the ISO/IEC 26580 (ISO/IEC 2021)
standard should serve as a lighthouse for adopting MBPLE. At its core,
MBPLE is “feature-based” product line engineering, where features act as a
“single source of variability.” Features are like the “conductor of an
orchestra,” guiding the implementation of variability within assets.
Variability and commonality are the symphonies, aiming to reuse the same
notes, while the score – the relationships and constraints of our feature
model – ensures everything plays in harmony. The model-based aspect of
MBPLE further optimizes this approach, akin to an orchestra performing in
the world’s finest theater, with superior acoustics and instruments, to deliver
a flawless and cohesive performance.
This leads to the second conclusion of this book which is that the basic
necessary standards, technologies, languages, and methods to implement an
operationally viable “MBPLE factory” are already available and could be
deployed in many organizations, albeit on a limited scope of shared assets.
In any case, attention must be paid to implement the MBPLE factory at the
speed of relevance, taking into account the maturity of teams and the
priority given to particular engineering artifacts to be transformed into
shared reusable assets.
A third conclusion is that, in order to operate MBPLE across all types of
shared assets and obtain the maximum benefits, software tools still have to
overcome some barriers to provide effective end-to-end digital continuity.
This endeavor must be assumed by software editors and tool vendors of
both authoring and Product Lifecycle Management/Application Lifecycle
Management tools.
The essential conclusion of this book is that implementing MBPLE in an
organization is far from being a purely technical issue. A successful
deployment of MBPLE will undoubtedly rely on effective systems
engineering practices. In particular, a very accurate formalization of the
results of business and opportunity analyses is paramount. However,
aligning all functions and sectors of the organization around clear business
goals and a common delivery approach is of utmost importance. The
business context, goals, and constraints provide the essential elements to
perform product line architecture and align the product line architecture
approach (and the organizational structure that should support it) to the
business needs. It becomes evident, then, that the implementation of
MBPLE should be considered a transformation initiative and executed
gradually within transition plans, which require upfront investment and
forethought.
We hope this book has equipped readers with the knowledge and tools
necessary to build a compelling business case for MBPLE and to embark on
a successful implementation journey. It is our strong belief that, by
embracing MBPLE, organizations can achieve greater profitability, reduce
lead time, improve product quality, and obtain a substantial return on
investment, securing their position at the forefront of modern engineering
practices.
As Sun Tzu wisely noted in the 5th century BC (Tzu 2010):
There are not more than five musical notes, yet the combinations of these
five give rise to more melodies than can ever be heard.
There are not more than five primary colours, yet in combination they
produce more blues than can ever be seen.
There are not more than five cardinal tastes, yet combinations of them
yield more flavours than can ever be tasted.
Variability is intrinsic to the world we live in. From quanta to DNA, from
colors to music, everything is governed by combinations of common
elements. The challenge lies in understanding and mastering the “rules” of
control and reuse.
Similarly, organizations can unleash the power of variability, enabling
innovation while maintaining control, managing complexity, and unlocking
the full potential of their product lines. To achieve this, we believe
organizations should embrace MBPLE, the feature-based path to product
line success.
References
ISO/IEC (2021). Software and systems engineering – methods and tools for
the feature-based approach to software and systems product line
engineering (ISO/IEC/IEEE 26580: 2021).
Tzu, S. (2010). The Art of War. Chichester: Capstone Publishing.

Annex A: MBPLE Glossary
Bill of features
See feature configuration.
Core element
A core element is an element in a shared assets superset used for all
member products of a product line.
Reference: VAMOS (Weilkiens 2016).
Domain asset
ISO/IEC 26550 defines the domain asset, which is then redefined and
named a shared asset in ISO/IEC 26580. See Shared Asset.
References: ISO/IEC (2015, 2021).
Feature
A feature is a characteristic of a product that sets it apart from other
products within the same product line.
Reference: ISO/IEC (2021).
Feature catalog
See feature model.
Feature configuration
A feature configuration is a valid set of selected features of a feature
model for a member product. The ISO/IEC 26580 standard calls the
feature configuration the bill of features.
Reference: ISO/IEC (2021).
Feature constraint
A feature constraint specifies an expression about features that must
always be true in the context of a feature configuration.
Reference: ISO/IEC (2021).
Feature group
A feature group specifies the cardinality and optionally further
constraints about a valid selection of alternative sub-features for feature
configurations.
Feature model
A feature model specifies a product line’s variability by features
organized in a hierarchical tree structure.
References: ISO/IEC 26580:2021 calls it a feature catalog (ISO/IEC
2021).
Feature tree
A feature tree is a graphical notation of a feature model.
Member product
A member product is a real-world product that belongs to a product line.
It is specified by a product model.
Reference: ISO/IEC (2021).
Model-based product line engineering
Model-based product line engineering (MBPLE) consists of applying
feature-based PLE in combination with MBSE, where architecture
models are central but not exclusive to the realization of the product
line.
Model-based systems engineering
Model-based systems engineering (MBSE) is systems engineering with
the formal application of models to make engineering information
accessible to machines to support the stakeholders.
Reference: Weilkiens, Vinarcik, and Fischer (2023).
OR feature group
A OR feature group has an upper cardinality larger than 1, which means
that a feature configuration can select more than one sub-feature.
PLE factory configurator
A PLE factory configurator is an automatic process deriving product
models from shared assets supersets for a given feature configuration.
Reference: ISO/IEC (2021).
Product asset instance
See product model.
Product line
A product line represents a family of products, which includes a
variability model, shared assets supersets, product models, and a
product portfolio.
Reference: ISO/IEC (2021).
Product line development
Product line development is the execution of tasks to develop a product
line.
Product line engineering
Product line engineering (PLE) is a discipline that involves developing
similar products or systems with shared features.
Product model
A product model is a model specifying a member product of the product
line. There are more than one product model for a member product, for
example, a SysML product model, a CAD product model, and so forth.
It is derived from shared assets supersets based on a feature
configuration. The ISO/IEC 26580 standard calls it a product asset
instance.
Reference: ISO/IEC (2021).
Product portfolio
A product portfolio defines the set of member products.
Root feature
A root feature is the top-level feature of a feature model.
Shared asset
A shared asset is a digital artifact that contributes to the architecting,
design, fabrication, operation, maintenance, and retirement of a product
(and of its components). It can be a member of one or more shared
assets supersets.
Reference: ISO/IEC (2021).
Shared assets superset
A shared assets superset contains all shared assets needed by all planned
member products.
Reference: ISO/IEC (2021).
Sub-feature
A sub-feature is a feature that acts as a specialized, usually more
detailed, feature of a broader parent feature within the hierarchical tree
structure of a feature model.
Variability model
A variability model contains a feature model and feature configurations.
Variant
A variant is a shared asset that implements a feature.
Variation
A variation is a shared asset that has design choices. It is also called a
variation point.
Variation point
A variation point marks a shared asset that has design choices. It is also
called a variation.
XOR feature group
An XOR feature group is a feature group with lower and upper
cardinality of 1, which means that only exactly one sub-feature of the
feature group can be selected by a feature configuration.
